Additive Density Regression
Eva-Maria Maier, Alexander Fottner, Sonja Greven, Almond Stöcker

Abstract
We present a structured additive regression approach to model conditional densities given scalar covariates, where only samples of the conditional distributions are observed. This links our approach to distributional regression models for scalar data. The model is formulated in a Bayes Hilbert space – preserving nonnegativity and integration to one under summation and scalar multiplication – with respect to an arbitrary finite measure. This allows to consider, amongst others, continuous, discrete and mixed densities. Our theoretical results include asymptotic existence, uniqueness, consistency, and asymptotic normality of the penalized maximum likelihood estimator, as well as confidence regions and inference for the (effect) densities. For estimation, we propose to maximize the penalized log-likelihood corresponding to an appropriate multinomial, or equivalently, Poisson regression model, which we show to approximate the original penalized maximum likelihood problem. We apply our framework to a motivating gender economic data set from the German Socio-Economic Panel Study (SOEP), analyzing the distribution of the woman’s share in a couple’s total labor income given covariate effects for year, place of residence and age of the youngest child. As the income share is a continuous variable having discrete point masses at zero and one for single-earner couples, the corresponding densities are of mixed type. Keywords : Bayes Hilbert Space; Distributional Regression; Structured Additive Model; Penalized Maximum Likelihood Estimation; Mixed Densities; Density-on-Scalar Regression.



1
Introduction

We consider a regression setting with pairs of scalar response and covariate vector observations
(y_{i},\mathbf{x}_{i})
,
i=1,\ldots,N
.
Classical regression approaches model the mean of the conditional response distribution given the covariates
\mathbf{x}_{i}
.
However, it can be very restrictive to account only for dependencies in the mean (and no further characteristics) of, typically, parametric distribution families.

Kneib et al. 2023a
in their ‘Rage Against the Mean’ review different “distributional regression” approaches that overcome this limitation by modeling the whole conditional response distribution. They focus on generalized additive models for location, scale and shape
(also sometimes called distributional regression, e.g.,
Rigby & Stasinopoulos 2005a
;
Klein et al. 2015a
, )
working with parametric distribution families, conditional transformation models and distribution regression modeling the cumulative distribution function (cdf) of the response
(e.g.,
Hothorn et al. 2014a
)
, quantile and expectile regression
(e.g.,
Koenker 2005a
;
Newey & Powell 1987a
)
, and an approach
(e.g.,
Dunson et al. 2007a
)
modeling conditional response densities as a mixture of densities of some known distribution family, with parameters as well as mixture weights depending on the covariates (extended in
Rodríguez-Álvarez et al. 2025a
to structured additive regression models for the mixture means).
While these methods allow for considerable flexibility,
Kneib et al. 2023a
point out that:

“One particular difficulty encountered frequently when starting to apply distributional regression to a given data set is the challenge of interpreting the resulting regression effects. In particular, all models discussed in this review require the analyst to move beyond the simple and convenient ceteris paribus type of interpretation, where the effect of differences in one covariate of interest can be interpreted while keeping all other covariates fixed”

(
Kneib et al. 2023a
, Section 7)
.

We aim to fill this gap, introducing flexible structured additive regression models for conditional response densities that allow for ceteris paribus interpretation of estimated covariate effects, while also developing inference and providing an implementation via our
R
package
DensityRegression
(developer version on
https://github.com/Eva2703/DensityRegression
).
Modeling the density (instead of say quantiles or the cdf) allows for straightforward interpretations and visualizations of bimodalities or shifts of probability mass depending on the covariates, and paves the way for future extensions to bi- or multivariate distributions.
In addition, our approach does not require an assumption of (mixtures of) known parametric distributions.

To respect nonnegativity and integration to one, we consider densities as
elements of a Bayes Hilbert space
(
Egozcue et al. 2006a
;
Boogaart et al. 2014a
)
– an extension of the Aitchison geometry for compositional data
(
Aitchison 1986a
)
. The type of densities is specified via a reference measure, in particular allowing for mixed continuous-discrete densities (as well as the common continuous and discrete special cases).
Those occur in our motivating gender economic application, where we analyze the woman’s share in a couple’s total labor income in Germany depending on the couples’ location in (West or East) Germany, its child status and the year:
While the conditional densities of the woman’s income share can be assumed to be continuous on
(0,1)
for double-earner couples, they exhibit point masses at
0
and
1
reflecting single-earner couples.
In addition to their mixed structure, the conditional densities of the woman’s income share are subject to further modeling challenges
(
Maier et al. 2025c
)
:
The continuous component of such densities is known to be multi-modal for some covariate combinations, which requires flexible nonparametric modeling. Moreover, the categorical and continuous covariates require different types of partial effects in the model (group-specific, smooth, and interactions).
Finally, it is of primary interest to interpret such effects on the women’s income share distribution, an established object of gender economics
(e.g.,
Bertrand et al. 2015a
;
Sprengholz et al. 2022a
)
, rather than to only estimate/predict conditional distributions.
Our approach is designed to account for these challenges:
Formulating our structured additive regression models in Bayes Hilbert spaces allows for mixed densities and (odds ratio) ceteris paribus interpretation of effects building on
Maier et al. 2025c
;
representing the partial effects using a tensor product basis, building on spline bases for mixed densities developed in
Maier et al. 2025c
as well as appropriate bases for the covariate effects, allows for flexible modeling of the shapes of the densities (in particular covering multimodality) and various types of partial effects.
The spline coefficients are estimated via penalized maximum likelihood estimation.
We show that the resulting estimator is asymptotically normally distributed and construct confidence regions and p-values for the estimated effect densities.
As the corresponding likelihood contains an integral term making maximization computationally expensive, we show that the likelihood, the penalized maximum likelihood estimator (PMLE), and its estimated covariance can be approximated via an appropriate multinomial, or equivalently, Poisson regression model obtained by binning the continuous component of the mixed density.

Interestingly, it turns out that modeling densities in Bayes Hilbert spaces – effectively corresponding to a Hilbert space structure on the log-density-level – together with maximum likelihood-based estimation is closely related to conditional logspline density estimation
(e.g.,
Stone 1991c
;
Gu 1995a
)
, despite coming from a very different perspective.

Stone 1991c
consider (unpenalized) maximum likelihood estimation for logspline density estimation, where continuous conditional densities given one continuous covariate are modeled as the normalized exponential function of a predictor given by a B-spline tensor product basis.
For identifiability, they choose a sum-to-zero constraint on the coefficients, similar to the integrate-to-zero constraint arising in the Bayes Hilbert space framework. In subsequent work,
Stone 1994a
also consider multivariate conditional densities with multiple covariates. Both manuscripts focus on theoretical results, deriving optimal rates of convergence, not on the (complicated) computation of the maximum likelihood estimator in practice nor on interpretation or inference.
Mâsse & Truong 1999a
address more practical problems for the setting of
Stone 1991c
, proposing to use a modified Newton algorithm to compute the (unpenalized) maximum likelihood estimator. They give pointwise confidence bounds, pointing out, however, that resulting bands are problematic as they do not only contain densities.

Gu 1995a
propose penalized maximum likelihood estimation for continuous or discrete (and also multivariate) densities given scalar covariates, also based on the logistic density transform. They model the predictor via a (trimmed) analysis of variance (ANOVA) decomposition in a reproducing kernel Hilbert space, which is determined by the roughness functional used for penalization.
The ANOVA components are then modeled via tensor product smoothing splines, where the observations serve as knots, estimated via a Newton algorithm.
Regarding identifiabilty,
Gu 1995a
propose to set some value “averaging” the modeled functions to zero, which our integrate-to-zero constraint is an example of.
They derive rates of convergence for the estimator of the conditional densities, but no asymptotic distribution and (thus) no confidence regions or p-values.
Such conditional logspline density estimation approaches are effectively covered as special cases by our density regression framework (except for differences in estimation details). The formulation in a Bayes Hilbert space adds, in particular, a novel more principled perspective on density modeling, facilitating new modeling flexibility well beyond previous models, practical ways of estimation, valid statistical inference, and means of (odds ratio-type ceteris paribus) interpretation.
At the same time, we are able to draw on some of the previous theory.

Binning procedures for (conditional) density estimation have been previously used:

Eilers & Marx 1996a
discuss histogram smoothing for estimating continuous densities (without covariates), while
Gao & Hastie 2022a
base conditional density estimation via boosted trees effectively on a Poisson histogram model, which they refer to as “Lindsey’s method”
(
Lindsey 1974a
;
Lindsey & Mersch 1992a
)
.
Our binning of the continuous density component’s support is related, while we add a formal proof of convergence.

Bayes Hilbert spaces have been used to model conditional densities before
(
Maier et al. 2025c
;
Jeon & Park 2020a
;
Talská et al. 2018a
)
.
Different from this paper and all of the above citations,
these approaches belong to the class of “distributional response regression”.
Such methods model the mean distribution/density given covariates
with respect to a metric on probability distributions – here the Bayes Hilbert space metric, in other cases, e.g., the
L^{2}
metric after log-quantile/-hazard transforming the densities
(
Han et al. 2020a
)
–,
typically assuming whole probability distributions/densities to be observed rather than samples thereof (with a few exceptions working with empirical distributions using an optimal transport metric,
Zhou & Müller 2024a
, or in Bayes Hilbert spaces,
Jeon & van 2022a
).
In the common case of only samples being available, these methods thus require a two-step procedure, first estimating the “density observations” from the sample, e.g., via kernel density estimation, before the actual analysis.
Such a two-step approach is particularly problematic if only few observations of each conditional density are available, which is usually the case in the presence of continuous covariates.
Also, it carries the well-known problem of zero observations in parts of the domain
(compare, e.g.,
Pawlowsky-Glahn et al. 2015a
)
for the case of distributional response regression in Bayes Hilbert spaces.
We provide a natural solution for these problems in the following by proposing a one-step approach that models the latent conditional density building on
Maier et al. 2025c
, while fitting the model on observed individual observations.

Our structured additive model framework formulated for densities in a Bayes Hilbert space avoids a two-step approach by working with samples of the conditional distributions, but marries distributional regression and distributional response perspectives, and yields a sound, flexible, and practically applicable framework, which to the best of our knowledge is unmatched by existing methods:
1) Our models allow for a variety of types of covariate effects (e.g., categorical, linear, smooth, interactions),
which can be, 2), interpreted ceteris paribus.
3) Penalties may be added to obtain regularization.
4) We provide a unified framework for different types of densities (including discrete, continuous, and mixed ones).
5) Showing asymptotic normality, we derive valid confidence regions and p-values on the level of densities.
6) We reduce the non-standard estimation problem for mixed (and continuous) densities to an additive Poisson model over bins, showing asymptotic equivalence for decreasing bin sizes. 7) We provide an implementation in our own
R
package
DensityRegression
, building on the numerous specification possibilities and estimation efficiency of the well-known
R
package
mgcv

(
Wood 2017c
)
for estimating our Poisson models.

Our structured additive density regression model and estimation approach are introduced in Section
2
, together with our theoretical results.
In Section
3
, we use our method to analyze female income share distributions, before presenting a simulation study based thereon in Section
4

and concluding with a brief summary and discussion in Section
5
.

2
Additive Density Regression

In this section, we present our penalized maximum-likelihood approach for estimating conditional densities from random samples.
Since densities are viewed as elements of a Bayes Hilbert space, we first introduce these spaces in Section
2.1
,
before formulating our structured additive density-on-scalar regression model and deriving the corresponding log-likelihood function in Section
2.2
.
We show that the PMLE asymptotically exists and is unique, consistent and asymptotically normally distributed.
Furthermore, we construct confidence regions. In Section
2.3
, we show that the log-likelihood, the PMLE and the penalized Fisher information can be approximated using a multinomial or equivalently Poisson distributional assumption.
Finally, we discuss interpretation of estimated effects in Section
2.4
.
Proofs are provided in appendix
6
.

2.1
Bayes Hilbert spaces

We briefly summarize Bayes Hilbert spaces, following our more detailed introduction in appendix A of
Maier et al. 2025c
based on
Boogaart et al. 2010a
;
Boogaart et al. 2014a
.
These manuscripts also provide proofs for all statements.

Consider a measure space
(\mathcal{Y},\mathcal{A},\mu)
, where
\mu
is a finite measure called
reference measure
.
Denote the set of
\sigma
-finite measures with the same null sets as
\mu
by
\mathcal{M}(\mu)=\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{Y},\mathcal{A},\mu)
.
Then,
there exists a
\mu
-almost everywhere (
\mu
-a.e.) positive and unique density with respect to
\mu
(Radon-Nikodym derivative)
for every measure in
\mathcal{M}(\mu)
.
The addendum “
\mu
-a.e.” is omitted in the remaining work for the sake of readability.
We thus can identify each measure in
\mathcal{M}(\mu)
with its density. Note that densities in
\mathcal{M}(\mu)
not necessarily integrate to one.
However, proportionality with respect to positive constants defines an equivalence relation
\propto
on the densities in
\mathcal{M}(\mu)
. We call the corresponding set of
\propto
-equivalence classes
Bayes space (with reference measure
\mu
)
and denote it by
\mathcal{B}(\mu)=\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{Y},\mathcal{A},\mu)
.
For an equivalence class
[f]\in\mathcal{B}(\mu)
containing densities with finite integral, the respective probability density
f/\int_{\mathcal{Y}}f\,\mathrm{d}\mu
is chosen as representative.
For the sake of readability, we omit the square brackets denoting equivalence classes in the following.
The
Bayes Hilbert space (with reference measure
\mu
)
is the set

B^{2}(\mu)=B^{2}(\mathcal{Y},\mathcal{A},\mu):=\{f\in\mathcal{B}(\mu)\penalty\ |\penalty\ \int_{\mathcal{Y}}\left(\log f\right)^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\mu<\infty\}
.
It is a vector space with addition (
perturbation
) and scalar multiplication (
powering
)
f_{1}\oplus f_{2}:=f_{1}\,f_{2}

and

\alpha\odot f_{1}:=(f_{1})^{\alpha}

for

f_{1},f_{2}\in B^{2}(\mu)

and
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}
.
The additive neutral element is
0:=\frac{1}{\mu(\mathcal{Y})}\in B^{2}(\mu)
, i.e., the equivalence class of constant functions, the additive inverse element of

f\in B^{2}(\mu)
is

\ominus f:=\frac{1}{f}
, and the multiplicative neutral element is
1\in\mathbb{R}
.
Consider the
centered log-ratio (clr) transformation

\clr[f]:=\log f-\frac{1}{\mu(\mathcal{Y})}\,\int_{\mathcal{Y}}\log f\,\mathrm{d}\mu
,
mapping an equivalence class of densities
f\in B^{2}(\mu)
to a function in
L^{2}_{0}(\mu)=L^{2}_{0}(\mathcal{Y},\mathcal{A},\mu):=\{\tilde{f}\in L^{2}(\mu)=L^{2}(\mathcal{Y},\mathcal{A},\mu)\penalty\ |\penalty\ \int_{\mathcal{Y}}\tilde{f}\,\mathrm{d}\mu=0\}
, which is a closed subspace of
L^{2}(\mu)
.
The clr transformation is bijective with inverse transformation
\clr^{-1}[\tilde{f}]=\exp\tilde{f}
.
Furthermore, it is a linear transformation, i.e., it is an isomorphism.
Then,
B^{2}(\mu)
is a Hilbert space with inner product

\langle f_{1},f_{2}\rangle_{B^{2}(\mu)}:=\int_{\mathcal{Y}}\operatorname{clr}[f_{1}]\cdot\operatorname{clr}[f_{2}]\,\mathrm{d}\mu

for

f_{1},f_{2}\in B^{2}(\mu)
.
Note that per definition

\langle f_{1},f_{2}\rangle_{B^{2}(\mu)}=\langle\clr[f_{1}],\clr[f_{2}]\rangle_{L^{2}(\mu)}
, i.e., the clr transformation is isometric.

While Bayes Hilbert spaces are defined for arbitrary measurable spaces
(\mathcal{Y},\mathcal{A})
as long as the reference measure
\mu
is finite,
we focus on
\mathcal{Y}\subset\mathbb{R}
with three common cases
(
Maier et al. 2025c
)
:
A
continuous
Bayes Hilbert space corresponds to
\mathcal{Y}=\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c}}=[a,b]
for
a,b\in\mathbb{R}
with
a<b
,
where
\mathcal{A}=\mathcal{B}
is the Borel
\sigma
-algebra restricted to
\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c}}
, and
\mu=\lambda
is the Lebesgue measure.
For a
discrete
Bayes Hilbert space, we have
\mathcal{Y}=\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{d}}=\{t_{1},\ldots,t_{D}\}
with
\mathcal{A}=\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{d}})
the power set of
\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{d}}
and
\mu=\delta:=\sum_{d=1}^{D}w_{d}\delta_{t_{d}}
a weighted sum of Dirac measures with
w_{d}>0
for all
d=1,\ldots,D
.
A
mixed
Bayes Hilbert space combines both of the aforementioned cases, with
\mathcal{Y}=\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c}}\cup\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{d}}
,
\mathcal{A}
chosen to be the smallest
\sigma
-algebra containing all closed subintervals of
\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c}}
and all points of
\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{d}}
, and

\mu=\delta+\lambda
.
If
\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{d}}\subset\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c}}
, we obtain
\mathcal{A}=\mathcal{B}
.
Allowing either
\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{d}}
or
\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c}}
to be empty includes continuous and discrete Bayes Hilbert spaces as special cases, thus it suffices to consider mixed Bayes Hilbert spaces in the following.

2.2
Penalized Maximum Likelihood Estimation for Additive Density Regression

In this section, we introduce our penalized maximum-likelihood approach to estimate conditional densities given scalar covariates.
For this purpose, let
B^{2}(\mu)
be a mixed Bayes Hilbert space and
\mathcal{X}=\bigtimes_{p=1}^{P}\mathcal{X}_{p}\subset\mathbb{R}^{P},\,P\in\mathbb{N}
, a covariate space, such that each
\mathcal{X}_{p}
is either a finite discrete set (categorical covariate)
or a compact non-trivial interval (continuous covariate).
We consider independent observations
(y_{i},\mathbf{x}_{i})\in\mathcal{Y}\times\mathcal{X}
for
i=1,\ldots,N,\penalty\ N\in\mathbb{N}
, where the
y_{i}
are realizations of the conditional distribution of
Y_{i}\penalty\ |\penalty\ \mathbf{x}_{i}
, whose density
f_{\mathbf{x}_{i}}\in B^{2}(\mu)
is not observed directly.
Assuming
(\mathbf{x},y)\mapsto f_{\mathbf{x}}(y)
is continuous on
\mathcal{X}\times(\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c}}\setminus\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{d}})
and
\mathbf{x}\mapsto f_{\mathbf{x}}(y)
is continuous on
\mathcal{X}
for every
y\in\mathcal{Y}
(which is both trivially fulfilled for categorical covariates)
we consider a structured additive density-on-scalar regression model

\displaystyle f_{\mathbf{x}_{i}}=\bigoplus_{j=1}^{J}h_{j}(\mathbf{x}_{i}),

(2.1)

where
J\in\mathbb{N}
and
h_{j}(\mathbf{x})\in B^{2}(\mu)
are partial effects depending on no, one, or more covariates in
\mathbf{x}=(x_{1},\ldots,x_{P})
.
E.g.,
h_{j}(\mathbf{x})
can be an intercept
\beta_{0}
, a linear effect
x_{k}\odot\beta
, a smooth effect
g_{1}(x_{k})
, a group-specific intercept
\beta_{x_{m}}
or smooth effect
g_{x_{m}}(x_{k})
, or a smooth interaction
g_{2}(x_{k},x_{l})
, all in
B^{2}(\mu)
, where
x_{k},x_{l}
denote continuous covariates and
x_{m}
a categorical covariate (grouping variable), for
k,l,m\in\{1,\ldots,P\}
pairwise distinct.
For more possible partial effects, see also Table B.1 in appendix B of
Maier et al. 2025c
.
To obtain identifiable models, we usually need to include constraints on the
h_{j}
.
For a model including an intercept, we thus center the partial effects, i.e.,

\frac{1}{N}\odot\bigoplus_{i=1}^{N}h_{j}(\mathbf{x}_{i})=0
for all
j=1,\ldots,J
.
Analogously, centering of interaction effects around the main effects can be obtained.
For more details see
Wood 2017c
, in particular Section 1.8.1 for inclusion of such constraints. Each partial effect is represented via a tensor product basis as

\displaystyle h_{j}(\mathbf{x}_{i})=\left(\mathbf{b}_{\mathcal{X},\,j}(\mathbf{x}_{i})\begin{picture}\put(1.5,0.6){\circle{2.0}}\put(1.5,0.6){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){$\otimes$}}\end{picture}\mathbf{b}_{\mathcal{Y}}\right)^{\top}\boldsymbol{\theta}_{j}=\bigoplus_{n=1}^{K_{\mathcal{X},\,j}}\bigoplus_{m=1}^{K_{\mathcal{Y}}}\theta_{j,n,m}\odot b_{\mathcal{X},\,j,n}(\mathbf{x}_{i})\odot b_{\mathcal{Y},\,m},

where
\mathbf{b}_{\mathcal{X},\,j}=(b_{\mathcal{X},\,j,1},\ldots,b_{\mathcal{X},\,j,K_{\mathcal{X},\,j}})^{\top}:\mathcal{X}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{K_{\mathcal{X},\,j}}
and
\mathbf{b}_{\mathcal{Y}}=(b_{\mathcal{Y},\,1},\ldots,b_{\mathcal{Y},\,K_{\mathcal{Y}}})^{\top}\in B^{2}(\mu)^{K_{\mathcal{Y}}}
are vectors of basis functions over the covariates and over
\mathcal{Y}
, respectively,
\boldsymbol{\theta}_{j}=(\theta_{j,1,1},\ldots,\theta_{j,K_{\mathcal{X},\,j},K_{\mathcal{Y}}})^{\top}\in\mathbb{R}^{K_{\mathcal{X},\,j}\,K_{\mathcal{Y}}}
is the unknown coefficient vector, and
denotes the Kronecker product of a real-valued matrix with a
B^{2}(\mu)
-valued matrix, defined by replacing multiplication with
\odot
in the usual Kronecker product of two real-valued matrices.
Accordingly, matrix multiplication of a real-valued with a
B^{2}(\mu)
-valued matrix is defined by using
\oplus
instead of sums and
\odot
instead of products in the usual matrix multiplication.
Note that different (numbers of) basis functions over
\mathcal{Y}
per
j
are possible, but not carried out for simplicity of notation.
The basis functions
\mathbf{b}_{\mathcal{X},\,j}
are chosen according to the presumed partial effect
h_{j}
.
E.g., a linear effect of a continuous covariate
x_{k},\,k\in\{1,\ldots,P\}
, is obtained by
\mathbf{b}_{\mathcal{X},\,j}:\mathcal{X}\rightarrow\mathbb{R},\mathbf{x}\mapsto x_{k}
, a smooth effect by a B-spline basis. Regularization can be obtained by including penalty matrices
\mathbf{P}_{\mathcal{X},\,j}\in\mathbb{R}^{K_{\mathcal{X},\,j}\times K_{\mathcal{X},\,j}}

(e.g., a difference penalty for a smooth effect)
as discussed below. Regarding the choice of
\mathbf{b}_{\mathcal{Y}}\in B^{2}(\mu)^{K_{\mathcal{Y}}}
, we first consider the discrete and continuous special cases, i.e,
\mathcal{Y}=\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c}}
and
\mu=\lambda
, or
\mathcal{Y}=\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{d}}
and
\mu=\delta
.
In both cases, we choose an appropriate basis in
L^{2}(\mu)^{K_{\mathcal{Y}}+1}
, transform it to
L^{2}_{0}(\mu)^{K_{\mathcal{Y}}}
(as in appendix D of
Maier et al. 2025c
), and apply the inverse clr transformation component-wise.
As initial basis in
L^{2}(\mu)^{K_{\mathcal{Y}}+1}
, we suggest a B-spline basis with pairwise distinct knots and degree at least
2
in the continuous case and a basis consisting of the indicator functions
\mathbbm{1}_{\{t_{d}\}}
for
d=1,\ldots,D=K_{\mathcal{Y}}+1
in the discrete case.
Transformed difference penalties
\mathbf{P}_{\mathcal{Y}}\in\mathbb{R}^{K_{\mathcal{Y}}\times K_{\mathcal{Y}}}

(as in appendix D of
Maier et al. 2025c
)

can be added as discussed below to obtain smoother estimates.
In the mixed case, we construct
\mathbf{b}_{\mathcal{Y}}\in B^{2}(\mu)^{K_{\mathcal{Y}}}
using both cases:

Maier et al. 2025c
show that a mixed Bayes Hilbert space
B^{2}(\mu)
, can be orthogonally decomposed into a continuous one,
B^{2}(\lambda)=B^{2}\left(\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c}},\mathcal{B}\cap\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c}},\lambda\right)
, and a discrete one,
B^{2}(\delta^{\bullet})=B^{2}\left(\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{d}}^{\bullet},\mathcal{P}\left(\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{d}}^{\bullet}\right),\delta^{\bullet}\right)
, where
\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{d}}^{\bullet}:=\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{d}}\cup\{t_{D+1}\}
for some
t_{D+1}\in\mathbb{R}\setminus\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{d}}
and
\delta^{\bullet}:=\sum_{d=1}^{D+1}w_{d}\,\delta_{t_{d}}
with
w_{D+1}:=\lambda(\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c}})
.
Both are subspaces of
B^{2}(\mu)
via the embeddings

\iota_{\mathrm{c}}:B^{2}(\lambda)\hookrightarrow B^{2}(\mu)
with
\iota_{\mathrm{c}}(f_{\mathrm{c}})=f_{\mathrm{c}}
on
\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c}}\setminus\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{d}}
and
\iota_{\mathrm{c}}(f_{\mathrm{c}})=\exp(\frac{1}{\lambda(\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c}})}\int_{\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c}}}\log f_{\mathrm{c}}\,\mathrm{d}\lambda)
on
\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{d}}
and
\iota_{\mathrm{d}}:B^{2}(\delta^{\bullet})\hookrightarrow B^{2}(\mu)
with
\iota_{\mathrm{d}}(f_{\mathrm{d}})=f_{\mathrm{d}}\left(t_{D+1}\right)
on
\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c}}\setminus\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{d}}
and
\iota_{\mathrm{d}}(f_{\mathrm{d}})=f_{\mathrm{d}}
on
\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{d}}
.
Thus, applying
\iota_{\mathrm{c}}
and
\iota_{\mathrm{d}}
to bases
\mathbf{b}_{\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c}}}\in B^{2}(\lambda)^{K_{\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c}}}}
and
\mathbf{b}_{\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{d}}^{\bullet}}\in B^{2}(\delta^{\bullet})^{K_{\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{d}}^{\bullet}}}
component-wise yields a basis in
B^{2}(\mu)^{K_{\mathcal{Y}}}
with
K_{\mathcal{Y}}=K_{\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c}}}+K_{\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{d}}^{\bullet}}
.
The penalty matrix
\mathbf{P}_{\mathcal{Y}}\in\mathbb{R}^{K_{\mathcal{Y}}\times K_{\mathcal{Y}}}

is obtained from the ones in the special cases as the block diagonal matrix
\mathrm{diag}(\mathbf{P}_{\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c}}},\mathbf{P}_{\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{d}}})

(yielding separate regularization in the continuous and discrete components).

To emphasize the dependence on the coefficient vector
\boldsymbol{\theta}
, we write
f_{\mathbf{x}_{i},\boldsymbol{\theta}}
for
f_{\mathbf{x}_{i}}
in the following.
Applying the clr transformation to (
2.1
) and using its linearity yields

\displaystyle\tilde{f}_{\mathbf{x}_{i},\boldsymbol{\theta}}:=\clr[f_{\mathbf{x}_{i},\boldsymbol{\theta}}]

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=1}^{J}\sum_{n=1}^{K_{\mathcal{X},\,j}}\sum_{m=1}^{K_{\mathcal{Y}}}\theta_{j,n,m}\,b_{\mathcal{X},\,j,n}(\mathbf{x}_{i})\,\tilde{b}_{\mathcal{Y},m}=\sum_{j=1}^{J}\left(\mathbf{b}_{\mathcal{X},\,j}(\mathbf{x}_{i})\otimes\tilde{\mathbf{b}}_{\mathcal{Y}}\right)^{\top}\boldsymbol{\theta}_{j}

\displaystyle=\left(\mathbf{b}_{\mathcal{X}}(\mathbf{x}_{i})\otimes\tilde{\mathbf{b}}_{\mathcal{Y}}\right)^{\top}\boldsymbol{\theta}=\tilde{\mathbf{b}}(\mathbf{x}_{i})^{\top}\boldsymbol{\theta},

(2.2)

where
\tilde{\mathbf{b}}_{\mathcal{Y}}:=(\tilde{b}_{\mathcal{Y},\,1},\ldots,\tilde{b}_{\mathcal{Y},\,K_{\mathcal{Y}}})^{\top}
with

\tilde{b}_{\mathcal{Y},m}:=\clr[b_{\mathcal{Y},\,m}]
,

\mathbf{b}_{\mathcal{X}}=(\mathbf{b}_{\mathcal{X},\,1}^{\top},\ldots,\mathbf{b}_{\mathcal{X},\,J}^{\top})^{\top}:\mathcal{X}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{K_{\mathcal{X}}}
for
K_{\mathcal{X}}:=\sum_{j=1}^{J}K_{\mathcal{X},\,j}
,

\boldsymbol{\theta}=(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{1}^{\top},\ldots,\boldsymbol{\theta}_{J}^{\top})^{\top}\in\mathbb{R}^{K}
for
K:=K_{\mathcal{X}}\,K_{\mathcal{Y}}
,
and
\tilde{\mathbf{b}}(\mathbf{x}):=\mathbf{b}_{\mathcal{X}}(\mathbf{x})\otimes\tilde{\mathbf{b}}_{\mathcal{Y}}\in L_{0}^{2}(\mu)^{K}
for
\mathbf{x}\in\mathcal{X}
.
To estimate the coefficient vector
\boldsymbol{\theta}
, consider the likelihood

\mathcal{L}(\boldsymbol{\theta}):=\prod_{i=1}^{N}f_{\mathbf{x}_{i},\boldsymbol{\theta}}(y_{i})=\prod_{i=1}^{N}\frac{\exp(\tilde{f}_{\mathbf{x}_{i},\boldsymbol{\theta}}(y_{i}))}{\int\exp(\tilde{f}_{\mathbf{x}_{i},\boldsymbol{\theta}})\,\mathrm{d}\mu},
which yields the log-likelihood

\displaystyle\ell(\boldsymbol{\theta})

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{N}\Bigl(\tilde{\mathbf{b}}(\mathbf{x}_{i})(y_{i})^{\top}\boldsymbol{\theta}-\log\int_{\mathcal{Y}}\exp\bigl[\tilde{\mathbf{b}}(\mathbf{x}_{i})^{\top}\boldsymbol{\theta}\bigr]\,\mathrm{d}\mu\Bigr).

(2.3)

Regularization is obtained by adding a penalty term

\pen(\boldsymbol{\theta}):=-\sum_{j=1}^{J}\boldsymbol{\theta}_{j}^{\top}\mathbf{P}_{j}\boldsymbol{\theta}_{j}=-\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\top}\mathbf{P}\boldsymbol{\theta}
,
resulting in the penalized log-likelihood

\ell_{\mathrm{pen}}(\boldsymbol{\theta}):=\ell(\boldsymbol{\theta})+\pen(\boldsymbol{\theta})
.
Here,

\mathbf{P}_{j}=\xi_{\mathcal{X},\,j}(\mathbf{P}_{\mathcal{X},\,j}\otimes\mathbf{I}_{K_{\mathcal{Y}}})+\xi_{\mathcal{Y},\,j}(\mathbf{I}_{K_{\mathcal{X},\,j}}\otimes\mathbf{P}_{\mathcal{Y}})

penalizes the
j
-th partial effect with smoothing parameters
\xi_{\mathcal{X},\,j},\xi_{\mathcal{Y},\,j}\geq 0
in the respective directions,
j=1,\ldots,J
, and

\mathbf{P}
is the block diagonal matrix
\mathrm{diag}(\mathbf{P}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{P}_{J})\in\mathbb{R}^{K\times K}
.
Simplifications like
\xi_{\mathcal{Y},\,j}=\xi_{\mathcal{Y}}
or
\xi_{\mathcal{X},\,j}=\xi_{\mathcal{Y},\,j}=\xi_{j}
(also known as isotropic penalty) for all
j\in\{1,\ldots,J\}
are possible.
The smoothing parameters can be determined, e.g., by restricted maximum likelihood (REML) optimization
(
Wood 2017c
, Section 6.5.2)
.
In the following, the special case

\mathbf{P}=\mathbf{0}
(corresponding to
\xi_{\mathcal{X},\,j}=0=\xi_{\mathcal{Y},\,j}
for all
j=1,\ldots,J
),
yielding the unpenalized log-likelihood, is always included,
the considered model is assumed to be identifiable,
and basis functions and penalty matrices are chosen as proposed above (basic properties necessary for the validity of the theorems/lemmas but fulfilled for standard basis choices are given in appendix
6.1.2
).
Note that then,
\ell_{\pen}
is defined on

\mathbb{R}^{K}
.

In the remainder of this section, we derive theoretical properties of the PMLE
\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}\in\mathbb{R}^{K}
of
\ell_{\pen}
.
For this purpose,
let
\boldsymbol{\nu}
be the product measure on
\mathcal{X}
consisting of Lebesgue measures for continuous covariates and sums of Dirac measures at the finitely many possible values for categorical covariates,
let
\mathbb{S}^{K-1}
be the unit sphere in
\mathbb{R}^{K}
, and

s_{\mathbf{v},\mathbf{x}}:\mathcal{Y}\rightarrow\mathbb{R},y\mapsto\tilde{\mathbf{b}}(\mathbf{x})(y)^{\top}\mathbf{v}

for
\mathbf{x}\in\mathcal{X}
and
\mathbf{v}\in\mathbb{S}^{K-1}
.
Consider the following assumptions:

(BX)

For
\mathbf{v}_{\mathcal{X}}\in\mathbb{R}^{K_{\mathcal{X}}}
:
If
\mathbf{b}_{\mathcal{X}}(\mathbf{x}_{i})^{\top}\mathbf{v}_{\mathcal{X}}=0
for all
i=1,\ldots,N
, then
\mathbf{v}_{\mathcal{X}}=\mathbf{0}
.

(BX’)

There exist
M^{\prime}>0
and
N^{\prime}\in\mathbb{N}
such that for all
N\geq N^{\prime}
and all
\mathbf{v}_{\mathcal{X}}\in\mathbb{R}^{K_{\mathcal{X}}}
:

M^{\prime}\,N\int_{\mathcal{X}}\left(\mathbf{b}_{\mathcal{X}}(\mathbf{x})^{\top}\mathbf{v}_{\mathcal{X}}\right)^{2}\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{\nu}(\mathbf{x})\leq\sum_{i=1}^{N}\left(\mathbf{b}_{\mathcal{X}}(\mathbf{x}_{i})^{\top}\mathbf{v}_{\mathcal{X}}\right)^{2}
.

(S
<
)

For all
\mathbf{v}\in\mathbb{S}^{K-1}
there exists
i\in\{1,\ldots,N\}
with
s_{\mathbf{v},\mathbf{x}_{i}}(y_{i})<\sup_{y\in\mathcal{Y}}s_{\mathbf{v},\mathbf{x}_{i}}(y)
.

All of them are rather mild (see also appendix
6.1.3
, which particularly includes the remarks/lemmas referred to in the following):
Assumption
(BX)

is fulfilled, if the matrix

\mathbf{B}_{\mathcal{X}}\in\mathbb{R}^{N\times K_{\mathcal{X}}}

containing
\mathbf{b}_{\mathcal{X}}(\mathbf{x}_{i})
in the
i
-th row,
i=1,\ldots,N
,
has rank
K_{\mathcal{X}}
, see Lemma
6.5

1)
.
This corresponds to the (marginal) design matrix having full column rank, which is a very common assumption for regression models.
In our case of
\mathbf{B}_{\mathcal{X}}
,
it
can be fulfilled by suitably choosing the covariate basis for given observations:
For
j\in\{1,\ldots,J\}
, the basis functions
\mathbf{b}_{\mathcal{X},\,j}
have to be chosen such that
(i) their number
K_{\mathcal{X},\,j}
is at most the number of unique observed values of the subvector of covariates, on which the
j
-th partial effect depends,
and
(ii) all functions contained in
\mathbf{b}_{\mathcal{X},\,j}
have at least one observation in their support. See Remark
6.2

1)
for a more detailed discussion and derivation of (i) and (ii).
Note that, for
N\geq N^{\prime}
,
assumption
(BX)
is furthermore implied by assumption
(BX’)
, which itself is fulfilled, if the observations of the covariates are spread reasonably over their domain with growing sample size (compare assumption
(BX”)
in appendix
6.1.3

for the formal statement), see Lemma
6.5

2)
.
Moreover, note that
(BX)
is equivalent to the nonsingular-condition for logspline regression models
(e.g.,
Stone 1991c
)
, while assumptions
(BX’)
and
(BX”)
are inspired by (1) and (2) of
Stone 1991c
. Finally, regarding assumption
(S

<

)
, note that under assumption
(BX)
, for every
\mathbf{v}\in\mathbb{S}^{K-1}
there exists an
i\in\{1,\ldots,N\}
such that

s_{\mathbf{v},\mathbf{x}_{i}}
is not constant (Lemma
6.3
) and thus, the assumption can be fulfilled. Furthermore, under assumption
(BX”)
, not only does
(BX)
hold, but also the probability that
(S

<

)
holds tends to one for
N\rightarrow\infty
, see Lemma
6.5

3)
. The following theorem then yields asymptotic existence of the PMLE
\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}
.

Theorem 2.1
.

Under
(BX)
,
\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}
is unique, if it exists.
If
(S

<

)
holds as well,

\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}
exists.

Asymptotic existence is also contained as byproduct in the following Theorem
2.2
on consistency and asymptotic normality.
While these two properties of the PMLE are well-known for “regular log-likelihoods”, this term is often not specified further in the literature, particularly in our case of non-identically distributed (but independent) observations. In contrast, we clearly state all necessary assumptions and provide proof in appendix
6.2
, which also includes a brief discussion of literature. Assuming the true functions
h_{j}
are in the span of the chosen basis functions,
j=1,\ldots,J
, denote the unknown true parameter vector with
\boldsymbol{\vartheta}=(\boldsymbol{\vartheta}_{1}^{\top},\ldots,\boldsymbol{\vartheta}_{J}^{\top})^{\top}
.
Furthermore, define the penalized Fisher information
\mathbf{F}_{\mathrm{pen}}(\boldsymbol{\theta}):=-D^{2}\ell_{\mathrm{pen}}(\boldsymbol{\theta})
,
set
\xi_{\max}:=\max\{\xi_{\mathcal{X},\,1},\ldots,\xi_{\mathcal{X},\,J},\xi_{\mathcal{Y},\,1},\ldots,\xi_{\mathcal{Y},\,J}\}
,
and let
\mathbf{Z}_{N}=\ocal_{\mathbb{P}}(a_{N})
denote
\frac{\mathbf{Z}_{N}}{a_{N}}
converging in probability to
\mathbf{0}
for random vectors
\mathbf{Z}_{N}
and real numbers
a_{N},\,N\in\mathbb{N}
.

Theorem 2.2
.

Assume that
(BX’)
holds.

1)

If

\xi_{\max}=\ocal_{\mathbb{P}}(N)
,
the probability that
\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}
exists in an arbitrary small neighborhood around
\boldsymbol{\vartheta}
converges to one for
N\rightarrow\infty
and
\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}
is a consistent estimator for
\boldsymbol{\vartheta}
.

2)

If

\xi_{\max}=\ocal_{\mathbb{P}}(\sqrt{N})
,
then

\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}\overset{\text{a}}{\sim}\mathcal{N}(\boldsymbol{\vartheta},\mathbf{F}_{\mathrm{pen}}(\boldsymbol{\vartheta})^{-1})
.

Regarding
2)
, note that
\mathbf{F}_{\pen}(\boldsymbol{\theta})
is invertible and equals its expectation for all
\boldsymbol{\theta}\in\mathbb{R}^{K}
(see Proposition
6.6
in appendix
6.1.4
).
Concerning the assumptions on the limiting behavior of

\xi_{\max}
, note that the one
in
2)
implies the one
in
1)
.
Assumptions like these are common in the literature, with similar (but more restrictive) ones, e.g., in
Kauermann et al. 2009a
or
Claeskens et al. 2009a
.

Based on this asymptotic distribution, we construct confidence regions for (clr transformed) sums of partial effects, denoting the
(1-\alpha)
-quantile of the
\chi^{2}(K_{\mathcal{Y}})
distribution with
\chi^{2}_{1-\alpha}(K_{\mathcal{Y}})
for
\alpha\in(0,1)
and its cumulative distribution function with
F_{\chi^{2}(K_{\mathcal{Y}})}
:

Lemma 2.3
.

Set

h_{\mathcal{J}}(\mathbf{x}):=\bigoplus_{j\in\mathcal{J}}h_{j}(\mathbf{x})=\bigoplus_{j\in\mathcal{J}}(\mathbf{b}_{\mathcal{X},\,j}(\mathbf{x})\begin{picture}\put(1.5,0.6){\circle{2.0}}\put(1.5,0.6){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){$\otimes$}}\end{picture}\mathbf{b}_{\mathcal{Y}})^{\top}\boldsymbol{\vartheta}_{j}
for
\mathbf{x}\in\mathcal{X}
and
\mathcal{J}\subseteq\{1,\ldots,J\}
.
Denote the matrix mapping the vector
\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}
to its
j
-th subvector
\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{j}
by
\mathbf{S}_{j}\in\mathbb{R}^{K_{\mathcal{X},\,j}K_{\mathcal{Y}}\times K}
and set

\mathbf{A}:=\sum_{j\in\mathcal{J}}(\mathbf{b}_{\mathcal{X},\,j}(\mathbf{x})^{\top}\otimes\mathbf{I}_{K_{\mathcal{Y}}})\mathbf{S}_{j}\in\mathbb{R}^{K_{\mathcal{Y}}\times K}
.
Set

\boldsymbol{\vartheta}_{\mathcal{Y}}:=\mathbf{A}\boldsymbol{\vartheta}
,

\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{\mathcal{Y}}:=\mathbf{A}\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}
,
and

\hat{\mathbf{V}}_{\mathcal{Y}}:=\mathbf{A}\mathbf{F}_{\mathrm{pen}}(\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}})^{-1}\mathbf{A}^{\top}
.
Assume
(BX’)
and
\xi_{\max}=\ocal_{\mathbb{P}}(\sqrt{N})
.
Then,
CR_{\boldsymbol{\vartheta}_{\mathcal{Y}}}:=\{\boldsymbol{\theta}_{\mathcal{Y}}\in\mathbb{R}^{K_{\mathcal{Y}}}\penalty\ |\penalty\ (\boldsymbol{\theta}_{\mathcal{Y}}-\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{\mathcal{Y}})^{\top}\hat{\mathbf{V}}_{\mathcal{Y}}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{\mathcal{Y}}-\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{\mathcal{Y}})\leq\chi^{2}_{\alpha}(K_{\mathcal{Y}})\}
,
is an asymptotic
\alpha\cdot 100\%
confidence region for
\boldsymbol{\vartheta}_{\mathcal{Y}}
,

\{\mathbf{b}_{\mathcal{Y}}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\theta}_{\mathcal{Y}}\penalty\ |\penalty\ \boldsymbol{\theta}_{\mathcal{Y}}\in CR_{\boldsymbol{\vartheta}_{\mathcal{Y}}}\}\subseteq B^{2}(\mu)
is an asymptotic
\alpha\cdot 100\%
confidence region for
h_{\mathcal{J}}(\mathbf{x})
, and

\{\tilde{\mathbf{b}}_{\mathcal{Y}}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\theta}_{\mathcal{Y}}\penalty\ |\penalty\ \boldsymbol{\theta}_{\mathcal{Y}}\in CR_{\boldsymbol{\vartheta}_{\mathcal{Y}}}\}\subseteq L_{0}^{2}(\mu)
is an asymptotic
\alpha\cdot 100\%
confidence region for

\clr[h_{\mathcal{J}}(\mathbf{x})]
.
Furthermore,
1-F_{\chi^{2}(K_{\mathcal{Y}})}(\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{\mathcal{Y}}^{\top}\hat{\mathbf{V}}_{\mathcal{Y}}^{-1}\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{\mathcal{Y}})
is a valid asymptotic p-value for
H_{0}:h_{\mathcal{J}}(\mathbf{x})=0\in B^{2}(\mu)
and for
H_{0}:\clr[h_{\mathcal{J}}(\mathbf{x})]=0\in L^{2}_{0}(\mu)
.

The special cases
\mathcal{J}=\{j\},\,j\in\{1,\ldots,J\}
, and
\mathcal{J}=\{1,\ldots,J\}
with
\mathbf{x}=\mathbf{x}_{i}
for an
i\in\{1,\ldots,N\}

yield confidence regions for the
j
-th partial effect and the density
f_{\mathbf{x}_{i},\boldsymbol{\vartheta}}
. Note that the confidence regions constructed in Lemma
2.3
are simultaneous over the domain
\mathcal{Y}
, but point-wise regarding the covariates.
Confidence regions and p-values, which are also simultaneous in the covariates for the
j
-th partial effect, can be obtained directly from the asymptotic distribution of
\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{j}
, compare Lemma
6.15
in appendix
6.2
.

2.3
Relation to Multinomial and Poisson Regression

The integral term in (
2.3
) makes maximizing
\ell_{\mathrm{pen}}
computationally expensive and requires specialized implementations. We thus show that
\ell_{\mathrm{pen}}(\boldsymbol{\theta})
and
\mathbf{F}_{\mathrm{pen}}(\boldsymbol{\theta})

can be approximated by a (shifted) penalized log-likelihood/Fisher information obtained from multinomially distributed data, which is constructed from the original response observations
y_{1},\ldots,y_{N}
by combining all observations of the same conditional distribution (i.e., all observations sharing identical values in all covariates) into a vector of counts via a histogram on
\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c}}\setminus\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{d}}
and counts on
\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{d}}
.
We show convergence for decreasing bin width and that this also transfers to the PMLEs under mild assumptions.
Furthermore, we derive that Poisson regression can be used equivalently to multinomial regression.
This is very appealing, since Poisson regression is well-known and flexible additive implementations are available.
Thus, in our
R
package
DensityRegression
we build on
mgcv

(
Wood 2017c
)
for fitting Poisson models.

To formally construct the multinomial data, let
\mathbf{x}^{(1)},\ldots,\mathbf{x}^{(L)}\in\mathcal{X}
be the unique combinations of observed covariate values and let
\mathcal{I}^{(l)}:=\{i\in\{1,\ldots,N\}\penalty\ |\penalty\ \mathbf{x}_{i}=\mathbf{x}^{(l)}\}
for
l=1,\ldots,L\leq N
denote the corresponding partition of indices.
Thus,
\{y_{i}\penalty\ |\penalty\ i\in\mathcal{I}^{(l)}\}
forms an independent and identically distributed random sample of
Y^{(l)}\penalty\ |\penalty\ \mathbf{x}^{(l)}
.
Note that in the presence of continuous covariates, we usually have distinct observed covariate values, i.e.,
\mathcal{I}^{(l)}=\{l\}
for
l=1,\ldots,L=N
, with each observation sampled from a different conditional distribution.
In the following, let
l\in\{1,\ldots,L\}
.
The set
\mathcal{I}^{(l)}
is partitioned depending on the corresponding response observation belonging to the discrete component or not, i.e.,

\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{d}}^{(l)}:=\{i\in\mathcal{I}^{(l)}\penalty\ |\penalty\ y_{i}\in\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{d}}\}

and

\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{c}}^{(l)}:=\{i\in\mathcal{I}^{(l)}\penalty\ |\penalty\ y_{i}\in\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c}}\setminus\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{d}}\}
. From the latter, we construct a histogram for the continuous component of the mixed Bayes Hilbert space, approximating the conditional density of
Y^{(l)}\penalty\ |\penalty\ \mathbf{x}^{(l)}
corresponding to the
l
-th covariate combination.
Thus, if
\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c}}\neq\emptyset
, let
G^{(l)}\in\mathbb{N}
be the numbers of histogram bins. Consider a partition

\mathcal{Z}^{(l)}=(a_{0}^{(l)},a_{1}^{(l)},\ldots,a_{G^{(l)}}^{(l)})\in\Xi:=\{(a_{0},a_{1},\ldots,a_{G})\penalty\ |\penalty\ a=a_{0}<a_{1}<\ldots<a_{G}=b,\penalty\ G\in\mathbb{N}\}
of the interval
\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c}}=[a,b]
, yielding disjoint bins
U_{g}^{(l)}:=[a_{g-1}^{(l)},a_{g}^{(l)})
for
g=1,\dots,G^{(l)}-1
and
U_{G^{(l)}}^{(l)}:=[a_{G^{(l)}-1}^{(l)},a_{G^{(l)}}^{(l)}]
.
Denote the width of bin
U_{g}^{(l)}
by

\Delta_{g}^{(l)}:=\mu(U_{g}^{(l)})=a_{g}^{(l)}-a_{g-1}^{(l)}>0
. If there exists a unique
y_{g}^{(l)}\in\{y_{i}\penalty\ |\penalty\ i\in\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{c}}^{(l)}\}\cap U_{g}^{(l)}
, choose
u_{g}^{(l)}=y_{g}^{(l)}
, otherwise let
u_{g}^{(l)}\in U_{g}^{(l)}
be the bin midpoint.
The values of the histogram then are
n_{g}^{(l)}:=\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{c}}^{(l)}}\mathbbm{1}_{U_{g}^{(l)}}(y_{i})
for
g=1,\ldots,G^{(l)}
.
Otherwise, if
\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c}}=\emptyset
, set
G^{(l)}=0
and
\mathcal{Z}^{(l)}=\emptyset
. For the discrete component of the mixed Bayes Hilbert space, we similarly count the number of response observations being equal to each discrete value:
For
g=G^{(l)}+1,\ldots,G^{(l)}+D=:\Gamma^{(l)}
, set
U_{g}^{(l)}:=\{t_{g-G^{(l)}}\}
,

\Delta_{g}^{(l)}:=\mu(U_{g}^{(l)})=w_{g-G^{(l)}}
,

u_{g}^{(l)}:=t_{g-G^{(l)}}
, and
n_{g}^{(l)}:=\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{d}}^{(l)}}\mathbbm{1}_{U_{g}^{(l)}}(y_{i})
.

We view the vectors
(n_{1}^{(l)},\ldots,n_{\Gamma^{(l)}}^{(l)})
as realizations of
L
independent multinomially distributed random variables with probabilities
p_{1}^{(l)},\ldots,p_{\Gamma^{(l)}}^{(l)}
, conditional on
n^{(l)}:=\sum_{g=1}^{\Gamma^{(l)}}n_{g}^{(l)}=|\mathcal{I}^{(l)}|
.
We model them via

p_{g}^{(l)}(\boldsymbol{\theta})=\frac{\Delta_{g}^{(l)}\exp[\tilde{\mathbf{b}}(\mathbf{x}^{(l)})(u_{g}^{(l)})^{\top}\boldsymbol{\theta}]}{\sum_{g^{\prime}=1}^{\Gamma^{(l)}}\Delta_{g^{\prime}}^{(l)}\exp[\tilde{\mathbf{b}}(\mathbf{x}^{(l)})(u_{g^{\prime}}^{(l)})^{\top}\boldsymbol{\theta}]}

for an unknown coefficient vector

\boldsymbol{\theta}\in\mathbb{R}^{K}
, where
\tilde{\mathbf{b}}
is the tensor product basis vector
as in (
2.2
).
Note that the integrate-to-zero constraint of
\tilde{\mathbf{b}}(\mathbf{x}^{(l)})\in L^{2}_{0}(\mu)^{K}
preserves identifiability despite the fraction. Omitting additive constants with respect to
\boldsymbol{\theta}
(indicated by
\propto
), the multinomial log-likelihood is

\displaystyle\sum_{l=1}^{L}\sum_{g=1}^{\Gamma^{(l)}}n_{g}^{(l)}\log\frac{\Delta_{g}^{(l)}\exp\bigl[\tilde{\mathbf{b}}(\mathbf{x}^{(l)})(u_{g}^{(l)})^{\top}\boldsymbol{\theta}\bigr]}{\sum_{g^{\prime}=1}^{\Gamma^{(l)}}\Delta_{g^{\prime}}^{(l)}\exp\bigl[\tilde{\mathbf{b}}(\mathbf{x}^{(l)})(u_{g^{\prime}}^{(l)})^{\top}\boldsymbol{\theta}\bigr]}

\displaystyle\propto

\displaystyle\sum_{l=1}^{L}\sum_{g=1}^{\Gamma^{(l)}}n_{g}^{(l)}\Bigl(\tilde{\mathbf{b}}(\mathbf{x}^{(l)})(u_{g}^{(l)})^{\top}\boldsymbol{\theta}-\log\sum_{g^{\prime}=1}^{\Gamma^{(l)}}\Delta_{g^{\prime}}^{(l)}\exp\bigl[\tilde{\mathbf{b}}(\mathbf{x}^{(l)})(u_{g^{\prime}}^{(l)})^{\top}\boldsymbol{\theta}\bigr]\Bigr)=:\ell^{\mathrm{mn}}_{\mathcal{Z}}(\boldsymbol{\theta}),

(2.4)

where the dependence on
\mathcal{Z}:=(\mathcal{Z}^{(1)},\ldots,\mathcal{Z}^{(L)})
is implicit via
\Gamma^{(l)}=G^{(l)}+D,n_{g}^{(l)},u_{g}^{(l)},
and
\Delta_{g}^{(l)}
for
l=1,\ldots,L
and
g=1,\ldots,G^{(l)}
.
Its penalized version, using the same penalty term as in Section
2.2
, is
\ell^{\mathrm{mn}}_{\mathcal{Z},\mathrm{pen}}(\boldsymbol{\theta}):=\ell^{\mathrm{mn}}_{\mathcal{Z}}(\boldsymbol{\theta})+\pen(\boldsymbol{\theta})
.
Consider the maximal histogram bin width
\Delta=\Delta(\mathcal{Z}):=\max\{\Delta_{g}^{(l)}\penalty\ |\penalty\ l=1,\ldots,L,\penalty\ g=1,\ldots,G^{(l)}\}
and the penalized Fisher information
\mathbf{F}_{\mathcal{Z},\mathrm{pen}}^{\mathrm{mn}}(\boldsymbol{\theta}):=-D^{2}\ell_{\mathcal{Z},\mathrm{pen}}^{\mathrm{mn}}(\boldsymbol{\theta})
.

Theorem 2.4
.

For all
\boldsymbol{\theta}\in\mathbb{R}^{K}
,
\lim_{\Delta\rightarrow 0}\ell^{\mathrm{mn}}_{\mathcal{Z},\mathrm{pen}}(\boldsymbol{\theta})=\ell_{\mathrm{pen}}(\boldsymbol{\theta})
,

\lim_{\Delta\rightarrow 0}\mathbf{F}^{\mathrm{mn}}_{\mathcal{Z},\mathrm{pen}}(\boldsymbol{\theta})=\mathbf{F}_{\mathrm{pen}}(\boldsymbol{\theta})
,
and (if the inverses exist)
\lim_{\Delta\rightarrow 0}\mathbf{F}^{\mathrm{mn}}_{\mathcal{Z},\mathrm{pen}}(\boldsymbol{\theta})^{-1}=\mathbf{F}_{\mathrm{pen}}(\boldsymbol{\theta})^{-1}
.
If
\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c}}=\emptyset
, all equalities hold without limit.

The proof in appendix
6.2
uses convergence of Riemann sums and that for small enough bin width, each bin contains at most one observed value.
We furthermore show in Proposition
6.6
in appendix
6.1.4
that
\mathbf{F}^{\mathrm{mn}}_{\mathcal{Z},\mathrm{pen}}(\boldsymbol{\theta})
is invertible and equals its expectation, if
(BX)
and the following assumption hold:

(BU)

For
\mathcal{Z}\in\Xi^{L}
,

l\in\{1,\ldots,L\}
,

\mathbf{v}_{\mathcal{Y}}\in\mathbb{R}^{K_{\mathcal{Y}}}
, and
c\in\mathbb{R}
:
If
\tilde{\mathbf{b}}_{\mathcal{Y}}(u_{g}^{(l)})^{\top}\mathbf{v}_{\mathcal{Y}}=c
for all
g=1,\ldots,\Gamma^{(l)}
, then

\mathbf{v}_{\mathcal{Y}}=\mathbf{0}
(and
c=0
).

This assumption is fulfilled, if the matrices

(\tilde{\mathbf{B}}_{\mathcal{Y}}^{(l)}:\mathbf{1}_{\Gamma^{(l)}})\in\mathbb{R}^{\Gamma^{(l)}\times(K_{\mathcal{Y}}+1)}
have rank
K_{\mathcal{Y}}+1
for all
l=1,\ldots,L
,
where

\tilde{\mathbf{B}}_{\mathcal{Y}}^{(l)}

is the matrix containing
\tilde{\mathbf{b}}_{\mathcal{Y}}(u_{g}^{(l)})
in the
g
-th row (given
\mathcal{Z}^{(l)}
),

\mathbf{1}_{\Gamma^{(l)}}
is the vector of length
\Gamma^{(l)}
containing
1
in every entry,
and
(\cdot:\cdot)
denotes matrix concatenation.
If
\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c}}=\emptyset
(discrete special case), this rank condition is immediately fulfilled.
If
\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c}}\neq\emptyset
, it is sufficient that (i) the number of basis functions over the continuous component plus
1
is at most the length of the vector of bins,
K_{\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c}}}+1\leq G^{(l)}
for all
l=1,\ldots,L
, and (ii) for all functions contained in the (unconstrained) B-spline basis yielding
\mathbf{b}_{\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c}}}
and for all
l\in\{1,\ldots,L\}
, there is at least one
g\in\{1,\ldots,G^{(l)}\}
such that
u_{g}^{(l)}
is in its support.
Both, (i) and (ii), and thus also
(BU)
, hold for small enough bin width.
See Lemma
6.5

4)

in appendix
6.1.3
for proof.
Hence, a sequence
\mathcal{Z}_{m}\in\Xi^{L}
,
m\in\mathbb{N}
, such that
(BU)
holds for every
\mathcal{Z}_{m},m\in\mathbb{N},
and
\lim_{m\rightarrow\infty}\Delta(\mathcal{Z}_{m})=0
(as considered in the following theorem transferring convergence to the PMLEs) exists.

Theorem 2.5
.

Assume that
(BX)
holds.

1)

Under
(BU)
,
\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{\mathcal{Z}}^{\mathrm{mn}}
is unique, if it exists.

2)

Assume that
(S

<

)

holds and
\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c}}\neq\emptyset
.
Then,
for any sequence
\mathcal{Z}_{m}\in\Xi^{L}
,
m\in\mathbb{N}
, such that
(BU)
holds for every
\mathcal{Z}_{m},m\in\mathbb{N}
, and
\lim_{m\rightarrow\infty}\Delta(\mathcal{Z}_{m})=0
, there exists an
M\in\mathbb{N}
such that
\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{\mathcal{Z}_{m}}^{\mathrm{mn}}
exists for all
m\geq M
and

\lim_{m\rightarrow\infty}\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{\mathcal{Z}_{m}}^{\mathrm{mn}}=\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}
.

Equivalently to the multinomial model constructed above, we can consider a Poisson model.
Compared to earlier work on this well-known equivalence
(e.g.,
Baker 1994c
)
, we include a penalty term for the coefficient vector
\boldsymbol{\theta}
.
Denote the upper left
K\times K
submatrix of a matrix
\mathbf{A}
with
\mathbf{A}_{[K]}
.

Theorem 2.6
.

For observations
\mathbf{n}^{(l)}=(n_{1}^{(l)},\ldots,n_{\Gamma^{(l)}}^{(l)})\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{\Gamma^{(l)}}
with
l=1,\ldots,L
, consider two different distributional assumptions:

1)

n_{g}^{(l)}
are realizations of Poisson variables
N_{g}^{(l)}\sim Po(\lambda_{g}^{(l)}(\boldsymbol{\theta},\boldsymbol{\tau}))
,
independent for
g=1,\ldots,\Gamma^{(l)},\,l=1,\ldots,L
, where the Poisson rates are modeled via

\lambda_{g}^{(l)}(\boldsymbol{\theta},\boldsymbol{\tau})=\exp(\eta_{g}^{(l)}(\boldsymbol{\theta})+\tau^{(l)}),
with a predictor
\eta_{g}^{(l)}(\boldsymbol{\theta})\in\mathbb{R}
for an unknown coefficient vector

\boldsymbol{\theta}\in\mathbb{R}^{K}
and

\boldsymbol{\tau}=(\tau^{(1)},\ldots,\tau^{(L)})^{\top}\in\mathbb{R}^{L}
.
Set
\lambda^{(l)}:=\sum_{g=1}^{\Gamma^{(l)}}\lambda_{g}^{(l)}(\boldsymbol{\theta},\boldsymbol{\tau})
,
n^{(l)}:=\sum_{g=1}^{\Gamma^{(l)}}n_{g}^{(l)}
,
\boldsymbol{\lambda}=(\lambda^{(1)},\ldots,\lambda^{(L)})
, and
\mathbf{n}=(n^{(1)},\ldots,n^{(L)})
for
l=1,\ldots,L
.
Given an additive penalty
\pen(\boldsymbol{\theta})
on the log-likelihood, denote the PMLE for
(\boldsymbol{\theta},\boldsymbol{\tau})
with
(\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{\mathrm{po}},\hat{\boldsymbol{\tau}})
, the penalized Fisher information at
(\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{\mathrm{po}},\hat{\boldsymbol{\tau}})

with
\hat{\mathbf{F}}_{\mathrm{pen}}^{\mathrm{po}}

and the expected penalized Fisher information (with respect to the Poisson distributional assumption given
(\boldsymbol{\theta},\boldsymbol{\tau})
) on the restricted parameter space

\{(\boldsymbol{\theta},\boldsymbol{\tau})\in\mathbb{R}^{K}\times\mathbb{R}^{L}\penalty\ |\penalty\ \boldsymbol{\lambda}=\mathbf{n}\}
with
\mathbb{E}^{\mathrm{po}}_{\boldsymbol{\theta},\boldsymbol{\tau}}(\mathbf{F}_{\mathrm{pen}}^{\mathrm{po}}\penalty\ |\penalty\ \boldsymbol{\lambda}=\mathbf{n})
.

2)

\mathbf{n}^{(l)}
are realizations of multinomial vectors

\mathbf{N}^{(l)}\sim M(n^{(l)},p_{1}^{(l)}(\boldsymbol{\theta}),\ldots,p_{\Gamma^{(l)}}^{(l)}(\boldsymbol{\theta})\,|

n^{(l)}=\sum_{g=1}^{\Gamma^{(l)}}n_{g}^{(l)})
, independent for
l=1,\ldots,L
, where the probabilities are modeled via

p_{g}^{(l)}(\boldsymbol{\theta})=

\frac{\exp[\eta_{g}^{(l)}(\boldsymbol{\theta})]}{\sum_{g^{\prime}=1}^{\Gamma^{(l)}}\exp[\eta_{g^{\prime}}^{(l)}(\boldsymbol{\theta})]}\,,
with
\eta_{g}^{(l)}(\boldsymbol{\theta})\in\mathbb{R}
the same predictor as in
1)
.
Given the penalty
\pen(\boldsymbol{\theta})
from
1)
, denote the PMLE for
\boldsymbol{\theta}
with
\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{\mathrm{mn}}
,
the penalized Fisher information at
\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{\mathrm{mn}}
with
\hat{\mathbf{F}}_{\mathrm{pen}}^{\mathrm{mn}}
,
and the expected penalized Fisher information (with respect to the multinomial distributional assumption given
\boldsymbol{\theta}
) with
\mathbb{E}^{\mathrm{mn}}_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\mathbf{F}_{\mathrm{pen}}^{\mathrm{mn}})
.

Then, the PMLEs for
\boldsymbol{\theta}
and the inverse (expected) penalized Fisher informations corresponding to
\boldsymbol{\theta}
are identical:

\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{\mathrm{po}}=\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{\mathrm{mn}}
,
((\hat{\mathbf{F}}_{\mathrm{pen}}^{\mathrm{po}})^{-1})_{[K]}=(\hat{\mathbf{F}}_{\mathrm{pen}}^{\mathrm{mn}})^{-1}
, and

((\mathbb{E}^{\mathrm{po}}_{\boldsymbol{\theta},\boldsymbol{\tau}}(\mathbf{F}_{\mathrm{pen}}^{\mathrm{po}}\,|

\boldsymbol{\lambda}=\mathbf{n}))^{-1})_{[K]}=(\mathbb{E}^{\mathrm{mn}}_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\mathbf{F}_{\mathrm{pen}}^{\mathrm{mn}}))^{-1}
.

Note that in general
(\mathbf{A}^{-1})_{[K]}\neq(\mathbf{A}_{[K]})^{-1}
, and hence the identities regarding the (sub-matrices of) Fisher informations do not hold without inversion.
Furthermore, note that the intercepts
\tau^{(l)}
indirectly model the absolute counts
n^{(l)}
for the
l
-th covariate combination (see the proof in appendix
6.2
for details on the relation), which are of interest in a Poisson distribution, while the probabilities modeled in the multinomial distribution only carry relative information, which is contained completely in
\boldsymbol{\theta}
.
The predictor should be chosen in such a way that the coefficients
\boldsymbol{\theta}
(and intercepts
\tau^{(l)}
in the Poisson distribution assumption) are identifiable.
When using Theorem
2.6
for our regression approach, we choose the predictor as

\eta_{g}^{(l)}(\boldsymbol{\theta})=\log\Delta_{g}^{(l)}+\tilde{\mathbf{b}}(\mathbf{x}^{(l)})(u_{g}^{(l)})^{\top}\boldsymbol{\theta},
for
l=1,\ldots,L
and
g=1,\ldots,\Gamma^{(l)}
.
The integrate-to-zero constraint of each component of
\tilde{\mathbf{b}}(\mathbf{x}^{(l)})\in L_{0}^{2}(\mu)^{K}
ensures that the coefficients are identifiable regarding additive constants. As before, we use the penalty

\pen(\boldsymbol{\theta})=-\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\top}\mathbf{P}\boldsymbol{\theta}
.
However, Theorem
2.6
is also valid for a general penalty (or prior taking a Bayesian view).

Theorems
2.5
and
2.6
imply that we can use Poisson regression to estimate the parameter vector in
\ell_{\mathrm{pen}}
and thus build our approach’s implementation on existing ones.
Furthermore, justified by our theorems, we may replace
\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}
with
\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{\mathrm{po}}
and

\mathbf{F}_{\mathrm{pen}}(\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}})^{-1}
with
((\hat{\mathbf{F}}_{\mathrm{pen}}^{\mathrm{po}})^{-1})_{[K]}
in Lemma
2.3
to construct confidence regions in practice.

Note that there might be numerous unique covariate combinations, in particular if metric covariates are involved. Thus, the number of intercepts
\tau^{(l)}
to estimate for
l=1,\ldots,L
in the Poisson regression model can get very large.
In practice, this problem might be circumvented by modeling the intercepts smoothly in the covariate basis as well, i.e.,
\tau^{(l)}=\mathbf{b}_{\mathcal{X}}(\mathbf{x}^{(l)})^{\top}\boldsymbol{\tau}
, where
\boldsymbol{\tau}\in\mathbb{R}^{K_{\mathcal{X}}}
.
However, as in any additive Poisson regression model like here for the absolute counts
n^{(l)}
, one needs to carefully inspect the counts to decide whether this smoothness assumption is reasonable.

In the beginning of this section, we constructed one vector of counts per unique observed covariate combination
\mathbf{x}^{(l)}
,
l\in\{1,\ldots,L\}
, combining all observations of the same conditional distribution. However, the construction described above can also be carried out for partitions of the corresponding set of indices
\mathcal{I}^{(l)}
, without changing estimation results, compare Lemma
6.16
in appendix
6.2
.
In the most extreme case, this yields one vector of counts per observation (with one count of
1
and all others
0
).
While this is usually not desirable with respect to computation time in practice, since it inflates the size of the data set artificially, it shows the estimation does not depend on aggregation of observations and the presented approach is thus overall coherent.

2.4
Interpretation

An advantage of formulating our structured additive regression models in Bayes Hilbert spaces is that it allows for ceteris paribus interpretation of partial effects as proposed by
Maier et al. 2025c
.
In the following, we briefly summarize two of their interpretation methods. For this purpose, let
h_{j}=h_{j}(\mathbf{x})\in B^{2}(\mu),j\in\{1,\ldots,J\}
be a partial effect as in model (
2.1
) and
h_{0}=h_{0}(\mathbf{x})\in B^{2}(\mu)
the sum of the remaining partial effects, omitting the covariates
\mathbf{x}\in\mathcal{X}
in the notation for the sake of readability.

For the first interpretation method, consider the infinitesimal odds ratio

O\!R_{j}(s,t):=\frac{(h_{0}(s)\oplus h_{j}(s))/(h_{0}(t)\oplus h_{j}(t))}{h_{0}(s)/h_{0}(t)}=h_{j}(s)/h_{j}(t)
for
s,t\in\mathcal{Y}
.
As then
\log O\!R_{j}(s,t)=\clr[h_{j}](s)-\clr[h_{j}](t)
, the log odds ratio can be easily read off as vertical differences from plots of the clr transformed effects.
As
O\!R_{j}(s,t)
is independent of
h_{0}
, these odds ratios can be interpreted ceteris paribus.
In addition to the infinitesimal interpretation,

Maier et al. 2025c
show that
O\!R_{j}(s,t)
corresponds to the limit of the usual odds ratio in the vicinity of
s
and
t
. Furthermore, Proposition 3.1 (a) in
Maier et al. 2025c
implies that if
O\!R_{j}(s,t)>1
for all
s\in A,\,t\in B
, where
A,B\in\{C\in\mathcal{A}\penalty\ |\penalty\ \mu(C)>0\}
, we have
\mathbb{P}(A)/\mathbb{P}(B)>\mathbb{P}_{0}(A)/\mathbb{P}_{0}(B)
where
\mathbb{P}
and
\mathbb{P}_{0}
denote the probability measures corresponding to the densities
h_{0}\oplus h_{j}
and
h_{0}
.

The second approach examines shifts of probability masses under summation:
For
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}
, let
A_{+}:=\{h_{j}\geq\alpha\}
and

A_{-}:=\{h_{j}<\alpha\}
.
Then,

\int_{A_{+}}h_{0}\oplus h_{j}\,\mathrm{d}\mu\geq\int_{A_{+}}h_{0}\,\mathrm{d}\mu

and

\int_{A_{-}}h_{0}\oplus h_{j}\,\mathrm{d}\mu\leq\int_{A_{-}}h_{0}\,\mathrm{d}\mu

(
Maier et al. 2025c
, Theorem F.1)
, i.e., any threshold
\alpha
decomposes
\mathcal{Y}
into two sets – one where the probability mass of any density

h_{0}\in B^{2}(\mu)
increases under perturbation with
h_{j}
and one where it decreases.
Note the result also holds, when constructing
A_{+}
and
A_{-}
based on
\clr[h_{j}]
instead of
h_{j}
, since the clr transformation retains a density’s monotonic behavior.

3
Application

The density of the woman’s share in a couple’s total labor income is considered in several gender economic contributions
(e.g.,
Bertrand et al. 2015a
;
Sprengholz et al. 2022a
)
.
A brief discussion thereof is available in
Maier et al. 2025c
, who (in contrast to the aforementioned gender economic literature) analyze the dependence of such income share densities on covariates.
Their density-on-scalar regression approach requires preliminary estimation of densities from samples of the conditional distributions, which then serve as response densities for the regression.
Our newly developed method avoids this two-step approach by directly working with the samples and additionally provides inference for the effect functions.
In the following, we apply our modeling approach to the same data set considered by
Maier et al. 2025c
, which is derived from the German Socio-Economic Panel (SOEP) (version 33, doi:10.5684/soep.v33, see
Goebel et al. 2019a
).
It contains
N=154,924
observations of opposite-sex couples living together in one household (not necessarily married), with at least one partner reporting positive labor income.

We aim to model conditional densities of the woman’s share
s
in a couple’s total gross labor income, given three covariates.
The binary covariate
West_East
indicates whether the couple’s household is located in
West
or
East
Germany (including Berlin).
The covariate
c_age
categorizes the couples via the age of the youngest child living in their household into three groups:
0-6
(zero to six years),
7-18
(seven to 18 years), and
other
(no minor children). The third covariate
year
ranges from 1984 for
West
Germany and from 1991 for
East
Germany to 2016.
Given these covariates, we consider the conditional densities
f_{\text{\emph{West\_East, c\_age, year}}}:[0,1]\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{+}

of the share
s
as elements of a mixed Bayes Hilbert space
B^{2}(\mu)=B^{2}([0,1],\mathcal{B},\mu)
with reference measure
\mu:=\delta_{0}+\lambda+\delta_{1}
, since the boundary values
0
and
1
of the share, corresponding to single-earner households, have positive probability mass.
We estimate the model

\displaystyle f_{\text{\emph{West\_East, c\_age, year}}}

\displaystyle=\beta_{0}\oplus\beta_{\text{\emph{West\_East}}}\oplus\beta_{c\_age}\oplus\beta_{\text{\emph{c\_age, West\_East}}}

\displaystyle\hskip 12.80365pt\oplus g(year)\oplus g_{\text{\emph{West\_East}}}(year)\oplus g_{c\_age}(year)

\displaystyle\hskip 12.80365pt\oplus g_{\text{\emph{c\_age, West\_East}}}(year),

(3.1)

where all summands are elements of the Bayes Hilbert space
B^{2}(\mu)
, i.e., densities of
s
.
We use reference coding with the same reference categories as
Maier et al. 2025c
, namely
\text{\emph{West\_East}}=\text{\emph{West}},\,\text{\emph{c\_age}}=\text{\emph{other}}
, and
\text{\emph{year}}=1991

(with the latter, for a continuous covariate, not directly specifiable in
mgcv
’s function
gam()
, which our
R
package
DensityRegression
uses for fitting Poisson models, requiring a transformation as discussed in appendix
7
). The intercept
\beta_{0}
corresponds to the density given these covariate values.
The other effects then describe the deviation from the reference for different values of the respective covariate, e.g., the smooth effect
g(\text{\emph{year}})
gives the deviation for each
year
from the reference 1991 (for
West
Germany and
c_age

other
).
The model also contains several interaction terms, namely, a group-specific intercept
\beta_{\text{\emph{c\_age, West\_East}}}
as well as group-specific smooth effects
g_{\text{\emph{West\_East}}}(\text{\emph{year}})
,
g_{\text{\emph{c\_age}}}(\text{\emph{year}})
, and
g_{\text{\emph{c\_age, West\_East}}}(\text{\emph{year}})
.
Note that more covariates might be included (at the expense of longer computation time), however for comparability purposes we consider (almost) the same model as in
Maier et al. 2025c
.
The only difference of model (
3.1
) to theirs is that they include an additional group specific intercept corresponding to a fourth covariate
region
, which contains a finer disaggregation of the covariate
West_East
into a total of six regions.
Since they discovered almost no variation within the regions corresponding to
West
and
East
Germany, respectively, we decided to not include this additional covariate for the sake of model parsimony. The basis functions
\mathbf{b}_{\mathcal{Y}}\in B^{2}(\mu)^{K_{\mathcal{Y}}}
are obtained from cubic B-splines for the continuous and indicator functions for the discrete component as described in Section
2.2
.
Like
Maier et al. 2025c
we use no penalty, setting
\xi_{\mathcal{Y},\,j}=0
for all
j=1,\ldots,J
. Since their two-step approach includes pre-smoothing by estimating the response densities, the number of basis functions used in the actual regression model is not comparable and we use a lower number (
K_{\mathcal{Y}}=15
) to obtain a similar level of smoothness.
For the (group-specific) smooth effects, we use cubic B-splines with second order difference penalty for
\mathbf{b}_{j}
and
\mathbf{P}_{j}

and REML optimization to determine the smoothing parameters
\xi_{\mathcal{X},\,j}
(given in appendix
9.1
).
For histogram construction on
(0,1)
, we use
G=100
equidistant bins for each covariate combination.

Note that the data set contains a sample weight per household, which we basically incorporate by using weighted counts as response data. However, these sample weights and thus the weighted counts are not natural numbers, technically not valid as response observations in a Poisson model. Thus, we construct an equivalent log-likelihood by weighting the respective log-likelihood contribution with the quotient of the weighted and the unweighted count and subtracting the quotient’s logarithm from the predictor. See appendix
8
for details.

For the level
\alpha=5\%
(even for
\alpha=1\%
), all effects except for the group-specific smooth effects
g_{\text{\emph{West\_East}}}(year)
and
g_{\text{\emph{c\_age, West\_East}}}(year)

are significant, compare the (simultaneous) p-values shown in Table
9.2
in appendix
9.2
.
There, we also give p-values which are pointwise in the covariates in Table
9.3
.
In the following, we discuss the main effects of model (
3.1
).
All effects are shown in appendix
9.3
, in particular the interaction effects, for which we cannot go into detail here, due to space constraints.
In Figures
3.1
to
3.3
, the left side illustrates the sum of the intercept and the respective main effect (i.e., the conditional densities given the different values of the considered covariate and the reference values for the remaining covariates), while the right side shows the clr transformed effects.
The latter is useful for interpreting effects via log odds ratios or shifts of probability mass, see Section
2.4
. Note that for the discrete values
0
and
1
, the density values are visualized as dashes and shifted slightly outwards for better distinction.

The estimated effect of
West_East
is shown in Figure
3.1
via the colored lines.
The grey ones illustrate samples (of size
100
) from the respective
95\%
confidence regions constructed as in Lemma
2.3
(simultaneous over
\mathcal{Y}=[0,1]
and point-wise regarding the covariates).
Note that
\hat{\beta}_{0}\oplus\hat{\beta}_{West}=\hat{\beta}_{0}
and
\clr[\hat{\beta}_{West}]=0
due to reference coding.
Considering double-earner couples (
s\in(0,1)
) in the left part of the figure, we observe both estimated conditional densities have a mode around
0.45
.
For couples in
West
Germany (without minor children in 1991) the density has a tendency towards a second mode around
s=0.1
. For the former communist
East
Germany, there is a shift of probability mass to the right yielding a more symmetric density.
This is also indicated by
\clr[\hat{\beta}_{East}]
on the right:
Using the value
\alpha\approx-0.65
, we obtain that the probability mass of the estimated densities in
East
Germany decreases on
\{\clr[\hat{\beta}_{East}]<\alpha\}=(0,0.2)
compared to
West
Germany (ceteris paribus).
Regarding the boundary values, nonworking women (
s=0
) are more frequent in
West
than in
East
Germany, while single-earner women (
s=1
) are more frequent in
East
Germany.
We can quantify this via the log odds ratio of
\hat{\beta}_{\text{\emph{East}}}
and
\hat{\beta}_{\text{\emph{West}}}
for
s=1
compared to
t=0
, which is

\clr[\hat{\beta}_{\text{\emph{East}}}](s)-\clr[\hat{\beta}_{\text{\emph{East}}}](t)-(\clr[\hat{\beta}_{\text{\emph{West}}}](s)-\clr[\hat{\beta}_{\text{\emph{West}}}](t))=0.23-(-0.09)=0.32
, which means that the odds for single-earner versus nonworking women in
East
Germany are
\exp(0.32)\approx 1.38
times the odds in
West
Germany.
The (clr transformed) densities sampled from the simultaneous (over
[0,1]
) confidence regions show similar shapes to the estimated densities over large parts of the domain, in particular regarding our findings.
However, towards the boundaries, i.e., for
s\rightarrow 0
and
s\rightarrow 1
with
s\in(0,1)
, the functions spread out, reflecting estimation uncertainty due to few observations in these areas.

Figure 3.1
:
Estimated conditional densities for couples without minor children in 1991 for
West
vs.
East
Germany [left] and clr transformed estimated effects of
West_East
[right] with
100
draws sampled uniformly from the respective
95\%
simultaneous (over
[0,1]
) confidence region.

Figure
3.2
shows the analogous plot for the effect of
c_age
.
To avoid overplotting, we do not display the whole sample of the confidence regions, but only the pointwise minimum and maximum thereof (grey). See Figure
9.2
in appendix
9.3
for the respective plots with the whole sample.
The tendency towards a second mode observed in Figure
3.1
for couples without minor children living in
West
Germany
amplifies for couples with minor children, as the densities for
c_age 0-6
and
7-18
are both bimodal.
Both have one mode around
s=0.1
with their second and higher modes further right around
s=0.35
for
c_age 0-6
and
s=0.25
for
c_age 7-18
, but clearly smaller than that for
c_age other
.
While the shapes of the densities for the two different groups of couples with minor children are similar, they differ in scale, reflecting the smaller share of double-earners (
s\in(0,1)
) among couples with small children (
c_age 0-6
) compared to the other groups.
The clr transformed effects are decreasing for most parts of
(0,1)
, i.e., odds (ratios)
\hat{\beta}_{\text{\emph{c\_age}}}(s)/\hat{\beta}_{\text{\emph{c\_age}}}(t)=\frac{\hat{\beta}_{\text{\emph{c\_age}}}(s)/\hat{\beta}_{\text{\emph{c\_age}}}(t)}{\hat{\beta}_{\text{\emph{other}}}(s)/\hat{\beta}_{\text{\emph{other}}}(t)}
for larger
s
vs. smaller
t\in(0,1)
tend to be small for
\text{\emph{c\_age}}\in\{\text{\emph{0-6}},\text{\emph{7-18}}\}
, indicating probability mass shifts towards the left compared to couples without minor children (ceteris paribus).
Note that regarding the estimated conditional densities for couples living in
East
Germany (in 1991, including interaction terms), there is (almost) no such shift, with the densities unimodal (and close to symmetric) for all three values of
c_age
, compare Figure
9.3
in appendix
9.3
, reflecting the different social norms in the two parts of Germany with formerly different political systems.
For both groups of couples with minor children (in
West
Germany), nonworking women (
s=0
) are more frequent than among couples without minor children and vice-versa for single-earner women (
s=1
).
The log odds ratio of
\hat{\beta}_{\text{\emph{0-6}}}
(respectively
\hat{\beta}_{\text{\emph{7-18}}}
) and
\hat{\beta}_{\text{\emph{other}}}
for
s=1
compared to
t=0
is

-1.11-1.37=-2.48

(respectively
-0.64-0.55=-1.19
), which means that the odds for single-earner versus nonworking women of couples with children aged zero to six (respectively seven to 18) are
\exp(-2.48)\approx 0.08
(respectively
\exp(-2.48)\approx 0.3
) times the odds of couples without minor children.
Again, the (clr transformed) densities sampled from the simultaneous (over
[0,1]
) confidence regions support our findings, having mostly similar shapes as the estimated densities.
As for
West_East
, estimation uncertainty shows in a spread towards the boundaries, in particular for
s\rightarrow 1
, where observations are very rare.

Figure 3.2
:
Estimated conditional densities for couples living in
West
Germany in 1991 for all three values of
c_age
[left] and clr transformed estimated effects of
c_age
[right] with pointwise minimum/maximum of
100
draws sampled uniformly from the respective
95\%
simultaneous (over
[0,1]
) confidence region.
Note that the y-axis is restricted to reasonable limits regarding the estimated functions, not spanning the whole range of functions sampled from the confidence regions.

The smooth effect of
year
is illustrated in Figure
3.3
, without samples of confidence regions to avoid overplotting.
See Figures
9.4
and
9.5
in appendix
9.3
for plots including samples of confidence regions.
Note that as for the other main effects, the functions sampled from the confidence region have similar shapes as the estimated ones with dispersion towards the boundaries.
A color gradient and different line types indicate the different
years
.
Considering the left part of the figure, in each
year
, the density (for couples without minor children living in
West
Germany) reaches its maximum around
s=0.45
with a second mode around
s=0.1
developing over time.
Furthermore, the estimated densities tend to disperse within
s\in(0,1)
over time, with probability mass particularly increasing for low positive income shares, while the share of nonworking women (
s=0
) decreases.
This is a sign of women switching from no paid work to part-time employment, emphasizing the need for considering mixed densities, since an analysis of double-earner couples only (
s\in(0,1)
) might misinterpret this development as a shift from larger to smaller income shares.
Moreover, the frequency of single-earner women (
s=1
) increases over time, in the most recent years reaching a similar level as the (strongly decreasing) frequency of nonworking women (
s=0
).
The right part of the figure also indicates the increasing dispersion on (
s\in(0,1)
), as the clr transformed effects tend to be smaller for low and high income shares (e.g.,
s\in A=(0,0.2)\cup(0.8,1)
) than for income shares in between (e.g.,
t\in B=(0.2,0.6)
) before the reference
year
1991.
This reverses afterwards. Hence,
the odds of the probabilities of
A
(outer area) versus
B
(central area) are smaller in earlier than in later
years
. This implies that over time the probability of
A
increases and the probability of
B
decreases.

Figure 3.3
:
Estimated conditional densities for couples without minor children living in
West
Germany over time [left] and clr transformed estimated effects of
year
[right].

All in all, our new approach finds similar effects as the one of
Maier et al. 2025c
, but by working directly with the samples of the conditional distributions more appropriately uses the available information and additionally provides uncertainty quantification.
Note that we also illustrate and briefly discuss all estimated conditional densities in appendix
9.4
, again confirming findings of
Maier et al. 2025c
for their pre-smoothed density-observations and predictions.

4
Simulation study

In this section, we present a simulation study to validate our approach.
We focus on the setting of our application with the estimated conditional densities obtained in Section
3
serving as true conditional densities
f_{\emph{\text{West\_East}},\emph{\text{c\_age}},\emph{\text{year}}}
, where

\emph{\text{West\_East}}\in\{\emph{\text{West}},\emph{\text{East}}\}
,

\emph{\text{c\_age}}\in\{\emph{\text{0-6}},\emph{\text{7-18}},\emph{\text{other}}\}
,
and
\emph{\text{year}}\in\{1984,\ldots,2016\}
for
\emph{\text{West\_East}}=\emph{\text{West}}

and
\emph{\text{year}}\in\{1991,\ldots,2016\}
for
\emph{\text{West\_East}}=\emph{\text{East}}
.
We consider four different sample sizes,
N\in\{50000,150000,500000,1000000\}
, where
150000
corresponds to the rounded sample size of the data set underlying the application, and three different bin numbers for the continuous component,
G\in\{50,100,200\}
, using equidistant bins that are equal for all covariate combinations.
We thus omit the superscript indicating the covariate combination in the notation compared to Section
2.3
. For
i=1,\ldots,N
, the
i
-th covariate combination
\mathbf{x}_{i}=(\emph{\text{West\_East}}_{i},\emph{\text{c\_age}}_{i},\emph{\text{year}}_{i})
is drawn with equal probabilities from all possible covariate combinations. Regarding the sampling of the women’s income share
s_{i}\in[0,1]
, note that due to the binning conducted to create the count data, the exact value within one bin of the continuous component is not relevant and it suffices to draw
s_{i}
from the set of bin midpoints united with the discrete values
\{0,1\}
, i.e., from
\{u_{1},\ldots,u_{\Gamma}\}
, where
\Gamma=G+2
. The probability of sampling
u_{g}
is given as

\int_{U_{g}}f_{\emph{\text{West\_East}}_{i},\emph{\text{c\_age}}_{i},\emph{\text{year}}_{i}}\,\mathrm{d}\mu

for
g=1,\ldots,\Gamma
.
Since the different bin sizes are chosen such that the bins are nested, for fixed
N
it suffices to sample
s_{1},\ldots,s_{N}
for
G=200
, which corresponds to the finest partition. The same simulated data is then also used for the two smaller values of
G
. We estimate model (
3.1
) based on the simulated data, using the same basis functions as in Section
3
(including identifiability constraints).
For each combination of
N
and
G
, we perform
200
replications.
Motivated by
Maier et al. 2025c
, we evaluate the quality of the estimated conditional densities (prediction) and the estimated partial effects using the relative mean squared error (relMSE), defined as

\mathrm{relMSE}(\hat{e}):=\frac{\frac{1}{N}\,\sum_{i=1}^{N}\|e_{i}\ominus\hat{e}_{i}\|_{B^{2}(\mu)}^{2}}{\frac{1}{N}\,\sum_{i=1}^{N}\|e_{i}\|_{B^{2}(\mu)}^{2}}
, where
e_{i}\in B^{2}(\mu)
is either the true conditional density

f_{i}=f_{\emph{\text{West\_East}}_{i},\emph{\text{c\_age}}_{i},\emph{\text{year}}_{i}}

or a true partial effect

h_{j}(\mathbf{x}_{i})
,
j=1,\ldots J
,
and
\hat{e}_{i}
is its estimated version.

Figure 4.1
:
RelMSE for prediction and main effects for different values of
G
[columns] and
N
[rows]. Numbers following a plus-sign at the right of a panel give the number of values larger than
3.35
(vertical line).
Empty space means all values are visible.

Figure
4.1
shows boxplots of the relMSEs for prediction and main effects, obtained based on
200
replications for each combination of
N
and
G
. All effects are illustrated in Figure
10.1
in appendix
10
.
For the smallest sample size,
N=50000
, while the other effects are well estimated, some iterations yield very large relMSEs for predictions and for the effect of
c_age
(also for interaction effects including
c_age
, compare Figure
10.1
), which are cut off with their count indicated in the plot.
In these iterations, the simulated data contains very few observations
s_{i}\in(0.9,1)
with
\text{\emph{c\_age}}_{i}=\text{\emph{0-6}}
, corresponding to areas where the respective true conditional densities we simulate from are close to zero due to the sharp drop for
s\rightarrow 1
on clr-transformed level (compare Figure
3.2
). This leads to the continuous component of the density being underestimated for
s\rightarrow 1
