(corresponding to an even sharper descent on clr-transformed level), which yields large squared norms
\|f_{i}\ominus\hat{f}_{i}\|_{B^{2}(\mu)}^{2}
and

\|\beta_{\text{\emph{c\_age}}_{i}}\ominus\hat{\beta}_{\text{\emph{c\_age}}_{i}}\|_{B^{2}(\mu)}^{2}

for the corresponding observations, drastically deteriorating the relMSE.
The problem disappears and estimation quality improves overall with increasing sample size
N
, while the three different values of
G
yield relMSEs at a similar level for given
N
.
Considering
\mathrm{relMSE}(\hat{f})
, we obtain a median of
14\%
(
G=100
) to
15\%
(
G\in\{50,200\}
) for
N=50000
, which decreases to
4.8\%
(
G\in\{100,200\}
) to
4.9\%
(
G=50
) for
N=150000
, further to
1.4\%
(
G\in\{50,100,200\}
) for
N=500000
, and down to
0.6\%
(
G=100
),
0.7\%
(
G=200
), and
0.9\%
(
G=50
) for
N=1000000
.
Among the main effects, we obtain the smallest relMSEs for
\hat{\beta}_{0}

(medians:
N=50000
:
0.9\%
to
1\%
;

N=150000
:
0.3\%
;

N=500000
:
0.1\%
;

N=1000000
:
0.05\%
to
0.06\%
),
while the other effects
\hat{\beta}_{\text{\emph{West\_East}}}
,
\hat{\beta}_{\text{\emph{c\_age}}}
, and
\hat{g}(\text{\emph{year}})
yield larger values, whose medians are on a similar level
(
N=50000
:
9\%
,
10\%
, and
12\%
;

N=150000
:
3.2\%
,
3.2\%
to
3.4\%
, and
4.8\%
;

N=500000
:
0.9\%
,
0.9\%
, and
1.6\%
;

N=1000000
:
0.4\%
to
0.5\%
,
0.4\%
to
0.6\%
, and
0.9\%
).
For the interaction effects illustrated in Figure
10.1
, the relMSEs tend to be larger, in particular for effects involving
c_age
due to underestimation of the effects for large
s<1
as discussed above.

Figure 4.2
:
Empirical coverage rates (empCR) for prediction and main effects (simultaneous in covariates) for different values of
G
[columns] and
N
[rows].
The boxplots for predictions summarize coverage for the conditional densities corresponding to the
177
unique covariate combinations.

The empirical coverage rates for predictions (as constructed in Lemma
2.3
) and main effects (as constructed in Lemma
6.15
, i.e., simultaneous in covariates) are illustrated in Figure
4.2
, where the green vertical line indicates the true theoretical value
\alpha=95\%
.
Appendix
10
contains the analogous figure showing all effects (Figure
10.2
), as well as empirical coverage rates for partial effects based on point-wise confidence regions as constructed in Lemma
2.3
(Figure
10.3
).
For predictions, the range of the empirical coverage over the
177
conditional distributions mostly reduces and converges to the theoretical value
95\%
with increasing
N
.
The only exception is the combination of smallest bin number,
G=50
, with largest sample size
N=1000000
. While the median improves slightly, from
92.5\%
to
93\%
compared to
N=500000
, the range also increases, possibly indicating that too-small bin numbers may induce undercoverage especially for larger sample sizes, where variance decreases and even small approximation biases thus may be non-negligible.
Similarly, for the intercept, the empirical coverage rate for the largest sample size
N=1000000
is worst for
G=50
, only
89\%
, while coverage rates are close to the theoretical value with
94.5\%
(
G=100
) and
93.5\%
(
G=200
) for more bins, and only slightly below the coverage rates of
95\%
(
G=100
) and
94\%
(
G=200
) obtained for
N=500000
.
For the group-specific intercepts
\hat{\beta}_{\text{\emph{West\_East}}}
and
\hat{\beta}_{\text{\emph{c\_age}}}
the empirical coverage rates are close to the true value. For
\hat{g}(\text{\emph{year}})
, we observe some undercoverage for the smallest sample size
N=50000
(again most severe for the smallest bin size:
87.5\%
for
G=50
,
89\%
for
G=100
and
89.5\%
for
G=200
), while for larger values of
N
, there is slight overcoverage.
This trend is similar for the interaction effects
\hat{g}_{\text{\emph{West\_East}}}(\text{\emph{year}})
and
\hat{g}_{\text{\emph{c\_age}}}(\text{\emph{year}})
(with even more severe undercoverage for
N=50000
), while for the three-way interaction
\hat{g}_{\text{\emph{c\_age, West\_East}}}(\text{\emph{year}})
, we observe slight overcoverage already for the smallest sample size,
N=50000
(compare Figure
10.2
).
The results based on point-wise confidence regions illustrated in Figure
10.3
are similar, with empirical coverage rates (mostly) close to the theoretical value for (group-specific) intercepts (with sometimes slightly worse results for
G=50
compared to
G\in\{100,200\}
given the same
N
), while the (group-specific) smooth effects of year yield more varied coverage rates (over the different years) for the smallest sample size,
N=50000
, contracting around
95\%
(or a larger value for the three-way interaction) with increasing
N
.

All in all, the smallest considered sample size,
N=50000
, yields large relMSEs in some iterations – mostly for
\beta_{c\_age}
, which exhibits a sharp drop on clr-level – and tends to yield some undercoverage for predictions and
year
-effects.
The available data size in the application,
N=150000
, already yields good results – especially given the complexity of the considered model with several interaction effects. Estimation quality unsurprisingly improves further with more data available.
Regarding the bin number
G
, we observe almost no influence on the relMSE values within the reasonable ranges considered here, while the empirical coverage rates in some cases suffer for the lowest bin number compared to good coverage for the larger two considered bin numbers.

5
Conclusion

We presented a penalized maximum-likelihood approach to estimate structured additive regression models for densities given scalar covariates from random samples of the underlying conditional distribution.
To respect their nature, densities are considered as elements of a Bayes Hilbert space
B^{2}(\mu)
, providing a unified framework for arbitrary finite measure spaces, in particular including mixed densities (and discrete and continuous special cases), which we focused on for estimation.
We derived the asymptotic distribution of the penalized maximum likelihood estimator (PMLE), which we used to construct confidence regions and p-values for estimated partial effects. Our framework is the first distributional regression approach to allow ceteris paribus interpretations for covariate effects.
Furthermore, we approximated the log-likelihood by a multinomial or equivalently Poisson log-likelihood, with the respective observations computed via histograms on the continuous and counts on the discrete part of the domain, and showed that the PMLE and its estimated covariance (the inverse Fisher information) converge to the ones of the limiting (Bayes Hilbert space) log-likelihood.
This simplifies estimation, allowing to take advantage of the well-established
R
package
mgcv

(
Wood 2017c
)
for our implementation in the
R
package
DensityRegression
.
We used the new additive density regression approach to analyze the motivating (mixed) densities of the woman’s income share for couples in Germany, observing similar covariate effects as
Maier et al. 2025c
, but avoiding a two-step estimation approach and additionally providing inference. In particular, we found significant main effects for West vs. East Germany, presence vs. absence of minor children in the household, and time.
A simulation study based on our application further validated our approach.

Formulating our regression models directly for densities in a Bayes Hilbert space not only allows to consider continuous, discrete, and mixed densities in a unified framework, and to include a variety of covariate effects, but also to interpret these effects ceteris paribus.
Thereby, our approach stands out from other distributional regression approaches modeling the conditional distribution based on individual observations, which lack such interpretability
(
Kneib et al. 2023a
)
.
In contrast, distributional response regression approaches benefiting from the Bayes Hilbert space framework
(e.g.,
Talská et al. 2018a
;
Maier et al. 2025c
)
assume whole densities to be observed as response objects, which is usually not possible and thus necessitates a two-step procedure. This leads to additional estimation uncertainty and requires enough observations to estimate conditional densities for each covariate combination, which typically cannot be expected in the presence of continuous covariates.
Our maximum-likelihood approach modeling the conditional densities directly based on the individual observations overcomes these issues.
In addition to these strong advantages, the Bayes Hilbert space framework also has limitations in certain settings.
As Bayes Hilbert spaces contain (
\mu
-a.e.) positive densities, densities that are zero on parts of the domain cannot be modeled.
However, our new estimation approach based on individual data avoids zero density values that often occur in two-step approaches due to presmoothing (or over-presmoothing to prevent zero values), providing a natural solution to this problem except in settings with structurally different density supports.
Furthermore, the reference measure needs to be finite, excluding the Lebesgue measure as reference for
\mathcal{Y}=\mathbb{R}
.
However, the probability measure of a reference distribution, such as the standard normal distribution, provides an alternative reference in this case
(
Boogaart et al. 2014a
)
, and the Lebesgue measure can be used for the finite domain case commonly observed in practice.

In contrast to existing literature
(e.g.,
Mâsse & Truong 1999a
)
, we provided simultaneous confidence regions, containing complete densities.
The asymptotic distribution of the PMLE these are based on was derived assuming a true parameter value to exist for the given finite-dimensional basis expansion. As the MLE is known to converge under standard assumptions to the minimizer of the Kullback-Leibler divergence within the modeled class of distributions, here spanned by the considered basis
(
Knight 1999a
;
Stone 1991c
)
,
our semi-parametric conditional distribution estimates can be interpreted as best approximations to the true conditional distributions within a finite but potentially high dimensional model space. Given the large model flexibility compared to existing approaches, and similarly to other penalized spline approaches
(
Ruppert 2002a
)
, this should not be much of a limitation
in practice.
Future work, motivated by
Stone 1991c
, might also study convergence and inference as the number of basis coefficients goes to infinity.
Furthermore, we aim to extend the presented approach from univariate to multivariate densities in future.

A. Stöcker acknowledges support from SNSF Grant 200020_207367.

6.1
Preliminaries

6.1.1
Notation and Lemma
6.1

In the remainder of Section
6
, we use the following notation (partially already introduced in Section
2
):

1)

Covariates:

Recall that the support of the covariates is a compact set
\mathcal{X}=\bigtimes_{p=1}^{P}\mathcal{X}_{p}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{P}
such that each
\mathcal{X}_{p}
is either a finite discrete set or a compact interval.
Set
\mathfrak{D}=\{p\in\{1,\ldots,P\}\penalty\ |\penalty\ \mathcal{X}_{p}\penalty\ \text{is discrete}\}
and
\mathfrak{C}:=\mathfrak{D}^{\complement}=\{p\in\{1,\ldots,P\}\penalty\ |\penalty\ \mathcal{X}_{p}\penalty\ \text{is an interval}\}
,
set
\boldsymbol{\nu}=\bigtimes_{p=1}^{P}\nu_{p}
, where
\nu_{p}:=\delta_{p}=\sum_{x\in\mathcal{X}_{p}}\delta_{x}
, if
p\in\mathfrak{D}
and
\nu_{p}:=\lambda
, if
p\in\mathfrak{C}
,
and set
\mathcal{X}^{\mathfrak{D}}:=\bigtimes_{p\in\mathfrak{D}}\mathcal{X}_{p}
and
\mathcal{X}^{\mathfrak{C}}:=\bigtimes_{p\in\mathfrak{C}}\mathcal{X}_{p}
.
For
\mathbf{x}\in\mathcal{X}
, we write
\mathbf{x}=\mathbf{x}^{\mathfrak{D}},\mathbf{x}^{\mathfrak{C}}
with
\mathbf{x}^{\mathfrak{D}}\in\mathcal{X}^{\mathfrak{D}}
and
\mathbf{x}^{\mathfrak{C}}\in\mathcal{X}^{\mathfrak{C}}
denoting the discrete and continuous covariates contained in
\mathbf{x}
.

2)

Spline functions for normalized coefficient vector:

Recall that
\mathcal{Y}=\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c}}\cup\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{d}}
, where
\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c}}=[a,b]
for
a,b\in\mathbb{R}
with
a<b
and
\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{d}}=\{t_{1},\ldots,t_{D}\}\subset\mathbb{R}
.
Denote the unit sphere in
\mathbb{R}^{K}
with
\mathbb{S}^{K-1}
.
For
\mathbf{v}\in\mathbb{S}^{K-1}
and
\mathbf{x}\in\mathcal{X}
,
let

\displaystyle s_{\mathbf{v},\mathbf{x}}:\mathcal{Y}\rightarrow\mathbb{R},

\displaystyle y\mapsto\tilde{\mathbf{b}}(\mathbf{x})(y)^{\top}\mathbf{v}=(\mathbf{b}_{\mathcal{X}}(\mathbf{x})\otimes\tilde{\mathbf{b}}_{\mathcal{Y}}(y))^{\top}\mathbf{v},

and
let
s_{\mathbf{v},\mathbf{x},\mathrm{c}}
be the unique continuous function on
\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c}}
with
s_{\mathbf{v},\mathbf{x},\mathrm{c}}(y)=s_{\mathbf{v},\mathbf{x}}(y)
for all
y\in\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c}}\setminus\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{d}}
.
Set
s_{\mathbf{v},\mathbf{x}}^{\max}:=\sup_{y\in\mathcal{Y}}s_{\mathbf{v},\mathbf{x}}(y)
,
s_{\mathbf{v},\mathbf{x},\mathrm{d}}^{\max}:=\sup_{y\in\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{d}}}s_{\mathbf{v},\mathbf{x}}(y)
, and
s_{\mathbf{v},\mathbf{x},\mathrm{c}}^{\max}:=\sup_{y\in\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c}}\setminus\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{d}}}s_{\mathbf{v},\mathbf{x}}(y)=\sup_{y\in\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c}}}s_{\mathbf{v},\mathbf{x},\mathrm{c}}(y)
.

3)

Mixed basis functions in
L^{2}_{0}(\mu)
:

Recall the construction of a basis
\mathbf{b}_{\mathcal{Y}}\in B^{2}(\mu)^{K_{\mathcal{Y}}}
in the mixed case described in Section
2.2
,
via the orthogonal decomposition of
B^{2}(\mu)
into
B^{2}(\lambda)
and
B^{2}(\delta^{\bullet})
of
Maier et al. 2025c
.
Equivalently, the basis
\tilde{\mathbf{b}}_{\mathcal{Y}}
(containing the clr transformation of the functions in
\mathbf{b}_{\mathcal{Y}}
) can be obtained via the orthogonal decomposition of
L^{2}_{0}(\mu)
into
L^{2}_{0}(\lambda)
and
L^{2}_{0}(\delta^{\bullet})
by
Maier et al. 2025c
:
We obtain the bases
\tilde{b}_{\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c}},\,1},\ldots,\tilde{b}_{\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c}},\,K_{\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c}}}}\in L^{2}_{0}(\lambda)
and
\tilde{b}_{\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{d}}^{\bullet},\,1},\ldots,\tilde{b}_{\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{d}}^{\bullet},\,K_{\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{d}}^{\bullet}}}\in L^{2}_{0}(\delta^{\bullet})
from appropriate bases
\bar{b}_{\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c}},\,1},\ldots,\bar{b}_{\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c}},\,K_{\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c}}}+1}\in L^{2}(\lambda)
and
\bar{b}_{\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{d}}^{\bullet},\,1},\ldots,\bar{b}_{\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{d}}^{\bullet},\,K_{\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{d}}^{\bullet}}+1}\in L^{2}(\delta^{\bullet})
.
1
1

1

Note that
K_{\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{d}}^{\bullet}}=D
is the number of discrete points in the mixed space
L^{2}_{0}(\mu)
(while
K_{\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{d}}^{\bullet}}+1=D+1
is the number of discrete points in the discrete space
L^{2}_{0}(\delta^{\bullet})
) and

\bar{b}_{\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{d}}^{\bullet},\,d}=\mathbbm{1}_{\{t_{d}\}}
for all
d=1,\ldots,K_{\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{d}}^{\bullet}}+1
.
For the sake of consistent notation, we write
K_{\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{d}}^{\bullet}}
and
\bar{b}_{\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{d}}^{\bullet},\,d}
, when the (number of) basis functions are concerned. For the number of discrete points, we write
D
.

In Section
2.2
we propose to use a B-spline basis with pairwise distinct knots and degree at least
2
for the former and indicator functions at the discrete values
t_{1},\ldots,t_{D+1}
for the latter.
More precisely, the basis functions for the continuous component are obtained as

\tilde{b}_{\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c}},\,m}:=\sum_{j=1}^{K_{\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c}}}+1}\bar{b}_{\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c}},\,j}z^{\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c}}}_{jm}
, where

(z^{\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c}}}_{1m},\ldots,z^{\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c}}}_{(K_{\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c}}}+1)m})
is the
m
-th column of a transformation matrix
\mathbf{Z}_{\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c}}}\in\mathbb{R}^{(K_{\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c}}}+1)\times K_{\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c}}}}
for
m=1,\ldots,K_{\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c}}}
.
Analogously for
\tilde{b}_{\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{d}},\,m}
with
m=1,\ldots,K_{\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{d}}}
and a transformation matrix
\mathbf{Z}_{\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{d}}}\in\mathbb{R}^{(K_{\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{d}}}+1)\times K_{\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{d}}}}
for the discrete component.
Both transformation matrices are obtained via QR decompositions of the vectors of integrals of the untransformed bases (viewed as a matrix with one row) as described in appendix B of
Maier et al. 2025c

(based on
Wood 2017c
, Section 1.8.1)
and have full rank per construction.
The transformed B-spline basis

\tilde{\mathbf{b}}_{\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c}}}=(\tilde{b}_{\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c}},\,1},\ldots,\tilde{b}_{\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c}},\,K_{\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c}}}})^{\top}\in L^{2}_{0}(\lambda)^{K_{\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c}}}}

and the transformed indicator functions

\tilde{\mathbf{b}}_{\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{d}}^{\bullet}}=(\tilde{b}_{\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{d}}^{\bullet},\,1},\ldots,\tilde{b}_{\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{d}}^{\bullet},\,K_{\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{d}}^{\bullet}}})^{\top}\in L^{2}_{0}(\delta^{\bullet})^{K_{\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{d}}^{\bullet}}}

are combined to a vector of basis functions
\tilde{\mathbf{b}}_{\mathcal{Y}}\in L^{2}_{0}(\mu)^{K_{\mathcal{Y}}}
by applying the embeddings

\displaystyle\tilde{\iota}_{\mathrm{c}}:L^{2}_{0}(\lambda)\hookrightarrow L^{2}_{0}(\mu)

\displaystyle\tilde{f}_{\mathrm{c}}\mapsto\begin{cases}\tilde{f}_{\mathrm{c}}&\mathrm{on}\penalty\ \mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c}}\setminus\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{d}}\\
0&\mathrm{on}\penalty\ \mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{d}}\end{cases}

(6.1)

\displaystyle\tilde{\iota}_{\mathrm{d}}:L^{2}_{0}(\delta^{\bullet})\hookrightarrow L^{2}_{0}(\mu)

\displaystyle\tilde{f}_{\mathrm{d}}\mapsto\begin{cases}\tilde{f}_{\mathrm{d}}\left(t_{D+1}\right)&\mathrm{on}\penalty\ \mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c}}\setminus\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{d}}\\
\tilde{f}_{\mathrm{d}}&\mathrm{on}\penalty\ \mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{d}}\end{cases}\,.

(6.2)

of
Maier et al. 2025c
component-wise to the functions contained in
\tilde{\mathbf{b}}_{\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c}}}
and
\tilde{\mathbf{b}}_{\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{d}}^{\bullet}}
(equivalently to the approach described to obtain the basis functions in
B^{2}(\mu)
in Section
2.2
).

Related to the notation introduced in
3)
, we show a brief auxiliary statement, which is used for discussing the properties of penalty matrices/basis functions in Section
6.1.2
and in the proof of Lemma
6.5
in Section
6.1.3
.

Lemma 6.1
.

Let
\bar{b}_{\mathcal{T},\,1},\ldots,\bar{b}_{\mathcal{T},\,K_{\mathcal{T}}+1}
be linearly independent real-valued functions defined on
\mathcal{T}\subset\mathbb{R}
, let
1_{\mathcal{T}}:\mathcal{T}\rightarrow\{1\},t\mapsto 1
, let

\mathbf{Z}_{\mathcal{T}}=(z_{j,m}^{\mathcal{T}})_{j=1,\ldots,K_{\mathcal{T}}+1,m=1,\ldots,K_{\mathcal{T}}}
have rank
K_{\mathcal{T}}
, and for
m=1,\ldots,K_{\mathcal{T}}
, let

\tilde{b}_{\mathcal{T},\,m}:=\sum_{j=1}^{K_{\mathcal{T}}+1}\bar{b}_{\mathcal{T},\,j}z^{\mathcal{T}}_{jm}
.
If
1_{\mathcal{T}}\in\spano(\bar{\mathbf{b}}_{\mathcal{T}}):=\spano(\bar{b}_{\mathcal{T},1},\ldots,\bar{b}_{\mathcal{T},K_{\mathcal{T}}+1})

and

1_{\mathcal{T}}\notin\spano(\tilde{\mathbf{b}}_{\mathcal{T}}):=\spano(\tilde{b}_{\mathcal{T},1},\ldots,\tilde{b}_{\mathcal{T},K_{\mathcal{T}}})
,
then the functions
\tilde{b}_{\mathcal{T},\,1},\ldots,\tilde{b}_{\mathcal{T},\,K_{\mathcal{T}}},1_{\mathcal{T}}
are linearly independent and

\displaystyle\spano(\bar{\mathbf{b}}_{\mathcal{T}}):=\spano(\bar{b}_{\mathcal{T},1},\ldots,\bar{b}_{\mathcal{T},K_{\mathcal{T}}+1})=\spano(\tilde{b}_{\mathcal{T},1},\ldots,\tilde{b}_{\mathcal{T},K_{\mathcal{T}}},1_{\mathcal{T}}).

In particular, this holds for

\mathcal{T}=\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c}}
with
\bar{b}_{\mathcal{T},1}=\bar{b}_{\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c}},1},\ldots,\bar{b}_{\mathcal{T},K_{\mathcal{T}}+1}=\bar{b}_{\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c}},K_{\mathcal{C}}+1}
a B-spline basis and
\mathbf{Z}_{\mathcal{T}}=\mathbf{Z}_{\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c}}}
, and for

\mathcal{T}=\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{d}}
with
\bar{b}_{\mathcal{T},1}=\bar{b}_{\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{d}},1},\ldots,\bar{b}_{\mathcal{T},K_{\mathcal{T}}+1}=\bar{b}_{\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{d}},K_{\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{d}}}+1}
the basis of indicator functions at the discrete values
t_{1},\ldots,t_{D}
and
\mathbf{Z}_{\mathcal{T}}=\mathbf{Z}_{\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{d}}}
as in notation
3)
above.

Proof.

Since the functions
\bar{b}_{\mathcal{T},\,1},\ldots,\bar{b}_{\mathcal{T},\,K_{\mathcal{T}}+1}\in L^{2}(\lambda)
are linearly independent and the transformation matrix
\mathbf{Z}_{\mathcal{T}}
has full rank,
the transformed basis
\tilde{b}_{\mathcal{T},\,1},\ldots,\tilde{b}_{\mathcal{T},\,K_{\mathcal{T}}}\in L^{2}_{0}(\lambda)
consists of linearly independent functions
(
Liesen & Mehrmann 2015a
, Lemma 9.23)
.
Furthermore, per construction via linear combinations, the basis functions contained in
\tilde{\mathbf{b}}_{\mathcal{T}}
are elements of

\spano(\bar{\mathbf{b}}_{\mathcal{T}})
.
Since
1_{\mathcal{T}}\notin\spano(\tilde{\mathbf{b}}_{\mathcal{T}})
, the functions
\tilde{b}_{\mathcal{T},\,1},\ldots,\tilde{b}_{\mathcal{T},\,K_{\mathcal{T}}},1_{\mathcal{T}}
are linearly independent.
Furthermore, as
1_{\mathcal{T}}\in\spano(\bar{\mathbf{b}}_{\mathcal{T}})
, the
K_{\mathcal{T}}+1
functions
\tilde{b}_{\mathcal{T},\,1},\ldots,\tilde{b}_{\mathcal{T},\,K_{\mathcal{T}}},1_{\mathcal{T}}
span the same space as the
K_{\mathcal{T}}+1
functions
\bar{b}_{\mathcal{T},\,1},\ldots,\bar{b}_{\mathcal{T},\,K_{\mathcal{T}}+1}
, i.e.,

\spano(\bar{\mathbf{b}}_{\mathcal{T}})=\spano(\tilde{b}_{\mathcal{T},1},\ldots,\tilde{b}_{\mathcal{T},K_{\mathcal{T}}},1_{\mathcal{T}})
.

Consider a B-spline basis
\bar{b}_{\mathcal{T},\,1}=\bar{b}_{\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c}},1},\ldots,\bar{b}_{\mathcal{T},\,K_{\mathcal{T}}+1}=\bar{b}_{\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c}},K_{\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c}}}+1}
, which consists of linearly independent functions and fulfills
1_{\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c}}}\in\spano(\bar{\mathbf{b}}_{\mathcal{T}})
due to
De 2001a
.
Since the functions contained in
\tilde{\mathbf{b}}_{\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c}}}
all integrate to zero, we have
1_{\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c}}}\notin\spano(\tilde{\mathbf{b}}_{\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c}}})
.
The basis of indicator functions
\bar{b}_{\mathcal{T},\,1}=\bar{b}_{\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{d}},1},\ldots,\bar{b}_{\mathcal{T},\,K_{\mathcal{T}}+1}=\bar{b}_{\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{d}},K_{\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{d}}}+1}
fulfills the linear independence assumption and
1_{\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{d}}}\in\spano(\bar{\mathbf{b}}_{\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{d}}})
straightforwardly, while
1_{\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{d}}}\notin\spano(\tilde{\mathbf{b}}_{\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{d}}})
follows analogously with the integrate-to-zero constraint.
Thus, in both cases the assumptions are fulfilled.
∎

6.1.2
Necessary Properties of Bases and Penalties

For penalty matrices and basis functions not chosen as in Section
2.2
, we assume:

(P)

All penalty matrices
\mathbf{P}_{j}
are positive semi-definite,
j=1,\ldots,J
.

(SPP)

There exist
R\in\mathbb{N}
and
\kappa\geq 2
such that
every function contained in
\spano(\tilde{\mathbf{b}}_{\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c}}})
is a
(\kappa-1)
-times continuously differentiable piecewise polynomial consisting of
R
polynomials of degree at most
\kappa
.

(B)

The basis functions contained in
\tilde{\mathbf{b}}_{\mathcal{Y}}
as well as the basis functions contained in
\mathbf{b}_{\mathcal{X}}
are linearly independent, continuous in any
y_{\mathrm{c}}\in\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c}}\setminus\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{d}}
respectively in any
\mathbf{x}\in\mathcal{X}
,
and bounded.

Since these are very common properties of penalty matrices/(spline) basis functions, these are not mentioned explicitly in the remaining work to not overload the statements.
Now, consider the choices of penalty matrices and basis functions recommended in Section
2.2
, included in an identifiable model.
We briefly discuss, that the above assumptions are fulfilled:

•

(P)
:
As marginal penalty matrices
\mathbf{P}_{\mathcal{X},\,j},j=1,\ldots,J,
and
\mathbf{P}_{\mathcal{Y}}
in Section
2.2
, we propose to use difference penalties and identity matrices, which are positive semi-definite.
Since Kronecker products and linear combinations (for non-negative scalars) of positive semi-definite matrices are positive semi-definite (see
Horn & Johnson 1994a
, Corollary 4.2.13
, and
Horn & Johnson 2012a
, Observation 7.1.3
, respectively),

\mathbf{P}_{j}=\xi_{\mathcal{X},\,j}(\mathbf{P}_{\mathcal{X},\,j}\otimes\mathbf{I}_{K_{\mathcal{Y}}})+\xi_{\mathcal{Y},\,j}(\mathbf{I}_{K_{\mathcal{X},\,j}}\otimes\mathbf{P}_{\mathcal{Y}})
is positive semi-definite for all
j=1,\ldots,J
i.e., assumption
(P)
is fulfilled.

•

(SPP)
:
Let
\kappa\geq 2
denote the degree of the (unconstrained) B-splines contained in
\bar{\mathbf{b}}_{\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c}}}
.
Every function contained in
\spano(\bar{\mathbf{b}}_{\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c}}})
is a
(\kappa-1)
times continuously differentiable piecewise polynomial with the breakpoints corresponding to the knots defining the B-spline basis

(
De 2001a
)
.
By Lemma
6.1
, we have
\spano(\tilde{\mathbf{b}}_{\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c}}})\subset\spano(\bar{\mathbf{b}}_{\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c}}})
and thus every function contained in
\spano(\tilde{\mathbf{b}}_{\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c}}})
is also such a piecewise polynomial and
we obtain
(SPP)
.

•

(B)
:
We first consider
\tilde{\mathbf{b}}_{\mathcal{Y}}
. In the continuous or discrete special case, linear independence of the functions contained in
\tilde{\mathbf{b}}_{\mathcal{Y}}
was already shown in Lemma
6.1
.
In the mixed case, the basis in
L^{2}_{0}(\mu)
is obtained by applying the embedding

\tilde{\iota}_{\mathrm{c}}
given in (
6.1
)
to the linear independent functions
\tilde{b}_{\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c}},\,1},\ldots,\tilde{b}_{\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c}},\,K_{\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c}}}}\in L^{2}_{0}(\lambda)

and the embedding
\tilde{\iota}_{\mathrm{d}}
given in (
6.2
)
to the linear independent functions
\tilde{b}_{\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{d}}^{\bullet},\,1},\ldots,\tilde{b}_{\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{d}}^{\bullet},\,K_{\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{d}}^{\bullet}}}\in L^{2}_{0}(\delta^{\bullet})
.
Since these embeddings are injective, they retain linear independence and thus the functions contained in the vector
\tilde{\mathbf{b}}_{\mathcal{Y}}
are linearly independent.
Continuity and boundedness of the functions contained in
\tilde{\mathbf{b}}_{\mathcal{Y}}
follows per construction from continuity and boundedness of the B-spline basis
\bar{b}_{\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c}},\,1},\ldots,\bar{b}_{\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c}},\,K_{\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c}}}+1}
.

Regarding
\mathbf{b}_{\mathcal{X}}
,
the linear independence, continuity, and boundedness assumptions are fulfilled for usual choices of basis functions
\mathbf{b}_{\mathcal{X},\,j},j\in\{1,\ldots,J\}
, in particular the ones we recommend in Section
2.2
(recalling the covariate domain
\mathcal{X}
is compact).
Note that across different partial effects, i.e., for
j\neq j^{\prime}\in\{1,\ldots,J\}
, identifiability constraints ensure linear independence, where necessary (e.g., if both partial effects are an effect of the same smooth covariate – one a main effect, the other an interaction effect).
Hence, assumption
(B)
holds.

6.1.3
Assumptions
(BX)
,
(BX’)
,
(BX”)
,
(S

<

)
, and
(BU)

We now recall the assumptions, which were used to formulate the statements in Section
2
and introduce the additional assumption
(BX”)
.
For this purpose, denote the diameter of a set
X\subset\mathcal{X}^{\mathfrak{C}}
with
\diam(X):=\sup\{\|\mathbf{x}-\tilde{\mathbf{x}}\|_{2}\penalty\ |\penalty\ \mathbf{x},\tilde{\mathbf{x}}\in X\}
.

(BX)

For
\mathbf{v}_{\mathcal{X}}\in\mathbb{R}^{K_{\mathcal{X}}}
:
If
\mathbf{b}_{\mathcal{X}}(\mathbf{x}_{i})^{\top}\mathbf{v}_{\mathcal{X}}=0
for all
i=1,\ldots,N
, then
\mathbf{v}_{\mathcal{X}}=\mathbf{0}
.

(BX’)

There exist
M^{\prime}>0
(independent of
N
) and
N^{\prime}\in\mathbb{N}
such that for all
N\geq N^{\prime}
and all
\mathbf{v}_{\mathcal{X}}\in\mathbb{R}^{K_{\mathcal{X}}}
,

\displaystyle M^{\prime}\,N\int_{\mathcal{X}}\left(\mathbf{b}_{\mathcal{X}}(\mathbf{x})^{\top}\mathbf{v}_{\mathcal{X}}\right)^{2}\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{\nu}(\mathbf{x})\leq\sum_{i=1}^{N}\left(\mathbf{b}_{\mathcal{X}}(\mathbf{x}_{i})^{\top}\mathbf{v}_{\mathcal{X}}\right)^{2},

(6.3)

(BX”)

There exist
\alpha\in(0,1)
,

M^{\prime\prime}>0
, and
N^{\prime\prime}\in\mathbb{N}
such that for all
N\geq N^{\prime\prime}
, all
\mathbf{x}^{\mathfrak{D}}\in\mathcal{X}^{\mathfrak{D}}
, and all subsets
X\subseteq\mathcal{X}^{\mathfrak{C}}
with
\diam(X)\geq N^{\alpha-1}
,

\displaystyle M^{\prime\prime}\,N\,\diam(X)^{|\mathfrak{C}|}\leq\sum_{i=1}^{N}\mathbbm{1}_{\{\mathbf{x}^{\mathfrak{D}}\}\times X}(\mathbf{x}^{\mathfrak{D}}_{i},\mathbf{x}^{\mathfrak{C}}_{i}).

(6.4)

(S
<
)

For all
\mathbf{v}\in\mathbb{S}^{K-1}
there exists
i\in\{1,\ldots,N\}
with
s_{\mathbf{v},\mathbf{x}_{i}}(y_{i})<s_{\mathbf{v},\mathbf{x}_{i}}^{\max}
.

(BU)

For
\mathcal{Z}\in\Xi^{L}
,

l\in\{1,\ldots,L\}
,

\mathbf{v}_{\mathcal{Y}}\in\mathbb{R}^{K_{\mathcal{Y}}}
, and
c\in\mathbb{R}
:
If
\tilde{\mathbf{b}}_{\mathcal{Y}}(u_{g}^{(l)})^{\top}\mathbf{v}_{\mathcal{Y}}=c
for all
g=1,\ldots,\Gamma^{(l)}
, then

\mathbf{v}_{\mathcal{Y}}=\mathbf{0}
(and
c=0
).

In the following remark, we discuss these assumptions, amplifying the respective discussion paragraphs after introducing the assumptions in Sections
2.2
and
2.3
.
For this purpose, recall that

\mathbf{B}_{\mathcal{X}}\in\mathbb{R}^{N\times K_{\mathcal{X}}}
denotes the matrix containing
\mathbf{b}_{\mathcal{X}}(\mathbf{x}_{i})
in the
i
-th row,
i=1,\ldots,N
,

\tilde{\mathbf{B}}_{\mathcal{Y}}^{(l)}\in\mathbb{R}^{\Gamma^{(l)}\times K_{\mathcal{Y}}}
the matrix containing
\tilde{\mathbf{b}}_{\mathcal{Y}}(u_{g}^{(l)})

in the
g
-th row,
g=1,\ldots,\Gamma^{(l)}
,
\mathbf{1}_{\Gamma^{(l)}}
the vector of length
\Gamma^{(l)}
containing
1
in every entry,
and
(\mathbf{A}:\mathbf{B})
concatenation of matrices
\mathbf{A},\mathbf{B}
with equal number of rows.

Remark 6.2
.

1)

We will show in Lemma
6.5

1)
that assumption
(BX)
holds, if
\mathbf{B}_{\mathcal{X}}
has rank
K_{\mathcal{X}}
.
Here, we thus focus on fulfilling this rank condition, which can actually be achieved by choosing the basis functions
\mathbf{b}_{\mathcal{X},\,j},j\in\{1,\ldots,J\}
appropriately for given observations
\mathbf{x}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{x}_{N}
. More precisely, for
j\in\{1,\ldots,J\}
, the basis functions
\mathbf{b}_{\mathcal{X},\,j}
have to be chosen such that

(i)

their number
K_{\mathcal{X},\,j}
is at most the number of unique observed values of the subvector of covariates, on which the
j
-th partial effect depends,
2
2

2

The subvector might contain only one covariate. If it depends on more than one covariate, the number of unique observed values of the subvector corresponds to the unique observed combinations of the respective covariates.

(ii)

all functions contained in
\mathbf{b}_{\mathcal{X},\,j}
have at least one observation in their support (using B-splines to model smooth effects of continuous covariates).

Let
\mathbf{B}_{\mathcal{X},\,j}\in\mathbb{R}^{N\times K_{\mathcal{X},\,j}}
denote the matrix containing
\mathbf{b}_{\mathcal{X},\,j}(\mathbf{x}_{i})
in the
i
-th row.
First we derive that under conditions
1)(i)
and
1)(ii)
,
\mathbf{B}_{\mathcal{X},\,j}
has rank
K_{\mathcal{X},\,j}
.
To see this, note that the rank of
\mathbf{B}_{\mathcal{X},j}
equals the number of linearly independent rows, which is at most the number of unique rows.
Thus,
1)(i)
ensures that
K_{\mathcal{X},\,j}
is at most the number of unique rows.
The second condition
1)(ii)
then ensures that
\mathbf{B}_{\mathcal{X},\,j}
has linearly independent columns, or equivalently, rank
K_{\mathcal{X},\,j}
.
For a linear effect of a continuous covariate or a group specific intercept of a grouping variable this is straightforward. For smooth effects modeled via a B-spline basis see
De 2001a
.
3
3

3

De 2001a
actually states that our condition of interest, i.e., any B-spline function having at least one observation in its support, is equivalent to a function
\|\cdot\|_{2}
on the span of the B-spline basis, which
De 2001a
denote with
\$_{k,t}
(where
k
is the order of the B-spline basis and
t
corresponds to the knot sequence), being a norm on
\$_{k,t}
.
However,
\|\cdot\|_{2}
being a norm on
\$_{k,t}
implies that
\mathbf{B}_{\mathcal{X},j}
has full column rank, which is stated as part of
De 2001a
: “Assume that
\|\cdot\|_{2}
is a norm on
\$_{k,t}
(that is,
\rank A=n
) […]”, where
A
corresponds to our matrix
\mathbf{B}_{\mathcal{X},j}
and
n
corresponds to the number of B-spline functions, ultimately yielding the referenced statement.

Note that in practice, these bases might be transformed to enforce necessary identifiability constraints. Per construction this retains linear independence of columns in
\mathbf{B}_{\mathcal{X},j}
for each
j\in\{1,\ldots,J\}
and yields linear independence of columns in the concatenated matrix across different partial effects
(\mathbf{B}_{\mathcal{X},j}:\mathbf{B}_{\mathcal{X},j^{\prime}})
for
j\neq j^{\prime}\in\{1,\ldots,J\}
.

All in all, the full column rank condition and thus assumption
(BX)
is not restrictive and actually requiring full column rank of the design matrix is very common for regression models.
Furthermore, note that
(BX)
is similar to the nonsingular-condition for logspline regression models
(e.g.,
Stone 1991c
)
.

2)

We will show in Lemma
6.5

2)
that assumption
(BX”)
implies
(BX’)
, which implies
(BX)
for
N\geq N^{\prime}
. Assumptions
(BX’)
and
(BX”)
generalize the lower bounds of Equations (1) and (2) in
Stone 1991c
, where only one continuous covariate is considered and no proof is provided for the implication of the first assumption by the second one.
We simplify the second assumption, demanding it to hold for one
\alpha\in(0,1)
, while the corresponding assumption (2) in
Stone 1991c
is formulated to hold for all
\alpha>0
(with constants
M^{\prime\prime}
and
N^{\prime\prime}
depending on
\alpha
), which is not necessary for the implication of
(BX’)
.
4
4

4

Noteworthy, if
(BX”)
holds for an
\alpha\in(0,1)
, it immediately holds for all
\alpha^{\prime}>\alpha
, using the same constants
M^{\prime\prime}>0
and
N^{\prime\prime}\in\mathbb{N}
, since
N^{\alpha^{\prime}-1}>N^{\alpha-1}
for all
N\geq N^{\prime\prime}
.

While some statements only assume
(BX’)
(e.g., Theorem
2.2
), this assumption is not very tangible.
However, assumption
(BX”)
is: It basically requires that the covariate observations are spread reasonably over the whole domain, i.e., the fraction of observations in large enough subregions is lower-bounded by a function of the subregion’s volume. In particular, assumption
(BX”)
also implies that for any
\mathbf{x}^{\mathfrak{D}}\in\mathcal{X}^{\mathfrak{D}}
, the number of observations taking this particular
\mathbf{x}^{\mathfrak{D}}
grows with
N
.

3)

Assumption
(S

<

)
is always satisfiable under assumption
(BX)
, as we will show in Lemma
6.3
that for all
\mathbf{v}\in\mathbb{S}^{K-1}
there exists an
i\in\{1,\ldots,N\}
such that the function
s_{\mathbf{v},\mathbf{x}_{i}}
is not constant. Thus, there exists a subset of
\mathcal{Y}
, on which
s_{\mathbf{v},\mathbf{x}_{i}}
does not reach its maximum.
We will furthermore show in Lemma
6.5

3)
that under assumption
(BX”)
, the probability that
(S

<

)
holds tends to one for
N\rightarrow\infty
.

4)

We will show in Lemma
6.5

4)
that assumption
(BU)
is fulfilled, if

(\tilde{\mathbf{B}}_{\mathcal{Y}}^{(l)}:\mathbf{1}_{\Gamma^{(l)}})\in\mathbb{R}^{\Gamma^{(l)}\times(K_{\mathcal{Y}}+1)}
has rank
K_{\mathcal{Y}}+1
for any
l\in\{1,\ldots,L\}
,
which for
\tilde{\mathbf{b}}_{\mathcal{Y}}
chosen as in Section
2.2
is fulfilled for small enough bin width.

Before formulating and proving Lemma
6.5
, we show two auxiliary statements (Lemmas
6.3
and
6.4
).

Lemma 6.3
.

Assume that
(BX)
holds and let
\mathbf{v}\in\mathbb{S}^{K-1}
.
If the function
s_{\mathbf{v},\mathbf{x}_{i}}=\tilde{\mathbf{b}}(\mathbf{x}_{i})^{\top}\mathbf{v}
is constant over
\mathcal{Y}
for every
i\in\{1,\ldots,N\}
, then
\mathbf{v}=\mathbf{0}
.

Proof.

Let
i\in\{1,\ldots,N\}
.
Since
\tilde{\mathbf{b}}(\mathbf{x}_{i})\in L^{2}_{0}(\mu)^{K}
, we obtain that
\tilde{\mathbf{b}}(\mathbf{x}_{i})^{\top}\mathbf{v}
being constant over
\mathcal{Y}
implies
\tilde{\mathbf{b}}(\mathbf{x}_{i})^{\top}\mathbf{v}=0
.
Per assumption, this holds for every
i\in\{1,\ldots,N\}
.
Let
\mathbf{v}_{\mathcal{X}}=(\tilde{\mathbf{b}}_{\mathcal{Y}}^{\top}\mathbf{v}_{1,1},\ldots,\tilde{\mathbf{b}}_{\mathcal{Y}}^{\top}\mathbf{v}_{J,K_{\mathcal{X},\,J}})
with
\mathbf{v}_{j,n}=(v_{j,n,1},\ldots,v_{j,n,K_{\mathcal{Y}}})^{\top}
, where
v_{j,n,m}
for
j=1,\ldots,J,\penalty\ n=1,\ldots,K_{\mathcal{X},\,j}
, and
m=1,\ldots,K_{\mathcal{Y}}
, corresponds to the respective entry of
\mathbf{v}
, where the indices are arranged in the same order as the vector
\boldsymbol{\theta}\in\mathbb{R}^{K}
containing the values
\theta_{j,n,m}
in Section
2.2
.
It is straightforward to show
\tilde{\mathbf{b}}(\mathbf{x}_{i})^{\top}\mathbf{v}=\mathbf{b}_{\mathcal{X}}(\mathbf{x}_{i})^{\top}\mathbf{v}_{\mathcal{X}}
and from assumption
(BX)
we obtain
\mathbf{v}_{\mathcal{X}}=\mathbf{0}
, i.e.,
\tilde{\mathbf{b}}_{\mathcal{Y}}^{\top}\mathbf{v}_{j,n}=0
for all
j=1,\ldots,J,\penalty\ n=1,\ldots,K_{\mathcal{X},\,j}
.
Since the functions contained in
\tilde{\mathbf{b}}_{\mathcal{Y}}
are linearly independent, this implies
\mathbf{v}_{j,n}=\mathbf{0}
for all
j=1,\ldots,J,\penalty\ n=1,\ldots,K_{\mathcal{X},\,j}
, i.e.,
\mathbf{v}=\mathbf{0}
.
∎

Lemma 6.4
.

The function

\mathbb{S}^{K-1}\times\mathcal{X}\rightarrow\mathbb{R},(\mathbf{v},\mathbf{x})\mapsto s_{\mathbf{v},\mathbf{x}}^{\max}

is (uniformly) continuous.

Proof.

Let
\varepsilon>0
and denote the maximum norm, defined as the maximum of the absolute values of the entries of a vector, by
\|\cdot\|_{\max}
.
We show that there exists
\delta>0
such that for all
(\mathbf{v},\mathbf{x}),(\mathbf{v}^{\prime},\mathbf{x}^{\prime})\in\mathbb{S}^{K-1}\times\mathcal{X}
, we have
|s_{\mathbf{v},\mathbf{x}}^{\max}-s_{\mathbf{v}^{\prime},\mathbf{x}^{\prime}}^{\max}|<\varepsilon
, if
\|(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{v})-(\mathbf{x}^{\prime},\mathbf{v}^{\prime})\|_{\max}<\delta
.
For this purpose, we use uniform continuity of
s_{\mathbf{v},\mathbf{x},\mathrm{c}}(y)
on
\mathbb{S}^{K-1}\times\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c}}
and continuity of
s_{\mathbf{v},\mathbf{x}}(y_{\mathrm{d}})
on
\mathbb{S}^{K-1}\times\mathcal{X}
for each
y_{\mathrm{d}}
in the finite set
\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{d}}
.

Since
s_{\mathbf{v},\mathbf{x},\mathrm{c}}(y)
is continuous and
\mathbb{S}^{K-1}\times\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c}}
is compact,
s_{\mathbf{v},\mathbf{x},\mathrm{c}}(y)
is uniformly continuous on
\mathbb{S}^{K-1}\times\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c}}
.
Thus, there exists a
\delta_{\mathrm{c}}>0
such that
\|(\mathbf{v},\mathbf{x},y)-(\mathbf{v}^{\prime},\mathbf{x}^{\prime},y^{\prime})\|_{\max}<\delta_{\mathrm{c}}
(or equivalently,
\|(\mathbf{v},\mathbf{x})-(\mathbf{v}^{\prime},\mathbf{x}^{\prime})\|_{\max}<\delta_{\mathrm{c}}
and
|y-y^{\prime}|<\delta_{\mathrm{c}}
) implies
|s_{\mathbf{v},\mathbf{x},\mathrm{c}}(y)-s_{\mathbf{v}^{\prime},\mathbf{x}^{\prime},\mathrm{c}}(y)|<\varepsilon
. Furthermore, as
s_{\mathbf{v},\mathbf{x}}(y_{\mathrm{d}})
is continuous on the compact set
\mathbb{S}^{K-1}\times\mathcal{X}
for every
y_{\mathrm{d}}\in\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{d}}
, there exists a
\delta_{y_{\mathrm{d}}}>0
such that
\|(\mathbf{v},\mathbf{x})-(\mathbf{v}^{\prime},\mathbf{x}^{\prime})\|_{\max}<\delta_{y_{\mathrm{d}}}
implies
|s_{\mathbf{v},\mathbf{x}}(y_{\mathrm{d}})-s_{\mathbf{v}^{\prime},\mathbf{x}^{\prime}}(y_{\mathrm{d}})|<\varepsilon
. Set
\delta:=\min(\{\delta_{\mathrm{c}}\}\cup\{\delta_{y_{\mathrm{d}}}\penalty\ |\penalty\ y_{\mathrm{d}}\in\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{d}}\})
.
Note that this minimum exists as
\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{d}}
is a finite set.
In the following, let
(\mathbf{v},\mathbf{x}),(\mathbf{v}^{\prime},\mathbf{x}^{\prime})\in\mathbb{S}^{K-1}\times\mathcal{X}
with
\|(\mathbf{v},\mathbf{x})-(\mathbf{v}^{\prime},\mathbf{x}^{\prime})\|_{\max}<\delta
.
Since
s_{\mathbf{v},\mathbf{x},\mathrm{c}}
is continuous on the compact set
\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c}}
, there exists
y_{\mathbf{v},\mathbf{x},\mathrm{c}}\in\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c}}
such that
s_{\mathbf{v},\mathbf{x},\mathrm{c}}(y_{\mathbf{v},\mathbf{x},\mathrm{c}})=s_{\mathbf{v},\mathbf{x},\mathrm{c}}^{\max}
.
Furthermore, since
\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{d}}
is a finite set, there exists
y_{\mathbf{v},\mathbf{x},\mathrm{d}}\in\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{d}}
such that
s_{\mathbf{v},\mathbf{x}}(y_{\mathbf{v},\mathbf{x},\mathrm{d}})=s_{\mathbf{v},\mathbf{x},\mathrm{d}}^{\max}
.
Since
s_{\mathbf{v},\mathbf{x}}^{\max}=\max\{s_{\mathbf{v},\mathbf{x},\mathrm{c}}^{\max},s_{\mathbf{v},\mathbf{x},\mathrm{d}}^{\max}\}
, we have
(i)
s_{\mathbf{v},\mathbf{x},\mathrm{c}}(y_{\mathbf{v},\mathbf{x},\mathrm{c}})=s_{\mathbf{v},\mathbf{x}}^{\max}

or
(ii)
s_{\mathbf{v},\mathbf{x}}(y_{\mathbf{v},\mathbf{x},\mathrm{d}})=s_{\mathbf{v},\mathbf{x}}^{\max}
.
In case (i), we have

s_{\mathbf{v},\mathbf{x},\mathrm{c}}(y_{\mathbf{v},\mathbf{x},\mathrm{c}})-\varepsilon<s_{\mathbf{v}^{\prime},\mathbf{x}^{\prime},\mathrm{c}}(y_{\mathbf{v},\mathbf{x},\mathrm{c}})

and thus

\displaystyle s_{\mathbf{v}^{\prime},\mathbf{x}^{\prime}}^{\max}\geq s_{\mathbf{v}^{\prime},\mathbf{x}^{\prime},\mathrm{c}}^{\max}\geq s_{\mathbf{v}^{\prime},\mathbf{x}^{\prime},\mathrm{c}}(y_{\mathbf{v},\mathbf{x},\mathrm{c}})>s_{\mathbf{v},\mathbf{x},\mathrm{c}}(y_{\mathbf{v},\mathbf{x},\mathrm{c}})-\varepsilon=s_{\mathbf{v},\mathbf{x}}^{\max}-\varepsilon.

Similarly, in case (ii), we have

s_{\mathbf{v},\mathbf{x}}(y_{\mathbf{v},\mathbf{x},\mathrm{d}})-\varepsilon<s_{\mathbf{v}^{\prime},\mathbf{x}^{\prime}}(y_{\mathbf{v},\mathbf{x},\mathrm{d}})

and thus

\displaystyle s_{\mathbf{v}^{\prime},\mathbf{x}^{\prime}}^{\max}\geq s_{\mathbf{v}^{\prime},\mathbf{x}^{\prime}}(y_{\mathbf{v},\mathbf{x},\mathrm{d}})>s_{\mathbf{v},\mathbf{x}}(y_{\mathbf{v},\mathbf{x},\mathrm{d}})-\varepsilon=s_{\mathbf{v},\mathbf{x}}^{\max}-\varepsilon.

Analogously, we obtain

s_{\mathbf{v},\mathbf{x}}^{\max}>s_{\mathbf{v}^{\prime},\mathbf{x}^{\prime}}^{\max}-\varepsilon

and
thus,
|s_{\mathbf{v},\mathbf{x}}^{\max}-s_{\mathbf{v}^{\prime},\mathbf{x}^{\prime}}^{\max}|<\varepsilon
.
∎

Lemma 6.5
.

1)

Assumption
(BX)
is fulfilled, if
\mathbf{B}_{\mathcal{X}}\in\mathbb{R}^{N\times K_{\mathcal{X}}}
has rank
K_{\mathcal{X}}
,

2)

Assumption
(BX”)
implies
(BX’)
which implies
(BX)
for all
N\geq N^{\prime}
.

3)

If assumption
(BX”)
holds, then the probability that assumption
(S

<

)
holds tends to
1
for
N\rightarrow\infty
.

4)

Assumption
(BU)
is fulfilled, if
(\tilde{\mathbf{B}}_{\mathcal{Y}}^{(l)}:\mathbf{1}_{\Gamma^{(l)}})\in\mathbb{R}^{\Gamma^{(l)}\times(K_{\mathcal{Y}}+1)}
has rank
K_{\mathcal{Y}}+1
for any
l\in\{1,\ldots,L\}
.
Assume the vector of basis functions
\tilde{\mathbf{b}}_{\mathcal{Y}}
is chosen as proposed in Section
2.2
.
If
\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c}}=\emptyset
, the rank condition holds.
If
\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c}}\neq\emptyset
,
the rank condition holds, if

K_{\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c}}}+1\leq G^{(l)}
and
for each
m=1,\ldots,K_{\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c}}}+1
, there exists a
g\in\{1,\ldots,G^{(l)}\}
such that

\bar{b}_{\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c}},\,m}(u_{g}^{(l)})>0
.
This is fulfilled for small enough bin width.

Proof.

1)

Since
\mathbf{B}_{\mathcal{X}}
has full column rank
K_{\mathcal{X}}
, the equation system
\mathbf{B}_{\mathcal{X}}\mathbf{v}_{\mathcal{X}}=\mathbf{0}
(which is simply the matrix notation of the equation system in
(BX)
) has a unique solution
(
Liesen & Mehrmann 2015a
, Corollary 6.6 (2))
.
Hence, the obvious solution
\mathbf{v}_{\mathcal{X}}=\mathbf{0}
is the unique solution.
This shows
(BX)
.

2)

We first show the second implication.
Thus, let
M^{\prime}>0
and
N^{\prime}\in\mathbb{N}
be the constants from
(BX’)
.
It is easily seen that then also
(BX)
holds for
N\geq N^{\prime}
:
Let
\mathbf{v}_{\mathcal{X}}\in\mathbb{R}^{K_{\mathcal{X}}}
such that
\mathbf{b}_{\mathcal{X}}(\mathbf{x}_{i})^{\top}\mathbf{v}_{\mathcal{X}}=0
for all
i=1,\ldots,N
.
Then,
\sum_{i=1}^{N}\left(\mathbf{b}_{\mathcal{X}}(\mathbf{x}_{i})^{\top}\mathbf{v}_{\mathcal{X}}\right)^{2}=0
and we obtain

M^{\prime}\,N\int_{\mathcal{X}}\left(\mathbf{b}_{\mathcal{X}}(\mathbf{x})^{\top}\mathbf{v}_{\mathcal{X}}\right)^{2}\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{\nu}(\mathbf{x})=0
with
(BX’)
and using that the integrand is nonnegative.
This implies
\mathbf{b}_{\mathcal{X}}(\mathbf{x})^{\top}\mathbf{v}_{\mathcal{X}}=0
for all
\mathbf{x}\in\mathcal{X}
and linear independence of the functions contained in
\mathbf{b}_{\mathcal{X}}
yields
\mathbf{v}_{\mathcal{X}}=\mathbf{0}
, i.e.,
(BX)
.

Now, we show that
(BX”)
implies
(BX’)
.
For this purpose, first set
\boldsymbol{\lambda}=\lambda^{|\mathfrak{C}|}
and let
\alpha\in(0,1)
,
M^{\prime\prime}>0
, and
N^{\prime\prime}\in\mathbb{N}
be the constants from
(BX”)
.
The main ideas are to use multidimensional Riemann sums for the continuous covariates, and to use that the mean of the function values at the observations whose continuous covariates are contained in some connected subset of
\mathcal{X}^{\mathfrak{C}}
and whose discrete covariates are
\mathbf{x}^{\mathfrak{D}}
lies between the function’s infimum and supremum on this set.
In the following, when considering partitions, each member is assumed to be a connected set.

We first show that for every
\tilde{M}>1
, there exists a
\delta>0
such that if a partition
X_{1},\ldots,X_{T}
of
\mathcal{X}^{\mathfrak{C}}
fulfills
\max_{t\in\{1,\ldots,T\}}\diam(X_{t})<\delta
, we have for any
(\boldsymbol{\xi}_{1}^{\mathfrak{C}},\ldots,\boldsymbol{\xi}_{T}^{\mathfrak{C}})\in\bigtimes_{t=1}^{T}X_{t}
, any
\mathbf{x}^{\mathfrak{D}}\in\mathcal{X}^{\mathfrak{D}}
, and any
\mathbf{v}_{\mathcal{X}}\in\mathbb{R}^{K_{\mathcal{X}}}
,

\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{X}}(\mathbf{b}_{\mathcal{X}}^{\top}\mathbf{v}_{\mathcal{X}})^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{\nu}\leq\tilde{M}\,\sum_{\mathbf{x}^{\mathfrak{D}}\in\mathcal{X}^{\mathfrak{D}}}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\boldsymbol{\lambda}(X_{t})\,\bigl(\mathbf{b}_{\mathcal{X}}(\mathbf{x}^{\mathfrak{D}},\boldsymbol{\xi}^{\mathfrak{C}}_{t})^{\top}\mathbf{v}_{\mathcal{X}}\bigr)^{2}

(6.5)

Thus, let
\tilde{M}>1
.
First note that

\langle\mathbf{v}_{\mathcal{X}},\mathbf{w}_{\mathcal{X}}\rangle_{\mathbf{b}_{\mathcal{X}}}:=\int_{\mathcal{X}}(\mathbf{b}_{\mathcal{X}}^{\top}\mathbf{v}_{\mathcal{X}})\,(\mathbf{b}_{\mathcal{X}}^{\top}\mathbf{w}_{\mathcal{X}})\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{\nu}
defines an inner product on
\mathbb{R}^{K_{\mathcal{X}}}\times\mathbb{R}^{K_{\mathcal{X}}}
, which is straightforwardly verified, using the linear independence of the functions contained in
\mathbf{b}_{\mathcal{X}}
.
Then,
\|\mathbf{v}_{\mathcal{X}}\|_{\mathbf{b}_{\mathcal{X}}}:=[\int_{\mathcal{X}}(\mathbf{b}_{\mathcal{X}}^{\top}\mathbf{v}_{\mathcal{X}})^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{\nu}]^{\frac{1}{2}}
defines a norm on
\mathbb{R}^{K_{\mathcal{X}}}
and thus there exists
M_{\mathbf{b}_{\mathcal{X}}}>0
such that

\|\mathbf{v}_{\mathcal{X}}\|_{2}\leq M_{\mathbf{b}_{\mathcal{X}}}\,\|\mathbf{v}_{\mathcal{X}}\|_{\mathbf{b}_{\mathcal{X}}}

(e.g.,
Horn & Johnson 2012a
, Corollary 5.4.5)
.
For
\mathbf{x}^{\mathfrak{D}}\in\mathcal{X}^{\mathfrak{D}}
, consider the real-valued continuous function
\mathbf{x}^{\mathfrak{C}}\mapsto b_{k,k^{\prime}}(\mathbf{x}^{\mathfrak{D}},\mathbf{x}^{\mathfrak{C}}):=\mathbf{b}_{\mathcal{X}}(\mathbf{x}^{\mathfrak{D}},\mathbf{x}^{\mathfrak{C}})_{[k]}\mathbf{b}_{\mathcal{X}}(\mathbf{x}^{\mathfrak{D}},\mathbf{x}^{\mathfrak{C}})_{[k^{\prime}]}
, where the index
{[k]}
denotes the
k
-th entry of the vector.
Then, there exist
\delta_{\mathbf{x}^{\mathfrak{D}},k,k^{\prime}}>0
such that if a partition
X_{1},\ldots,X_{T}
of
\mathcal{X}^{\mathfrak{C}}
fulfills
\max_{t\in\{1,\ldots,T\}}\diam(X_{t})<\delta_{\mathbf{x}^{\mathfrak{D}},k,k^{\prime}}
, we have

\displaystyle\Bigl|\int_{\mathcal{X}^{\mathfrak{C}}}b_{k,k^{\prime}}(\mathbf{x}^{\mathfrak{D}},\mathbf{x}^{\mathfrak{C}})\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{\lambda}(\mathbf{x}^{\mathfrak{C}})-\sum_{t=1}^{T}\boldsymbol{\lambda}(X_{t})\,b_{k,k^{\prime}}(\mathbf{x}^{\mathfrak{D}},\boldsymbol{\xi}_{t}^{\mathfrak{C}})\Bigr|<\frac{1-\tilde{M}^{-1}}{M_{\mathbf{b}_{\mathcal{X}}}\,|\mathcal{X}^{\mathfrak{D}}|\,K_{\mathcal{X}}},

for any
(\boldsymbol{\xi}_{1}^{\mathfrak{C}},\ldots,\boldsymbol{\xi}_{T}^{\mathfrak{C}})\in\bigtimes_{t=1}^{T}X_{t}

(e.g.,
Fusco et al. 2022a
, p. 376:
Integral of a Continuous Function as Limit
)
.
Let
\delta:=\min\{\delta_{\mathbf{x}^{\mathfrak{D}},k,k^{\prime}}\penalty\ |\penalty\ \mathbf{x}^{\mathfrak{D}}\in\mathcal{X}^{\mathfrak{D}},\,k,k^{\prime}\in\{1,\ldots,K_{\mathcal{X}}\}\}>0
and
X_{1},\ldots,X_{T}
a partition of
\mathcal{X}^{\mathfrak{C}}
with
\max_{t\in\{1,\ldots,T\}}\diam(X_{t})

<\delta
.
Then, for any
\mathbf{v}_{\mathcal{X}}=(v_{\mathcal{X},\,1},\ldots,v_{\mathcal{X},\,K_{\mathcal{X}}})\in\mathbb{R}^{K_{\mathcal{X}}}
and for any
(\boldsymbol{\xi}_{1}^{\mathfrak{C}},\ldots,\boldsymbol{\xi}_{T}^{\mathfrak{C}})\in\bigtimes_{t=1}^{T}X_{t}
, we have

\displaystyle\penalty\ \Bigl|\int_{\mathcal{X}}(\mathbf{b}_{\mathcal{X}}^{\top}\mathbf{v}_{\mathcal{X}})^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{\nu}-\sum_{\mathbf{x}^{\mathfrak{D}}\in\mathcal{X}^{\mathfrak{D}}}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\boldsymbol{\lambda}(X_{t})\,\bigl(\mathbf{b}_{\mathcal{X}}(\mathbf{x}^{\mathfrak{D}},\boldsymbol{\xi}^{\mathfrak{C}}_{t})^{\top}\mathbf{v}_{\mathcal{X}}\bigr)^{2}\Bigr|

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\penalty\ \Bigl|\sum_{k,k^{\prime}=1}^{K_{\mathcal{X}}}v_{\mathcal{X},\,k}v_{\mathcal{X},\,k^{\prime}}\Bigl(\sum_{\mathbf{x}^{\mathfrak{D}}\in\mathcal{X}^{\mathfrak{D}}}\Bigl(\int_{\mathcal{X}^{\mathfrak{C}}}b_{k,k^{\prime}}(\mathbf{x}^{\mathfrak{D}},\mathbf{x}^{\mathfrak{C}})\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{\lambda}(\mathbf{x}^{\mathfrak{C}})-\sum_{t=1}^{T}\boldsymbol{\lambda}(X_{t})\,b_{k,k^{\prime}}(\mathbf{x}^{\mathfrak{D}},\boldsymbol{\xi}_{t}^{\mathfrak{C}})\Bigr)\Bigr)\Bigr|

\displaystyle\overset{(\ast)}{\leq}\hskip-2.84544pt

\displaystyle\penalty\ \Bigl(\sum_{k,k^{\prime}=1}^{K_{\mathcal{X}}}v_{\mathcal{X},\,k}^{2}v_{\mathcal{X},\,k^{\prime}}^{2}\Bigr)^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle\penalty\ \Bigl(\sum_{k,k^{\prime}=1}^{K_{\mathcal{X}}}\Bigl(\sum_{\mathbf{x}^{\mathfrak{D}}\in\mathcal{X}^{\mathfrak{D}}}\int_{\mathcal{X}^{\mathfrak{C}}}b_{k,k^{\prime}}(\mathbf{x}^{\mathfrak{D}},\mathbf{x}^{\mathfrak{C}})\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{\lambda}(\mathbf{x}^{\mathfrak{C}})-\sum_{t=1}^{T}\boldsymbol{\lambda}(X_{t})\,b_{k,k^{\prime}}(\mathbf{x}^{\mathfrak{D}},\boldsymbol{\xi}_{t}^{\mathfrak{C}})\Bigr)^{2}\Bigr)^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle<

\displaystyle\penalty\ \Bigl(\sum_{k=1}^{K_{\mathcal{X}}}v_{\mathcal{X},\,k}^{2}\Bigr)\,\Bigl(K_{\mathcal{X}}^{2}\,|\mathcal{X}^{\mathfrak{D}}|^{2}\,\frac{(1-\tilde{M}^{-1})^{2}}{M_{\mathbf{b}_{\mathcal{X}}}^{2}\,|\mathcal{X}^{\mathfrak{D}}|^{2}\,K_{\mathcal{X}}^{2}}\Bigr)^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\penalty\ \|\mathbf{v}_{\mathcal{X}}\|_{2}^{2}\,\frac{1-\tilde{M}^{-1}}{M_{\mathbf{b}_{\mathcal{X}}}}\leq(1-\tilde{M}^{-1})\,\int_{\mathcal{X}}(\mathbf{b}_{\mathcal{X}}^{\top}\mathbf{v}_{\mathcal{X}})^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{\nu},

where we used the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality
(e.g.,
Horn & Johnson 2012a
, Theorem 5.1.4)
in
(\ast)
.
This implies (
6.5
).

In the following, let
\tilde{M}>1
and
\delta>0
such that (
6.5
) holds for partitions of fineness
\delta
.
Since
\alpha\in(0,1)
, we have
\lim_{N\rightarrow\infty}N^{\alpha-1}=0
, and thus
there exists
N_{\alpha}\in\mathbb{N}
such that

N^{\alpha-1}<\delta
for
N\geq N_{\alpha}
. In the following, let
N\geq\max\{N_{\alpha},N^{\prime\prime}\}=:N^{\prime}

and let

X_{1},\ldots,X_{T}

be a partition
of
\mathcal{X}^{\mathfrak{C}}
such that

N^{\alpha-1}<\diam(X_{t})<\delta
for all
t\in\{1,\ldots,T\}
. Then,
(
6.4
) holds for every
X_{t},\penalty\ t\in\{1,\ldots,T\}
.
Furthermore,
for any such
X_{t}
, any

\mathbf{v}_{\mathcal{X}}\in\mathbb{R}^{K_{\mathcal{X}}}
,
and any
\mathbf{x}^{\mathfrak{D}}\in\mathcal{X}^{\mathfrak{D}}
, the mean of the function values of
\mathbf{x}^{\mathfrak{C}}\mapsto(\mathbf{b}_{\mathcal{X}}(\mathbf{x}^{\mathfrak{D}},\mathbf{x}^{\mathfrak{C}})^{\top}\mathbf{v}_{\mathcal{X}})^{2}
at the observations whose continuous covariates are contained in
X_{t}
and whose discrete covariates are
\mathbf{x}^{\mathfrak{D}}
lies between the function’s infimum and supremum:

\displaystyle\inf_{\mathbf{x}^{\mathfrak{C}}\in X_{t}}(\mathbf{b}_{\mathcal{X}}(\mathbf{x}^{\mathfrak{D}},\mathbf{x}^{\mathfrak{C}})^{\top}\mathbf{v}_{\mathcal{X}})^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{N}\mathbbm{1}_{\{\mathbf{x}^{\mathfrak{D}}\}\times X_{t}}(\mathbf{x}^{\mathfrak{D}}_{i},\mathbf{x}^{\mathfrak{C}}_{i})\,(\mathbf{b}_{\mathcal{X}}(\mathbf{x}^{\mathfrak{D}}_{i},\mathbf{x}^{\mathfrak{C}}_{i})^{\top}\mathbf{v}_{\mathcal{X}})^{2}}{\sum_{i=1}^{N}\mathbbm{1}_{\{\mathbf{x}^{\mathfrak{D}}\}\times X_{t}}(\mathbf{x}^{\mathfrak{D}}_{i},\mathbf{x}^{\mathfrak{C}}_{i})}

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{\mathbf{x}^{\mathfrak{C}}\in X_{t}}(\mathbf{b}_{\mathcal{X}}(\mathbf{x}^{\mathfrak{D}},\mathbf{x}^{\mathfrak{C}})^{\top}\mathbf{v}_{\mathcal{X}})^{2}.

With the Intermediate Value Theorem
(e.g.,
Fusco et al. 2022a
, p.93)
, there exists a
\boldsymbol{\xi}^{\mathfrak{C}}_{\mathbf{x}^{\mathfrak{D}},\,t}
in the interior
X_{t}^{\circ}
of
X_{t}
, such that

\displaystyle(\mathbf{b}_{\mathcal{X}}(\mathbf{x}^{\mathfrak{D}},\boldsymbol{\xi}^{\mathfrak{C}}_{\mathbf{x}^{\mathfrak{D}},\,t})^{\top}\mathbf{v}_{\mathcal{X}})^{2}=\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{N}\mathbbm{1}_{\{\mathbf{x}^{\mathfrak{D}}\}\times X_{t}}(\mathbf{x}^{\mathfrak{D}}_{i},\mathbf{x}^{\mathfrak{C}}_{i})\,(\mathbf{b}_{\mathcal{X}}(\mathbf{x}^{\mathfrak{D}}_{i},\mathbf{x}^{\mathfrak{C}}_{i})^{\top}\mathbf{v}_{\mathcal{X}})^{2}}{\sum_{i=1}^{N}\mathbbm{1}_{\{\mathbf{x}^{\mathfrak{D}}\}\times X_{t}}(\mathbf{x}^{\mathfrak{D}}_{i},\mathbf{x}^{\mathfrak{C}}_{i})}.

Then,

\displaystyle\penalty\ \int_{\mathcal{X}}(\mathbf{b}_{\mathcal{X}}^{\top}\mathbf{v}_{\mathcal{X}})^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{\nu}

\displaystyle\overset{\eqref{P2a:eq_stone_implication_ingetral_bound}}{\leq}

\displaystyle\penalty\ \tilde{M}\,\sum_{\mathbf{x}^{\mathfrak{D}}\in\mathcal{X}^{\mathfrak{D}}}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\boldsymbol{\lambda}(X_{t})\,\bigl(\mathbf{b}_{\mathcal{X}}(\mathbf{x}^{\mathfrak{D}},\boldsymbol{\xi}^{\mathfrak{C}}_{\mathbf{x}^{\mathfrak{D}},\,t})^{\top}\mathbf{v}_{\mathcal{X}}\bigr)^{2}

\displaystyle\overset{(\ast)}{\leq}\hskip 7.11317pt

\displaystyle\penalty\ \tilde{M}\,\sum_{\mathbf{x}^{\mathfrak{D}}\in\mathcal{X}^{\mathfrak{D}}}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\diam(X_{t})^{|\mathfrak{C}|}\,\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{N}\mathbbm{1}_{\{\mathbf{x}^{\mathfrak{D}}\}\times X_{t}}(\mathbf{x}^{\mathfrak{D}}_{i},\mathbf{x}^{\mathfrak{C}}_{i})\,(\mathbf{b}_{\mathcal{X}}(\mathbf{x}^{\mathfrak{D}}_{i},\mathbf{x}^{\mathfrak{C}}_{i})^{\top}\mathbf{v}_{\mathcal{X}})^{2}}{\sum_{i=1}^{N}\mathbbm{1}_{\{\mathbf{x}^{\mathfrak{D}}\}\times X_{t}}(\mathbf{x}^{\mathfrak{D}}_{i},\mathbf{x}^{\mathfrak{C}}_{i})}

\displaystyle\overset{\eqref{P2a:eq_stone_2}}{\leq}

\displaystyle\penalty\ \tilde{M}\,\sum_{\mathbf{x}^{\mathfrak{D}}\in\mathcal{X}^{\mathfrak{D}}}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\diam(X_{t})^{|\mathfrak{C}|}\,\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{N}\mathbbm{1}_{\{\mathbf{x}^{\mathfrak{D}}\}\times X_{t}}(\mathbf{x}^{\mathfrak{D}}_{i},\mathbf{x}^{\mathfrak{C}}_{i})\,(\mathbf{b}_{\mathcal{X}}(\mathbf{x}^{\mathfrak{D}}_{i},\mathbf{x}^{\mathfrak{C}}_{i})^{\top}\mathbf{v}_{\mathcal{X}})^{2}}{M^{\prime\prime}\,N\,\diam(X_{t})^{|\mathfrak{C}|}}

\displaystyle=\hskip 7.11317pt

\displaystyle\penalty\ \frac{\tilde{M}}{M^{\prime\prime}\,N}\,\sum_{i=1}^{N}\,(\mathbf{b}_{\mathcal{X}}(\mathbf{x}_{i})^{\top}\mathbf{v}_{\mathcal{X}})^{2},

where
(\ast)
holds since for each
t\in\{1,\ldots,T\}
, there exists a
{|\mathfrak{C}|}
-dimensional cube
Q_{t}
with edge length
\diam(X_{t})
such that
X_{t}\subseteq Q_{t}
and due to monotonicity of measures
\boldsymbol{\lambda}(X_{t})\leq\boldsymbol{\lambda}(Q_{t})=\diam(X_{t})^{|\mathfrak{C}|}
.
Setting
M^{\prime}:=\tilde{M}^{-1}\,M^{\prime\prime}
, we obtain
(BX’)
as desired.

3)

Consider the function

\displaystyle\rho:\mathbb{S}^{K-1}\times\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{Y}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{+}_{0},

\displaystyle(\mathbf{v},\mathbf{x},y)\mapsto s_{\mathbf{v},\mathbf{x}}^{\max}-s_{\mathbf{v},\mathbf{x}}(y).

We aim to show

\lim_{N\rightarrow\infty}\mathbb{P}(A_{N})=1
,
where

A_{N}:=\bigl\{(y_{1},\ldots,y_{N})\in\mathcal{Y}^{N}\penalty\ |\penalty\ \forall\mathbf{v}\in\mathbb{S}^{K-1}\exists i\in\{1,\ldots,N\}:0<\rho(\mathbf{v},\mathbf{x}_{i},y_{i})\bigr\}.

This probability is hard to compute directly due to
\mathbb{S}^{K-1}
being uncountable.
Thus, we construct a subset of
A_{N}
(which will be denoted as
\tilde{A}_{N}
), whose probability tends to
1
for
N\rightarrow\infty
, based on a finite covering of
\mathbb{S}^{K-1}
, which does not depend on
N
.
More precisely, we define subsets of
\mathbb{S}^{K-1}\times\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{Y}
, on which
\rho
is positive (i.e., fulfilling the condition of
A_{N}
such that
\tilde{A}_{N}\subset A_{N}
holds), whose projection onto
\mathbb{S}^{K-1}
yields a finite covering, whose projection onto
\mathcal{Y}
has positive measure
\mu
, and whose projection onto
\mathcal{X}
allows to apply
(BX”)
.
The latter two properties particularly will help us to prove that the probability of
\tilde{A}_{N}
tends to one.
To construct those subsets, we first introduce some notation.
Denote the maximum norm, defined as the maximum of the absolute values of the entries of a vector, by
\|\cdot\|_{\max}
.
For
\mathbf{v}\in\mathbb{S}^{K-1},\mathbf{x}\in\mathcal{X}
,
y\in\mathcal{Y}
, and

\varepsilon>0
, set

\displaystyle\upsilon_{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{v},\mathbf{x},y)

\displaystyle:=\{(\tilde{\mathbf{v}},\tilde{\mathbf{x}},\tilde{y})\in\mathbb{S}^{K-1}\times\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{Y}\penalty\ |\penalty\ \|(\tilde{\mathbf{v}},\tilde{\mathbf{x}},\tilde{y})-(\mathbf{v},\mathbf{x},y)\|_{\max}<\varepsilon\}.

Analogously, define sets
\upsilon_{\varepsilon}(\cdot)
, where
(\cdot)
corresponds to a sub-tupel of
(\mathbf{v},\mathbf{x},y)

e.g.,

\upsilon_{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{v}):=\{\tilde{\mathbf{v}}\in\mathbb{S}^{K-1}\penalty\ |\penalty\ \|\tilde{\mathbf{v}}-\mathbf{v}\|_{\max}<\varepsilon\}
.

Let
\mathbf{v}\in\mathbb{S}^{K-1}
. Then,
\mathbf{v}\neq\mathbf{0}
and thus by Lemma
6.3
, for all observations
\mathbf{x}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{x}_{N}\in\mathcal{X}
fulfilling assumption
(BX)
, there exists an
i\in\{1,\ldots,N\}
such that
\tilde{\mathbf{b}}(\mathbf{x}_{i})^{\top}\mathbf{v}=s_{\mathbf{v},\mathbf{x}_{i}}
is not constant.
As by
2)
there exist observations fulfilling
(BX)
(for large enough
N
), there then in particular exists an
\mathbf{x}_{\mathbf{v}}\in\mathcal{X}
such that
s_{\mathbf{v},\mathbf{x}_{\mathbf{v}}}
is not constant.
Thus, there exists an
y_{\mathbf{v}}\in\mathcal{Y}
with
\rho(\mathbf{v},\mathbf{x}_{\mathbf{v}},y_{\mathbf{v}})>0
.
In the following, we consider
\mathbf{x}_{\mathbf{v}}
and
y_{\mathbf{v}}
given
\mathbf{v}
to be fixed (i.e., for each
\mathbf{v}
, we choose one particular pair
(\mathbf{x}_{\mathbf{v}},y_{\mathbf{v}})
from the set
\{(\mathbf{x}_{\mathbf{v}},y_{\mathbf{v}})\in\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{Y}\penalty\ |\penalty\ \rho(\mathbf{v},\mathbf{x}_{\mathbf{v}},y_{\mathbf{v}})>0\}
).
To define the desired set
\tilde{A}_{N}\subset A_{N}
, we first construct
a subset of
\mathbb{S}^{K-1}\times\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{Y}
containing
\{(\mathbf{v},\mathbf{x}_{\mathbf{v}},y_{\mathbf{v}})\}
and fulfilling the properties mentioned above.
For this purpose, we distinguish the two cases
y_{\mathbf{v}}\in\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c}}\setminus\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{d}}
and
