.
In the first case, we have
\mu(\{y_{\mathbf{v}}\})=\lambda(\{y_{\mathbf{v}}\})=0
and use continuity of the function
s_{\mathbf{v},\mathbf{x}_{\mathbf{v}}}
on
\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c}}\setminus\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{d}}
to construct a neighborhood of
y_{\mathbf{v}}
with positive measure.
In the second case, we have
\mu(\{y_{\mathbf{v}}\})=\delta(\{y_{\mathbf{v}}\})>0
and thus can use
\{y_{\mathbf{v}}\}
directly.
Note that in this case,
s_{\mathbf{v},\mathbf{x}_{\mathbf{v}}}
is not continuous in
y_{\mathbf{v}}
and thus the construction from the first case is not applicable, making separate treatment of the cases necessary.
Nevertheless, in both cases, we proceed similarly:

(a)

If
y_{\mathbf{v}}\in\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c}}\setminus\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{d}}
,
as
(\mathbf{v},\mathbf{x},y)\mapsto s_{\mathbf{v},\mathbf{x}}(y)
is continuous on

\mathbb{S}^{K-1}\times\mathcal{X}\times(\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c}}\setminus\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{d}})
,
5
5

5

Note that for categorical covariates this is trivially fulfilled as any function defined on a finite set is continuous.

we obtain that
\rho
is continuous in
(\mathbf{v},\mathbf{x}_{\mathbf{v}},y_{\mathbf{v}})
using Lemma
6.4
.
Thus, there exists an
\varepsilon_{\mathbf{v}}>0
, such that for all
(\tilde{\mathbf{v}},\tilde{\mathbf{x}},\tilde{y})\in\upsilon_{\varepsilon_{\mathbf{v}}}(\mathbf{v},\mathbf{x}_{\mathbf{v}},y_{\mathbf{v}})
, we have

\rho(\tilde{\mathbf{v}},\tilde{\mathbf{x}},\tilde{y})>0
.
Set

\Upsilon_{\mathbf{v}}:=\upsilon_{\varepsilon_{\mathbf{v}}}(\mathbf{x}_{\mathbf{v}},y_{\mathbf{v}})
for
y_{\mathbf{v}}\in\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c}}\setminus\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{d}}
.

(b)

If
y_{\mathbf{v}}\in\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{d}}
,
as
(\mathbf{v},\mathbf{x})\mapsto s_{\mathbf{v},\mathbf{x}}(y_{\mathbf{v}})
is continuous on

\mathbb{S}^{K-1}\times\mathcal{X}
,
5

we obtain that

(\mathbf{v},\mathbf{x})\mapsto\rho(\mathbf{v},\mathbf{x},y_{\mathbf{v}})

is continuous in
(\mathbf{v},\mathbf{x}_{\mathbf{v}})
using Lemma
6.4
.
Thus, there exists an
\varepsilon_{\mathbf{v}}>0
, such that for all
(\tilde{\mathbf{v}},\tilde{\mathbf{x}})\in\upsilon_{\varepsilon_{\mathbf{v}}}(\mathbf{v},\mathbf{x}_{\mathbf{v}})
,
we have

\rho(\tilde{\mathbf{v}},\tilde{\mathbf{x}},y_{\mathbf{v}})>0
.
Set

\Upsilon_{\mathbf{v}}:=\upsilon_{\varepsilon_{\mathbf{v}}}(\mathbf{x}_{\mathbf{v}})\times\{y_{\mathbf{v}}\}
for
y_{\mathbf{v}}\in\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{d}}
.

Since
\mathbb{S}^{K-1}
is compact, there exist
Z\in\mathbb{N}
and
\mathbf{v}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{v}_{Z}\in\mathbb{S}^{K-1}
such that

\mathbb{S}^{K-1}=\bigcup_{z=1}^{Z}\upsilon_{\varepsilon_{\mathbf{v}_{z}}}(\mathbf{v}_{z})
.
Note that the construction of this covering does not depend on
N
and is considered to be fix in the remaining proof.
Consider the set

\displaystyle\tilde{A}_{N}

\displaystyle:=\bigl\{(y_{1},\ldots,y_{N})\in\mathcal{Y}^{N}\penalty\ |\penalty\ \forall z\in\{1,\ldots,Z\}\exists i\in\{1,\ldots,N\}:(\mathbf{x}_{i},y_{i})\in\Upsilon_{\mathbf{v}_{z}}\bigr\}.

We show that

\tilde{A}_{N}\subseteq A_{N}
.
For this purpose, let

(y_{1},\ldots,y_{N})\in\tilde{A}_{N}
and let
\mathbf{v}\in\mathbb{S}^{K-1}
.
We need to show that there exists an
i\in\{1,\ldots,N\}
with
s_{\mathbf{v},\mathbf{x}_{i}}(y_{i})<s_{\mathbf{v},\mathbf{x}_{i}}^{\max}
.
As
\mathbf{v}\in\mathbb{S}^{K-1}=\bigcup_{z=1}^{Z}\upsilon_{\varepsilon_{\mathbf{v}_{z}}}(\mathbf{v}_{z})
,
there exists a
z\in\{1,\ldots,Z\}
with

\mathbf{v}\in\upsilon_{\varepsilon_{\mathbf{v}_{z}}}(\mathbf{v}_{z})
.
Furthermore, as
(y_{1},\ldots,y_{N})\in\tilde{A}_{N}
, there exists

i\in\{1,\ldots,N\}
for this
z
with

(\mathbf{x}_{i},y_{i})\in\Upsilon_{\mathbf{v}_{z}}
.
Let
\mathbf{x}_{\mathbf{v}_{z}}\in\mathcal{X}
and
y_{\mathbf{v}_{z}}\in\mathcal{Y}
be as above, i.e., such that
\rho(\mathbf{v}_{z},\mathbf{x}_{\mathbf{v}_{z}},y_{\mathbf{v}_{z}})>0
.
We again distinguish based on
y_{\mathbf{v}_{z}}
:

(a)

If
y_{\mathbf{v}_{z}}\in\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c}}\setminus\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{d}}
,
we have

(\mathbf{x}_{i},y_{i})\in\Upsilon_{\mathbf{v}_{z}}=\upsilon_{\varepsilon_{\mathbf{v}_{z}}}(\mathbf{x}_{\mathbf{v}_{z}},y_{\mathbf{v}_{z}})
,
which together with

\mathbf{v}\in\upsilon_{\varepsilon_{\mathbf{v}_{z}}}(\mathbf{v}_{z})

yields

(\mathbf{v},\mathbf{x}_{i},y_{i})\in\upsilon_{\varepsilon_{\mathbf{v}_{z}}}(\mathbf{v}_{z},\mathbf{x}_{\mathbf{v}_{z}},y_{\mathbf{v}_{z}})
.
By (a) above, we obtain

\rho(\mathbf{v},\mathbf{x}_{i},y_{i})>0
and thus

s_{\mathbf{v},\mathbf{x}_{i}}(y_{i})<s_{\mathbf{v},\mathbf{x}_{i}}^{\max}
.

(b)

If
y_{\mathbf{v}_{z}}\in\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{d}}
,
we have

(\mathbf{x}_{i},y_{i})\in\Upsilon_{\mathbf{v}_{z}}=\upsilon_{\varepsilon_{\mathbf{v}_{z}}}(\mathbf{x}_{\mathbf{v}_{z}})\times\{y_{\mathbf{v}_{z}}\}
,
which together with

\mathbf{v}\in\upsilon_{\varepsilon_{\mathbf{v}_{z}}}(\mathbf{v}_{z})

yields

(\mathbf{v},\mathbf{x}_{i})\in\upsilon_{\varepsilon_{\mathbf{v}_{z}}}(\mathbf{v}_{z},\mathbf{x}_{\mathbf{v}_{z}})
.
By (b) above, we obtain

\rho(\mathbf{v},\mathbf{x}_{i},y_{i})=(\mathbf{v},\mathbf{x}_{i},y_{\mathbf{v}_{z}})>0
and thus

s_{\mathbf{v},\mathbf{x}_{i}}(y_{i})<s_{\mathbf{v},\mathbf{x}_{i}}^{\max}
.

This shows
(y_{1},\ldots,y_{N})\in A_{N}
and thus
\tilde{A}_{N}\subseteq A_{N}
, which yields

\displaystyle\penalty\ \mathbb{P}(A_{N})\geq\mathbb{P}(\tilde{A}_{N})

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\penalty\ 1-\sum_{z=1}^{Z}\mathbb{P}\bigl(\left\{(y_{1},\ldots,y_{N})\in\mathcal{Y}^{N}\penalty\ |\penalty\ \forall i\in\{1,\ldots,N\}:(\mathbf{x}_{i},y_{i})\notin\Upsilon_{\mathbf{v}_{z}}\right\}\bigr).

As the defintion of
\Upsilon_{\mathbf{v}_{z}}
depends on whether
y_{\mathbf{v}_{z}}\in\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c}}\setminus\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{d}}
or
y_{\mathbf{v}_{z}}\in\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{d}}
, we split the sum over
\{1,\ldots,Z\}
into two sums:
One over

\mathcal{Z}_{\mathrm{d}}:=\{z\in\{1,\ldots,Z\}\penalty\ |\penalty\ y_{\mathbf{v}_{z}}\in\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{d}}\}

and one over

\mathcal{Z}_{\mathrm{c}}:=\{z\in\{1,\ldots,Z\}\penalty\ |\penalty\ y_{\mathbf{v}_{z}}\in\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c}}\setminus\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{d}}\}
.
Then, the last line equals

\displaystyle\penalty\ 1-\sum_{z\in\mathcal{Z}_{\mathrm{d}}}\mathbb{P}\bigl(\bigl\{(y_{1},\ldots,y_{N})\in\mathcal{Y}^{N}\penalty\ |\penalty\ \forall i\in\{1,\ldots,N\}:

\displaystyle\hskip 173.56198pt\mathbf{x}_{i}\notin\upsilon_{\varepsilon_{\mathbf{v}_{z}}}(\mathbf{x}_{\mathbf{v}_{z}})\vee y_{i}\neq y_{\mathbf{v}_{z}}\bigr\}\bigr)

\displaystyle\hskip 9.95863pt-\sum_{z\in\mathcal{Z}_{\mathrm{c}}}\mathbb{P}\bigl(\bigl\{(y_{1},\ldots,y_{N})\in\mathcal{Y}^{N}\penalty\ |\penalty\ \forall i\in\{1,\ldots,N\}:

\displaystyle\hskip 173.56198pt\mathbf{x}_{i}\notin\upsilon_{\varepsilon_{\mathbf{v}_{z}}}(\mathbf{x}_{\mathbf{v}_{z}})\vee y_{i}\notin\upsilon_{\varepsilon_{\mathbf{v}_{z}}}(y_{\mathbf{v}_{z}})\bigr\}\bigr)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\penalty\ 1-\sum_{z\in\mathcal{Z}_{\mathrm{d}}}\prod_{i=1}^{N}\Bigl(1-\mathbb{P}\bigl(\bigl\{y_{i}\in\mathcal{Y}\penalty\ |\penalty\ \mathbf{x}_{i}\in\upsilon_{\varepsilon_{\mathbf{v}_{z}}}(\mathbf{x}_{\mathbf{v}_{z}})\wedge y_{i}=y_{\mathbf{v}_{z}}\bigr\}\bigr)\Bigr)

\displaystyle\hskip 9.95863pt-\sum_{z\in\mathcal{Z}_{\mathrm{c}}}\prod_{i=1}^{N}\Bigl(1-\mathbb{P}\bigl(\bigl\{y_{i}\in\mathcal{Y}\penalty\ |\penalty\ \mathbf{x}_{i}\in\upsilon_{\varepsilon_{\mathbf{v}_{z}}}(\mathbf{x}_{\mathbf{v}_{z}})\wedge y_{i}\in\upsilon_{\varepsilon_{\mathbf{v}_{z}}}(y_{\mathbf{v}_{z}})\bigr\}\bigr)\Bigr),

where we used independence of
y_{1},\ldots,y_{N}
in the last equality.
For
z\in\{1,\ldots,Z\}
, set
\mathcal{I}_{z}:=\{i\in\{1,\ldots,N\}\penalty\ |\penalty\ \mathbf{x}_{i}\in\upsilon_{\varepsilon_{\mathbf{v}_{z}}}(\mathbf{x}_{\mathbf{v}_{z}})\}
.
Then,
\mathbf{x}_{i}\notin\upsilon_{\varepsilon_{\mathbf{v}_{z}}}(\mathbf{x}_{\mathbf{v}_{z}})
for any
i\in\mathcal{I}_{z}^{\complement}
and thus, the corresponding factors in each of the products are
1
.
Thus, the last line equals

\displaystyle\penalty\ 1-\sum_{z\in\mathcal{Z}_{\mathrm{d}}}\prod_{i\in\mathcal{I}_{z}}\Bigl(1-\mathbb{P}\bigl(\bigl\{y_{i}\in\mathcal{Y}\penalty\ |\penalty\ y_{i}=y_{\mathbf{v}_{z}}\bigr\}\bigr)\Bigr)

\displaystyle-\sum_{z\in\mathcal{Z}_{\mathrm{c}}}\prod_{i\in\mathcal{I}_{z}}\Bigl(1-\mathbb{P}\bigl(\bigl\{y_{i}\in\mathcal{Y}\penalty\ |\penalty\ y_{i}\in\upsilon_{\varepsilon_{\mathbf{v}_{z}}}(y_{\mathbf{v}_{z}})\bigr\}\bigr)\Bigr)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\penalty\ 1-\sum_{z\in\mathcal{Z}_{\mathrm{d}}}\prod_{i\in\mathcal{I}_{z}}\Bigl(1-\int_{\{y_{\mathbf{v}_{z}}\}}f_{\mathbf{x}_{i}}\,\mathrm{d}\mu\Bigr)-\sum_{z\in\mathcal{Z}_{\mathrm{c}}}\prod_{i\in\mathcal{I}_{z}}\Bigl(1-\int_{\upsilon_{\varepsilon_{\mathbf{v}_{z}}}(y_{\mathbf{v}_{z}})}f_{\mathbf{x}_{i}}\,\mathrm{d}\mu\Bigr),

Since
f_{\mathbf{x}}
is (
\mu
-a.e.) positive for all
\mathbf{x}\in\mathcal{X}
,

\mathcal{X}
and
\mathcal{Y}
are compact,
and
f_{\mathbf{x}}(y)
is continuous on
\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{Y}
,

\min\{f_{\mathbf{x}}(y)\penalty\ |\penalty\ \mathbf{x}\in\mathcal{X},y\in\mathcal{Y}\}>0
exists.
Furthermore,
we have

\mu(\{y_{\mathbf{v}_{z}}\})=\delta(\{y_{\mathbf{v}_{z}}\})>0
for all
z\in\mathcal{Z}_{\mathrm{d}}

and

\mu(\upsilon_{\varepsilon_{\mathbf{v}_{z}}}(y_{\mathbf{v}_{z}}))\geq\lambda(\upsilon_{\varepsilon_{\mathbf{v}_{z}}}(y_{\mathbf{v}_{z}}))=2\,\varepsilon_{\mathbf{v}_{z}}>0
for all
z\in\mathcal{Z}_{\mathrm{c}}
.
Thus,

\displaystyle\delta

\displaystyle:=\min\Bigl(\Bigl\{\int_{\{y_{\mathbf{v}_{z}}\}}f_{\mathbf{x}}\,\mathrm{d}\mu\penalty\ \big|\penalty\ z\in\mathcal{Z}_{\mathrm{d}},x\in\mathcal{X}\Bigr\}\cup\Bigl\{\int_{\upsilon_{\varepsilon_{\mathbf{v}_{z}}}(y_{\mathbf{v}_{z}})}f_{\mathbf{x}}\,\mathrm{d}\mu\penalty\ \big|\penalty\ z\in\mathcal{Z}_{\mathrm{c}},x\in\mathcal{X}\Bigr\}\Bigr)

exists and is in
(0,1)
.
Hence,

\mathbb{P}(A_{N})\geq 1-\sum_{z=1}^{Z}(1-\delta)^{|\mathcal{I}_{z}|}
.
Finally, we show
\lim_{N\rightarrow\infty}|\mathcal{I}^{(z)}|=\infty
for all
z=1,\ldots,Z
, which immediately implies the desired result
\lim_{N\rightarrow\infty}\mathbb{P}(A_{N})=1
.
For this purpose, let
\tilde{M}\in\mathbb{R}
be an arbitrary constant.
We show that there exists an
\tilde{N}\in\mathbb{N}
such that
\tilde{M}\leq|\mathcal{I}^{(z)}|
for all
N\geq\tilde{N}
.
Let
\alpha\in(0,1),M^{\prime\prime}>0
, and
N^{\prime\prime}\in\mathbb{N}
be the constants from
(BX”)
.
Since
\varepsilon_{\mathbf{v}_{z}}
is independent of
N
and
\lim_{N\rightarrow\infty}N^{\alpha-1}=0
, there exists an
N_{\alpha}\in\mathbb{N}
such that
N^{\alpha-1}<\diam\left(\upsilon_{\varepsilon_{\mathbf{v}_{z}}}(\mathbf{x}_{\mathbf{v}_{z}}^{\mathfrak{C}})\right)
for all
N\geq N_{\alpha}
.
Setting

\tilde{N}:=\max\Bigl\{N_{\alpha},N^{\prime\prime},\frac{\tilde{M}}{M^{\prime\prime}\,\diam\left(\upsilon_{\varepsilon_{\mathbf{v}_{z}}}(\mathbf{x}_{\mathbf{v}_{z}}^{\mathfrak{C}})\right)^{|\mathfrak{C}|}}\Bigr\}
,
we obtain for any

N\geq\tilde{N},
by assumption
(BX”)
,

\displaystyle\tilde{M}\leq M^{\prime\prime}\,N\,\diam\left(\upsilon_{\varepsilon_{\mathbf{v}_{z}}}(\mathbf{x}_{\mathbf{v}_{z}}^{\mathfrak{C}})\right)^{|\mathfrak{C}|}\leq\sum_{i=1}^{N}\mathbbm{1}_{\{\mathbf{x}_{\mathbf{v}_{z}}^{\mathfrak{D}}\}\times[\upsilon_{\varepsilon_{\mathbf{v}_{z}}}(\mathbf{x}_{\mathbf{v}_{z}}^{\mathfrak{C}})]}(\mathbf{x}^{\mathfrak{D}}_{i},\mathbf{x}^{\mathfrak{C}}_{i})\leq|\mathcal{I}^{(z)}|.

This shows

\lim_{N\rightarrow\infty}|\mathcal{I}^{(z)}|=\infty
and thus
\lim_{N\rightarrow\infty}\mathbb{P}(A_{N})=1
.

4)

We first show that
(BU)
holds, if

(\tilde{\mathbf{B}}_{\mathcal{Y}}^{(l)}:\mathbf{1}_{\Gamma^{(l)}})
has rank
K_{\mathcal{Y}}+1

for all
l\in\{1,\ldots,L\}
.
Thus, let
l\in\{1,\ldots,L\}
and assume that the matrix

(\tilde{\mathbf{B}}_{\mathcal{Y}}^{(l)}:\mathbf{1}_{\Gamma^{(l)}})
has rank
K_{\mathcal{Y}}+1
.
Then,
\tilde{\mathbf{B}}_{\mathcal{Y}}^{(l)}
has rank
K_{\mathcal{Y}}<K_{\mathcal{Y}}+1
and there thus is no
\mathbf{v}_{\mathcal{Y}}\in\mathbb{R}^{K_{\mathcal{Y}}}
such that
\tilde{\mathbf{B}}_{\mathcal{Y}}^{(l)}\mathbf{v}_{\mathcal{Y}}=\mathbf{1}_{\Gamma^{(l)}}

(
Liesen & Mehrmann 2015a
, Corollary 6.6 (1))
.
Equivalently, for
c\in\mathbb{R}\setminus\{0\}
, there is no
\mathbf{v}_{\mathcal{Y}}\in\mathbb{R}^{K_{\mathcal{Y}}}
such that
\tilde{\mathbf{B}}_{\mathcal{Y}}^{(l)}\mathbf{v}_{\mathcal{Y}}=c\,\mathbf{1}_{\Gamma^{(l)}}
. However, for
c=0
, we obtain
\mathbf{v}_{\mathcal{Y}}=\mathbf{0}
as the unique solution by
Liesen & Mehrmann 2015a
.
This shows assumption
(BU)
.

Now assume that
\tilde{\mathbf{b}}_{\mathcal{Y}}
is chosen as proposed in Section
2.2
.
If
\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c}}\neq\emptyset
, furthermore assume that
K_{\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c}}}+1\leq G^{(l)}
and that for each unconstrained B-spline basis
\bar{b}_{\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c}},\,m}
, there exists a
u_{g}^{(l)}
in its support.
We show that then, the matrix

(\tilde{\mathbf{B}}_{\mathcal{Y}}^{(l)}:\mathbf{1}_{\Gamma^{(l)}})\in\mathbb{R}^{\Gamma^{(l)}\times(K_{\mathcal{Y}}+1)}
has rank
K_{\mathcal{Y}}+1
.
We first consider the continuous and discrete special cases (where
\Gamma^{(l)}=G^{(l)}
and
\Gamma^{(l)}=D
, respectively).
By Lemma
6.1
and
Liesen & Mehrmann 2015a
,
there exist transformation matrices

\mathbf{A}_{\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c}}}\in\mathbb{R}^{(K_{\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c}}}+1)\times(K_{\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c}}}+1)}
with rank
K_{\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c}}}+1

and

\mathbf{A}_{\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{d}}}\in\mathbb{R}^{(K_{\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{d}}}+1)\times(K_{\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{d}}}+1)}
with rank
K_{\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{d}}}+1

such that

(\tilde{b}_{\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c}},\,1},\ldots,\tilde{b}_{\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c}},\,K_{\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c}}}},1_{\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c}}})=\bar{\mathbf{b}}_{\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c}}}\mathbf{A}_{\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c}}}

and

(\tilde{b}_{\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{d}},\,1},\ldots,\tilde{b}_{\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{d}},\,K_{\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{d}}}},1_{\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{d}}})=\bar{\mathbf{b}}_{\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{d}}}\mathbf{A}_{\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{d}}}
.
6
6

6

Actually, the first
K_{\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c}}}
(
K_{\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{d}}}
) columns of
\mathbf{A}_{\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c}}}
(
\mathbf{A}_{\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{d}}}
) equal the transformation matrix
\mathbf{Z}_{\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c}}}
(
\mathbf{Z}_{\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{d}}}
), while the last column is
\mathbf{1}_{K_{\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c}}}+1}
(
\mathbf{1}_{K_{\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{d}}}+1}
).
Furthermore, the matrix
\bar{\mathbf{B}}_{\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c}}}^{(l)}\in\mathbb{R}^{G^{(l)}\times(K_{\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c}}}+1)}
containing
\bar{\mathbf{b}}_{\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c}}}(u_{g}^{(l)})
in the
g
-th row,
g=1,\ldots,G^{(l)}
, has rank
K_{\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c}}}+1
, since for every unconstrained B-spline basis function
\bar{b}_{\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c}},\,1},\ldots,\bar{b}_{\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c}},\,K_{\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c}}}+1}
there is at least one
g\in\{1,\ldots,G^{(l)}\}
such that
u_{g}^{(l)}
is in its support.
This follows from
De 2001a
, compare also footnote
3
in Remark
6.2

1)
.
Similarly, the matrix
\bar{\mathbf{B}}_{\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{d}}}\in\mathbb{R}^{D\times(K_{\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{d}}}+1)}
containing
\bar{\mathbf{b}}_{\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{d}}}(t_{d})
in the
d
-th row,
d=1,\ldots,D
, is simply the identity matrix of dimension
K_{\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{d}}}+1=D
and thus has rank
K_{\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{d}}}+1
.
Thus,

(\tilde{\mathbf{B}}_{\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c}}}^{(l)}:\mathbf{1}_{G^{(l)}})=\bar{\mathbf{B}}_{\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c}}}^{(l)}\mathbf{A}_{\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c}}}

has rank
K_{\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c}}}+1

and

(\tilde{\mathbf{B}}_{\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{d}}}:\mathbf{1}_{D})=\bar{\mathbf{B}}_{\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{d}}}\mathbf{A}_{\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{d}}}

has rank
K_{\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{d}}}+1

(
Liesen & Mehrmann 2015a
, Lemma 9.23)
.

In the mixed case, using the embeddings
\tilde{\iota}_{\mathrm{c}}
and
\tilde{\iota}_{\mathrm{d}}
as given in (
6.1
) and (
6.2
)
and recalling
u_{g}^{(l)}=t_{g-G^{(l)}}
for
g=G^{(l)}+1,\ldots,\Gamma^{(l)}
, we obtain

\displaystyle\bigl(\tilde{\mathbf{B}}_{\mathcal{Y}}^{(l)}:\mathbf{1}_{\Gamma^{(l)}}\bigr)=\begin{pmatrix}\tilde{\mathbf{B}}_{\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c}}}^{(l)}&\tilde{\mathbf{b}}_{\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{d}}^{\bullet}}(t_{D+1})^{\top}\otimes\mathbf{1}_{G^{(l)}}&\mathbf{1}_{G^{(l)}}\\
\mathbf{0}_{D\times K_{\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c}}}}&\tilde{\mathbf{B}}_{\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{d}}^{\bullet}}(t_{1},\ldots,t_{D})&\mathbf{1}_{D}\end{pmatrix},

where
\mathbf{0}_{K_{\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{d}}^{\bullet}}\times K_{\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c}}}}
is the
(K_{\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{d}}^{\bullet}}\times K_{\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c}}})
-dimensional matrix containing zero in every entry and
\tilde{\mathbf{B}}_{\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{d}}^{\bullet}}(t_{1},\ldots,t_{D})\in\mathbb{R}^{D\times K_{\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{d}}^{\bullet}}}
is the matrix containing
\tilde{\mathbf{b}}_{\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{d}}^{\bullet}}(t_{d})
in the
d
-th row,
d=1,\ldots,D
, i.e., the matrix
\tilde{\mathbf{B}}_{\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{d}}^{\bullet}}
without the last row
\tilde{\mathbf{b}}_{\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{d}}^{\bullet}}(t_{D+1})
.
Note that this last row is, however, contained
G^{(l)}
times in the block above.
Since we already showed the statement for the continuous case, we in particular have that
\tilde{\mathbf{B}}_{\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c}}}^{(l)}
has rank
K_{\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c}}}
.
Thus, using elementary row operations, the upper left matrix
\tilde{\mathbf{B}}_{\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c}}}^{(l)}
can be transformed to
a
(G^{(l)}\times K_{\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c}}})
-dimensional matrix containing the
K_{\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c}}}
-dimensional identity matrix
\mathbf{I}_{K_{\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c}}}}
in the upper
K_{\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c}}}
rows and
G^{(l)}-K_{\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c}}}\geq 1
rows containing zeros below
(
Liesen & Mehrmann 2015a
, Section 5)
. Applying these row operations to the whole matrix, the lower blocks are unaffected, while the remaining matrix in the upper block sharing the same rows as the upper left block
\tilde{\mathbf{B}}_{\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c}}}^{(l)}
contains the same vector
(\tilde{b}_{\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{d}}^{\bullet},\,1}(t_{D+1}),\ldots,\tilde{b}_{\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{d}}^{\bullet},\,K_{\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{d}}^{\bullet}}}(t_{D+1}),1)
in each row.
Thus, performing the row operations on this block simply results in each row containing a multiple of the original vector (with the multiplicity depending on the row).
The first
K_{\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c}}}
rows in this upper middle-right block can now be eliminated via elementary column operations using the identity matrix in the left block.
Since the blocks below the identity matrix contains only zeros, the remaining rows stay unchanged, when applying these column operations to the whole matrix.
After these transformations, the matrix has the form

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}\mathbf{I}_{K_{\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c}}}}&\mathbf{0}_{K_{\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c}}}\times K_{\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{d}}^{\bullet}}}&\mathbf{0}_{K_{\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c}}}\times 1}\\
\mathbf{0}_{(G^{(l)}-K_{\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c}}})\times K_{\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c}}}}&\tilde{\mathbf{b}}_{\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{d}}^{\bullet}}(t_{D+1})^{\top}\otimes\mathbf{v}_{G^{(l)}-K_{\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c}}}}&\mathbf{v}_{G^{(l)}-K_{\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c}}}}\\
\mathbf{0}_{D\times K_{\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c}}}}&\tilde{\mathbf{B}}_{\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{d}}^{\bullet}}(t_{1},\ldots,t_{D})&\mathbf{1}_{D}\end{pmatrix},

where
\mathbf{v}_{G^{(l)}-K_{\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c}}}}\in\mathbb{R}^{G^{(l)}-K_{\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c}}}}
is the vector containing the multiplicity of the original row
(\tilde{b}_{\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{d}}^{\bullet},\,1}(t_{D+1}),\ldots,\tilde{b}_{\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{d}}^{\bullet},\,K_{\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{d}}^{\bullet}}}(t_{D+1}),1)
for each row.
Thus, the last of these rows can be used to eliminate all of the rows above in the middle-row-blocks.
Now, the left block stays unchanged as it contains only zero.
Finally, we divide the last row by its multiplicity and obtain

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}\mathbf{I}_{K_{\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c}}}}&\mathbf{0}_{K_{\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c}}}\times K_{\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{d}}^{\bullet}}}&\mathbf{0}_{K_{\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c}}}\times 1}\\
\mathbf{0}_{(G^{(l)}-K_{\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c}}}-1)\times K_{\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c}}}}&\mathbf{0}_{(G^{(l)}-K_{\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c}}}-1)\times K_{\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{d}}^{\bullet}}}&\mathbf{0}_{(G^{(l)}-K_{\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c}}}-1)\times 1}\\
\mathbf{0}_{(D+1)\times K_{\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c}}}}&\tilde{\mathbf{B}}_{\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{d}}^{\bullet}}&\mathbf{1}_{D+1}\end{pmatrix}.

Using the results above for the discrete case, we have that

(\tilde{\mathbf{B}}_{\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{d}}^{\bullet}}:\mathbf{1}_{D+1})
has rank
K_{\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{d}}^{\bullet}}+1

and thus

(\tilde{\mathbf{B}}_{\mathcal{Y}}^{(l)}:\mathbf{1}_{\Gamma^{(l)}})
has rank
K_{\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c}}}+K_{\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{d}}^{\bullet}}+1=K_{\mathcal{Y}}+1
.

Finally, the assumptions for the case
\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c}}\neq\emptyset
are fulfilled for small enough bin width:
The first assumption
K_{\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c}}}+1\leq G^{(l)}
means that the number of bins needs to be at least the number of unconstrained B-spline functions is straightforwardly fulfilled for small enough bin widths.
The second assumption that for each unconstrained B-spline basis
\bar{b}_{\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c}},\,m}
, there exists a
u_{g}^{(l)}
in its support, is fulfilled, if the maximal bin width is so small, that each pair of adjacent knots underlying the unconstrained B-spline basis contains at least one bin. Thus, both conditions are fulfilled for small enough bin width.
∎

6.1.4
Auxiliary statements used in multiple proofs in Section
6.2

Before we start formulating and proving the auxiliary statements, we give a brief overview.
In Proposition
6.6
, we show negative (positive) definiteness of the Hessian matrices (penalized Fisher informations) of
\ell_{\pen}
and
\ell_{\mathcal{Z},\pen}^{\mathrm{mn}}
, which also implies invertibility of those matrices.
Lemma
6.7
states the technical result that matrix inversion is a continuous function.
The remaining theorems,
6.8
,
6.9
and
6.10
actually form a cascade of statements:
We derive a generalization of Laplace’s approximation in Theorem
6.8
,
which is used to show
\lim_{\|\boldsymbol{\theta}\|_{2}\rightarrow\infty}\ell_{\pen}(\boldsymbol{\theta})=-\infty
in Theorem
6.9
,
which helps us to prove that
-\ell_{\pen}
is level-bounded in Theorem
6.10
.
Ultimately, of these last three theorems in this section, only Theorem
6.10
is referred to in the proofs in Section
6.2
.

Denote the (multivariate) expectation with respect to the conditional distributions
\mathbf{Y}_{i}\penalty\ |\penalty\ \mathbf{x}_{i},\penalty\ i=1,\ldots,N
, with
\mathbb{E}
and the (multivariate) expectation with respect to the corresponding multinomial distributions with respect to partitions
\mathcal{Z}
with
\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{Z}}^{\mathrm{mn}}
.

Proposition 6.6
.

Assume that
(BX)
holds. Then, for any
\boldsymbol{\theta}\in\mathbb{R}^{K}
,

1)

the Hessian matrix
\mathbf{H}_{\mathrm{pen}}(\boldsymbol{\theta})
of
\ell_{\mathrm{pen}}(\boldsymbol{\theta})
is negative definite and thus invertible, and
\mathbf{H}_{\mathrm{pen}}(\boldsymbol{\theta})=\mathbb{E}(\mathbf{H}_{\mathrm{pen}}(\boldsymbol{\theta}))
,

2)

under assumption
(BU)
,
the Hessian matrix
\mathbf{H}_{\mathcal{Z},\mathrm{pen}}^{\mathrm{mn}}(\boldsymbol{\theta})
of
\ell_{\mathcal{Z},\mathrm{pen}}^{\mathrm{mn}}(\boldsymbol{\theta})
is negative definite and thus invertible, and
\mathbf{H}_{\mathcal{Z},\mathrm{pen}}^{\mathrm{mn}}(\boldsymbol{\theta})=\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{Z}}^{\mathrm{mn}}(\mathbf{H}_{\mathcal{Z},\mathrm{pen}}^{\mathrm{mn}}(\boldsymbol{\theta}))
.

The corresponding penalized Fisher informations are positive definite, invertible, and equal their expected versions.

Proof.

We have
\mathbf{H}_{\mathrm{pen}}(\boldsymbol{\theta})=\mathbf{H}(\boldsymbol{\theta})+D^{2}\pen(\boldsymbol{\theta})
and
\mathbf{H}_{\mathcal{Z},\mathrm{pen}}^{\mathrm{mn}}(\boldsymbol{\theta})=\mathbf{H}_{\mathcal{Z}}^{\mathrm{mn}}(\boldsymbol{\theta})+D^{2}\pen(\boldsymbol{\theta})
.
Since the matrices
\mathbf{P}_{j}
are positive semi-definite for all
j\in\{1,\ldots,J\}
, the matrix
D^{2}\pen(\boldsymbol{\theta})=-2\mathbf{P}
is negative semi-definite.
Thus, it suffices to show that the Hessian matrices
\mathbf{H}(\boldsymbol{\theta})
and
\mathbf{H}_{\mathcal{Z}}^{\mathrm{mn}}(\boldsymbol{\theta})
are negative definite, since a sum of a negative definite and a negative semi-definite matrix is negative definite and in particular invertible.
The statements for the penalized Fisher informations follows immediately.

In the following, we use the notation
\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{[k]}
to denote the
k
-th entry of a vector
\boldsymbol{\gamma}
.

1)

Setting
C_{i}(\boldsymbol{\theta}):=-\log\int_{\mathcal{Y}}\exp[\tilde{\mathbf{b}}(\mathbf{x}_{i})^{\top}\boldsymbol{\theta}]\,\mathrm{d}\mu
for
i=1,\ldots,N
, we have

\ell(\boldsymbol{\theta})=\sum_{i=1}^{N}[\tilde{\mathbf{b}}(\mathbf{x}_{i})(y_{i})^{\top}\boldsymbol{\theta}-C_{i}(\boldsymbol{\theta})]

and thus
\mathbf{H}(\boldsymbol{\theta})=\sum_{i=1}^{N}\mathbf{H}_{i}(\boldsymbol{\theta})
,
where
\mathbf{H}_{i}(\boldsymbol{\theta})\in\mathbb{R}^{K\times K}
denotes the Hessian matrix of

C_{i}(\boldsymbol{\theta})
for
i=1,\ldots,N
.
For its computation, we briefly show that
\frac{\partial}{\partial\theta_{k}}\int_{\mathcal{Y}}\exp[\tilde{\mathbf{b}}(\mathbf{x}_{i})^{\top}\boldsymbol{\theta}]\,\mathrm{d}\mu=\int_{\mathcal{Y}}\frac{\partial}{\partial\theta_{k}}\exp[\tilde{\mathbf{b}}(\mathbf{x}_{i})^{\top}\boldsymbol{\theta}]\,\mathrm{d}\mu
by verifying the three properties given in Corollary 16.3 in
Bauer 2001a
for the considered function
\exp[\tilde{\mathbf{b}}(\mathbf{x}_{i})^{\top}\boldsymbol{\theta}]
.
The first two required properties are straightforward:
For all
\boldsymbol{\theta}\in\mathbb{R}^{K}
,
\exp[\tilde{\mathbf{b}}(\mathbf{x}_{i})^{\top}\boldsymbol{\theta}]
is
\mu
-integrable
and for all
y\in\mathcal{Y}
and
k\in\{1,\ldots,K\}
,
the partial derivative

\frac{\partial}{\partial\theta_{k}}\exp[\tilde{\mathbf{b}}(\mathbf{x}_{i})(y)^{\top}\boldsymbol{\theta}]=\exp[\tilde{\mathbf{b}}(\mathbf{x}_{i})(y)^{\top}\boldsymbol{\theta}]\,(\tilde{\mathbf{b}}(\mathbf{x}_{i}))_{[k]}
exists.
To show the third required property, note that since the basis functions contained in
\tilde{\mathbf{b}}_{\mathcal{Y}}
are bounded and
\mu
is finite,
|\frac{\partial}{\partial\theta_{k}}\exp[\tilde{\mathbf{b}}(\mathbf{x}_{i})(y)^{\top}\boldsymbol{\theta}]|
is bounded on an open ball

\upsilon_{\varepsilon}(\boldsymbol{\theta}):=\{\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{K}\penalty\ |\penalty\ \|\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\prime}-\boldsymbol{\theta}\|_{2}<\varepsilon\}
for each

\boldsymbol{\theta}\in\mathbb{R}^{K}
and
\varepsilon>0
by a nonnegative finite constant,
defining a constant and thus
\mu
-integrable function on
\mathcal{Y}
.
Thus,
\frac{\partial}{\partial\theta_{k}}\int_{\mathcal{Y}}\exp[\tilde{\mathbf{b}}(\mathbf{x}_{i})^{\top}\boldsymbol{\theta}]\,\mathrm{d}\mu=\int_{\mathcal{Y}}\frac{\partial}{\partial\theta_{k}}\exp[\tilde{\mathbf{b}}(\mathbf{x}_{i})^{\top}\boldsymbol{\theta}]\,\mathrm{d}\mu
for every

\boldsymbol{\theta}\in\mathbb{R}^{K}

(
Bauer 2001a
, Corollary 16.3)
.
Hence, for each
\boldsymbol{\theta}\in\mathbb{R}^{K}
,
k=1,\ldots,K
and
i=1,\ldots,N
, we have

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial\theta_{k}}C_{i}(\boldsymbol{\theta})

\displaystyle=-\frac{\int_{\mathcal{Y}}\exp[\tilde{\mathbf{b}}(\mathbf{x}_{i})^{\top}\boldsymbol{\theta}]\,(\tilde{\mathbf{b}}(\mathbf{x}_{i}))_{[k]}\,\mathrm{d}\mu}{\int_{\mathcal{Y}}\exp[\tilde{\mathbf{b}}(\mathbf{x}_{i})^{\top}\boldsymbol{\theta}]\,\mathrm{d}\mu},

(6.6)

Using the same arguments as above, we show for
k,k^{\prime}=1,\ldots,K
, we have
\frac{\partial}{\partial\theta_{k^{\prime}}}\int_{\mathcal{Y}}\exp[\tilde{\mathbf{b}}(\mathbf{x}_{i})^{\top}\boldsymbol{\theta}]\,(\tilde{\mathbf{b}}(\mathbf{x}_{i}))_{[k]}\,\mathrm{d}\mu=\int_{\mathcal{Y}}\frac{\partial}{\partial\theta_{k^{\prime}}}\exp[\tilde{\mathbf{b}}(\mathbf{x}_{i})^{\top}\boldsymbol{\theta}]\,(\tilde{\mathbf{b}}(\mathbf{x}_{i}))_{[k]}\,\mathrm{d}\mu
.
Thus, the
(k,k^{\prime})
-th element of
\mathbf{H}_{i}(\boldsymbol{\theta}),\penalty\ i=1,\ldots,N
, is

\displaystyle\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial\theta_{k}\partial\theta_{k^{\prime}}}C_{i}(\boldsymbol{\theta})

\displaystyle=-\frac{\int_{\mathcal{Y}}\exp[\tilde{\mathbf{b}}(\mathbf{x}_{i})^{\top}\boldsymbol{\theta}]\,(\tilde{\mathbf{b}}(\mathbf{x}_{i}))_{[k]}\,(\tilde{\mathbf{b}}(\mathbf{x}_{i}))_{[k^{\prime}]}\,\mathrm{d}\mu}{\int_{\mathcal{Y}}\exp[\tilde{\mathbf{b}}(\mathbf{x}_{i})^{\top}\boldsymbol{\theta}]\,\mathrm{d}\mu}

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt+\frac{\int_{\mathcal{Y}}\exp[\tilde{\mathbf{b}}(\mathbf{x}_{i})^{\top}\boldsymbol{\theta}]\cdot(\tilde{\mathbf{b}}(\mathbf{x}_{i}))_{[k]}\,\mathrm{d}\mu}{\int_{\mathcal{Y}}\exp[\tilde{\mathbf{b}}(\mathbf{x}_{i})^{\top}\boldsymbol{\theta}]\,\mathrm{d}\mu}

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt\cdot\frac{\int_{\mathcal{Y}}\exp[\tilde{\mathbf{b}}(\mathbf{x}_{i})^{\top}\boldsymbol{\theta}]\cdot(\tilde{\mathbf{b}}(\mathbf{x}_{i}))_{[k^{\prime}]}\,\mathrm{d}\mu}{\int_{\mathcal{Y}}\exp[\tilde{\mathbf{b}}(\mathbf{x}_{i})^{\top}\boldsymbol{\theta}]\,\mathrm{d}\mu}\,.

(6.7)

Using
f_{\mathbf{x}_{i},\boldsymbol{\theta}}=\frac{\exp[\tilde{\mathbf{b}}(\mathbf{x}_{i})^{\top}\boldsymbol{\theta}]}{\int_{\mathcal{Y}}\exp[\tilde{\mathbf{b}}(\mathbf{x}_{i})^{\top}\boldsymbol{\theta}]\,\mathrm{d}\mu}
, we obtain

\displaystyle\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial\theta_{k}\partial\theta_{k^{\prime}}}C_{i}(\boldsymbol{\theta})

\displaystyle=-\int_{\mathcal{Y}}f_{\mathbf{x}_{i},\boldsymbol{\theta}}\cdot(\tilde{\mathbf{b}}(\mathbf{x}_{i}))_{[k]}\,(\tilde{\mathbf{b}}(\mathbf{x}_{i}))_{[k^{\prime}]}\,\mathrm{d}\mu

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt+\int_{\mathcal{Y}}f_{\mathbf{x}_{i},\boldsymbol{\theta}}\cdot(\tilde{\mathbf{b}}(\mathbf{x}_{i}))_{[k]}\,\mathrm{d}\mu\,\int_{\mathcal{Y}}f_{\mathbf{x}_{i},\boldsymbol{\theta}}\cdot(\tilde{\mathbf{b}}(\mathbf{x}_{i}))_{[k^{\prime}]}\,\mathrm{d}\mu.

(6.8)

Then,
with
\int_{\mathcal{Y}}f_{\mathbf{x}_{i},\boldsymbol{\theta}}\,\mathrm{d}\mu=1
for all
i=1,\ldots,N
,
we have for
\mathbf{v}\in\mathbb{R}^{K}
,

\displaystyle\mathbf{v}^{\top}\mathbf{H}_{i}(\boldsymbol{\theta})\mathbf{v}

\displaystyle=-\int_{\mathcal{Y}}f_{\mathbf{x}_{i},\boldsymbol{\theta}}\cdot\bigl(\tilde{\mathbf{b}}(\mathbf{x}_{i})^{\top}\mathbf{v}\bigr)^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\mu+\Bigl(\int_{\mathcal{Y}}f_{\mathbf{x}_{i},\boldsymbol{\theta}}\cdot\tilde{\mathbf{b}}(\mathbf{x}_{i})^{\top}\mathbf{v}\,\mathrm{d}\mu\Bigr)^{2}

\displaystyle=-\Bigl[\int_{\mathcal{Y}}f_{\mathbf{x}_{i},\boldsymbol{\theta}}\cdot\bigl(\tilde{\mathbf{b}}(\mathbf{x}_{i})^{\top}\mathbf{v}\bigr)^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\mu-2\Bigl(\int_{\mathcal{Y}}f_{\mathbf{x}_{i},\boldsymbol{\theta}}\cdot\tilde{\mathbf{b}}(\mathbf{x}_{i})^{\top}\mathbf{v}\,\mathrm{d}\mu\Bigr)^{2}\Bigr.

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt\Bigl.+\Bigl(\int_{\mathcal{Y}}f_{\mathbf{x}_{i},\boldsymbol{\theta}}\cdot\tilde{\mathbf{b}}(\mathbf{x}_{i})^{\top}\mathbf{v}\,\mathrm{d}\mu\Bigr)^{2}\cdot\int_{\mathcal{Y}}f_{\mathbf{x}_{i},\boldsymbol{\theta}}\,\mathrm{d}\mu\Bigr]

\displaystyle=-\int_{\mathcal{Y}}f_{\mathbf{x}_{i},\boldsymbol{\theta}}\cdot\Bigl(\tilde{\mathbf{b}}(\mathbf{x}_{i})^{\top}\mathbf{v}-\int f_{\mathbf{x}_{i},\boldsymbol{\theta}}\cdot\tilde{\mathbf{b}}(\mathbf{x}_{i})^{\top}\mathbf{v}\,\mathrm{d}\mu\Bigr)^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\mu,

which gives us

\displaystyle\mathbf{v}^{\top}\mathbf{H}(\boldsymbol{\theta})\mathbf{v}=-\sum_{i=1}^{N}\int_{\mathcal{Y}}f_{\mathbf{x}_{i},\boldsymbol{\theta}}\cdot\Bigl(\tilde{\mathbf{b}}(\mathbf{x}_{i})^{\top}\mathbf{v}-\int f_{\mathbf{x}_{i},\boldsymbol{\theta}}\cdot\tilde{\mathbf{b}}(\mathbf{x}_{i})^{\top}\mathbf{v}\,\mathrm{d}\mu\Bigr)^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\mu.

(6.9)

Since
f_{\mathbf{x}_{i},\boldsymbol{\theta}}>0
on
\mathcal{Y}
for all
i=1,\ldots,N
, all summands are non-negative and thus
\mathbf{v}^{\top}\mathbf{H}(\boldsymbol{\theta})\mathbf{v}\leq 0
.
Now, assume
\mathbf{v}^{\top}\mathbf{H}(\boldsymbol{\theta})\mathbf{v}=0
, which then implies
\tilde{\mathbf{b}}(\mathbf{x}_{i})^{\top}\mathbf{v}=\int f_{\mathbf{x}_{i},\boldsymbol{\theta}}\cdot\tilde{\mathbf{b}}(\mathbf{x}_{i})^{\top}\mathbf{v}\,\mathrm{d}\mu
for
i=1,\ldots,N
.
This means,
\tilde{\mathbf{b}}(\mathbf{x}_{i})^{\top}\mathbf{v}
is constant over
\mathcal{Y}
for every
i=1,\ldots,N
and
by Lemma
6.3
, we obtain
\mathbf{v}=\mathbf{0}
.
Hence, we have
\mathbf{v}^{\top}\mathbf{H}(\boldsymbol{\theta})\mathbf{v}<0
for all
\mathbf{v}\in\mathbb{R}^{K}\setminus\{\mathbf{0}\}
, i.e.,
\mathbf{H}(\boldsymbol{\theta})
is negative definite, which implies it is invertible.
As
\mathbf{H}_{i}(\boldsymbol{\theta})+D^{2}\pen(\boldsymbol{\theta})
is constant regarding
\mathbb{E}
(compare (
6.7
)), we have
\mathbb{E}(\mathbf{H}_{\mathrm{pen}}(\boldsymbol{\theta}))=\mathbf{H}_{\mathrm{pen}}(\boldsymbol{\theta})
.
The results for the penalized Fisher information follow immediately with
\mathbf{F}_{\mathrm{pen}}(\boldsymbol{\theta})=-\mathbf{H}_{\mathrm{pen}}(\boldsymbol{\theta})
.

2)

Setting

C_{\mathcal{Z}}^{\text{mn}\,(s)}(\boldsymbol{\theta}):=-\log\sum_{g^{\prime}=1}^{\Gamma^{(l)}}\Delta_{g^{\prime}}^{(l)}\exp[\tilde{\mathbf{b}}(\mathbf{x}^{(l)})(u_{g^{\prime}}^{(l)})^{\top}\boldsymbol{\theta}]

for
l=1,\ldots,L
, we have

\ell^{\text{mn}}_{\mathcal{Z}}(\boldsymbol{\theta})=\sum_{l=1}^{L}\sum_{g=1}^{\Gamma^{(l)}}n_{g}^{(l)}[\tilde{\mathbf{b}}(\mathbf{x}^{(l)})(u_{g}^{(l)})^{\top}\boldsymbol{\theta}-C_{\mathcal{Z}}^{\text{mn}\,(s)}(\boldsymbol{\theta})]
.
Then,
we obtain

\mathbf{H}^{\text{mn}}_{\mathcal{Z}}(\boldsymbol{\theta})=\sum_{l=1}^{L}\sum_{g=1}^{\Gamma^{(l)}}n_{g}^{(l)}\mathbf{H}_{\mathcal{Z}}^{\text{mn}\,(s)}(\boldsymbol{\theta})
, where
\mathbf{H}_{\mathcal{Z}}^{\text{mn}\,(s)}(\boldsymbol{\theta})
denotes the Hessian matrix of
C_{\mathcal{Z}}^{\text{mn}\,(s)}(\boldsymbol{\theta})
.
The
(k,k^{\prime})
-th element of
\mathbf{H}_{\mathcal{Z}}^{\text{mn}\,(s)}(\boldsymbol{\theta})
is

\displaystyle\penalty\ \frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial\theta_{k}\partial\theta_{k^{\prime}}}C_{\mathcal{Z}}^{\text{mn}\,(s)}(\boldsymbol{\theta})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\penalty\ -\frac{\sum_{g^{\prime}=1}^{\Gamma^{(l)}}\bigl[\Delta_{g^{\prime}}^{(l)}\exp[\tilde{\mathbf{b}}(\mathbf{x}^{(l)})(u_{g^{\prime}}^{(l)})^{\top}\boldsymbol{\theta}]\,(\tilde{\mathbf{b}}(\mathbf{x}^{(l)})(u_{g^{\prime}}^{(l)}))_{[k]}\,(\tilde{\mathbf{b}}(\mathbf{x}^{(l)})(u_{g^{\prime}}^{(l)}))_{[k^{\prime}]}\bigr]}{\sum_{g^{\prime}=1}^{\Gamma^{(l)}}\Delta_{g^{\prime}}^{(l)}\exp[\tilde{\mathbf{b}}(\mathbf{x}^{(l)})(u_{g^{\prime}}^{(l)})^{\top}\boldsymbol{\theta}]}

\displaystyle\penalty\ +\frac{\sum_{g^{\prime}=1}^{\Gamma^{(l)}}\bigl[\Delta_{g^{\prime}}^{(l)}\exp[\tilde{\mathbf{b}}(\mathbf{x}^{(l)})(u_{g^{\prime}}^{(l)})^{\top}\boldsymbol{\theta}]\,(\tilde{\mathbf{b}}(\mathbf{x}^{(l)})(u_{g^{\prime}}^{(l)}))_{[k]}\bigr]}{\sum_{g^{\prime}=1}^{\Gamma^{(l)}}\Delta_{g^{\prime}}^{(l)}\exp[\tilde{\mathbf{b}}(\mathbf{x}^{(l)})(u_{g^{\prime}}^{(l)})^{\top}\boldsymbol{\theta}]}

\displaystyle\penalty\ \cdot\frac{\sum_{g^{\prime}=1}^{\Gamma^{(l)}}\bigl[\Delta_{g^{\prime}}^{(l)}\exp[\tilde{\mathbf{b}}(\mathbf{x}^{(l)})(u_{g^{\prime}}^{(l)})^{\top}\boldsymbol{\theta}]\,(\tilde{\mathbf{b}}(\mathbf{x}^{(l)})(u_{g^{\prime}}^{(l)}))_{[k^{\prime}]}\bigr]\,}{\sum_{g^{\prime}=1}^{\Gamma^{(l)}}\Delta_{g^{\prime}}^{(l)}\exp[\tilde{\mathbf{b}}(\mathbf{x}^{(l)})(u_{g^{\prime}}^{(l)})^{\top}\boldsymbol{\theta}]}.

(6.10)

Using
p_{g^{\prime}}^{(l)}(\boldsymbol{\theta})=\frac{\Delta_{g^{\prime}}^{(l)}\exp[\tilde{\mathbf{b}}(\mathbf{x}^{(l)})(u_{g^{\prime}}^{(l)})^{\top}\boldsymbol{\theta}]}{\sum_{g=1}^{\Gamma^{(l)}}\Delta_{g}^{(l)}\exp[\tilde{\mathbf{b}}(\mathbf{x}^{(l)})(u_{g}^{(l)})^{\top}\boldsymbol{\theta}]}
,
we obtain,

\displaystyle\penalty\ \frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial\theta_{k}\partial\theta_{k^{\prime}}}C_{\mathcal{Z}}^{\text{mn}\,(s)}(\boldsymbol{\theta})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\penalty\ -\sum_{g^{\prime}=1}^{\Gamma^{(l)}}p_{g^{\prime}}^{(l)}(\boldsymbol{\theta})\,(\tilde{\mathbf{b}}(\mathbf{x}^{(l)})(u_{g^{\prime}}^{(l)}))_{[k]}\,(\tilde{\mathbf{b}}(\mathbf{x}^{(l)})(u_{g^{\prime}}^{(l)}))_{[k^{\prime}]}

\displaystyle\penalty\ +\sum_{g^{\prime}=1}^{\Gamma^{(l)}}\Bigl[p_{g^{\prime}}^{(l)}(\boldsymbol{\theta})\,(\tilde{\mathbf{b}}(\mathbf{x}^{(l)})(u_{g^{\prime}}^{(l)}))_{[k]}\Bigr]\,\sum_{g^{\prime}=1}^{\Gamma^{(l)}}\Bigl[p_{g^{\prime}}^{(l)}(\boldsymbol{\theta})\,(\tilde{\mathbf{b}}(\mathbf{x}^{(l)})(u_{g^{\prime}}^{(l)}))_{[k^{\prime}]}\Bigr],

Then,
with
\sum_{g^{\prime}=1}^{\Gamma^{(l)}}p_{g^{\prime}}^{(l)}(\boldsymbol{\theta})=1
for all
l=1,\ldots,L
, we have for
\mathbf{v}\in\mathbb{R}^{K}

\displaystyle\mathbf{v}^{\top}\mathbf{H}_{\mathcal{Z}}^{\text{mn}\,(s)}(\boldsymbol{\theta})\mathbf{v}=-\sum_{g^{\prime}=1}^{\Gamma^{(l)}}p_{g^{\prime}}^{(l)}(\boldsymbol{\theta})\,\Bigl(\tilde{\mathbf{b}}(\mathbf{x}^{(l)})(u_{g^{\prime}}^{(l)})^{\top}\mathbf{v}-\sum_{g^{\prime\prime}=1}^{\Gamma^{(l)}}p_{g^{\prime\prime}}^{(l)}(\boldsymbol{\theta})\,\tilde{\mathbf{b}}(\mathbf{x}^{(l)})(u_{g^{\prime\prime}}^{(l)})^{\top}\mathbf{v}\Bigr)^{2},

which gives us

\displaystyle\mathbf{v}^{\top}\mathbf{H}^{\text{mn}}_{\mathcal{Z}}(\boldsymbol{\theta})\mathbf{v}=\sum_{l=1}^{L}\sum_{g=1}^{\Gamma^{(l)}}n_{g}^{(l)}\mathbf{v}^{\top}\mathbf{H}_{\mathcal{Z}}^{\text{mn}\,(s)}(\boldsymbol{\theta})\mathbf{v}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\sum_{l=1}^{L}\sum_{g=1}^{\Gamma^{(l)}}n_{g}^{(l)}\sum_{g^{\prime}=1}^{\Gamma^{(l)}}p_{g^{\prime}}^{(l)}(\boldsymbol{\theta})\,\Bigl(\tilde{\mathbf{b}}(\mathbf{x}^{(l)})(u_{g^{\prime}}^{(l)})^{\top}\mathbf{v}-\sum_{g^{\prime\prime}=1}^{\Gamma^{(l)}}p_{g^{\prime\prime}}^{(l)}(\boldsymbol{\theta})\,\tilde{\mathbf{b}}(\mathbf{x}^{(l)})(u_{g^{\prime\prime}}^{(l)})^{\top}\mathbf{v}\Bigr)^{2}.

Since
n_{g}^{(l)}\geq 0
and
p_{g}^{(l)}(\boldsymbol{\theta})>0
for all
l=1,\ldots,L,\penalty\ g,k=1,\ldots,\Gamma^{(l)}
, all summands are non-negative and thus
\mathbf{v}^{\top}\mathbf{H}^{\text{mn}}_{\mathcal{Z}}(\boldsymbol{\theta})\mathbf{v}\leq 0
.
Assuming
\mathbf{v}^{\top}\mathbf{H}^{\text{mn}}_{\mathcal{Z}}(\boldsymbol{\theta})\mathbf{v}=0
implies
\tilde{\mathbf{b}}(\mathbf{x}^{(l)})(u_{g^{\prime}}^{(l)})^{\top}\mathbf{v}=\sum_{g^{\prime\prime}=1}^{\Gamma^{(l)}}p_{g^{\prime\prime}}^{(l)}(\boldsymbol{\theta})\,\tilde{\mathbf{b}}(\mathbf{x}^{(l)})(u_{g^{\prime\prime}}^{(l)})^{\top}\mathbf{v}
for all
l=1,\ldots,L,\penalty\ g^{\prime}=1,\ldots,\Gamma^{(l)}
, since for all
l=1,\ldots,L
, there is a
g\in\{1,\ldots,\Gamma^{(l)}\}
with
n_{g}^{(l)}>0
.
This means,
\tilde{\mathbf{b}}(\mathbf{x}^{(l)})(u_{1}^{(l)})^{\top}\mathbf{v}=\ldots=\tilde{\mathbf{b}}(\mathbf{x}^{(l)})(u_{\Gamma^{(l)}}^{(l)})^{\top}\mathbf{v}
.
Let
\mathbf{v}_{\mathcal{Y}}^{(l)}=(\mathbf{b}_{\mathcal{X}}(\mathbf{x}^{(l)})^{\top}\mathbf{v}_{1}
,
\ldots,\mathbf{b}_{\mathcal{X}}(\mathbf{x}^{(l)})^{\top}\mathbf{v}_{K_{\mathcal{Y}}})

with
\mathbf{v}_{m}=(v_{1,1,m},\ldots,v_{J,K_{\mathcal{X},\,J},m})^{\top}
.
It is straightforward to show that then
\tilde{\mathbf{b}}(\mathbf{x}^{(l)})^{\top}\mathbf{v}=\tilde{\mathbf{b}}_{\mathcal{Y}}^{\top}\mathbf{v}_{\mathcal{Y}}^{(l)}
. Thus, by assumption
(BU)
, we have
\mathbf{v}_{\mathcal{Y}}^{(l)}=\mathbf{0}
, i.e.,
\mathbf{b}_{\mathcal{X}}(\mathbf{x}^{(l)})^{\top}\mathbf{v}_{m}=0

for all
l=1,\ldots,L,m=1,\ldots,K_{\mathcal{Y}}
.
This is equivalent to
\mathbf{b}_{\mathcal{X}}(\mathbf{x}_{i})^{\top}\mathbf{v}_{m}=0
for all
i=1,\ldots,N
and thus, by assumption
(BX)
,
\mathbf{v}_{m}=0
for
m=1,\ldots,K_{\mathcal{Y}}
,
i.e.,
\mathbf{v}=\mathbf{0}
.
Hence, we have
\mathbf{v}^{\top}\mathbf{H}^{\text{mn}}_{\mathcal{Z}}(\boldsymbol{\theta})\mathbf{v}<0
for all
\mathbf{v}\in\mathbb{R}^{K}\setminus\{\mathbf{0}\}
, i.e.,
\mathbf{H}^{\text{mn}}_{\mathcal{Z}}(\boldsymbol{\theta})
is negative definite and thus invertible.

Note that

\mathbf{H}_{\mathcal{Z}}^{\text{mn}\,(s)}(\boldsymbol{\theta})
is constant regarding
\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{Z}}^{\mathrm{mn}}
,
compare (
6.10
).
Replacing the observations
n_{g}^{(l)}
with the underlying random variables
N_{g}^{(l)}
, the random vectors
(N_{1}^{(l)},\ldots,N_{\Gamma^{(l)}}^{(l)})
are conditionally multinomially distributed given
\sum_{g=1}^{\Gamma^{(l)}}N_{g}^{(l)}=n^{(l)}
,
g=1,\ldots,\Gamma^{(l)},\,l=1,\ldots,L
.
Thus,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{Z}}^{\mathrm{mn}}(\mathbf{H}_{\mathcal{Z}}^{\mathrm{mn}}(\boldsymbol{\theta}))

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{Z}}^{\mathrm{mn}}\Bigl(\sum_{l=1}^{L}\sum_{g=1}^{\Gamma^{(l)}}N_{g}^{(l)}\mathbf{H}_{\mathcal{Z}}^{\text{mn}\,(s)}(\boldsymbol{\theta})\Bigr)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{Z}}^{\mathrm{mn}}\Bigl(\sum_{l=1}^{L}n^{(l)}\mathbf{H}_{\mathcal{Z}}^{\text{mn}\,(s)}(\boldsymbol{\theta})\Bigr)=\sum_{l=1}^{L}n^{(l)}\mathbf{H}_{\mathcal{Z}}^{\text{mn}\,(s)}(\boldsymbol{\theta})=\mathbf{H}_{\mathcal{Z}}^{\mathrm{mn}}(\boldsymbol{\theta}),

and adding the constant
D^{2}\pen(\boldsymbol{\theta})
yields

\mathbf{H}_{\mathcal{Z},\mathrm{pen}}^{\mathrm{mn}}(\boldsymbol{\theta})=\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{Z}}^{\mathrm{mn}}(\mathbf{H}_{\mathcal{Z},\mathrm{pen}}^{\mathrm{mn}}(\boldsymbol{\theta}))
.

Since
\mathbf{F}_{\mathcal{Z},\mathrm{pen}}^{\mathrm{mn}}(\boldsymbol{\theta})=-\mathbf{H}_{\mathcal{Z},\mathrm{pen}}^{\mathrm{mn}}(\boldsymbol{\theta})
,
the results for the penalized Fisher information follow immediately.
∎

Lemma 6.7
.

Denote the set of invertible
(K\times K)
-matrices with
\GL(K,\mathbb{R})
.
Then,
\inv:\GL(K,\mathbb{R})\rightarrow\GL(K,\mathbb{R}),\,\mathbf{F}\mapsto\mathbf{F}^{-1}
is differentiable and thus continuous.

Proof.

For any matrix
\mathbf{A}\in\mathbb{R}^{K\times K}
with
spectral norm

\|\mathbf{A}\|_{\mathrm{op}}:=\sup_{\boldsymbol{\theta}\in\mathbb{R}^{K}\setminus\{\mathbf{0}\}}\frac{\|\mathbf{A}\boldsymbol{\theta}\|_{2}}{\|\boldsymbol{\theta}\|_{2}}<1
we have the Neumann series
(
Alt 2016a
, Statement 5.7)

\displaystyle(\mathbf{I}+\mathbf{A})^{-1}

\displaystyle=\sum_{m=0}^{\infty}(-1)^{m}\mathbf{A}^{m}=\mathbf{I}-\mathbf{A}+O(\|\mathbf{A}\|_{\mathrm{op}}^{2}).

(6.11)

For
\mathbf{F}\in\GL(K,\mathbb{R})
consider some matrix
\mathbf{B}\in\mathbb{R}^{K\times K}
, such that
\|\mathbf{B}\|_{\mathrm{op}}<\frac{1}{\|\mathbf{F}^{-1}\|_{\mathrm{op}}}
.
Then,
\|\mathbf{F}^{-1}\mathbf{B}\|_{\mathrm{op}}\leq\|\mathbf{F}^{-1}\|_{\mathrm{op}}\,\|\mathbf{B}\|_{\mathrm{op}}<1
and with (
6.11
),
\mathbf{F}^{-1}\mathbf{B}
is invertible.
Thus, also
(\mathbf{F}+\mathbf{B})=\mathbf{F}(\mathbf{I}+\mathbf{F}^{-1}\mathbf{B})
is invertible with

\displaystyle(\mathbf{F}+\mathbf{B})^{-1}=(\mathbf{I}+\mathbf{F}^{-1}\mathbf{B})^{-1}\,\mathbf{F}^{-1}\overset{\eqref{P2a:eq_geometric_series}}{=}\mathbf{F}^{-1}-\mathbf{F}^{-1}\mathbf{B}\,\mathbf{F}^{-1}+O(\|\mathbf{F}^{-1}\mathbf{B}\|_{\mathrm{op}}^{2})

and the matrix inversion function
\inv
is differentiable with the derivative given as
D\inv(\mathbf{F}):\mathbb{R}^{K\times K}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{K\times K},\,\mathbf{B}\mapsto-\mathbf{F}^{-1}\mathbf{B}\,\mathbf{F}^{-1}
.
Thus,
\inv
is also continuous.
∎

Theorem 6.8
.

For
\kappa\geq 2
, let
s_{\mathrm{c}}:\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c}}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
be a
(\kappa-1)
times continuously differentiable piecewise polynomial consisting of finitely many polynomials of degree at most
\kappa

and let
s:\mathcal{Y}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
such that
s(y)=s_{\mathrm{c}}(y)
for all
y\in\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c}}\setminus\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{d}}
.
For

s^{\max}:=\sup_{y\in\mathcal{Y}}s(y)
consider the sets

\mathcal{D}^{\max}:=\mathcal{D}^{\max}(s):=\{d\in\{1,\ldots,D\}\penalty\ |\penalty\ s(t_{d})=s^{\max}\}

and

\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c}}^{\max}:=\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c}}^{\max}(s):=\{y\in\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c}}\penalty\ |\penalty\ s_{\mathrm{c}}(y)=s^{\max}\}
.
Then, at least one of these sets is not empty.
Furthermore,
\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c}}^{\max}=\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c},\,-}^{\max}\sqcup\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c},\,\bullet}^{\max}
, where

\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c},\,-}^{\max}=\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c},\,-}^{\max}(s)=\bigsqcup_{m=1}^{M}[a_{m},b_{m}]

for
a_{m}<b_{m},m=1,\ldots,M\in\mathbb{N}_{0},

and

\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c},\,\bullet}^{\max}=\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c},\,\bullet}^{\max}(s)
is a finite (possibly empty) set containing isolated values of
\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c}}^{\max}
. Then, for each

y\in\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c},\,\bullet}^{\max}
, there exist
c_{y}>0
and
\alpha_{y}\in\mathbb{N}
with
\alpha_{y}\leq\kappa
such that

\displaystyle\lim_{z\rightarrow\infty}\frac{\int_{\mathcal{Y}}\exp[z\,s]\,\mathrm{d}\mu}{\exp[z\,s^{\max}]\,\Bigl(\sum_{d\in\mathcal{D}^{\max}}w_{d}+\lambda\bigl(\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c},\,-}^{\max}\bigr)+\sum_{y\in\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c},\,\bullet}^{\max}}c_{y}\,z^{-\alpha_{y}^{-1}}\Bigr)}=1.

(6.12)

Proof.

We first show that at least one of
\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c}}^{\max}
and
\mathcal{D}^{\max}
is not empty.
Since
\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c}}
is a compact interval and
s_{\mathrm{c}}
is continuous, there exists a
y_{\mathrm{c}}\in\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c}}
with
s_{\mathrm{c}}(y_{\mathrm{c}})=\max_{y\in\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c}}}s_{\mathrm{c}}(y)=:s_{\mathrm{c}}^{\max}
.
Furthermore, since
\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{d}}
is a finite set, there exists a
y_{\mathrm{d}}\in\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{d}}
with
s(y_{\mathrm{d}})=\max_{y\in\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{d}}}s(y)=:s_{\mathrm{d}}^{\max}
.
Noting that
s^{\max}=\max\{s_{\mathrm{c}}^{\max},s_{\mathrm{d}}^{\max}\}
, we obtain the result.

Now, let
\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c},\,\bullet}^{\max}\subseteq\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c}}^{\max}
be the set of all isolated values of
\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c}}^{\max}
.
We show that
\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c}}^{\max}=\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c},\,-}^{\max}\sqcup\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c},\,\bullet}^{\max}
, where
\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c},\,-}^{\max}
is a disjoint union of
M\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
nontrivial closed intervals.
For this purpose, we assume
\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c}}^{\max}\neq\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c},\,\bullet}^{\max}
(otherwise
M=0
and there is nothing left to show) and show that any
y_{\mathrm{c}}\in\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c}}^{\max}\setminus\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c},\,\bullet}^{\max}
is contained in a nontrivial closed interval.
Thus, let
y_{\mathrm{c}}\in\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c}}^{\max}\setminus\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c},\,\bullet}^{\max}
and recall that
s_{\mathrm{c}}
is a piecewise polynomial.
Since
y_{\mathrm{c}}
might be a breakpoint of
s_{\mathrm{c}}
, we distinguish between the polynomial to the left and the one to the right of
y_{\mathrm{c}}
.
However, if
y_{\mathrm{c}}
is not a breakpoint, both polynomials coincide.
If
y_{\mathrm{c}}\in\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c}}^{\max}\cap(a,b]
, there exists a polynomial

p_{<}
with degree

\kappa_{<}\leq\kappa
and
\varepsilon>0
,
such that
s_{\mathrm{c}}(y)=p_{<}(y)
for all
y\in[y_{\mathrm{c}}-\varepsilon,y_{\mathrm{c}}]
. Let
\varepsilon_{<}=\varepsilon_{<}(y_{\mathrm{c}})>0
be maximal with this property (i.e.,
y_{\mathrm{c}}-\varepsilon_{<}
is a breakpoint of the piecewise polynomial) and set

\overline{\mathcal{U}(y_{\mathrm{c}},\varepsilon_{<})}_{<}:=[y_{\mathrm{c}}-\varepsilon_{<},y_{\mathrm{c}}]
.
Analogously, if
y_{\mathrm{c}}\in\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c}}^{\max}\cap[a,b)
, there exists a polynomial

p_{>}
with degree

\kappa_{>}\leq\kappa
and a maximal
\varepsilon_{>}=\varepsilon_{>}(y_{\mathrm{c}})>0

such that
s_{\mathrm{c}}(y)=p_{>}(y)
for all
y\in[y_{\mathrm{c}},y_{\mathrm{c}}+\varepsilon_{>}]=:\overline{\mathcal{U}(y_{\mathrm{c}},\varepsilon_{>})}_{>}
.
In the following, we treat both polynomials simultaneously with the subscript “
≶
” either corresponding to “
<
” or to “
>
”.
For the boundary values, only consider the respective existent case, i.e., if
y_{\mathrm{c}}=a
, only consider the objects with subscript “
>
”, if
y_{\mathrm{c}}=b
only the objects with subscript “
<
”.
Since
y_{\mathrm{c}}\in\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c}}^{\max}\setminus\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c},\,\bullet}^{\max}
is not isolated, it is possible to find further points belonging to
\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c}}^{\max}
in any neighborhood of
y_{\mathrm{c}}
and thus by successively diminishing the size of the neighborhoods we obtain arbitrary many values in
\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c}}^{\max}
.
More precisely, let
y_{0,<}:=y_{\mathrm{c}}-\varepsilon_{<}
and
y_{0,>}:=y_{\mathrm{c}}+\varepsilon_{>}
.
Recursively, there exist
y_{m,<}\in(y_{m-1,<},y_{\mathrm{c}})
and
y_{m,>}\in(y_{\mathrm{c}},y_{m-1,>})
with

y_{m,\lessgtr}\in\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c}}^{\max}
,
i.e., with

p_{\lessgtr}(y_{m,\lessgtr})=s^{\max}
, for

m=1,\ldots,\kappa_{\lessgtr}+1
.
These
\kappa_{\lessgtr}+1
values are thus pairwise distinct roots of the polynomial
p_{\lessgtr}-s^{\max}
, which
(e.g., using
Laczkovich & Sós 2015a
, Lemma 11.1)
implies
p_{\lessgtr}(y)=s^{\max}
for all
y\in\overline{\mathcal{U}(y_{\mathrm{c}},\varepsilon_{\lessgtr})}_{\lessgtr}
.
Thus, setting
\varepsilon_{<}:=0
if
y_{\mathrm{c}}=a
and
\varepsilon_{>}:=0
, if
y_{\mathrm{c}}=b
, we obtain that if
y_{\mathrm{c}}\in\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c}}^{\max}
is not isolated, it is contained in the nontrivial closed interval
[y_{\mathrm{c}}-\varepsilon_{<},y_{\mathrm{c}}+\varepsilon_{>}]
on which
s_{\mathrm{c}}
is constantly
s^{\max}
. Note that this interval cannot be contained in a larger open interval, since
\overline{\mathcal{U}(y_{\mathrm{c}},\varepsilon_{\lessgtr})}_{\lessgtr}
was constructed as the maximal neighborhood to the left/right of
y_{\mathrm{c}}
, on which
p_{\lessgtr}=s_{\mathrm{c}}
.
In particular, the boundaries of this interval then correspond to two adjacent breakpoints of the piecewise polynomial
s_{\mathrm{c}}
.
As there are only finitely many breakpoints, we obtain that there exist only finitely many nontrivial closed intervals on which
s_{\mathrm{c}}
is constantly
s^{\max}
and thus
\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c}}^{\max}=\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c},\,-}^{\max}\sqcup\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c},\,\bullet}^{\max}
with
\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c},\,-}^{\max}=\bigsqcup_{m=1}^{M}[a_{m},b_{m}]
, where
a_{m}<b_{m},m=1,\ldots,M\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
.
Furthermore, we have
|\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c},\,\bullet}^{\max}|<\infty
as each polynomial can take the value
s^{\max}
at most
\kappa
times (or even less, if its degree is smaller than
\kappa
) and
s_{\mathrm{c}}
consists of a finite number of polynomials.

Finally, we show equation (
6.12
), first proving that the limit of the fraction on the left is at least
1
and afterwards that it is at most
1
.
In both cases, we proceed similarly:
The idea is to bound the integral in the numerator of the fraction by a term that mimics the denominator.
More precisely, these bounds will differ from the denominator in the terms corresponding to
\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c},\,\bullet}^{\max}
(and
\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c},\,-}^{\max}
in case of the upper bound)
by some small value
\varepsilon>0
, but converge to the denominator for
\varepsilon\rightarrow 0
, which then yields the result.
As we ultimately consider
z\rightarrow\infty
, we always assume
z>0
in the following.
Furthermore, note that

\int_{\mathcal{Y}}\exp[z\,s]\,\mathrm{d}\mu=\int_{\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c}}}\exp[z\,s]\,\mathrm{d}\lambda+\int_{\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{d}}}\exp[z\,s]\,\mathrm{d}\delta

and

\int_{\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c}}}\exp[z\,s]\,\mathrm{d}\lambda=\int_{\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c}}}\exp[z\,s_{\mathrm{c}}]\,\mathrm{d}\lambda
.

To show that the limit in (
6.12
) is at least
1
,
let
\varepsilon>0
be small enough such that

\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c},\,-}^{\max}(\varepsilon)\cap\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c},\,\bullet}^{\max}=\emptyset
, where
\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c},\,-}^{\max}(\varepsilon):=\bigcup_{m=1}^{M}(a_{m}-\varepsilon,b_{m}+\varepsilon)\cap\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c}}
.
We have

\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c},\,-}^{\max}(\varepsilon)}\exp[z\,s_{\mathrm{c}}]\,\mathrm{d}\lambda\geq\int_{\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c},\,-}^{\max}}\exp[z\,s_{\mathrm{c}}]\,\mathrm{d}\lambda=\exp[z\,s^{\max}]\,\lambda\bigl(\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c},\,-}^{\max}\bigr).

(6.13)

For
y_{\mathrm{c}}\in\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c},\,\bullet}^{\max}
, consider
p_{<}
and
p_{>}
as above (if existent). If
y_{\mathrm{c}}\in(a,b)
, then
y_{\mathrm{c}}
being isolated implies that
s_{\mathrm{c}}
has a strict local maximum at
y_{\mathrm{c}}
, with first derivative equal to zero.
At the boundary values
a
and
b
, this is not necessarily true.
However, if the first derivative is not zero, it then has to be negative for
y_{\mathrm{c}}=a
and positive for
y_{\mathrm{c}}=b
.
Furthermore recall that
s_{\mathrm{c}}(y_{\mathrm{c}})=p_{<}(y_{\mathrm{c}})=p_{>}(y_{\mathrm{c}})
with the first
\kappa-1
derivatives of
p_{<}
and
p_{>}
coinciding as well.
With these considerations in mind, we now define the smallest non-zero derivative of the piecewise polynomials:
Since
\kappa_{\lessgtr}
is the degree of
p_{\lessgtr}
, the
\kappa_{\lessgtr}
-st derivative of
p_{\lessgtr}
is a non-zero constant and thus there exists a natural number
\alpha_{y_{\mathrm{c}},\,\lessgtr}\leq\kappa_{\lessgtr}\leq\kappa
such that
p_{\lessgtr}^{(\alpha_{y_{\mathrm{c}},\,\lessgtr})}(y_{\mathrm{c}})\neq 0
and
p_{\lessgtr}^{(\alpha)}(y_{\mathrm{c}})=0
for all
\alpha\in\{\alpha^{\prime}\in\mathbb{N}\penalty\ |\penalty\ \alpha^{\prime}<\alpha_{y_{\mathrm{c}},\,\lessgtr}\}
.
Note that this set is empty, if
\alpha_{y_{\mathrm{c}},\,\lessgtr}=1
(which can only occur if
y_{\mathrm{c}}\in\{a,b\}
). Since the first
\kappa-1
derivatives of
p_{<}
and
p_{>}
coincide in
y_{\mathrm{c}}
,
\alpha_{y_{\mathrm{c}},\,>}=\alpha_{y_{\mathrm{c}},\,<}=:\alpha_{y_{\mathrm{c}}}
.
Note that if
\alpha_{y_{\mathrm{c}}}>1
(which is always fulfilled, if
y_{\mathrm{c}}\in(a,b)
and might be fulfilled, if
y_{\mathrm{c}}\in\{a,b\}
), then
p_{\lessgtr}
(and
s_{\mathrm{c}}
) has a strict local maximum at
y_{\mathrm{c}}
,
which implies that
\alpha_{y_{\mathrm{c}}}
is even with
p_{\lessgtr}^{(\alpha_{y_{\mathrm{c}}})}(y_{\mathrm{c}})<0

(
Laczkovich & Sós 2015a
, Theorem 12.62)
.
Thus, one of the following applies to
y_{\mathrm{c}}
:

(i)

y_{\mathrm{c}}\in(a,b)
with
\alpha_{y_{\mathrm{c}}}
even and
p_{\lessgtr}^{(\alpha_{y_{\mathrm{c}}})}(y_{\mathrm{c}})<0
,

(ii)

y_{\mathrm{c}}=a
with
\alpha_{y_{\mathrm{c}}}
even and
p_{>}^{(\alpha_{y_{\mathrm{c}}})}(y_{\mathrm{c}})<0
(
p_{<}
not existent),

(iii)

y_{\mathrm{c}}=a
with
\alpha_{y_{\mathrm{c}}}=1
and
p_{>}^{(\alpha_{y_{\mathrm{c}}})}(y_{\mathrm{c}})=p_{>}^{\prime}(a)<0
(
p_{<}
not existent),

(iv)

y_{\mathrm{c}}=b
with
\alpha_{y_{\mathrm{c}}}
even and
p_{<}^{(\alpha_{y_{\mathrm{c}}})}(y_{\mathrm{c}})<0
(
p_{>}
not existent),

(v)

y_{\mathrm{c}}=b
with
\alpha_{y_{\mathrm{c}}}=1
and
p_{<}^{(\alpha_{y_{\mathrm{c}}})}(y_{\mathrm{c}})=p_{<}^{\prime}(b)>0
(
p_{>}
not existent).

As before, we only proceed with the existent case regarding the subscripts “
>
” and “
<
”, i.e., if
y_{\mathrm{c}}=a
(cases
(ii)
and
(iii)
), only consider the objects with subscript “
>
”, if
y_{\mathrm{c}}=b
(cases
(iv)
and
(v)
), only the objects with subscript “
<
”.
For
\delta>0
, set

\mathcal{U}_{>}(y_{\mathrm{c}},\delta):=[y_{\mathrm{c}},y_{\mathrm{c}}+\delta)
and

\mathcal{U}_{<}(y_{\mathrm{c}},\delta):=(y_{\mathrm{c}}-\delta,y_{\mathrm{c}}]
. As
p_{\lessgtr}^{(\alpha_{y_{\mathrm{c}}})}
is continuous, for any
y_{\mathrm{c}}\in\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c},\,\bullet}^{\max}
, there exists
\tilde{\delta}_{y_{\mathrm{c}},\,\lessgtr}>0
such that

\displaystyle p_{\lessgtr}^{(\alpha_{y_{\mathrm{c}}})}(\tilde{y})\geq p_{\lessgtr}^{(\alpha_{y_{\mathrm{c}}})}(y_{\mathrm{c}})-\varepsilon

and

\displaystyle p_{\lessgtr}^{(\alpha_{y_{\mathrm{c}}})}(\tilde{y})\leq p_{\lessgtr}^{(\alpha_{y_{\mathrm{c}}})}(y_{\mathrm{c}})+\varepsilon

(6.14)

for all
\tilde{y}\in\mathcal{U}_{\lessgtr}(y_{\mathrm{c}},\tilde{\delta}_{y_{\mathrm{c}},\,\lessgtr})
.
Furthermore, there exists
\delta_{y_{\mathrm{c}}}>0
with

\mathcal{U}_{\lessgtr}(y_{\mathrm{c}},\delta_{y_{\mathrm{c}}})\subset\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c}}
,

\mathcal{U}_{\lessgtr}(y_{\mathrm{c}},\delta_{y_{\mathrm{c}}})\cap\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c},\,-}^{\max}(\varepsilon)=\emptyset
,

\mathcal{U}_{\lessgtr}(y_{\mathrm{c}},\delta_{y_{\mathrm{c}}})\subseteq\mathcal{U}_{\lessgtr}(y_{\mathrm{c}},\tilde{\delta}_{y_{\mathrm{c}},\lessgtr})
,
and
\delta_{y_{\mathrm{c}}}\leq\max\{\varepsilon_{<}(y_{\mathrm{c}}),\varepsilon_{>}(y_{\mathrm{c}})\}
.
By Taylor’s formula
(e.g.,
Laczkovich & Sós 2017a
, Theorem 1.97)
, for any

y\in\mathcal{U}_{\lessgtr}(y_{\mathrm{c}},\delta_{y_{\mathrm{c}}})
, we have

\displaystyle p_{\lessgtr}(y)=p_{\lessgtr}(y_{\mathrm{c}})+\frac{1}{\alpha_{y_{\mathrm{c}}}!}\,p_{\lessgtr}^{(\alpha_{y_{\mathrm{c}}})}(\tilde{y})\,(y-y_{\mathrm{c}})^{\alpha_{y_{\mathrm{c}}}}

(6.15)

for a
\tilde{y}
between
y
and
y_{\mathrm{c}}
, which implies
\tilde{y}\in\mathcal{U}_{\lessgtr}(y_{\mathrm{c}},\tilde{\delta}_{y_{\mathrm{c}},\,\lessgtr})
. First consider cases
(i)
-
(iv)
.
Then,
(y-y_{\mathrm{c}})^{\alpha_{y_{\mathrm{c}}}}\geq 0
(in cases
(i)
,
(ii)
, and
(iv)
this follows from
\alpha_{y_{\mathrm{c}}}
being even, while in case
(iii)
, only the case with subscript
>
is considered, with
y-y_{\mathrm{c}}\geq 0
for all
y\in\mathcal{U}_{>}(y_{\mathrm{c}},\tilde{\delta}_{y_{\mathrm{c}},\,>})
)
and with the left inequality of (
6.14
), we obtain

\displaystyle p_{\lessgtr}(y)\geq p_{\lessgtr}(y_{\mathrm{c}})+\frac{1}{\alpha_{y_{\mathrm{c}}}!}(p_{\lessgtr}^{(\alpha_{y_{\mathrm{c}}})}(y_{\mathrm{c}})-\varepsilon)(y-y_{\mathrm{c}})^{\alpha_{y_{\mathrm{c}}}}

for any
y\in\mathcal{U}_{\lessgtr}(y_{\mathrm{c}},\delta_{y_{\mathrm{c}}})
. Then,

\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{U}_{\lessgtr}(y_{\mathrm{c}},\delta_{y_{\mathrm{c}}})}\exp[z\,p_{\lessgtr}]\,\mathrm{d}\lambda

\displaystyle\geq\penalty\

\displaystyle\exp\left(z\,p_{\lessgtr}(y_{\mathrm{c}})\right)\int_{\mathcal{U}_{\lessgtr}(y_{\mathrm{c}},\delta_{y_{\mathrm{c}}})}\exp\Bigl(z\,\frac{1}{\alpha_{y_{\mathrm{c}}}!}\left(p_{\lessgtr}^{(\alpha_{y_{\mathrm{c}}})}(y_{\mathrm{c}})-\varepsilon\right)(y-y_{\mathrm{c}})^{\alpha_{y_{\mathrm{c}}}}\Bigr)\,\mathrm{d}\lambda(y).

(6.16)

Note that as we consider cases
(i)
-
(iv)
,
we have
p_{\lessgtr}^{(\alpha_{y_{\mathrm{c}}})}(y_{\mathrm{c}})<0
,
i.e.,

-p_{\lessgtr}^{(\alpha_{y_{\mathrm{c}}})}(y_{\mathrm{c}})>0
.
Using

\exp\left(z\,p_{\lessgtr}(y_{\mathrm{c}})\right)=\exp\left(z\,s^{\max}\right)
,
substituting

\upsilon=[z\,\frac{1}{\alpha_{y_{\mathrm{c}}}!}\,(-p_{\lessgtr}^{(\alpha_{y_{\mathrm{c}}})}(y_{\mathrm{c}})+\varepsilon)]^{\alpha_{y_{\mathrm{c}}}^{-1}}(y-y_{\mathrm{c}})
,
and setting

\displaystyle\mathcal{U}_{>}(y_{\mathrm{c}},z,\varepsilon)

\displaystyle:=\Bigl[0\,,\,\delta_{y_{\mathrm{c}}}\,\bigl[z\,\frac{1}{\alpha_{y_{\mathrm{c}}}!}\,(|p_{>}^{(\alpha_{y_{\mathrm{c}}})}(y_{\mathrm{c}})|+\varepsilon)\bigr]^{\alpha_{y_{\mathrm{c}}}^{-1}}\Bigr)

\displaystyle\mathcal{U}_{<}(y_{\mathrm{c}},z,\varepsilon)

\displaystyle:=\Bigl(-\delta_{y_{\mathrm{c}}}\,\bigl[z\,\frac{1}{\alpha_{y_{\mathrm{c}}}!}\,(|p_{<}^{(\alpha_{y_{\mathrm{c}}})}(y_{\mathrm{c}})|+\varepsilon)\bigr]^{\alpha_{y_{\mathrm{c}}}^{-1}}\,,\,0\Bigr]

in the right side of (
6.1.4
) yields

\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{U}_{\lessgtr}(y_{\mathrm{c}},\delta_{y_{\mathrm{c}}})}\exp[z\,p_{\lessgtr}]\,\mathrm{d}\lambda\geq\penalty\

\displaystyle\frac{\exp\left(z\,s^{\max}\right)}{\left(z\,\frac{1}{\alpha_{y_{\mathrm{c}}}!}\,\left(-p_{>}^{(\alpha_{y_{\mathrm{c}}})}(y_{\mathrm{c}})+\varepsilon\right)\right)^{\alpha_{y_{\mathrm{c}}}^{-1}}}\int_{\mathcal{U}_{\lessgtr}(y_{\mathrm{c}},z,\varepsilon)}\exp\left(-\upsilon^{\alpha_{y_{\mathrm{c}}}}\right)\,\mathrm{d}\lambda(\upsilon).

Defining

I_{\lessgtr}(y_{\mathrm{c}},z,\varepsilon):=\int_{\mathcal{U}_{\lessgtr}(y_{\mathrm{c}},z,\varepsilon)}\exp\left(-\upsilon^{\alpha_{y_{\mathrm{c}}}}\right)\,\mathrm{d}\lambda(\upsilon)

and recalling that

on

,

\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{U}_{\lessgtr}(y_{\mathrm{c}},\delta_{y_{\mathrm{c}}})}\exp[z\,s_{\mathrm{c}}]\,\mathrm{d}\lambda\geq\frac{\exp(z\,s^{\max})\,I_{\lessgtr}(y_{\mathrm{c}},z,\varepsilon)\,z^{-\alpha_{y_{\mathrm{c}}}^{-1}}}{\bigl(\frac{1}{\alpha_{y_{\mathrm{c}}}!}\,\left(|p_{>}^{(\alpha_{y_{\mathrm{c}}})}(y_{\mathrm{c}})|+\varepsilon\right)\bigr)^{\alpha_{y_{\mathrm{c}}}^{-1}}}.

(6.17)

In case
(v)
, where
y_{\mathrm{c}}=b
with
\alpha_{y_{\mathrm{c}}}=1
and

p_{<}^{(\alpha_{y_{\mathrm{c}}})}(y_{\mathrm{c}})=p_{<}^{\prime}(b)>0
(and
p_{>}
not existent), we have to make small adjustments to obtain (

).
As
(y-y_{\mathrm{c}})^{\alpha_{y_{\mathrm{c}}}}=(y-b)\leq 0
for all
y\in\mathcal{U}_{<}(b,\tilde{\delta}_{b,\,<})
,
we insert the right inequality of (
6.14
) into (
6.15
) and obtain
p_{<}(y)\geq p_{<}(b)+(p_{<}^{\prime}(b)+\varepsilon)(y-b)
for any
y\in\mathcal{U}_{<}(b,\delta_{b})
.
Then,

\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{U}_{<}(b,\delta_{b})}\exp[z\,p_{<}]\,\mathrm{d}\lambda\geq\exp\left(z\,p_{<}(b)\right)\int_{\mathcal{U}_{<}(b,\delta_{b})}\exp\Bigl(z\,\left(p_{<}^{\prime}(b)+\varepsilon\right)(y-b)\Bigr)\,\mathrm{d}\lambda(y).

Substituting

\upsilon=z\,(p_{<}^{\prime}(b)+\varepsilon)(y-b)

and setting

I_{<}(b,z,\varepsilon):=\int_{\mathcal{U}_{<}(b,z,\varepsilon)}\exp\left(\upsilon\right)\,\mathrm{d}\lambda(\upsilon)

yields (
6.17
).

To define the constants
c_{y_{\mathrm{c}}}
for
y_{\mathrm{c}}\in\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c},\,\bullet}^{\max}
in the denominator of the fraction on the left of (
6.12
),
we first set
I_{<}(a,z,\varepsilon):=0=:I_{>}(b,z,\varepsilon)
,
p_{<}^{(\alpha_{y_{\mathrm{c}}})}(a):=p_{>}^{(\alpha_{y_{\mathrm{c}}})}(a)
, and
p_{>}^{(\alpha_{y_{\mathrm{c}}})}(b):=p_{<}^{(\alpha_{y_{\mathrm{c}}})}(b)

for the non-existent polynomials in cases
(ii)
-
(v)
.
Furthermore, set
I_{<}(y_{\mathrm{c}}):=\lim_{z\rightarrow\infty}I_{<}(y_{\mathrm{c}},z,\varepsilon)

and

I_{>}(y_{\mathrm{c}}):=\lim_{z\rightarrow\infty}I_{>}(y_{\mathrm{c}},z,\varepsilon)
.
Then,

\displaystyle I_{>}(y_{\mathrm{c}})

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\int_{0}^{\infty}\exp(-\upsilon^{\alpha_{y_{\mathrm{c}}}})\,\mathrm{d}\lambda(\upsilon)=\frac{1}{\alpha_{y_{\mathrm{c}}}}\,\Gamma(\frac{1}{\alpha_{y_{\mathrm{c}}}})&\hskip 110.96556pt\text{\ref{P2a:isolated_maximum_interior},\penalty\ \ref{P2a:isolated_maximum_a_even} or\penalty\ \ref{P2a:isolated_maximum_a_1}}\\
0&\hskip 110.96556pt\text{\ref{P2a:isolated_maximum_b_even} or\penalty\ \ref{P2a:isolated_maximum_b_1}}\end{cases}

\displaystyle I_{<}(y_{\mathrm{c}})

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\int_{-\infty}^{0}\exp(-\upsilon^{\alpha_{y_{\mathrm{c}}}})\,\mathrm{d}\lambda(\upsilon)=\int_{0}^{\infty}\exp(-\upsilon^{\alpha_{y_{\mathrm{c}}}})\,\mathrm{d}\lambda(\upsilon)=\frac{1}{\alpha_{y_{\mathrm{c}}}}\,\Gamma(\frac{1}{\alpha_{y_{\mathrm{c}}}})&\text{\ref{P2a:isolated_maximum_interior} or \penalty\ \ref{P2a:isolated_maximum_b_even}}\\
0&\text{\ref{P2a:isolated_maximum_a_even} or\penalty\ \ref{P2a:isolated_maximum_a_1}}\,,\\
\int_{-\infty}^{0}\exp(\upsilon)\,\mathrm{d}\lambda(\upsilon)=1&\text{\ref{P2a:isolated_maximum_b_1}}\end{cases}

with
\Gamma(x)
denoting the Gamma function at
x>0
, where we used
Gradshteyn & Ryzhik 2007a
and that
\alpha_{y_{\mathrm{c}}}
is even.
7
7

7

Note that
\Gamma(1)=1
, i.e.,
I_{>}(y_{\mathrm{c}})=I_{>}(a)=1
in case
(iii)
.

Then, for any
y_{\mathrm{c}}\in\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c},\,\bullet}^{\max}
, both limits
I_{>}(y_{\mathrm{c}})
and
I_{<}(y_{\mathrm{c}})
are nonnegative and finite with at least one limit being positive.
Thus,

c_{y_{\mathrm{c}}}:=\frac{I_{>}(y_{\mathrm{c}})}{[\frac{1}{\alpha_{y_{\mathrm{c}}}!}\,|p_{>}^{(\alpha_{y_{\mathrm{c}}})}(y_{\mathrm{c}})|]^{\alpha_{y_{\mathrm{c}}}^{-1}}}+\frac{I_{<}(y_{\mathrm{c}})}{[\frac{1}{\alpha_{y_{\mathrm{c}}}!}\,|p_{<}^{(\alpha_{y_{\mathrm{c}}})}(y_{\mathrm{c}})|]^{\alpha_{y_{\mathrm{c}}}^{-1}}}

is a positive constant for any
y_{\mathrm{c}}\in\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c},\,\bullet}^{\max}
.

Note that if
p_{>}=p_{<}
(i.e., if
y_{\mathrm{c}}\in(a,b)
is not a breackpoint of the piecewise polynomial
s_{\mathrm{c}}
) and if
\alpha_{y_{\mathrm{c}}}<\kappa
, both summands in the definition of
