are equal.

To finally bound the numerator of the fraction on the left of (
6.12
) from below, set

\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c}}^{\max}(\varepsilon):=\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c},\,-}^{\max}(\varepsilon)\cup\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c},\,\bullet}^{\max}(\varepsilon)
,
where

\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c},\,\bullet}^{\max}(\varepsilon):=\bigcup_{y_{\mathrm{c}}\in\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c},\,\bullet}^{\max}}\left(\mathcal{U}_{<}(y_{\mathrm{c}},\delta_{y_{\mathrm{c}}})\cup\mathcal{U}_{>}(y_{\mathrm{c}},\delta_{y_{\mathrm{c}}})\right)
.
Then,

\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{Y}}\exp[z\,s]\,\mathrm{d}\mu

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{d}}}\exp[z\,s]\,\mathrm{d}\delta+\int_{\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c}}}\exp[z\,s]\,\mathrm{d}\lambda

\displaystyle=\sum_{d=1}^{D}w_{d}\exp[z\,s(t_{d})]+\int_{(\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c}}^{\max}(\varepsilon))^{\complement}}\exp[z\,s]\,\mathrm{d}\lambda

\displaystyle\hskip 11.38092pt+\int_{\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c},\,-}^{\max}(\varepsilon)}\exp[z\,s]\,\mathrm{d}\lambda+\int_{\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c},\,\bullet}^{\max}(\varepsilon)}\exp[z\,s]\,\mathrm{d}\lambda

\displaystyle\geq\exp[z\,s^{\max}]\,\biggl(\sum_{d\in\mathcal{D}^{\max}}w_{d}+\lambda(\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c},\,-}^{\max})

\displaystyle\hskip 11.38092pt+\sum_{y_{\mathrm{c}}\in\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c},\,\bullet}^{\max}}\Bigl(\frac{z^{-\alpha_{y_{\mathrm{c}}}^{-1}}\,I_{<}(y_{\mathrm{c}},z,\varepsilon)}{[\frac{1}{\alpha_{y_{\mathrm{c}}}!}\,(|p_{<}^{(\alpha_{y_{\mathrm{c}}})}(y_{\mathrm{c}})|+\varepsilon)]^{\alpha_{y_{\mathrm{c}}}^{-1}}}+\frac{z^{-\alpha_{y_{\mathrm{c}}}^{-1}}\,I_{>}(y_{\mathrm{c}},z,\varepsilon)}{[\frac{1}{\alpha_{y_{\mathrm{c}}}!}\,(|p_{>}^{(\alpha_{y_{\mathrm{c}}})}(y_{\mathrm{c}})|+\varepsilon)]^{\alpha_{y_{\mathrm{c}}}^{-1}}}\Bigr)\biggr),

where we used (
6.13
) and (
6.17
) to obtain the last inequality.
Dividing the inequality by

\exp(z\,s^{\max})\,[\,\sum_{d\in\mathcal{D}^{\max}}w_{d}+\lambda(\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c},\,-}^{\max})+\sum_{y\in\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c},\,\bullet}^{\max}}c_{y}\,z^{-\alpha_{y}^{-1}}]
,
which is nonzero as at least one of the sets
\mathcal{D}^{\max}
,
\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c},\,-}^{\max}
, and
\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c},\,\bullet}^{\max}
is nonempty and thus at least one of the summands in the second factor is positive (recalling
c_{y}>0
for any
y\in\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c},\,\bullet}^{\max}
),
yields

\displaystyle\penalty\ \frac{\int_{\mathcal{Y}}\exp[z\,s]\,\mathrm{d}\mu}{\exp[z\,s^{\max}]\,\bigl(\sum_{d\in\mathcal{D}^{\max}}w_{d}+\lambda(\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c},\,-}^{\max})+\sum_{y\in\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c},\,\bullet}^{\max}}c_{y}\,z^{-\alpha_{y}^{-1}}\bigr)}

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\penalty\ \frac{\sum_{d\in\mathcal{D}^{\max}}w_{d}+\lambda(\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c},\,-}^{\max})+\sum_{y_{\mathrm{c}}\in\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c},\,\bullet}^{\max}}\Bigl(\frac{z^{-\alpha_{y_{\mathrm{c}}}^{-1}}\,I_{<}(y_{\mathrm{c}},z,\varepsilon)}{[\frac{1}{\alpha_{y_{\mathrm{c}}}!}\,(|p_{<}^{(\alpha_{y_{\mathrm{c}}})}(y_{\mathrm{c}})|+\varepsilon)]^{\alpha_{y_{\mathrm{c}}}^{-1}}}+\frac{z^{-\alpha_{y_{\mathrm{c}}}^{-1}}\,I_{>}(y_{\mathrm{c}},z,\varepsilon)}{[\frac{1}{\alpha_{y_{\mathrm{c}}}!}\,(|p_{>}^{(\alpha_{y_{\mathrm{c}}})}(y_{\mathrm{c}})|+\varepsilon)]^{\alpha_{y_{\mathrm{c}}}^{-1}}}\Bigr)}{\sum_{d\in\mathcal{D}^{\max}}w_{d}+\lambda(\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c},\,-}^{\max})+\sum_{y_{\mathrm{c}}\in\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c},\,\bullet}^{\max}}\Bigl(\frac{z^{-\alpha_{y}^{-1}}\,I_{<}(y_{\mathrm{c}})}{[\frac{1}{\alpha_{y_{\mathrm{c}}}!}\,|p_{<}^{(\alpha_{y_{\mathrm{c}}})}(y_{\mathrm{c}})|]^{\alpha_{y_{\mathrm{c}}}^{-1}}}+\frac{z^{-\alpha_{y}^{-1}}\,I_{>}(y_{\mathrm{c}})}{[\frac{1}{\alpha_{y_{\mathrm{c}}}!}\,|p_{>}^{(\alpha_{y_{\mathrm{c}}})}(y_{\mathrm{c}})|]^{\alpha_{y_{\mathrm{c}}}^{-1}}}\Bigr)}\,.

Consider the limit for
z\rightarrow\infty
.
If
\mathcal{D}^{\max}\neq\emptyset
or
\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c},\,-}^{\max}\neq\emptyset
, the limit in the second line can be computed separately in the numerator and the denominator with the last terms converging to zero, respectively, and thus numerator and denominator are equal.
Otherwise, we multiply numerator and denominator (then both consisting of only their last term) by
z^{\alpha^{-1}}
for
\alpha=\max_{y_{\mathrm{c}}\in\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c},\,\bullet}^{\max}}\alpha_{y_{\mathrm{c}}}
(which exists as
|\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c},\,\bullet}^{\max}|<\infty
) and again can compute the limit separately for the numerator and the denominator.
Then, all summands with
\alpha_{y_{\mathrm{c}}}<\alpha
converge to zero and the remaining terms in the numerator only include
z
in
I_{\lessgtr}(y_{\mathrm{c}},z,\varepsilon)
, which converges to
I_{\lessgtr}(y_{\mathrm{c}})
for
z\rightarrow\infty
.
Then, the numerator converges to the denominator for
\varepsilon\rightarrow 0
.
Thus, in all cases, we have

\lim_{z\rightarrow\infty}\frac{\int_{\mathcal{Y}}\exp[z\,s]\,\mathrm{d}\mu}{\exp[z\,s^{\max}]\,[\,\sum_{d\in\mathcal{D}^{\max}}w_{d}+\lambda(\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c},\,-}^{\max})+\sum_{y\in\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c},\,\bullet}^{\max}}c_{y}\,z^{-\alpha_{y}^{-1}}]}\geq 1.

Now, we show that the limit is at most
1
, proceeding similarly.
Let
\varepsilon>0
be small enough such that

\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c},\,-}^{\max}(\varepsilon)\cap\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c},\,\bullet}^{\max}=\emptyset
,

p_{\lessgtr}^{(\alpha_{y_{\mathrm{c}}})}(y_{\mathrm{c}})+\varepsilon<0
for all
y_{\mathrm{c}}\in\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c},\,\bullet}^{\max}
with
p_{\lessgtr}^{(\alpha_{y_{\mathrm{c}}})}(y_{\mathrm{c}})<0
(cases
(i)
-
(iv)
),
and
p_{<}^{\prime}(b)-\varepsilon>0
if
b\in\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c},\,\bullet}^{\max}
with
p_{<}^{\prime}(b)>0
(case
(v)
).
Note that such an
\varepsilon
exists as
|\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c},\,\bullet}^{\max}|<\infty
and recall for
y_{\mathrm{c}}\in\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c},\,\bullet}^{\max}
that if
y_{\mathrm{c}}=a
, we only consider the objects with subscript “
>
”, if
y_{\mathrm{c}}=b
, we only consider the objects with subscript “
<
”.
We have

\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c},\,-}^{\max}(\varepsilon)}\exp[z\,s_{\mathrm{c}}]\,\mathrm{d}\lambda\leq\exp[z\,s^{\max}]\,\bigl(\lambda(\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c},\,-}^{\max})+2\,M\,\varepsilon\bigr).

(6.18)

Furthermore, for any
y_{\mathrm{c}}\in\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c},\,\bullet}^{\max}
, as in the proof of the lower bound above,
there exists
\delta_{y_{\mathrm{c}}}>0
such that
the inequalities (
6.14
) hold
for all
\tilde{y}\in\mathcal{U}_{\lessgtr}(y_{\mathrm{c}},\delta_{y_{\mathrm{c}}})
,

\mathcal{U}_{\lessgtr}(y_{\mathrm{c}},\delta_{y_{\mathrm{c}}})\subset\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c}}
,

\mathcal{U}_{\lessgtr}(y_{\mathrm{c}},\delta_{y_{\mathrm{c}}})\cap\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c},\,-}^{\max}(\varepsilon)=\emptyset
,
and
\delta_{y_{\mathrm{c}}}\leq\max\{\varepsilon_{<}(y_{\mathrm{c}}),\varepsilon_{>}(y_{\mathrm{c}})\}
. Consider
y\in\mathcal{U}_{\lessgtr}(y_{\mathrm{c}},\delta_{y_{\mathrm{c}}})
and recall that
(y-y_{\mathrm{c}})^{\alpha_{y_{\mathrm{c}}}}\geq 0
in cases
(i)
-
(iv)
and
(y-y_{\mathrm{c}})^{\alpha_{y_{\mathrm{c}}}}=y-b\leq 0
in case
(v)
.
Thus, inserting the right inequality of (
6.14
) into equality (
6.15
) in cases
(i)
-
(iv)

and inserting the left inequality of (
6.14
) into (
6.15
) in case
(v)
,
we obtain

\displaystyle p_{\lessgtr}(y)

\displaystyle\leq p_{\lessgtr}(y_{\mathrm{c}})+\frac{1}{\alpha_{y_{\mathrm{c}}}!}(p_{\lessgtr}^{(\alpha_{y_{\mathrm{c}}})}(y_{\mathrm{c}})+\varepsilon)(y-y_{\mathrm{c}})^{\alpha_{y_{\mathrm{c}}}}

\displaystyle\text{in cases\penalty\ \ref{P2a:isolated_maximum_interior}-\ref{P2a:isolated_maximum_b_even}},

\displaystyle p_{<}(y)

\displaystyle\leq p_{<}(b)+(p_{<}^{\prime}(b)-\varepsilon)(y-b)

\displaystyle\text{in case\penalty\ \ref{P2a:isolated_maximum_b_1}}.

Thus,

\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{U}_{\lessgtr}(y_{\mathrm{c}},\delta_{y_{\mathrm{c}}})}\exp[z\,s_{\mathrm{c}}]\,\mathrm{d}\lambda=\int_{\mathcal{U}_{\lessgtr}(y_{\mathrm{c}},\delta_{y_{\mathrm{c}}})}\exp[z\,p_{\lessgtr}]\,\mathrm{d}\lambda

\displaystyle\leq\penalty\

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\exp[z\,p_{\lessgtr}(y_{\mathrm{c}})]\,\int_{\mathcal{U}_{\lessgtr}(y_{\mathrm{c}},\delta_{y_{\mathrm{c}}})}\exp\bigl(z\,\frac{1}{\alpha_{y_{\mathrm{c}}}!}\left(p_{\lessgtr}^{(\alpha_{y_{\mathrm{c}}})}(y_{\mathrm{c}})+\varepsilon\right)(y-y_{\mathrm{c}})^{\alpha_{y_{\mathrm{c}}}}\bigr)\,\mathrm{d}\lambda(y)&\text{\ref{P2a:isolated_maximum_interior}-\ref{P2a:isolated_maximum_b_even}},\\[5.69046pt]
\exp[z\,p_{<}(b)]\,\int_{\mathcal{U}_{<}(b,\delta_{b})}\exp\bigl(z\,\left(p_{<}^{\prime}(b)-\varepsilon\right)(y-b)\bigr)\,\mathrm{d}\lambda(y)&\text{\ref{P2a:isolated_maximum_b_1}}\end{cases}

\displaystyle=\penalty\

\displaystyle\frac{\exp\left(z\,s^{\max}\right)}{\left(z\,\frac{1}{\alpha_{y_{\mathrm{c}}}!}\,(|p_{\lessgtr}^{(\alpha_{y_{\mathrm{c}}})}(y_{\mathrm{c}})|-\varepsilon)\right)^{\alpha_{y_{\mathrm{c}}}^{-1}}}\,I_{\lessgtr}(y_{\mathrm{c}},z,\varepsilon)\,,

where we substitute as in the proof of the lower bound, but changing the sign before
\varepsilon
from
+
to
-
to obtain the last equality.
Note that this also changes the sign before
\varepsilon
from
+
to
-
in the definition of the sets
\mathcal{U}_{\lessgtr}(y_{\mathrm{c}},z,\varepsilon)
, on which the integrals
I_{\lessgtr}(y_{\mathrm{c}},z,\varepsilon)
are defined.
Since
\mathcal{U}_{<}(y_{\mathrm{c}},z,\varepsilon)\subset(-\infty,0]
and
\mathcal{U}_{>}(y_{\mathrm{c}},z,\varepsilon)\subset[0,\infty)
for all
y_{\mathrm{c}}\in\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c},\,\bullet}^{\max}
, all
z>0
, and all
\varepsilon>0
, we have
I_{\lessgtr}(y_{\mathrm{c}},z,\varepsilon)\leq I_{\lessgtr}(y_{\mathrm{c}})
for all
z>0
and all
\varepsilon>0
and thus

\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{U}_{\lessgtr}(y_{\mathrm{c}},\delta_{y_{\mathrm{c}}})}\exp[z\,s_{\mathrm{c}}]\,\mathrm{d}\lambda\leq\frac{\exp\left(z\,s^{\max}\right)\,I_{\lessgtr}(y_{\mathrm{c}})\,z^{-\alpha_{y_{\mathrm{c}}}^{-1}}}{\left(\frac{1}{\alpha_{y_{\mathrm{c}}}!}\,(|p_{\lessgtr}^{(\alpha_{y_{\mathrm{c}}})}(y_{\mathrm{c}})|-\varepsilon\right)^{\alpha_{y_{\mathrm{c}}}^{-1}}}\,.

(6.19)

Per construction, there exists a
\xi>0
such that
s_{\mathrm{c}}(y)<s^{\max}-\xi
for all
y\in\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c}}^{\max}(\varepsilon)^{\complement}
and
s(t_{d})<s^{\max}-\xi
for all
d\in(\mathcal{D}^{\max})^{\complement}
.
Then, by (
6.18
) and (
6.19
), we have

\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{Y}}\exp[z\,s]\,\mathrm{d}\mu=\int_{\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{d}}}\exp[z\,s]\,\mathrm{d}\delta+\int_{\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c}}}\exp[z\,s]\,\mathrm{d}\lambda

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\penalty\ \sum_{d\in\mathcal{D}^{\max}}w_{d}\exp[z\,s(t_{d})]+\int_{\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c},\,-}^{\max}(\varepsilon)}\exp[z\,s]\,\mathrm{d}\lambda+\int_{\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c},\,\bullet}^{\max}(\varepsilon)}\exp[z\,s]\,\mathrm{d}\lambda

\displaystyle\penalty\ +\sum_{d\in(\mathcal{D}^{\max})^{\complement}}w_{d}\exp[z\,s(t_{d})]+\int_{(\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c}}^{\max}(\varepsilon))^{\complement}}\exp[z\,s]\,\mathrm{d}\lambda

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\penalty\ \exp[z\,s^{\max}]\,\biggl(\sum_{d\in\mathcal{D}^{\max}}w_{d}+\lambda(\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c},\,-}^{\max})+2\,M\,\varepsilon

\displaystyle\hskip 62.59596pt\penalty\ +\sum_{y_{\mathrm{c}}\in\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c},\,\bullet}^{\max}}\Bigl(\frac{z^{-\alpha_{y_{\mathrm{c}}}^{-1}}\,I_{<}(y_{\mathrm{c}})}{[\frac{1}{\alpha_{y_{\mathrm{c}}}!}\,(|p_{<}^{(\alpha_{y_{\mathrm{c}}})}(y_{\mathrm{c}})|-\varepsilon)]^{\alpha_{y_{\mathrm{c}}}^{-1}}}+\frac{z^{-\alpha_{y_{\mathrm{c}}}^{-1}}\,I_{>}(y_{\mathrm{c}})}{[\frac{1}{\alpha_{y_{\mathrm{c}}}!}\,(|p_{<}^{(\alpha_{y_{\mathrm{c}}})}(y_{\mathrm{c}})|-\varepsilon)]^{\alpha_{y_{\mathrm{c}}}^{-1}}}\Bigr)

\displaystyle\hskip 62.59596pt\penalty\ +\lambda\left((\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c}}^{\max}(\varepsilon))^{\complement}\right)\,\exp[-z\,\xi]+\sum_{d\in(\mathcal{D}^{\max})^{\complement}}w_{d}\exp[-z\,\xi]\biggr).

Dividing the inequality by

\exp(z\,s^{\max})\,[\,\sum_{d\in\mathcal{D}^{\max}}w_{d}+\lambda(\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c},\,-}^{\max})+\sum_{y\in\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c},\,\bullet}^{\max}}c_{y}\,z^{-\alpha_{y}^{-1}}]

yields

\displaystyle\frac{\int_{\mathcal{Y}}\exp[z\,s]\,\mathrm{d}\mu}{\exp[z\,s^{\max}]\,\bigl(\sum_{d\in\mathcal{D}^{\max}}w_{d}+\lambda(\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c},\,-}^{\max})+\sum_{y\in\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c},\,\bullet}^{\max}}c_{y}\,z^{-\alpha_{y}^{-1}}\bigr)}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\penalty\ \frac{\sum_{d\in\mathcal{D}^{\max}}w_{d}+\lambda(\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c},\,-}^{\max})+2\,M\,\varepsilon+\sum_{y_{\mathrm{c}}\in\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c},\,\bullet}^{\max}}\Biggl(\frac{z^{-\alpha_{y_{\mathrm{c}}}^{-1}}\,I_{<}(y_{\mathrm{c}})}{\bigl[\frac{|p_{<}^{(\alpha_{y_{\mathrm{c}}})}(y_{\mathrm{c}})|-\varepsilon}{\alpha_{y_{\mathrm{c}}}!}\bigr]^{\alpha_{y_{\mathrm{c}}}^{-1}}}+\frac{z^{-\alpha_{y_{\mathrm{c}}}^{-1}}\,I_{>}(y_{\mathrm{c}})}{\bigl[\frac{|p_{>}^{(\alpha_{y_{\mathrm{c}}})}(y_{\mathrm{c}})|-\varepsilon}{\alpha_{y_{\mathrm{c}}}!}\bigr]^{\alpha_{y_{\mathrm{c}}}^{-1}}}\Biggr)}{\sum_{d\in\mathcal{D}^{\max}}w_{d}+\lambda(\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c},\,-}^{\max})+\sum_{y_{\mathrm{c}}\in\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c},\,\bullet}^{\max}}\Biggl(\frac{z^{-\alpha_{y}^{-1}}\,I_{<}(y_{\mathrm{c}})}{\bigl[\frac{|p_{<}^{(\alpha_{y_{\mathrm{c}}})}(y_{\mathrm{c}})|}{\alpha_{y_{\mathrm{c}}}!}\bigr]^{\alpha_{y_{\mathrm{c}}}^{-1}}}+\frac{z^{-\alpha_{y}^{-1}}\,I_{>}(y_{\mathrm{c}})}{\bigl[\frac{|p_{>}^{(\alpha_{y_{\mathrm{c}}})}(y_{\mathrm{c}})|}{\alpha_{y_{\mathrm{c}}}!}\bigr]^{\alpha_{y_{\mathrm{c}}}^{-1}}}\Biggr)}

\displaystyle\penalty\ +\frac{\lambda\left((\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c}}^{\max}(\varepsilon))^{\mathrm{c}}\right)\,\exp[-z\,\xi]+\sum_{d\in(\mathcal{D}^{\max})^{\complement}}w_{d}\exp[-z\,\xi]}{\sum_{d\in\mathcal{D}^{\max}}w_{d}+\lambda(\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c},\,-}^{\max})+\sum_{y\in\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c},\,\bullet}^{\max}}c_{y}\,z^{-\alpha_{y}^{-1}}}.

Consider the limit for
z\rightarrow\infty
.
The limit of the fraction in the last line is zero, which can be seen by expanding the fraction by
\exp[z\,\xi]
and using
\lim_{z\rightarrow\infty}\exp[z\,\xi]\,z^{-\alpha_{y_{\mathrm{c}}}^{-1}}=\infty
for all
\alpha_{y_{\mathrm{c}}}\in\mathbb{N}
as
\xi>0
.
If
\mathcal{D}^{\max}\neq\emptyset
or
\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c},\,-}^{\max}\neq\emptyset
, the limit of the fraction in the second line can be computed separately in the numerator and the denominator with the last terms converging to zero, respectively.
Otherwise, expand the fraction (where numerator/denominator then only consist of the last term) by
z^{\alpha^{-1}}
where
\alpha=\max_{y_{\mathrm{c}}\in\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c},\,\bullet}^{\max}}\alpha_{y_{\mathrm{c}}}
.
Finally,

\lim_{z\rightarrow\infty}\frac{\int_{\mathcal{Y}}\exp[z\,s]\,\mathrm{d}\mu}{\exp[z\,s^{\max}]\,[\,\sum_{d\in\mathcal{D}^{\max}}w_{d}+\lambda(\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c},\,-}^{\max})+\sum_{y\in\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c},\,\bullet}^{\max}}c_{y}\,z^{-\alpha_{y}^{-1}}]}\leq 1
is obtained by considering
\varepsilon\rightarrow 0
.
∎

Theorem 6.9
.

Under assumption
(S

<

)
,

\lim_{\|\boldsymbol{\theta}\|_{2}\rightarrow\infty}\ell_{\pen}(\boldsymbol{\theta})=-\infty
.

Proof.

We first show the result for the unpenalized log-likelihood, i.e.,

\lim_{\|\boldsymbol{\theta}\|_{2}\rightarrow\infty}\ell(\boldsymbol{\theta})

=-\infty
.
Let
i\in\{1,\ldots,N\}
.
For
\boldsymbol{\theta}\in\mathbb{R}^{K}\setminus\{\mathbf{0}\}
, let
z:=\|\boldsymbol{\theta}\|_{2}>0
and
\mathbf{v}:=\frac{\boldsymbol{\theta}}{\|\boldsymbol{\theta}\|_{2}}\in\mathbb{S}^{K-1}
, i.e.,
\boldsymbol{\theta}=z\,\mathbf{v}
.
As we later apply Theorem
6.8
to
s_{\mathbf{v},\mathbf{x}_{i}}
, we briefly show that the assumptions are fulfilled, i.e., that
s_{\mathbf{v},\mathbf{x}_{i},\mathrm{c}}
is a
(\kappa-1)
times continuously differentiable piecewise polynomial consisting of finitely many polynomials of degree at most
\kappa
for some
\kappa\geq 2
.
In the continuous special case, this is straightforward (using property
(SPP)
),
since for every
\mathbf{v}\in\mathbb{S}^{K-1}
and every
\mathbf{x}\in\mathcal{X}
, we have
s_{\mathbf{v},\mathbf{x},\mathrm{c}}\in\spano(\tilde{\mathbf{b}}_{\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c}}})
. In the mixed case, recalling the construction of the basis functions via orthogonal decomposition of
B^{2}(\mu)
from appropriate bases for the continuous and discrete special cases via the embeddings (
6.1
) and (
6.2
), we have

\tilde{\mathbf{b}}_{\mathcal{Y}}=(\tilde{\iota}_{\mathrm{c}}(\tilde{b}_{\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c}},\,1}),\ldots,\tilde{\iota}_{\mathrm{c}}(\tilde{b}_{\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c}},\,K_{\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c}}}}),\tilde{\iota}_{\mathrm{d}}(\tilde{b}_{\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{d}}^{\bullet},\,1}),\ldots,\tilde{\iota}_{\mathrm{d}}(\tilde{b}_{\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{d}}^{\bullet},\,K_{\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{d}}^{\bullet}}}))^{\top}
, where
\tilde{\iota}_{\mathrm{c}}(\tilde{b}_{\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c}},\,m})
is a piecewise polynomial with the desired properties on
\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c}}\setminus\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{d}}
for all
m=1,\ldots,K_{\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c}}}
and
\tilde{\iota}_{\mathrm{d}}(\tilde{b}_{\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{d}},\,m})
is constant on
\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c}}\setminus\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{d}}
for all
m=1,\ldots,K_{\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{d}}^{\bullet}}
.
Thus, for all
\mathbf{v}\in\mathbb{S}^{K-1}
and all
\mathbf{x}\in\mathcal{X}
, as
s_{\mathbf{v},\mathbf{x}}\in\spano(\tilde{\mathbf{b}}_{\mathcal{Y}})
, the function
s_{\mathbf{v},\mathbf{x},\mathrm{c}}
is a

(\kappa-1)
times continuously differentiable piecewise polynomial consisting of finitely many polynomials of degree at most
\kappa
for some
\kappa\geq 2
.

Thus, let
s_{\mathbf{v},\mathbf{x}_{i}}^{\max}:=\sup_{y\in\mathcal{Y}}s_{\mathbf{v},\mathbf{x}_{i}}(y)
and let
\mathcal{D}^{\max}(s_{\mathbf{v},\mathbf{x}_{i}})
and
\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c}}^{\max}(s_{\mathbf{v},\mathbf{x}_{i}})=\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c},\,-}^{\max}(s_{\mathbf{v},\mathbf{x}_{i}})\sqcup\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c},\,\bullet}^{\max}(s_{\mathbf{v},\mathbf{x}_{i}})
be the sets as in Theorem
6.8
.
Furthermore, for
y\in\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c},\,\bullet}^{\max}(s_{\mathbf{v},\mathbf{x}_{i}})
let
c_{\mathbf{v},\mathbf{x}_{i},y}>0
and
\alpha_{\mathbf{v},\mathbf{x}_{i},y}\in\mathbb{N}
with
\alpha_{\mathbf{v},\mathbf{x}_{i},y}\leq\kappa
such that (
6.12
) holds.
Then, setting

\ell_{i}(\boldsymbol{\theta}):=\tilde{\mathbf{b}}(\mathbf{x}_{i})(y_{i})^{\top}\boldsymbol{\theta}-\log\int_{\mathcal{Y}}\exp\bigl[\tilde{\mathbf{b}}(\mathbf{x}_{i})^{\top}\boldsymbol{\theta}\bigr]\,\mathrm{d}\mu
,
we have

\ell(\boldsymbol{\theta})=\sum_{i=1}^{N}\ell_{i}(\boldsymbol{\theta})

with
\ell_{i}(\boldsymbol{\theta})
for
i\in\{1,\ldots,N\}
equal to

\displaystyle\penalty\ z\,s_{\mathbf{v},\mathbf{x}_{i}}(y_{i})

\displaystyle-\log\frac{\int_{\mathcal{Y}}\exp[z\,s_{\mathbf{v},\mathbf{x}_{i}}]\,\mathrm{d}\mu}{\exp[z\,s_{\mathbf{v},\mathbf{x}_{i}}^{\max}]\Bigl(\sum_{d\in\mathcal{D}^{\max}(s_{\mathbf{v},\mathbf{x}_{i}})}w_{d}+\lambda\bigl(\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c},\,-}^{\max}(s_{\mathbf{v},\mathbf{x}_{i}})\bigr)+\sum_{y\in\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c},\,\bullet}^{\max}(s_{\mathbf{v},\mathbf{x}_{i}})}c_{\mathbf{v},\mathbf{x}_{i},y}\,z^{-\alpha_{\mathbf{v},\mathbf{x}_{i},y}^{-1}}\Bigr)}

\displaystyle-z\,s_{\mathbf{v},\mathbf{x}_{i}}^{\max}-\log\Bigl(\sum_{d\in\mathcal{D}^{\max}(s_{\mathbf{v},\mathbf{x}_{i}})}w_{d}+\lambda\bigl(\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c},\,-}^{\max}(s_{\mathbf{v},\mathbf{x}_{i}})\bigr)+\sum_{y\in\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c},\,\bullet}^{\max}(s_{\mathbf{v},\mathbf{x}_{i}})}c_{\mathbf{v},\mathbf{x}_{i},y}\,z^{-\alpha_{\mathbf{v},\mathbf{x}_{i},y}^{-1}}\Bigr)\,.

By
Theorem
6.8

and setting

C_{\mathbf{v},\mathbf{x}_{i}}:=\sum_{d\in\mathcal{D}^{\max}(s_{\mathbf{v},\mathbf{x}_{i}})}w_{d}+\lambda(\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c},\,-}^{\max}(s_{\mathbf{v},\mathbf{x}_{i}}))
and

c_{\mathbf{v}}^{\min}:=\min\bigl\{c_{\mathbf{v},\mathbf{x}_{i},y}\penalty\ |\penalty\ y\in\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c},\,\bullet}^{\max}(s_{\mathbf{v},\mathbf{x}_{i}}),i\in\{1,\ldots,N\}\bigr\}
,
we obtain

\displaystyle\lim_{\|\boldsymbol{\theta}\|_{2}\rightarrow\infty}\ell(\boldsymbol{\theta})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\penalty\ \lim_{z\rightarrow\infty}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\Bigl(z\,[s_{\mathbf{v},\mathbf{x}_{i}}(y_{i})-s_{\mathbf{v},\mathbf{x}_{i}}^{\max}]-\log\bigl(C_{\mathbf{v},\mathbf{x}_{i}}+\sum_{y\in\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c},\,\bullet}^{\max}(s_{\mathbf{v},\mathbf{x}_{i}})}c_{\mathbf{v},\mathbf{x}_{i},y}\,z^{-\alpha_{\mathbf{v},\mathbf{x}_{i},y}^{-1}}\bigr)\Bigr)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\penalty\ \lim_{z\rightarrow\infty}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\Bigl(z\,[s_{\mathbf{v},\mathbf{x}_{i}}(y_{i})-s_{\mathbf{v},\mathbf{x}_{i}}^{\max}]-\log\bigl(|\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c},\,\bullet}^{\max}(s_{\mathbf{v},\mathbf{x}_{i}})|\,c_{\mathbf{v}}^{\min}\,z^{-1}\bigr)\Bigr)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\penalty\ \lim_{z\rightarrow\infty}\biggl(\sum_{i=1}^{N}\bigl(z\,[s_{\mathbf{v},\mathbf{x}_{i}}(y_{i})-s_{\mathbf{v},\mathbf{x}_{i}}^{\max}]\bigr)+N\,\log z\biggr)-\sum_{i=1}^{N}\log\bigl(|\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c},\,\bullet}^{\max}(s_{\mathbf{v},\mathbf{x}_{i}})|\,c_{\mathbf{v}}^{\min}\bigr).

We have

s_{\mathbf{v},\mathbf{x}_{i}}(y_{i})-s_{\mathbf{v},\mathbf{x}_{i}}^{\max}\leq 0
for all
i\in\{1,\ldots,N\}
.
Per assumption
(S

<

)
, there exists an
i\in\{1,\ldots,N\}
such that

s_{\mathbf{v},\mathbf{x}_{i}}(y_{i})-s_{\mathbf{v},\mathbf{x}_{i}}^{\max}<0
and thus,

\lim_{z\rightarrow\infty}z\,[s_{\mathbf{v},\mathbf{x}_{i}}(y_{i})-s_{\mathbf{v},\mathbf{x}_{i}}^{\max}]=-\infty
.
Since this linear term dominates the logarithmic term
N\,\log z
(which tends towards
+\infty
) for
z\rightarrow\infty
, we obtain

\lim_{\|\boldsymbol{\theta}\|_{2}\rightarrow\infty}\ell(\boldsymbol{\theta})=-\infty
.
As the matrices
\mathbf{P}_{j}
are positive semi-definite for all
j\in\{1,\ldots,J\}
, also
\mathbf{P}
is positive semi-definite and thus
\lim_{\|\boldsymbol{\theta}\|_{2}\rightarrow\infty}\ell_{\pen}(\boldsymbol{\theta})=\lim_{\|\boldsymbol{\theta}\|_{2}\rightarrow\infty}\ell(\boldsymbol{\theta})-\lim_{\|\boldsymbol{\theta}\|_{2}\rightarrow\infty}\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\top}\mathbf{P}\boldsymbol{\theta}=-\infty
.
∎

Theorem 6.10
.

Under assumption
(S

<

)
, the function
-\ell_{\mathrm{pen}}
is level-bounded, i.e., for every
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}
, the set

\{\boldsymbol{\theta}\in\mathbb{R}^{K}\penalty\ |\penalty\ -\ell_{\pen}(\boldsymbol{\theta})\leq\alpha\}
is bounded.

Proof.

Assume
-\ell_{\mathrm{pen}}
is not level-bounded.
Then, there exists
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}
, such that the set

\{\boldsymbol{\theta}\in\mathbb{R}^{K}\penalty\ |\penalty\ -\ell_{\pen}(\boldsymbol{\theta})\leq\alpha\}
is not bounded.
Thus, there exist

\boldsymbol{\theta}^{(t)}\in\{\boldsymbol{\theta}\in\mathbb{R}^{K}\penalty\ |\penalty\ -\ell_{\pen}(\boldsymbol{\theta})\leq\alpha\},t\in\mathbb{N},
with
\lim_{t\rightarrow\infty}\|\boldsymbol{\theta}^{(t)}\|_{2}=\infty
.
However, we have
\lim_{\|\boldsymbol{\theta}\|_{2}\rightarrow\infty}\ell_{\pen}(\boldsymbol{\theta})=-\infty
by Theorem
6.9
, since assumption
(S

<

)
holds.
Thus,
\lim_{t\rightarrow\infty}-\ell_{\pen}(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{(t)})=\infty
, which is a contradiction to
-\ell_{\pen}(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{(t)})\leq\alpha
for all
t\in\mathbb{N}
.
Thus,
-\ell_{\mathrm{pen}}
is level-bounded and
\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}
exists.
∎

6.2
Proofs of Theorems and Lemmas from the Main Manuscript (and Formulation and Proof of Lemma
6.15
)

Proof of Theorem
2.1
.

We first show uniqueness of the PMLE.
From Proposition
6.6

1)
, we have that the Hessian matrix of
\ell_{\pen}(\boldsymbol{\theta})
is negative definite, which implies that
\ell_{\pen}(\boldsymbol{\theta})
is strictly concave on
\mathbb{R}^{K}
.
Per assumption, there exists a PMLE
\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}
. Now, assume that it is not unique, i.e., there are

\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{1}\neq\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{2}\in\mathbb{R}^{K}
such that

\ell_{\text{pen}}(\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{1})=\ell_{\text{pen}}(\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{2})=\max_{\boldsymbol{\theta}\in\mathbb{R}^{K}}\ell_{\text{pen}}(\boldsymbol{\theta})
.
Due to the strict concavity of
\ell_{\text{pen}}
, we have

\ell_{\text{pen}}((1-\alpha)\,\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{1}+\alpha\,\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{2})>(1-\alpha)\,\ell_{\text{pen}}(\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{1})+\alpha\,\ell_{\text{pen}}(\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{2})=\max_{\boldsymbol{\theta}\in\mathbb{R}^{K}}\ell_{\text{pen}}(\boldsymbol{\theta})

for
\alpha\in(0,1)
, which is a contradiction, i.e., the PMLE is unique, if it exists.

To obtain existence of the PMLE
\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}
(which is also the minimizer of
-\ell_{\mathrm{pen}}
) it suffices to show that
-\ell_{\mathrm{pen}}
is lower semicontinuous, level-bounded and proper
(
Rockafellar & Wets 2009a
, Theorem 1.9)
.
As
-\ell_{\mathrm{pen}}
takes values in
\mathbb{R}
, it is proper.
Furthermore, it is continuous, implying it is lower semicontinuous
(
Rockafellar & Wets 2009a
, Exercise 1.12)
.
Finally, by Theorem
6.10
,
-\ell_{\mathrm{pen}}
is level-bounded and thus
\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}
exists. ∎

Proof of Theorem
2.2

The following auxiliary Lemmas
6.11
,
6.12
, and
6.14
are dedicated to ultimately prove consistency and asymptotic normal distribution of the PMLE
\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}
, i.e., Theorem
2.2
.
Both of these properties are well-known for “regular log-likelihoods”.
However, this term is often not specified further in the literature, especially in our case of non-identically distributed (but independent) observations.
Exceptions are
Hoadley 1971a
;
Philippou & Roussas 1975a
who both discuss concrete conditions in this case for asymptotic normality of the unpenalized MLE
(
Hoadley 1971a
, also for consistency)
, however, without obtaining the result of asymptotic existence.
Furthermore, one condition of
Philippou & Roussas 1975a
is consistency, whose proof already requires some ideas needed to show asymptotic normality as well.
Our proof generalizes the one of
Cramér 1946a
for a scalar parameter in the i.i.d. case for unpenalized estimation to a parameter vector in the i.non-i.d. case for penalized estimation.
This includes replacing the weak Law of Large Numbers (Khintchine’s Theorem) and the (Lindeberg-Lévy) Central Limit Theorem with variants not requiring identically distributed data and including the penalty term, as well as
using a multivariate generalization of the intermediate value theorem.

We denote convergence in probability with
\xrightarrow[N\rightarrow\infty]{\mathbb{P}}
and convergence in distribution with
\xrightarrow[N\rightarrow\infty]{\mathrm{D}}
.
Furthermore, we write
\mathbf{Z}_{N}=\ocal_{\mathbb{P}}(a_{N})
to denote
\frac{\mathbf{Z}_{N}}{a_{N}}\xrightarrow[N\rightarrow\infty]{\mathbb{P}}0
for a sequence of random vectors
(\mathbf{Z}_{N})_{N\in\mathbb{N}}
and a sequence of real numbers
(a_{N})_{N\in\mathbb{N}}
.

Lemma 6.11
.

For any sequence of real numbers
(a_{N})_{N\in\mathbb{N}}
, such that

\displaystyle\xi_{\max}:=\max\{\xi_{\mathcal{X},\,1},\ldots,\xi_{\mathcal{X},\,J},\xi_{\mathcal{Y},\,1},\ldots,\xi_{\mathcal{Y},\,J}\}=\ocal_{\mathbb{P}}(a_{N}),

we have

\frac{1}{a_{N}}\|\mathbf{P}\|_{\mathrm{op}}\xrightarrow[N\rightarrow\infty]{\mathbb{P}}0

and

\frac{1}{a_{N}}\mathbf{P}\boldsymbol{\theta}\xrightarrow[N\rightarrow\infty]{\mathbb{P}}\mathbf{0}
for every
\boldsymbol{\theta}\in\mathbb{R}^{K}
, where
\|\mathbf{A}\|_{\mathrm{op}}:=\sup_{\mathbf{w}\in\mathbb{R}^{K}\setminus\{\mathbf{0}\}}\frac{\|\mathbf{A}\mathbf{w}\|_{2}}{\|\mathbf{w}\|_{2}}
denotes the spectral norm of
\mathbf{A}\in\mathbb{R}^{K\times K}
.

Proof.

Set
P_{\max}:=\max\left\{\|(\mathbf{P}_{\mathcal{X},\,j}\otimes\mathbf{I}_{K_{\mathcal{Y}}})+(\mathbf{I}_{K_{\mathcal{X},\,j}}\otimes\mathbf{P}_{\mathcal{Y}})\|_{\mathrm{op}}\penalty\ |\penalty\ j\in\{1,\ldots,J\}\right\}
.
Since
\mathbf{P}
is block diagonal, we have
\|\mathbf{P}\|_{\mathrm{op}}=\max\{\|\mathbf{P}_{1}\|_{\mathrm{op}},\ldots,\|\mathbf{P}_{J}\|_{\mathrm{op}}\}

(e.g.,
Meyer 2000a
, (5.2.12))
.
Furthermore, for any
j\in\{1,\ldots,J\}
, we have

\|\mathbf{P}_{j}\|_{\mathrm{op}}\leq\xi_{\max}\,P_{\max}
and thus in particular
\|\mathbf{P}\|_{\mathrm{op}}\leq\xi_{\max}\,P_{\max}
.
Hence, for any
\varepsilon>0
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{\boldsymbol{\vartheta}}\left(\Bigl|\frac{1}{a_{N}}\|\mathbf{P}\|_{\mathrm{op}}-0\Bigr|\geq\varepsilon\right)\leq\mathbb{P}_{\boldsymbol{\vartheta}}\left(\frac{1}{a_{N}}\xi_{\max}\geq\frac{\varepsilon}{P_{\max}}\right)\xrightarrow[N\rightarrow\infty]{}0,

and using
\|\mathbf{P}\boldsymbol{\theta}\|_{2}\leq\|\mathbf{P}\|_{\mathrm{op}}\,\|\boldsymbol{\theta}\|_{2}
(by definition of the spectral norm),

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{\boldsymbol{\vartheta}}\left(\Bigl\|\frac{1}{a_{N}}\mathbf{P}\boldsymbol{\theta}-\mathbf{0}\Bigr\|_{2}\geq\varepsilon\right)\leq\mathbb{P}_{\boldsymbol{\vartheta}}\left(\frac{1}{a_{N}}\xi_{\max}\geq\frac{\varepsilon}{P_{\max}\,\|\boldsymbol{\theta}\|_{2}}\right)\xrightarrow[N\rightarrow\infty]{}0,

for any
\boldsymbol{\theta}\in\mathbb{R}^{K}
, i.e.,
\frac{1}{a_{N}}\|\mathbf{P}\|_{\mathrm{op}}\xrightarrow[N\rightarrow\infty]{\mathbb{P}}0

and

\frac{1}{a_{N}}\|\mathbf{P}\boldsymbol{\theta}\|_{2}\xrightarrow[N\rightarrow\infty]{\mathbb{P}}0
.
∎

Lemma 6.12
.

Let
\nabla\varphi:=(\frac{\partial}{\partial\theta_{1}}\varphi(\boldsymbol{\theta}),\ldots,\frac{\partial}{\partial\theta_{K}}\varphi(\boldsymbol{\theta}))^{\top}
denote the gradient of a function
\varphi:\mathbb{R}^{K}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}

and
\mathbf{F}(\boldsymbol{\vartheta})
the Fisher information of
\ell(\boldsymbol{\vartheta})
.

1)

\mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol{\vartheta}}\left(\nabla\log f_{\mathbf{x}_{i},\boldsymbol{\vartheta}}(Y_{i})\right)=\mathbf{0}
for all
i\in\{1,\ldots,N\}
,

2)

\mathrm{Var}_{\boldsymbol{\vartheta}}(\nabla\ell(\boldsymbol{\vartheta}))=\mathrm{Var}_{\boldsymbol{\vartheta}}(\sum_{i=1}^{N}\nabla\log f_{\mathbf{x}_{i},\boldsymbol{\vartheta}}(Y_{i}))=\mathbf{F}(\boldsymbol{\vartheta})
,

3)

\frac{1}{N}\nabla\ell(\boldsymbol{\vartheta})\xrightarrow[N\rightarrow\infty]{\mathbb{P}}\mathbf{0}
,

4)

Assume that

\xi_{\max}=\ocal_{\mathbb{P}}(N)
.
Then,
\frac{1}{N}\nabla\ell_{\mathrm{pen}}(\boldsymbol{\vartheta})\xrightarrow[N\rightarrow\infty]{\mathbb{P}}\mathbf{0}
.

Remark 6.13
.

For independent and identically distributed observations,
3)
– which is also used to show
4)
– would follow from the weak Law of Large Numbers. However, we consider independent, but non-identically distributed data and thus prove the result by showing the variances of the first derivatives of the log-likelihood contribution are bounded and applying Chebyshev’s inequality.

Proof of Lemma
6.12
.

1)

Let
i\in\{1,\ldots,N\}
.
Provided that

\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{Y}}\nabla f_{\mathbf{x}_{i},\boldsymbol{\vartheta}}\,\mathrm{d}\mu=\nabla\int_{\mathcal{Y}}f_{\mathbf{x}_{i},\boldsymbol{\vartheta}}\,\mathrm{d}\mu,

(6.20)

we directly obtain the result, using that
f_{\mathbf{x}_{i},\boldsymbol{\theta}}
integrates to
1
for all

\boldsymbol{\theta}\in\mathbb{R}^{K}
:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol{\vartheta}}(\nabla\log f_{\mathbf{x}_{i},\boldsymbol{\vartheta}}(Y_{i}))=\int_{\mathcal{Y}}\Bigl(\frac{1}{f_{\mathbf{x}_{i},\boldsymbol{\vartheta}}}\nabla f_{\mathbf{x}_{i},\boldsymbol{\vartheta}}\Bigr)f_{\mathbf{x}_{i},\boldsymbol{\vartheta}}\,\mathrm{d}\mu=\nabla\int_{\mathcal{Y}}f_{\mathbf{x}_{i},\boldsymbol{\vartheta}}\,\mathrm{d}\mu=\mathbf{0}.

(6.21)

To show (
6.20
), we verify the three properties given in Corollary 16.3 in
Bauer 2001a
for the considered function
f_{\mathbf{x}_{i},\boldsymbol{\theta}}=\frac{\exp[\tilde{\mathbf{b}}(\mathbf{x}_{i})^{\top}\boldsymbol{\theta}]}{\int_{\mathcal{Y}}\exp[\tilde{\mathbf{b}}(\mathbf{x}_{i})^{\top}\boldsymbol{\theta}]\,\mathrm{d}\mu}
.
First,
f_{\mathbf{x}_{i},\boldsymbol{\theta}}
is
\mu
-integrable for all

\boldsymbol{\theta}\in\mathbb{R}^{K}
.
Second,
the partial derivative

\frac{\partial}{\partial\theta_{k}}f_{\mathbf{x}_{i},\boldsymbol{\theta}}(y_{i})
exists.
It is given as

\displaystyle\frac{\exp[\tilde{\mathbf{b}}(\mathbf{x}_{i})(y_{i})^{\top}\boldsymbol{\theta}]\bigl(\int_{\mathcal{Y}}\exp[\tilde{\mathbf{b}}(\mathbf{x}_{i})^{\top}\boldsymbol{\theta}]\,\mathrm{d}\mu\,\tilde{\mathbf{b}}(\mathbf{x}_{i})(y_{i})_{[k]}-\int_{\mathcal{Y}}\exp[\tilde{\mathbf{b}}(\mathbf{x}_{i})^{\top}\boldsymbol{\theta}]\,\tilde{\mathbf{b}}(\mathbf{x}_{i})_{[k]}\,\mathrm{d}\mu\bigr)}{\bigl(\int_{\mathcal{Y}}\exp[\tilde{\mathbf{b}}(\mathbf{x}_{i})^{\top}\boldsymbol{\theta}]\,\mathrm{d}\mu\bigr)^{2}},

where we used
\frac{\partial}{\partial\theta_{k}}\int_{\mathcal{Y}}\exp[\tilde{\mathbf{b}}(\mathbf{x}_{i})^{\top}\boldsymbol{\theta}]\,\mathrm{d}\mu=\int_{\mathcal{Y}}\frac{\partial}{\partial\theta_{k}}\exp[\tilde{\mathbf{b}}(\mathbf{x}_{i})^{\top}\boldsymbol{\theta}]\,\mathrm{d}\mu
as shown in the paragraph before (
6.6
).
For the third required property, note that since the basis functions contained in
\tilde{\mathbf{b}}_{\mathcal{Y}}
and
\mathbf{b}_{\mathcal{X}}
are bounded and
\mu
is finite,
|\frac{\partial}{\partial\theta_{k}}f_{\mathbf{x}_{i},\boldsymbol{\theta}}|
is bounded on an open ball

\upsilon_{\varepsilon}(\boldsymbol{\theta}):=\{\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{K}\penalty\ |\penalty\ \|\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\prime}-\boldsymbol{\theta}\|_{2}<\varepsilon\}
for each

\boldsymbol{\theta}\in\mathbb{R}^{K}
and
\varepsilon>0
by a nonnegative finite constant, which defines a constant and thus
\mu
-integrable function on
\mathcal{Y}
.
Thus, (
6.20
) follows with
Bauer 2001a
and (
6.21
) is proven.

2)

From
1)
, we have

\displaystyle\mathrm{Var}_{\boldsymbol{\vartheta}}\Bigl(\sum_{i=1}^{N}\nabla\log f_{\mathbf{x}_{i},\boldsymbol{\vartheta}}(Y_{i})\Bigr)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol{\vartheta}}\Bigl[\Bigl(\sum_{i=1}^{N}\nabla\log f_{\mathbf{x}_{i},\boldsymbol{\vartheta}}(Y_{i})\Bigr)\Bigl(\sum_{j=1}^{N}\nabla\log f_{\mathbf{x}_{j},\boldsymbol{\vartheta}}(Y_{j})\Bigr)^{\top}\Bigr],

whose
(k,k^{\prime})
-th entry is given as
\sum_{i,j=1}^{N}\mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol{\vartheta}}(\frac{\partial}{\partial\theta_{k}}\log f_{\mathbf{x}_{i},\boldsymbol{\vartheta}}(Y_{i})\frac{\partial}{\partial\theta_{k^{\prime}}}\log f_{\mathbf{x}_{j},\boldsymbol{\vartheta}}(Y_{j}))
for
k,k^{\prime}\in\{1,\ldots,K\}
.
Since
(Y_{1},\mathbf{X}_{1}),\ldots,(Y_{N},\mathbf{X}_{N})
are independent,
\frac{\partial}{\partial\theta_{k}}\log f_{\mathbf{x}_{i},\boldsymbol{\vartheta}}(Y_{i})
and
\frac{\partial}{\partial\theta_{k^{\prime}}}\log f_{\mathbf{x}_{j},\boldsymbol{\vartheta}}(Y_{j})
are independent as well. With (
6.21
), we obtain that all summands with
i\neq j
are zero.
Using (
6.6
),
the remaining summands (with
i=j
) are

\displaystyle\,\int_{\mathcal{Y}}\frac{\partial}{\partial\theta_{k}}\log f_{\mathbf{x}_{i},\boldsymbol{\vartheta}}\,\frac{\partial}{\partial\theta_{k^{\prime}}}\log f_{\mathbf{x}_{i},\boldsymbol{\vartheta}}\,f_{\mathbf{x}_{i},\boldsymbol{\vartheta}}\,\mathrm{d}\mu

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\,\int_{\mathcal{Y}}\Bigl(\tilde{\mathbf{b}}(\mathbf{x}_{i})_{[k]}-\int_{\mathcal{Y}}f_{\mathbf{x}_{i},\boldsymbol{\vartheta}}\,\tilde{\mathbf{b}}(\mathbf{x}_{i})_{[k]}\,\mathrm{d}\mu\Bigr)\,\Bigl(\tilde{\mathbf{b}}(\mathbf{x}_{i})_{[k^{\prime}]}-\int_{\mathcal{Y}}f_{\mathbf{x}_{i},\boldsymbol{\vartheta}}\,\tilde{\mathbf{b}}(\mathbf{x}_{i})_{[k^{\prime}]}\,\mathrm{d}\mu\Bigr)\,f_{\mathbf{x}_{i},\boldsymbol{\vartheta}}\,\mathrm{d}\mu

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{Y}}f_{\mathbf{x}_{i},\boldsymbol{\vartheta}}\,\tilde{\mathbf{b}}(\mathbf{x}_{i})_{[k]}\,\tilde{\mathbf{b}}(\mathbf{x}_{i})_{[k^{\prime}]}\,\mathrm{d}\mu-\int_{\mathcal{Y}}f_{\mathbf{x}_{i},\boldsymbol{\vartheta}}\,\tilde{\mathbf{b}}(\mathbf{x}_{i})_{[k]}\,\mathrm{d}\mu\,\int_{\mathcal{Y}}f_{\mathbf{x}_{i},\boldsymbol{\vartheta}}\,\tilde{\mathbf{b}}(\mathbf{x}_{i})_{[k^{\prime}]}\,\mathrm{d}\mu.

From (
6.8
)
we obtain that this is the
(k,k^{\prime})
-th element of the
i
-th contribution of the negative Hessian matrix of
\ell(\boldsymbol{\vartheta})
and thus,

\mathrm{Var}_{\boldsymbol{\vartheta}}(\sum_{i=1}^{N}\nabla\log f_{\mathbf{x}_{i},\boldsymbol{\vartheta}}(Y_{i}))=\mathbf{F}(\boldsymbol{\vartheta})
.

3)

Since the basis functions contained in
\tilde{\mathbf{b}}_{\mathcal{Y}}
and
\mathbf{b}_{\mathcal{X}}
are bounded and
\mu
is finite, there exists
\sigma_{\boldsymbol{\vartheta}}
such that for all
k=1,\ldots,K
and all
i=1,\ldots,N

\displaystyle\Bigl|\frac{\partial}{\partial\theta_{k}}\log f_{\mathbf{x}_{i},\boldsymbol{\vartheta}}\Bigr|=\Bigl|\tilde{\mathbf{b}}(\mathbf{x}_{i})(y_{i})_{[k]}-\int_{\mathcal{Y}}f_{\mathbf{x}_{i},\boldsymbol{\vartheta}}\,\,(\tilde{\mathbf{b}}(\mathbf{x}_{i}))_{[k]}\,\mathrm{d}\mu\Bigr|\leq\sigma_{\boldsymbol{\vartheta}}

and thus,

\mathrm{Var}_{\boldsymbol{\vartheta}}(\frac{\partial}{\partial\theta_{k}}\log f_{\mathbf{x}_{i},\boldsymbol{\vartheta}}(Y_{i}))=\int_{\mathcal{Y}}(\frac{\partial}{\partial\theta_{k}}\log f_{\mathbf{x}_{i},\boldsymbol{\vartheta}})^{2}\,f_{\mathbf{x}_{i},\boldsymbol{\vartheta}}\,\mathrm{d}\mu\leq\sigma_{\boldsymbol{\vartheta}}^{2}
.
Noting that
\{\|\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\nabla\log f_{\mathbf{x}_{i},\boldsymbol{\vartheta}}(Y_{i})\|_{2}\geq\varepsilon\}\subseteq\bigcup_{k=1}^{K}\{|\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\frac{\partial}{\partial\vartheta_{k}}\log f_{\mathbf{x}_{i},\boldsymbol{\vartheta}}(Y_{i})|\geq\frac{\varepsilon}{\sqrt{K}}\}
for all
\varepsilon>0
,
using
1)
, and applying Chebyshev’s inequality
(e.g.,
Borovkov 2013a
, Corollary 4.7.3)
, we have for all
\varepsilon>0
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{\boldsymbol{\vartheta}}\Bigl(\Bigl\|\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\nabla\log f_{\mathbf{x}_{i},\boldsymbol{\vartheta}}(Y_{i})-\mathbf{0}\Bigr\|_{2}\geq\varepsilon\Bigr)

\displaystyle\leq\penalty\

\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{K}\mathbb{P}_{\boldsymbol{\vartheta}}\Bigl(\Bigl|\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\frac{\partial}{\partial\theta_{k}}\log f_{\mathbf{x}_{i},\boldsymbol{\vartheta}}(Y_{i})\Bigr|\geq\frac{\varepsilon}{\sqrt{K}}\Bigr)

\displaystyle\leq\penalty\

\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{K}\frac{K\,\mathrm{Var}_{\boldsymbol{\vartheta}}(\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\frac{\partial}{\partial\theta_{k}}\log f_{\mathbf{x}_{i},\boldsymbol{\vartheta}}(Y_{i}))}{\varepsilon^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq\penalty\

\displaystyle\frac{K^{2}N\sigma_{\boldsymbol{\vartheta}}^{2}}{N^{2}\varepsilon^{2}}\xrightarrow[N\rightarrow\infty]{}0,

where we used that
(Y_{i},\mathbf{X}_{i})
and thus

\frac{\partial}{\partial\theta_{k}}\log f_{\mathbf{x}_{i},\boldsymbol{\vartheta}}(Y_{i})
are independent for
i=1,\ldots,N
. Hence,
\frac{1}{N}\nabla\ell(\boldsymbol{\vartheta})\xrightarrow[N\rightarrow\infty]{\mathbb{P}}\mathbf{0}
.

4)

For the proof of

\frac{1}{N}\nabla\ell_{\mathrm{pen}}(\boldsymbol{\vartheta})\xrightarrow[N\rightarrow\infty]{\mathbb{P}}\mathbf{0}
, note that

\frac{1}{N}\nabla\ell_{\mathrm{pen}}(\boldsymbol{\vartheta})=\frac{1}{N}\nabla\ell(\boldsymbol{\vartheta})-\frac{1}{N}\,2\,\mathbf{P}\boldsymbol{\vartheta}
.
By
3)
, the first term converges in probability to
\mathbf{0}
.
Furthermore, using
\xi_{\max}=\ocal_{\mathbb{P}}(N)
, also the second term converges in probability to
\mathbf{0}
by Lemma
6.11
.
Let
\varepsilon>0
.
Noting that

\{\|\frac{1}{N}\nabla\ell(\boldsymbol{\vartheta})-\frac{1}{N}\,2\,\mathbf{P}\boldsymbol{\vartheta}\|_{2}\geq\varepsilon\}\subseteq\{\|\frac{1}{N}\nabla\ell(\boldsymbol{\vartheta})\|_{2}+\|\frac{1}{N}\,2\,\mathbf{P}\boldsymbol{\vartheta}\|_{2}\geq\varepsilon\}\subseteq\{\|\frac{1}{N}\nabla\ell(\boldsymbol{\vartheta})\|_{2}\geq\frac{\varepsilon}{2}\penalty\ \vee\penalty\ \|\frac{1}{N}\,2\,\mathbf{P}\boldsymbol{\vartheta}\|_{2}\geq\frac{\varepsilon}{2}\}

we obtain,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\Bigl(\Bigl\|\frac{1}{N}\nabla\ell_{\mathrm{pen}}(\boldsymbol{\vartheta})-\mathbf{0}\Bigr\|_{2}\geq\varepsilon\Bigr)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}\Bigl(\Bigl\|\frac{1}{N}\nabla\ell(\boldsymbol{\vartheta})-\frac{1}{N}\,2\,\mathbf{P}\boldsymbol{\vartheta}\Bigr\|_{2}\geq\varepsilon\Bigr)

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{P}\Bigl(\Bigl\|\frac{1}{N}\nabla\ell(\boldsymbol{\vartheta})\Bigr\|_{2}\geq\frac{\varepsilon}{2}\Bigr)+\mathbb{P}\Bigl(\Bigl\|\frac{1}{N}\,2\,\mathbf{P}\boldsymbol{\vartheta}\Bigr\|_{2}\geq\frac{\varepsilon}{2}\Bigr)

\displaystyle\xrightarrow[N\rightarrow\infty]{}0,

i.e.,
\frac{1}{N}\nabla\ell_{\mathrm{pen}}(\boldsymbol{\vartheta})\xrightarrow[N\rightarrow\infty]{\mathbb{P}}\mathbf{0}
.
∎

Lemma 6.14
.

Assume that assumption
(BX’)
holds.
Then, for each
\boldsymbol{\theta}\in\mathbb{R}^{K}
, there exist
M>0
(independent of
N
) such that for all
\mathbf{v}\in\mathbb{R}^{K}
and all
N\geq N^{\prime}
,

\displaystyle N\,M\,\|\mathbf{v}\|_{2}^{2}\leq\mathbf{v}^{\top}\mathbf{F}_{\mathrm{pen}}(\boldsymbol{\theta})\mathbf{v},

where
\mathbf{F}_{\mathrm{pen}}(\boldsymbol{\theta})
denotes the Fisher information of the penalized log-likelihood
\ell_{\mathrm{pen}}(\boldsymbol{\theta})
. In particular, this shows that there exists
N^{\prime}\in\mathbb{N}
such that for all
N\geq N^{\prime}
, we have

N\,M\,\mathbf{I}_{K}\preceq\mathbf{F}_{\mathrm{pen}}(\boldsymbol{\theta})
,
where
\mathbf{A}\preceq\mathbf{B}
denotes that
\mathbf{B}-\mathbf{A}
is positive semi-definite for symmetric matrices
\mathbf{A},\mathbf{B}\in\mathbb{R}^{K\times K}
.

Proof.

We first show the statement for the unpenalized setting. The proof is inspired by
Stone 1991c
, but uses some different ideas to obtain the result. Let
\boldsymbol{\theta}\in\mathbb{R}^{K}
.
Since
\mathbf{b}_{\mathcal{X}}
and
\tilde{\mathbf{b}}_{\mathcal{Y}}
are bounded, and
\mu
is finite,
M_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}:=\inf_{\mathbf{x}\in\mathcal{X},\,y\in\mathcal{Y}}f_{\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\theta}}(y)>0
exists.
For
\mathbf{v}\in\mathbb{R}^{K}
, set
\mathbf{v}_{\mathcal{X}}:=(\tilde{\mathbf{b}}_{\mathcal{Y}}^{\top}\mathbf{v}_{1,1},\ldots,\tilde{\mathbf{b}}_{\mathcal{Y}}^{\top}\mathbf{v}_{J,K_{\mathcal{X},\,J}})\in L_{0}^{2}(\mu)^{K_{\mathcal{X}}}
with
\mathbf{v}_{j,n}=(v_{j,n,1},\ldots,v_{j,n,K_{\mathcal{Y}}})^{\top}
, where
v_{j,n,m}
for
j=1,\ldots,J,\penalty\ n=1,\ldots,K_{\mathcal{X},\,j}
, and
m=1,\ldots,K_{\mathcal{Y}}
, corresponds to the respective entry of
\mathbf{v}
, where the indices are arranged in the same order as the vector
\boldsymbol{\theta}\in\mathbb{R}^{K}
containing the values
\theta_{j,n,m}
in Section 2.2. It is straightforward to show
\tilde{\mathbf{b}}(\mathbf{x}_{i})^{\top}\mathbf{v}=\mathbf{b}_{\mathcal{X}}(\mathbf{x}_{i})^{\top}\mathbf{v}_{\mathcal{X}}
for
i=1,\ldots,N
.
Let
M^{\prime}>0
and
N^{\prime}\in\mathbb{N}
such that (
6.3
) holds for
N\geq N^{\prime}
.
From (
6.9
),
we obtain for the Fisher information
\mathbf{F}(\boldsymbol{\theta})
of
\ell(\boldsymbol{\theta})
,

\displaystyle\mathbf{v}^{\top}\mathbf{F}(\boldsymbol{\theta})\mathbf{v}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{N}\int_{\mathcal{Y}}f_{\mathbf{x}_{i},\boldsymbol{\theta}}\,\Bigl(\tilde{\mathbf{b}}(\mathbf{x}_{i})^{\top}\mathbf{v}-\int f_{\mathbf{x}_{i},\boldsymbol{\theta}}\cdot\tilde{\mathbf{b}}(\mathbf{x}_{i})^{\top}\mathbf{v}\,\mathrm{d}\mu\Bigr)^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\mu

\displaystyle\geq M_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\int_{\mathcal{Y}}\Bigl(\tilde{\mathbf{b}}(\mathbf{x}_{i})^{\top}\mathbf{v}-\int f_{\mathbf{x}_{i},\boldsymbol{\theta}}\cdot\tilde{\mathbf{b}}(\mathbf{x}_{i})^{\top}\mathbf{v}\,\mathrm{d}\mu\Bigr)^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\mu

\displaystyle\geq M_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\int_{\mathcal{Y}}\left(\tilde{\mathbf{b}}(\mathbf{x}_{i})^{\top}\mathbf{v}\right)^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\mu-2\,\int_{\mathcal{Y}}\tilde{\mathbf{b}}(\mathbf{x}_{i})^{\top}\mathbf{v}\,\mathrm{d}\mu\,\int f_{\mathbf{x}_{i},\boldsymbol{\theta}}\cdot\tilde{\mathbf{b}}(\mathbf{x}_{i})^{\top}\mathbf{v}\,\mathrm{d}\mu

\displaystyle\hskip-2.84544pt\overset{(\ast_{1})}{=}M_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}\int_{\mathcal{Y}}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\left(\mathbf{b}_{\mathcal{X}}(\mathbf{x}_{i})^{\top}\mathbf{v}_{\mathcal{X}}\right)^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\mu

\displaystyle\hskip-7.11317pt\overset{\eqref{P2a:eq_stone_1}}{\geq}M_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}\,M^{\prime}\,N\int_{\mathcal{Y}}\int_{\mathcal{X}}\left(\tilde{\mathbf{b}}(\mathbf{x})^{\top}\mathbf{v}\right)^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{\nu}(\mathbf{x})\,\mathrm{d}\mu

\displaystyle\hskip-2.84544pt\overset{(\ast_{2})}{\geq}N\,(M_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}\,M^{\prime}\,M_{\tilde{\mathbf{b}}})\|\mathbf{v}\|_{2}^{2}

where in
(\ast_{1})
, we used
\tilde{\mathbf{b}}(\mathbf{x}_{i})^{\top}\mathbf{v}\in L_{0}^{2}(\mu)
and thus
\int_{\mathcal{Y}}\tilde{\mathbf{b}}(\mathbf{x}_{i})^{\top}\mathbf{v}\,\mathrm{d}\mu=0
.
For
(\ast_{2})
, note that
\|\mathbf{v}\|_{\tilde{\mathbf{b}}}:=[\int_{\mathcal{Y}}\int_{\mathcal{X}}(\tilde{\mathbf{b}}(\mathbf{x})^{\top}\mathbf{v})^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{\nu}(\mathbf{x})\,\mathrm{d}\mu]^{\frac{1}{2}}
defines a norm on
\mathbb{R}^{K}
(which is verified analogously to
\|\cdot\|_{\mathbf{b}_{\mathcal{X}}}
being a norm in the proof of Lemma
6.5

2)
via the corresponding inner product, using the linear independence of the functions contained in
\tilde{\mathbf{b}}_{\mathcal{Y}}
and of the functions contained in
\mathbf{b}_{\mathcal{X}}
) and thus
M_{\tilde{\mathbf{b}}}>0
as in
(\ast_{2})
exists
(e.g.,
Horn & Johnson 2012a
, Corollary 5.4.5)
.
Finally, for
M:=M_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}\,M^{\prime}\,M_{\tilde{\mathbf{b}}}
we then have
\mathbf{v}^{\top}\mathbf{F}(\boldsymbol{\theta})\mathbf{v}-N\,M\,\|\mathbf{v}\|_{2}^{2}\geq 0
and equivalently
\mathbf{v}^{\top}(\mathbf{F}(\boldsymbol{\theta})-N\,M\,\mathbf{I})\mathbf{v}\geq 0
.

Now consider the penalized case, i.e.,
\mathbf{F}_{\mathrm{pen}}(\boldsymbol{\theta})=\mathbf{F}(\boldsymbol{\theta})+2\,\mathbf{P}
.
Since
\mathbf{P}
is positive semi-definite (which follows from the matrices
\mathbf{P}_{j}
being positive semi-definite for all
j\in\{1,\ldots,J\}
), we have
\mathbf{v}^{\top}2\,\mathbf{P}\mathbf{v}\geq 0
and thus

\mathbf{v}^{\top}(\mathbf{F}_{\mathrm{pen}}(\boldsymbol{\theta})-N\,M\,\mathbf{I})\mathbf{v}\geq\mathbf{v}^{\top}(\mathbf{F}(\boldsymbol{\theta})-N\,M\,\mathbf{I})\mathbf{v}\geq 0
and equivalently
\mathbf{v}^{\top}(\mathbf{F}_{\mathrm{pen}}(\boldsymbol{\theta})-N\,M\,\mathbf{I})\mathbf{v}\geq 0
.
Thus,
N\,M\,\mathbf{I}\preceq\mathbf{F}_{\mathrm{pen}}(\boldsymbol{\theta})
for
N\geq N^{\prime}
.
∎

Proof of Theorem
2.2
.

1)

We showed in Lemma
6.5

2)
that assumption
(BX’)
implies assumption
(BX)
and thus, by Proposition
6.6
, the Hessian matrix
\mathbf{H}_{\mathrm{pen}}(\boldsymbol{\theta})
of
\ell_{\mathrm{pen}}(\boldsymbol{\theta})
is negative definite for large enough
N
.
Hence, every root of
\nabla\ell_{\mathrm{pen}}
is a maximizer of
\ell_{\mathrm{pen}}
.
Furthermore,

\frac{1}{N}\,\mathbf{H}_{\mathrm{pen}}(\boldsymbol{\vartheta})
is a symmetric (and thus normal) matrix and the spectral theorem
(e.g.,
Horn & Johnson 2012a
, Theorem 2.5.3)
yields that there is an orthonormal basis
\mathbf{v}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{v}_{K}
of
\mathbb{R}^{K}
and values

such that
\frac{1}{N}\,\mathbf{H}_{\mathrm{pen}}(\boldsymbol{\vartheta})\mathbf{v}_{k}=\lambda_{k}\mathbf{v}_{k}
for all
k=1,\ldots,K
.
We show:

(Q)

For all
\varepsilon,\delta>0
, there exists an
N_{0}\in\mathbb{N}
, such that for all
N\geq N_{0}
, the equation
\nabla\ell_{\mathrm{pen}}(\boldsymbol{\theta})=\mathbf{0}
has a solution in the cube
Q_{\delta}(\boldsymbol{\vartheta}):=\bigtimes_{k=1}^{K}(\boldsymbol{\vartheta}-\frac{\delta}{K}\mathbf{v}_{k},\boldsymbol{\vartheta}+\frac{\delta}{K}\mathbf{v}_{k})
on a set whose probability exceeds

.

In the following, we write
f(\boldsymbol{\theta}):=f_{\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\theta}}(y)=\frac{\exp[\tilde{\mathbf{b}}(\mathbf{x})(y)^{\top}\boldsymbol{\theta}]}{\int_{\mathcal{Y}}\exp[\tilde{\mathbf{b}}(\mathbf{x})^{\top}\boldsymbol{\theta}]\,\mathrm{d}\mu}
for arbitrary
(y,\mathbf{x})\in\mathcal{Y}\times\mathcal{X}
and
f_{i}(\boldsymbol{\theta}):=f_{\mathbf{x}_{i},\boldsymbol{\theta}}(y_{i})
for
i=1,\ldots,N
, since we are interested in
\boldsymbol{\theta}
as function argument.
We have

\ell_{\mathrm{pen}}(\boldsymbol{\theta})=\sum_{i=1}^{N}(\log f_{i}(\boldsymbol{\theta})+\frac{1}{N}\pen(\boldsymbol{\theta}))
.
Note that for almost all
(y,\mathbf{x})\in\mathcal{Y}\times\mathcal{X}
the derivatives
\nabla(\log f(\boldsymbol{\theta})+\frac{1}{N}\pen(\boldsymbol{\theta})),D^{2}(\log f(\boldsymbol{\theta})+\frac{1}{N}\pen(\boldsymbol{\theta}))
, and
D^{3}(\log f(\boldsymbol{\theta})+\frac{1}{N}\pen(\boldsymbol{\theta}))=D^{3}\log f(\boldsymbol{\theta})
exist for every
\boldsymbol{\theta}\in\mathbb{R}^{K}
. Thus, we can expand the first derivative of
\log f(\boldsymbol{\theta})+\frac{1}{N}\pen(\boldsymbol{\theta})
around the true value
\boldsymbol{\vartheta}
using Taylor’s formula
(e.g.,
Laczkovich & Sós 2017a
, Theorem 1.97)
for every
\boldsymbol{\theta}\in\mathbb{R}^{K}
as

\displaystyle\nabla\Bigl(\log f(\boldsymbol{\theta})+\frac{1}{N}\pen(\boldsymbol{\theta})\Bigr)

\displaystyle=\nabla\log f(\boldsymbol{\vartheta})-\frac{1}{N}\,2\,\mathbf{P}\boldsymbol{\vartheta}

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt+\Bigl(D^{2}\log f(\boldsymbol{\vartheta})-\frac{1}{N}\,2\,\mathbf{P}\Bigr)(\boldsymbol{\theta}-\boldsymbol{\vartheta})

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt+\frac{1}{2}(\boldsymbol{\theta}-\boldsymbol{\vartheta})^{\top}D^{3}\log f(\tilde{\boldsymbol{\theta}})(\boldsymbol{\theta}-\boldsymbol{\vartheta}),

(6.22)

for a
\tilde{\boldsymbol{\theta}}=\tilde{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{(y,\mathbf{x})}
in the segment

\{\boldsymbol{\vartheta}+\alpha\,(\boldsymbol{\theta}-\boldsymbol{\vartheta})\penalty\ |\penalty\ \alpha\in[0,1]\}

connecting
\boldsymbol{\vartheta}
and
\boldsymbol{\theta}
, and
D^{3}\log f(\tilde{\boldsymbol{\theta}}):=(D^{2}\frac{\partial}{\partial\theta_{1}}\log f(\tilde{\boldsymbol{\theta}}),\ldots,D^{2}\frac{\partial}{\partial\theta_{K}}\log f(\tilde{\boldsymbol{\theta}}))\in\mathbb{R}^{K\times K\times K}
.
9
9

9

For
\mathbf{v}\in\mathbb{R}^{K}
and
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{W}}=(\mathbf{W}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{W}_{K})\in\mathbb{R}^{K\times K\times K}

\mathbf{W}_{k}\in\mathbb{R}^{K\times K}

.

Building on (

where

is a sum of products, whose factors have the form

\pm\frac{\int_{\mathcal{Y}}\exp[\tilde{\mathbf{b}}(\mathbf{x})^{\top}\boldsymbol{\theta}]\,\prod_{j\in\{k,k^{\prime},k^{\prime\prime}\}}(\tilde{\mathbf{b}}(\mathbf{x})_{[j]})^{\delta_{j}}\,\mathrm{d}\mu}{\int_{\mathcal{Y}}\exp[\tilde{\mathbf{b}}(\mathbf{x})^{\top}\boldsymbol{\theta}]\,\mathrm{d}\mu},

.
Since the basis functions contained in

for almost all

, where

\|\boldsymbol{\mathcal{W}}\|_{\mathrm{op}}:=\sup_{\mathbf{v}\in\mathbb{R}^{K}\setminus\{\mathbf{0}\}}\frac{\|\mathbf{v}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\mathcal{W}}\|_{\mathrm{op}}}{\|\mathbf{v}\|_{2}}

denotes the operator norm of
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{W}}\in\mathbb{R}^{K\times K\times K}
with
\|\mathbf{A}\|_{\mathrm{op}}:=\sup_{\mathbf{w}\in\mathbb{R}^{K}\setminus\{\mathbf{0}\}}\frac{\|\mathbf{A}\mathbf{w}\|_{2}}{\|\mathbf{w}\|_{2}}
the spectral norm of
\mathbf{A}\in\mathbb{R}^{K\times K}
.
Thus,

\displaystyle\Bigl\|\frac{D^{3}\log f(\tilde{\boldsymbol{\theta}})}{M}\Bigr\|_{\mathrm{op}}\leq\frac{1}{M}\sup_{\boldsymbol{\theta}\in Q_{\delta}(\boldsymbol{\vartheta})}\left\|D^{3}\log f(\boldsymbol{\theta})\right\|_{\mathrm{op}}\leq 1,

and (

\displaystyle\nabla\Bigl(\log f(\boldsymbol{\theta})+\frac{1}{N}\pen(\boldsymbol{\theta})\Bigr)

\displaystyle=\nabla\log f(\boldsymbol{\vartheta})-\frac{1}{N}\,2\,\mathbf{P}\boldsymbol{\vartheta}

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt+\Bigl(D^{2}\log f(\boldsymbol{\vartheta})-\frac{1}{N}\,2\,\mathbf{P}\Bigr)(\boldsymbol{\theta}-\boldsymbol{\vartheta})

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt+\frac{M}{2}(\boldsymbol{\theta}-\boldsymbol{\vartheta})^{\top}\boldsymbol{\mathcal{E}}_{(y,\mathbf{x})}(\boldsymbol{\theta}-\boldsymbol{\vartheta}),

with

.
Thus,
the equation

is equivalent to

\displaystyle\mathbf{0}

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{\varsigma}_{1}+\boldsymbol{\varsigma}_{2}(\boldsymbol{\theta}-\boldsymbol{\vartheta})+\frac{M}{2}(\boldsymbol{\theta}-\boldsymbol{\vartheta})^{\top}\boldsymbol{\mathcal{E}}(\boldsymbol{\theta}-\boldsymbol{\vartheta}),

(6.23)

where

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\varsigma}_{1}

\displaystyle:=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\nabla\log f_{i}(\boldsymbol{\vartheta})-\frac{1}{N}\,2\,\mathbf{P}\boldsymbol{\vartheta}=\frac{1}{N}\nabla\ell_{\mathrm{pen}}(\boldsymbol{\vartheta}),

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\varsigma}_{2}

\displaystyle:=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}D^{2}\log f_{i}(\boldsymbol{\vartheta})-\frac{1}{N}\,2\,\mathbf{P}=\frac{1}{N}\,\mathbf{H}_{\mathrm{pen}}(\boldsymbol{\vartheta}),

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\mathcal{E}}:=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\boldsymbol{\mathcal{E}}_{(y_{i},\mathbf{x}_{i})}.

Note that
\|\boldsymbol{\mathcal{E}}\|_{\mathrm{op}}\leq\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\|\boldsymbol{\mathcal{E}}_{(y_{i},\mathbf{x}_{i})}\|_{\mathrm{op}}\leq 1
.
Now let
\varepsilon,\delta>0
.
Since

,

We show that there exists

10

The theorem is (without loss of generality) formulated for the cube

(e.g.,

)
.
Let

\displaystyle Q_{k}^{\pm}

\displaystyle:=\Bigl\{\boldsymbol{\theta}=\boldsymbol{\vartheta}+\frac{1}{K}\,\sum_{k^{\prime}=1}^{K}\alpha_{k^{\prime}}\mathbf{v}_{k^{\prime}}\penalty\ \Big|\penalty\ \alpha_{k}=\pm\delta\penalty\ \wedge\forall k^{\prime}\in\{1,\ldots,K\}:\alpha_{k^{\prime}}\in[-\delta,\delta]\Bigr\}.

We show that

\langle v_{k},\boldsymbol{\varsigma}_{1}+\boldsymbol{\varsigma}_{2}(\boldsymbol{\theta}-\boldsymbol{\vartheta})+\frac{M}{2}(\boldsymbol{\theta}-\boldsymbol{\vartheta})^{\top}\boldsymbol{\mathcal{E}}(\boldsymbol{\theta}-\boldsymbol{\vartheta})\rangle

and negative for all

.
For

\displaystyle\langle\mathbf{v}_{k},\boldsymbol{\varsigma}_{2}(\boldsymbol{\theta}-\boldsymbol{\vartheta})\rangle_{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{K}\,\Bigl\langle\mathbf{v}_{k},\boldsymbol{\varsigma}_{2}\sum_{k^{\prime}=1}^{K}\alpha_{k^{\prime}}\mathbf{v}_{k^{\prime}}\Bigr\rangle_{2}=\frac{1}{K}\,\sum_{k^{\prime}=1}^{K}\alpha_{k^{\prime}}\langle\mathbf{v}_{k},\boldsymbol{\varsigma}_{2}\mathbf{v}_{k^{\prime}}\rangle_{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{K}\,\sum_{k^{\prime}=1}^{K}\alpha_{k^{\prime}}\lambda_{k^{\prime}}\langle\mathbf{v}_{k},\mathbf{v}_{k^{\prime}}\rangle_{2}=\frac{1}{K}\,\alpha_{k}\lambda_{k}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{K}\,\begin{cases}-\delta\lambda_{k}&,\,\boldsymbol{\theta}\in Q_{k}^{-}\\
\delta\lambda_{k}&,\,\boldsymbol{\theta}\in Q_{k}^{+}\end{cases}\,.

From Lemma
6.14
,
we obtain that there exist

.
(

.
Set

and let

in the following.
Then,

\displaystyle\frac{\delta\,\Lambda}{K}\leq-\frac{\delta\,\lambda_{k}}{K}=\begin{cases}\langle\mathbf{v}_{k},\boldsymbol{\varsigma}_{2}(\boldsymbol{\theta}-\boldsymbol{\vartheta})\rangle_{2}&,\,\boldsymbol{\theta}\in Q_{k}^{-}\\
-\langle\mathbf{v}_{k},\boldsymbol{\varsigma}_{2}(\boldsymbol{\theta}-\boldsymbol{\vartheta})\rangle_{2}&,\,\boldsymbol{\theta}\in Q_{k}^{+}\end{cases}\,.

Furthermore, for data in

\displaystyle\,\Bigl|\Bigl\langle\mathbf{v}_{k},\boldsymbol{\varsigma}_{1}+\frac{M}{2}(\boldsymbol{\theta}-\boldsymbol{\vartheta})^{\top}\boldsymbol{\mathcal{E}}(\boldsymbol{\theta}-\boldsymbol{\vartheta})\Bigr\rangle_{2}\Bigr|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\,\|\mathbf{v}_{k}\|_{2}\,\Bigl\|\boldsymbol{\varsigma}_{1}+\frac{M}{2}(\boldsymbol{\theta}-\boldsymbol{\vartheta})^{\top}\boldsymbol{\mathcal{E}}(\boldsymbol{\theta}-\boldsymbol{\vartheta})\Bigr\|_{2}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\,\|\boldsymbol{\varsigma}_{1}\|_{2}+\frac{M}{2}\Bigl\|\Bigl(\frac{1}{K}\,\sum_{k^{\prime}=1}^{K}\alpha_{k^{\prime}}\mathbf{v}_{k^{\prime}}\Bigr)^{\top}\boldsymbol{\mathcal{E}}\Bigl(\frac{1}{K}\,\sum_{k^{\prime}=1}^{K}\alpha_{k^{\prime}}\mathbf{v}_{k^{\prime}}\Bigr)\Bigr\|_{2}

\displaystyle\,\delta^{2}+M\,\delta^{2}\leq(1+M\,)\delta^{2}<\frac{\Lambda\,\delta}{K}\leq\begin{cases}\langle\mathbf{v}_{k},\boldsymbol{\varsigma}_{2}(\boldsymbol{\theta}-\boldsymbol{\vartheta})\rangle_{2}&,\,\boldsymbol{\theta}\in Q_{k}^{-}\\

.
In particular, the inequality above yields

\displaystyle\,\Bigl\langle\mathbf{v}_{k},\boldsymbol{\varsigma}_{1}+\boldsymbol{\varsigma}_{2}(\boldsymbol{\theta}-\boldsymbol{\vartheta})+\frac{M}{2}(\boldsymbol{\theta}-\boldsymbol{\vartheta})^{\top}\boldsymbol{\mathcal{E}}(\boldsymbol{\theta}-\boldsymbol{\vartheta})\Bigr\rangle_{2}

<0&,\,\boldsymbol{\theta}\in Q_{k}^{+}\end{cases}\,,

and the Poincaré-Miranda Theorem yields that (

) has a solution in

. This proves statement
1)
.
Since

this also implies consistency of the PMLE.

2)

First note that
\xi_{\max}=\ocal_{\mathbb{P}}(\sqrt{N})

\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}

.
To show the asymptotic normality, we consider

for

for

.
For any

,

.
Thus, there exists a unique symmetric and positive definite matrix

such that
\mathbf{F}_{\alpha}(\boldsymbol{\vartheta})^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}\mathbf{F}_{\alpha}(\boldsymbol{\vartheta})^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}=(\mathbf{F}_{\alpha}(\boldsymbol{\vartheta})^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}})^{\top}\mathbf{F}_{\alpha}(\boldsymbol{\vartheta})^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}=\mathbf{F}_{\alpha}(\boldsymbol{\vartheta})
(

Horn & Johnson 2012a
, Theorem 7.2.6

12
12

12

The Theorem actually assumes a symmetric and positive semi-definite matrix

and yields existence of a unique symmetric positive semi-definite square root

. However if

is even strictly positive definite, one can prove identically that also the unique
\mathbf{A}^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}
is strictly positive definite.
).
Since it is positive definite, its inverse
\mathbf{F}_{\alpha}(\boldsymbol{\vartheta})^{-\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}:=(\mathbf{F}_{\alpha}(\boldsymbol{\vartheta})^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}})^{-1}
exists.
Considering (
6.23
) for
\boldsymbol{\theta}=\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}
, we obtain after reorganizing,

\displaystyle(\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}-\boldsymbol{\vartheta})

\displaystyle=\Bigl(\mathbf{F}_{\mathrm{pen}}(\boldsymbol{\vartheta})-\frac{NM}{2}(\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}-\boldsymbol{\vartheta})^{\top}\boldsymbol{\mathcal{E}}\Bigr)^{-1}N\boldsymbol{\varsigma}_{1}.

Multiplying from the left with

, inserting

, and reorganizing yields

\displaystyle\mathbf{F}_{\mathrm{pen}}(\boldsymbol{\vartheta})^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}(\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}-\boldsymbol{\vartheta})

\displaystyle=\penalty\

\displaystyle\Bigl(\mathbf{F}_{\mathrm{pen}}(\boldsymbol{\vartheta})^{-\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}\bigl(\mathbf{F}_{\mathrm{pen}}(\boldsymbol{\vartheta})-\frac{N\,M}{2}(\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}-\boldsymbol{\vartheta})^{\top}\boldsymbol{\mathcal{E}}\bigr)\mathbf{F}_{\mathrm{pen}}(\boldsymbol{\vartheta})^{-\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}\Bigr)^{-1}\,\mathbf{F}_{\mathrm{pen}}(\boldsymbol{\vartheta})^{-\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}\,N\,\boldsymbol{\varsigma}_{1},

\displaystyle\mathbf{F}_{\mathrm{pen}}(\boldsymbol{\vartheta})^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}(\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}-\boldsymbol{\vartheta})

\displaystyle\Bigl(\mathbf{I}_{K}-\frac{N\,M}{2}\mathbf{F}_{\mathrm{pen}}(\boldsymbol{\vartheta})^{-\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}(\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}-\boldsymbol{\vartheta})^{\top}\boldsymbol{\mathcal{E}}\mathbf{F}_{\mathrm{pen}}(\boldsymbol{\vartheta})^{-\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}\Bigr)^{-1}\mathbf{F}_{\mathrm{pen}}(\boldsymbol{\vartheta})^{-\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}\,N\,\boldsymbol{\varsigma}_{1}.

(6.24)

Here, the matrix
\mathbf{J}:=\mathbf{I}_{K}-\frac{N\,M}{2}\mathbf{F}_{\mathrm{pen}}(\boldsymbol{\vartheta})^{-\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}(\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}-\boldsymbol{\vartheta})^{\top}\boldsymbol{\mathcal{E}}\mathbf{F}_{\mathrm{pen}}(\boldsymbol{\vartheta})^{-\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}

.
To see this, we first show for
\mathbf{F}_{\alpha}(\boldsymbol{\vartheta})
and in particular for
\mathbf{F}_{\mathrm{pen}}(\boldsymbol{\vartheta})=\mathbf{F}_{2}(\boldsymbol{\vartheta})
:

(F)

For every
\alpha\geq 0
, there exist
N_{3}\in\mathbb{N}
and

such that for all

:

\|\mathbf{F}_{\alpha}(\boldsymbol{\vartheta})^{-\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}\|_{\mathrm{op}}\leq\frac{1}{\sqrt{N\,C}}.

Since
\mathbf{F}_{\alpha}(\boldsymbol{\vartheta})^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}
is symmetric, also
\mathbf{F}_{\alpha}(\boldsymbol{\vartheta})^{-\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}
is symmetric and thus, denoting the set of eigenvalues of a matrix
\mathbf{A}\in\mathbb{R}^{K\times K}
with
\sigma(\mathbf{A})
,
\max\sigma(\mathbf{F}_{\alpha}(\boldsymbol{\vartheta})^{-\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}})=\|\mathbf{F}_{\alpha}(\boldsymbol{\vartheta})^{-\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}\|_{\mathrm{op}}

(
Rudin 1991a
, Theorem 11.28 (b))
. Furthermore,
\sigma(\mathbf{F}_{\alpha}(\boldsymbol{\vartheta})^{-\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}})=\{\frac{1}{\sqrt{\lambda}}\penalty\ |\penalty\ \lambda\in\sigma(\mathbf{F}_{\alpha}(\boldsymbol{\vartheta}))\}
due to the spectral mapping theorem
(
Rudin 1991a
, Theorem 10.28 (b))
.
From Lemma
6.14
,
there exist
C>0
and

such that for

, we have

and thus

for all

(
Horn & Johnson 2012a
, Corollary 7.7.4 (c))
.
This implies
\frac{1}{\sqrt{\lambda}}\leq\frac{1}{\sqrt{N\,C}}
for all
\lambda\in\sigma(\mathbf{F}_{\alpha}(\boldsymbol{\vartheta}))
and in particular
\|\mathbf{F}_{\alpha}(\boldsymbol{\vartheta})^{-\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}\|_{\mathrm{op}}=\max\sigma(\mathbf{F}_{\alpha}(\boldsymbol{\vartheta})^{-\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}})\leq\frac{1}{\sqrt{N\,C}}
, which proves statement
2)
.

Noting that
\|\mathbf{A}\mathbf{B}\|_{\mathrm{op}}\leq\|\mathbf{A}\|_{\mathrm{op}}\|\mathbf{B}\|_{\mathrm{op}}
for
\mathbf{A},\mathbf{B}\in\mathbb{R}^{K\times K}

(
Horn & Johnson 2012a
, Theorem 5.6.2 (c))
and recalling that

, we then obtain for

,

\displaystyle\Bigl\|\frac{N\,M}{2}\mathbf{F}_{\mathrm{pen}}(\boldsymbol{\vartheta})^{-\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}(\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}-\boldsymbol{\vartheta})^{\top}\boldsymbol{\mathcal{E}}\mathbf{F}_{\mathrm{pen}}(\boldsymbol{\vartheta})^{-\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}\Bigr\|_{\mathrm{op}}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{N\,M}{2}\frac{1}{\sqrt{N\,C}}\|\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}-\boldsymbol{\vartheta}\|_{2}\frac{1}{\sqrt{N\,C}}

\displaystyle=\frac{M}{2\,C}\|\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}-\boldsymbol{\vartheta}\|_{2},

(6.25)

and thus

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{\boldsymbol{\vartheta}}\Bigl(\Bigl\|\frac{N\,M}{2}\mathbf{F}_{\mathrm{pen}}(\boldsymbol{\vartheta})^{-\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}(\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}-\boldsymbol{\vartheta})^{\top}\boldsymbol{\mathcal{E}}\mathbf{F}_{\mathrm{pen}}(\boldsymbol{\vartheta})^{-\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}\Bigr\|_{\mathrm{op}}<1\Bigr)

\displaystyle\geq\penalty\

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{\boldsymbol{\vartheta}}\Bigl(\frac{M}{2\,C}\|\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}-\boldsymbol{\vartheta}\|_{2}<1\Bigr)=\mathbb{P}_{\boldsymbol{\vartheta}}\Bigl(\|\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}-\boldsymbol{\vartheta}\|_{2}<\frac{2\,C}{M}\Bigr)\xrightarrow[N\rightarrow\infty]{}1,

since
\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}\xrightarrow[N\rightarrow\infty]{\mathbb{P}}\boldsymbol{\vartheta}
.
The invertibility of
\mathbf{J}
with probability tending to one then follows via convergence of the Neumann series
(
Alt 2016a
, Statement 5.7)
. Using
N\,\boldsymbol{\varsigma}_{1}=\nabla\ell(\boldsymbol{\vartheta})-2\,\mathbf{P}\boldsymbol{\vartheta}
and multiplying with
\mathbf{I}_{K}=\mathbf{F}(\boldsymbol{\vartheta})^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}\,\mathbf{F}(\boldsymbol{\vartheta})^{-\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}

\displaystyle\mathbf{F}_{\mathrm{pen}}(\boldsymbol{\vartheta})^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}(\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}-\boldsymbol{\vartheta})

\displaystyle=\mathbf{J}^{-1}\mathbf{F}_{\mathrm{pen}}(\boldsymbol{\vartheta})^{-\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}\,\mathbf{F}(\boldsymbol{\vartheta})^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}\,\mathbf{F}(\boldsymbol{\vartheta})^{-\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}\,\nabla\ell(\boldsymbol{\vartheta})

\displaystyle\hskip 11.38092pt-\mathbf{J}^{-1}\mathbf{F}_{\mathrm{pen}}(\boldsymbol{\vartheta})^{-\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}\,2\,\mathbf{P}\boldsymbol{\vartheta}

(6.26)

We now show

(a)

\mathbf{J}=\mathbf{I}_{K}-\frac{N\,M}{2}\mathbf{F}_{\mathrm{pen}}(\boldsymbol{\vartheta})^{-\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}(\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}-\boldsymbol{\vartheta})^{\top}\boldsymbol{\mathcal{E}}\mathbf{F}_{\mathrm{pen}}(\boldsymbol{\vartheta})^{-\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}\xrightarrow[N\rightarrow\infty]{\mathbb{P}}\mathbf{I}_{K}
,

(b)

\mathbf{F}_{\mathrm{pen}}(\boldsymbol{\vartheta})^{-\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}\,\mathbf{F}(\boldsymbol{\vartheta})^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}\xrightarrow[N\rightarrow\infty]{\mathbb{P}}\mathbf{I}_{K}
,

(c)

\mathbf{F}(\boldsymbol{\vartheta})^{-\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}\,\nabla\ell(\boldsymbol{\vartheta})\xrightarrow[N\rightarrow\infty]{\mathrm{D}}\penalty\ N(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I}_{K})
,

(d)

\mathbf{J}^{-1}\mathbf{F}_{\mathrm{pen}}(\boldsymbol{\vartheta})^{-\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}\,2\,\mathbf{P}\boldsymbol{\vartheta}\xrightarrow[N\rightarrow\infty]{\mathbb{P}}\mathbf{0}
,

which then with (
6.26
) and Slutsky’s thoerem
(e.g.,
Vaart 1998a
, Lemma 2.8)
yields

\mathbf{F}_{\mathrm{pen}}(\boldsymbol{\vartheta})^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}(\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}-\boldsymbol{\vartheta})\xrightarrow[N\rightarrow\infty]{\mathrm{D}}N(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I}_{K})
,
i.e.,
\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}\overset{\text{a}}{\sim}\mathcal{N}(\boldsymbol{\vartheta},\mathbf{F}_{\mathrm{pen}}(\boldsymbol{\vartheta})^{-1})
.
Proof of
2)(a)
to
2)(d)
:

(a)

Using (
6.25
) and
\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}\xrightarrow[N\rightarrow\infty]{\mathbb{P}}\boldsymbol{\vartheta}
we obtain for any
\varepsilon>0
,

\displaystyle\,\mathbb{P}_{\boldsymbol{\vartheta}}\Bigl(\Bigl\|\mathbf{I}_{K}-\frac{N\,M}{2}\mathbf{F}_{\mathrm{pen}}(\boldsymbol{\vartheta})^{-\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}(\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}-\boldsymbol{\vartheta})^{\top}\boldsymbol{\mathcal{E}}\mathbf{F}_{\mathrm{pen}}(\boldsymbol{\vartheta})^{-\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}-\mathbf{I}_{K}\Bigr\|_{\mathrm{op}}\geq\varepsilon\Bigr)

\displaystyle\leq\,

\displaystyle\,\mathbb{P}_{\boldsymbol{\vartheta}}\Bigl(\frac{M}{2\,C}\|\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}-\boldsymbol{\vartheta}\|_{2}\geq\varepsilon\Bigr)=\,\mathbb{P}_{\boldsymbol{\vartheta}}\Bigl(\|\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}-\boldsymbol{\vartheta}\|_{2}\geq\frac{2\varepsilon\,C}{M}\Bigr)\xrightarrow[N\rightarrow\infty]{}0.

(b)

With
\mathbf{F}_{\mathrm{pen}}(\boldsymbol{\vartheta})=\mathbf{F}(\boldsymbol{\vartheta})+2\,\mathbf{P}
, we obtain

\displaystyle\,\bigl\|\mathbf{F}_{\mathrm{pen}}(\boldsymbol{\vartheta})^{-\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}\,\mathbf{F}(\boldsymbol{\vartheta})^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}-\mathbf{I}_{K}\bigr\|_{\mathrm{op}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\,\bigl\|\left((\mathbf{F}(\boldsymbol{\vartheta})+2\,\mathbf{P})^{-\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}-\mathbf{F}(\boldsymbol{\vartheta})^{-\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}\right)\,\mathbf{F}(\boldsymbol{\vartheta})^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}\bigr\|_{\mathrm{op}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\,\Bigl\|\Bigl(\int_{0}^{1}\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\left((\mathbf{F}(\boldsymbol{\vartheta})+t\,2\,\mathbf{P})^{-\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}\right)\,\mathrm{d}t\Bigr)\,\mathbf{F}(\boldsymbol{\vartheta})^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}\Bigr\|_{\mathrm{op}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\,\Bigl\|\Bigl(\int_{0}^{1}\,(\mathbf{F}(\boldsymbol{\vartheta})+t\,2\,\mathbf{P})^{-\nicefrac{{3}}{{2}}}\,\mathrm{d}t\Bigr)\,\mathbf{P}\,\mathbf{F}(\boldsymbol{\vartheta})^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}\Bigr\|_{\mathrm{op}}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\,\max_{t\in[0,1]}\|(\mathbf{F}(\boldsymbol{\vartheta})+t\,2\,\mathbf{P})^{-\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}\|_{\mathrm{op}}^{3}\,\|\mathbf{P}\|_{\mathrm{op}}\,\|\mathbf{F}(\boldsymbol{\vartheta})^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}\|_{\mathrm{op}}.

By
2)
, there exists
N_{3}\in\mathbb{N}
and
C>0
, such that

\displaystyle\max_{t\in[0,1]}\|(\mathbf{F}(\boldsymbol{\vartheta})+t\,2\,\mathbf{P})^{-\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}\|_{\mathrm{op}}^{3}\leq\frac{1}{\sqrt{N\,C}^{3}}

for all
N\geq N_{3}
.
Furthermore, we have
\|\mathbf{F}(\boldsymbol{\vartheta})^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}\|_{\mathrm{op}}=\|\mathbf{F}(\boldsymbol{\vartheta})\|_{\mathrm{op}}^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}
, which follows from the spectral norm of a symmetric matrix being equal to its maximal eigenvalue
(
Rudin 1991a
, Theorem 11.28 (b))
and the spectral mapping theorem
(
Rudin 1991a
, Theorem 10.28 (b))
, and

\displaystyle\|\mathbf{F}(\boldsymbol{\vartheta})\|_{\mathrm{op}}

\displaystyle=\Bigl\|\sum_{i=1}^{N}D^{2}\log f_{i}(\boldsymbol{\vartheta})\Bigr\|_{\mathrm{op}}\leq N\,\max_{i\in\{1,\ldots,N\}}\|D^{2}\log f_{i}(\boldsymbol{\vartheta})\|_{\mathrm{op}}.

Recalling from (
6.7
) that

\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial\theta_{k}\partial\theta_{k^{\prime}}}\log f(\boldsymbol{\vartheta})

is a sum of products, whose factors have the form

\pm\frac{\int_{\mathcal{Y}}\exp[\tilde{\mathbf{b}}(\mathbf{x})^{\top}\boldsymbol{\vartheta}]\,\prod_{j\in\{k,k^{\prime}\}}(\tilde{\mathbf{b}}(\mathbf{x})_{[j]})^{\delta_{j}}\,\mathrm{d}\mu}{\int_{\mathcal{Y}}\exp[\tilde{\mathbf{b}}(\mathbf{x})^{\top}\boldsymbol{\vartheta}]\,\mathrm{d}\mu},
where
\delta_{j}\in\{0,1\}
and
\tilde{\mathbf{b}}(\mathbf{x})_{[j]}
denotes the
j
-the entry of the vector
\tilde{\mathbf{b}}(\mathbf{x})
,
we obtain that
there exists an
M>0
such that
\sup_{\mathbf{x}\in\mathcal{X}}\|D^{2}\log f(\boldsymbol{\vartheta})\|_{\mathrm{op}}\leq M
, since the basis functions contained in
\tilde{\mathbf{b}}_{\mathcal{Y}}
and
\mathbf{b}_{\mathcal{X}}
are bounded and
\mu
is finite.
Hence,
\|\mathbf{F}(\boldsymbol{\vartheta})^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}\|_{\mathrm{op}}\leq\sqrt{N\,M}
.
All in all, we have for every
\varepsilon>0
and
N\geq N_{3}
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{\boldsymbol{\vartheta}}\Bigl(\Bigl\|\mathbf{F}_{\mathrm{pen}}(\boldsymbol{\vartheta})^{-\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}\,\mathbf{F}(\boldsymbol{\vartheta})^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}-\mathbf{I}_{K}\Bigr\|_{\mathrm{op}}\geq\varepsilon\Bigr)

\displaystyle\leq\penalty\

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{\boldsymbol{\vartheta}}\Bigl(\frac{1}{\sqrt{N\,C}^{3}}\,\|\mathbf{P}\|_{\mathrm{op}}\,\sqrt{N\,M}\geq\varepsilon\Bigr)

\displaystyle=\penalty\

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{\boldsymbol{\vartheta}}\Bigl(\frac{1}{N}\,\|\mathbf{P}\|_{\mathrm{op}}\geq\frac{\sqrt{C^{3}}\,\varepsilon}{\sqrt{M}}\Bigr)\xrightarrow[N\rightarrow\infty]{}0,

by Lemma
6.11
, since
\xi_{\max}=\ocal_{\mathbb{P}}(N)
.
Thus,
\mathbf{F}_{\mathrm{pen}}(\boldsymbol{\vartheta})^{-\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}\,\mathbf{F}(\boldsymbol{\vartheta})^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}\xrightarrow[N\rightarrow\infty]{\mathbb{P}}\mathbf{I}_{K}
.

(c)

Recall that
\nabla\ell(\boldsymbol{\vartheta})=\sum_{i=1}^{N}\nabla\log f_{i}(\boldsymbol{\vartheta})
.
We have
\mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol{\vartheta}}(\nabla\log f_{i}(\boldsymbol{\vartheta}))=\mathbf{0}
for all
i=1,\ldots,N
and

\mathrm{Var}(\sum_{i=1}^{N}\nabla\log f_{i}(\boldsymbol{\vartheta}))=\mathbf{F}(\boldsymbol{\vartheta})
from Lemma
6.12
.
Thus, the multivariate version of the Berry–Esseen bound
(e.g.,
Bentkus 2005a
, Theorem 1.1)
yields

\displaystyle\sup_{A\in\mathcal{C}}\Bigl|\mathbb{P}_{\boldsymbol{\vartheta}}\Bigl(\mathbf{F}(\boldsymbol{\vartheta})^{-\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\nabla\log f_{i}(\boldsymbol{\vartheta})\in A\Bigr)-\mathbb{P}_{\boldsymbol{\vartheta}}(\mathbf{Z}\in A)\Bigr|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\,c\,K^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{4}}}\,\sum_{i=1}^{N}\mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol{\vartheta}}(\|\mathbf{F}(\boldsymbol{\vartheta})^{-\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}\nabla\log f_{i}(\boldsymbol{\vartheta})\|_{2}^{3}),

(6.27)

where
\mathcal{C}
is the set of all convex subsets of

\mathbb{R}^{K}
,
\mathbf{Z}
is a
K
-dimensional multivariate standard normally distributed random vector, and
c
is some positive constant.
We now show

\lim_{N\rightarrow\infty}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol{\vartheta}}(\|\mathbf{F}(\boldsymbol{\vartheta})^{-\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}\nabla\log f_{i}(\boldsymbol{\vartheta})\|_{2}^{3})=0
, which together with (
6.27
) yields
2)(c)
, since
\{(v_{1},\ldots,v_{K})\in\mathbb{R}^{K}\penalty\ |\penalty\ v_{1}\leq z_{1},\ldots,v_{K}\leq z_{K}\}
is a convex subset of

\mathbb{R}^{K}
for all
(z_{1},\ldots,z_{K})\in\mathbb{R}^{K}
. Note that since
\mathbf{b}_{\mathcal{X}}
and
\tilde{\mathbf{b}}_{\mathcal{Y}}
are bounded and
\mu
is finite, there exists a
C_{2}>0
such that for all
\mathbf{x}_{i}\in\mathcal{X}
,

\displaystyle\,\mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol{\vartheta}}\bigl(\left\|\nabla\log f_{\mathbf{x}_{i},\boldsymbol{\vartheta}}(Y_{i})\right\|_{2}^{3}\bigr)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\,\int\Bigl(\sum_{k=1}^{K}\Bigl(\tilde{\mathbf{b}}(\mathbf{x}_{i})_{[k]}-\int_{\mathcal{Y}}f_{\mathbf{x}_{i},\boldsymbol{\vartheta}}\,\tilde{\mathbf{b}}(\mathbf{x}_{i})_{[k]}\,\mathrm{d}\mu\Bigr)^{2}\Bigr)^{\frac{3}{2}}\,f_{\mathbf{x}_{i},\boldsymbol{\vartheta}}\,\mathrm{d}\mu\leq C_{2},

where the partial derivatives are computed using (
6.6
).
Using
2)
, we obtain that there exist
N_{3}\in\mathbb{N}
and
C>0
, such that
\|\mathbf{F}(\boldsymbol{\vartheta})^{-\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}\|_{\mathrm{op}}\leq\frac{1}{\sqrt{N\,C}}
for
N\geq N_{3}
.
Thus, we have

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{N}\mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol{\vartheta}}\bigl(\left\|\mathbf{F}(\boldsymbol{\vartheta})^{-\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}\nabla\log f_{i}(\boldsymbol{\vartheta})\right\|_{2}^{3}\bigr)

\displaystyle\leq\left\|\mathbf{F}(\boldsymbol{\vartheta})^{-\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}\right\|_{\mathrm{op}}^{3}\,\sum_{i=1}^{N}\mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol{\vartheta}}\bigl(\left\|\nabla\log f_{i}(\boldsymbol{\vartheta})\right\|_{2}^{3}\bigr)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{\sqrt{N\,C}^{3}}\,N\,C_{2}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{N\,C^{3}}}\,C_{2}\xrightarrow[N\rightarrow\infty]{}0.

(d)

From
2)(a)
, we have
\mathbf{J}\xrightarrow[N\rightarrow\infty]{\mathbb{P}}\mathbf{I}_{K}
.
Since the matrix inversion function is continuous (compare Lemma
6.7
),
we obtain
\mathbf{J}^{-1}\xrightarrow[N\rightarrow\infty]{\mathbb{P}}\mathbf{I}_{K}
by the continuous mapping theorem
(e.g.,
Vaart 1998a
, Theorem 2.3)
.
Furthermore, by
2)
, there exist
N_{3}\in\mathbb{N}
and
C>0
, such that for
N\geq N_{3}

\displaystyle\left\|\mathbf{F}_{\mathrm{pen}}(\boldsymbol{\vartheta})^{-\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}\,2\,\mathbf{P}\boldsymbol{\vartheta}\right\|_{2}

\displaystyle\leq 2\,\left\|\mathbf{F}_{\mathrm{pen}}(\boldsymbol{\vartheta})^{-\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}\right\|_{\mathrm{op}}\,\left\|\mathbf{P}\boldsymbol{\vartheta}\right\|_{2}\leq\frac{2}{\sqrt{N\,C}}\left\|\mathbf{P}\boldsymbol{\vartheta}\right\|_{2},

Thus, using
\xi_{\max}=\ocal_{\mathbb{P}}(\sqrt{N})
, we obtain for any
\varepsilon>0
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{\boldsymbol{\vartheta}}\left(\left\|\mathbf{F}_{\mathrm{pen}}(\boldsymbol{\vartheta})^{-\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}\,2\,\mathbf{P}\boldsymbol{\vartheta}\right\|_{2}\geq\varepsilon\right)\leq\mathbb{P}_{\boldsymbol{\vartheta}}\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{N}}\,\left\|\mathbf{P}\boldsymbol{\vartheta}\right\|_{2}\geq\frac{\sqrt{C}\,\varepsilon}{2}\right)\xrightarrow[N\rightarrow\infty]{}0,

by Lemma
6.11
, i.e.,
\mathbf{F}_{\mathrm{pen}}(\boldsymbol{\vartheta})^{-\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}\,2\,\mathbf{P}\boldsymbol{\vartheta}\xrightarrow[N\rightarrow\infty]{\mathbb{P}}\mathbf{0}
.
Since convergence in distribution and convergence in probability are equivalent, if the limiting random vector is constant
(e.g.,
Vaart 1998a
, Theorem 2.7 (iii))
, this yields

\mathbf{J}^{-1}\mathbf{F}_{\mathrm{pen}}(\boldsymbol{\vartheta})^{-\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}\,2\,\mathbf{P}\boldsymbol{\vartheta}\xrightarrow[N\rightarrow\infty]{\mathbb{P}}\mathbf{I}_{K}\mathbf{0}=\mathbf{0}.
by Slutsky’s thoerem
(e.g.,
Vaart 1998a
, Lemma 2.8)
.
∎

Proof of Lemma
2.3
.

For the matrix
\mathbf{A}
as in Lemma
2.3
, set
\mathbf{V}_{\mathcal{Y}}:=\mathbf{A}\mathbf{F}_{\mathrm{pen}}(\boldsymbol{\vartheta})^{-1}\mathbf{A}^{\top}
.
From Theorem
2.2
, we have
\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}\overset{\text{a}}{\sim}\mathcal{N}(\boldsymbol{\vartheta},(\mathbf{F}_{\mathrm{pen}}(\boldsymbol{\vartheta}))^{-1})
,
which yields

\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{\mathcal{Y}}\overset{\mathrm{a}}{\sim}\mathcal{N}(\boldsymbol{\vartheta}_{\mathcal{Y}},\mathbf{V}_{\mathcal{Y}})
and thus,
(\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{\mathcal{Y}}-\boldsymbol{\vartheta}_{\mathcal{Y}})^{\top}\mathbf{V}_{\mathcal{Y}}^{-1}(\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{\mathcal{Y}}-\boldsymbol{\vartheta}_{\mathcal{Y}})\overset{\mathrm{a}}{\sim}\chi^{2}(K_{\mathcal{Y}})
. Hence,
CR_{\boldsymbol{\vartheta}_{\mathcal{Y}}}:=\{\boldsymbol{\theta}_{\mathcal{Y}}\in\mathbb{R}^{K_{\mathcal{Y}}}\penalty\ |\penalty\ (\boldsymbol{\theta}_{\mathcal{Y}}-\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{\mathcal{Y}})^{\top}\hat{\mathbf{V}}_{\mathcal{Y}}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{\mathcal{Y}}-\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{\mathcal{Y}})\leq\chi^{2}_{\alpha}(K_{\mathcal{Y}})\}
is an asymptotic
\alpha\cdot 100\%
confidence region for
\boldsymbol{\vartheta}_{\mathcal{Y}}
. Since the basis functions contained in the vector
\tilde{\mathbf{b}}_{\mathcal{Y}}
are linearly independent,
\spano(\tilde{\mathbf{b}}_{\mathcal{Y}})=\spano(\tilde{b}_{\mathcal{Y},1},\ldots,\tilde{b}_{\mathcal{Y},K_{\mathcal{Y}}})
is a
K_{\mathcal{Y}}
-dimensional subspace of
L^{2}_{0}(\mu)
and thus is isomorphic to
\mathbb{R}^{K_{\mathcal{Y}}}
via the mapping
\Phi:\spano(\tilde{\mathbf{b}}_{\mathcal{Y}})\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{K_{\mathcal{Y}}},\tilde{\mathbf{b}}_{\mathcal{Y}}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\theta}_{\mathcal{Y}}\mapsto\boldsymbol{\theta}_{\mathcal{Y}}
.
Then,

\{\tilde{\mathbf{b}}_{\mathcal{Y}}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\theta}_{\mathcal{Y}}\in\spano(\tilde{\mathbf{b}}_{\mathcal{Y}})\penalty\ |\penalty\ \boldsymbol{\theta}_{\mathcal{Y}}\in CR_{\boldsymbol{\vartheta}_{\mathcal{Y}}}\}
is an
\alpha\cdot 100\%
confidence region for

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathbf{b}}_{\mathcal{Y}}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\vartheta}_{\mathcal{Y}}

\displaystyle=\tilde{\mathbf{b}}_{\mathcal{Y}}^{\top}\mathbf{A}\boldsymbol{\vartheta}=\tilde{\mathbf{b}}_{\mathcal{Y}}^{\top}\Bigl(\sum_{j\in\mathcal{J}}\bigl(\mathbf{b}_{\mathcal{X},\,j}(\mathbf{x})^{\top}\otimes\mathbf{I}_{K_{\mathcal{Y}}}\bigr)\mathbf{S}_{j}\Bigr)\boldsymbol{\vartheta}

\displaystyle=\sum_{j\in\mathcal{J}}\tilde{\mathbf{b}}_{\mathcal{Y}}^{\top}\bigl(\mathbf{b}_{\mathcal{X},\,j}(\mathbf{x})^{\top}\otimes\mathbf{I}_{K_{\mathcal{Y}}}\bigr)\boldsymbol{\vartheta}_{j}=\sum_{j\in\mathcal{J}}\bigl(\mathbf{b}_{\mathcal{X},\,j}(\mathbf{x})\otimes\tilde{\mathbf{b}}_{\mathcal{Y}}\bigr)^{\top}\boldsymbol{\vartheta}_{j}=\clr[h_{\mathcal{J}}(\mathbf{x})].

Using that the inverse clr transformation is ismorphic, as well, we obtain that

\{\mathbf{b}_{\mathcal{Y}}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\theta}_{\mathcal{Y}}\in\spano(b_{\mathcal{Y},1},\ldots,b_{\mathcal{Y},K_{\mathcal{Y}}})\penalty\ |\penalty\ \boldsymbol{\theta}_{\mathcal{Y}}\in CR_{\boldsymbol{\vartheta}_{\mathcal{Y}}}\}
is an
\alpha\cdot 100\%
confidence region for
\mathbf{b}_{\mathcal{Y}}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\vartheta}_{\mathcal{Y}}=h_{\mathcal{J}}(\mathbf{x})
.

Denoting the probability measure corresponding to the
\chi^{2}(K_{\mathcal{Y}})
distribution by
\mathbb{P}_{\chi^{2}(K_{\mathcal{Y}})}
and considering a random variable
T\sim\chi^{2}(K_{\mathcal{Y}})
, we obtain asymptotic p-values for
\boldsymbol{\vartheta}_{\mathcal{Y}}=\mathbf{0}
and equivalently for
h_{\mathcal{J}}(\mathbf{x})=0\in B^{2}(\mu)
and for
\clr[h_{\mathcal{J}}(\mathbf{x})]=0\in L^{2}_{0}(\mu)
via

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{\chi^{2}(K_{\mathcal{Y}})}\left(T\geq(\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{\mathcal{Y}}-\boldsymbol{\vartheta}_{\mathcal{Y}})^{\top}\hat{\mathbf{V}}_{\mathcal{Y}}^{-1}(\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{\mathcal{Y}}-\boldsymbol{\vartheta}_{\mathcal{Y}})\penalty\ |\penalty\ \boldsymbol{\vartheta}_{\mathcal{Y}}=\mathbf{0}\right)

\displaystyle=1-F_{\chi^{2}(K_{\mathcal{Y}})}(\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{\mathcal{Y}}^{\top}\hat{\mathbf{V}}_{\mathcal{Y}}^{-1}\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{\mathcal{Y}}).\hskip 8.5359pt\qed

Lemma 6.15
.

Consider the
j
-th partial effect as a function of the covariates, i.e.,
h_{j}:\mathcal{X}\rightarrow B^{2}(\mu)
with
h_{j}=(\mathbf{b}_{\mathcal{X},\,j}\begin{picture}\put(1.5,0.6){\circle{2.0}}\put(1.5,0.6){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){$\otimes$}}\end{picture}\mathbf{b}_{\mathcal{Y}})^{\top}\boldsymbol{\vartheta}_{j}
.
For
K_{j}:=K_{\mathcal{X},\,j}K_{\mathcal{Y}}
, denote the matrix mapping the vector
\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}
to its
j
-th subvector
\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{j}
with
\mathbf{S}_{j}\in\mathbb{R}^{K_{j}\times K}
(i.e.,

\boldsymbol{\vartheta}_{j}=\mathbf{S}_{j}\boldsymbol{\vartheta}
,

\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{j}=\mathbf{S}_{j}\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}
)
and set

\hat{\mathbf{V}}_{j}:=\mathbf{S}_{j}\mathbf{F}_{\mathrm{pen}}(\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}})^{-1}\mathbf{S}_{j}^{\top}
.
Assume
(BX’)
and
\xi_{\max}=\ocal_{\mathbb{P}}(\sqrt{N})
.
Then,
CR_{\boldsymbol{\vartheta}_{j}}:=\{\boldsymbol{\theta}_{j}\in\mathbb{R}^{K_{j}}\penalty\ |\penalty\ (\boldsymbol{\theta}_{j}-\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{j})^{\top}\hat{\mathbf{V}}_{j}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{j}-\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{j})\leq\chi^{2}_{\alpha}(K_{j})\}
,
