is an asymptotic
\alpha\cdot 100\%
confidence region for
\boldsymbol{\vartheta}_{j}
,

\{(\mathbf{b}_{\mathcal{X},\,j}\begin{picture}\put(1.5,0.6){\circle{2.0}}\put(1.5,0.6){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){$\otimes$}}\end{picture}\mathbf{b}_{\mathcal{Y}})^{\top}\boldsymbol{\theta}_{j}\penalty\ |\penalty\ \boldsymbol{\theta}_{j}\in CR_{\boldsymbol{\vartheta}_{j}}\}\subseteq\{\mathcal{X}\rightarrow B^{2}(\mu)\}
is an asymptotic
\alpha\cdot 100\%
confidence region for
h_{j}
, and

\{(\mathbf{b}_{\mathcal{X},\,j}\otimes\tilde{\mathbf{b}}_{\mathcal{Y}})^{\top}\boldsymbol{\theta}_{j}\penalty\ |\penalty\ \boldsymbol{\theta}_{j}\in CR_{\boldsymbol{\vartheta}_{j}}\}\subseteq\{\mathcal{X}\rightarrow L_{0}^{2}(\mu)\}
is an asymptotic
\alpha\cdot 100\%
confidence region for

\tilde{h}_{j}:=(\mathbf{b}_{\mathcal{X},\,j}\otimes\tilde{\mathbf{b}}_{\mathcal{Y}})^{\top}\boldsymbol{\vartheta}_{j}
.
Furthermore,
1-F_{\chi^{2}(K_{j})}(\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{j}^{\top}\hat{\mathbf{V}}_{j}^{-1}\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{j})
is a valid asymptotic p-value for
H_{0}:[\forall\mathbf{x}\in\mathcal{X}:h_{j}(\mathbf{x})=0\in B^{2}(\mu)]

and for

H_{0}:[\forall\mathbf{x}\in\mathcal{X}:\tilde{h}_{j}(\mathbf{x})=0\in L^{2}_{0}(\mu)]
.

Proof.

For the matrix
\mathbf{S}_{j}
as in Lemma
6.15
, s
Set
\mathbf{V}_{j}:=\mathbf{S}_{j}\mathbf{F}_{\mathrm{pen}}(\boldsymbol{\vartheta})^{-1}\mathbf{S}_{j}^{\top}
.
From Theorem
2.2
, we have
\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}\overset{\text{a}}{\sim}\mathcal{N}(\boldsymbol{\vartheta},(\mathbf{F}_{\mathrm{pen}}(\boldsymbol{\vartheta}))^{-1})
,
yielding

\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{j}\overset{\mathrm{a}}{\sim}\mathcal{N}(\boldsymbol{\vartheta}_{j},\mathbf{V}_{j})
and thus,
(\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{j}-\boldsymbol{\vartheta}_{j})^{\top}\mathbf{V}_{j}^{-1}(\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{j}-\boldsymbol{\vartheta}_{j})\overset{\mathrm{a}}{\sim}\chi^{2}(K_{j})
. Hence,
CR_{\boldsymbol{\vartheta}_{j}}:=\{\boldsymbol{\theta}_{j}\in\mathbb{R}^{K_{j}}\penalty\ |\penalty\ (\boldsymbol{\theta}_{j}-\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{j})^{\top}\hat{\mathbf{V}}_{j}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{j}-\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{j})\leq\chi^{2}_{\alpha}(K_{j})\}
is an asymptotic
\alpha\cdot 100\%
confidence region for
\boldsymbol{\vartheta}_{j}
. Since the basis functions contained in the vector
\mathbf{b}_{\mathcal{X},\,j}\otimes\tilde{\mathbf{b}}_{\mathcal{Y}}
are linearly independent – which follows from the basis functions contained in
\mathbf{b}_{\mathcal{X},\,j}
and the basis functions contained in
\tilde{\mathbf{b}}_{\mathcal{Y}}
being linearly independent, respectively –,
\spano(\mathbf{b}_{\mathcal{X},\,j}\otimes\tilde{\mathbf{b}}_{\mathcal{Y}})=\spano((\mathbf{b}_{\mathcal{X},\,j}\otimes\tilde{\mathbf{b}}_{\mathcal{Y}})_{[1]},\ldots,(\mathbf{b}_{\mathcal{X},\,j}\otimes\tilde{\mathbf{b}}_{\mathcal{Y}})_{[K_{j}]})
is a
K_{j}
-dimensional subspace of
\{\mathcal{X}\mapsto L^{2}_{0}(\mu)\}
and thus is isomorphic to
\mathbb{R}^{K_{j}}
via the mapping
\Phi:\spano(\mathbf{b}_{\mathcal{X},\,j}\otimes\tilde{\mathbf{b}}_{\mathcal{Y}})\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{K_{j}},(\mathbf{b}_{\mathcal{X},\,j}\otimes\tilde{\mathbf{b}}_{\mathcal{Y}})^{\top}\boldsymbol{\theta}_{j}\mapsto\boldsymbol{\theta}_{j}
.
Then,

\{(\mathbf{b}_{\mathcal{X},\,j}\otimes\tilde{\mathbf{b}}_{\mathcal{Y}})^{\top}\boldsymbol{\theta}_{j}\in\spano(\mathbf{b}_{\mathcal{X},\,j}\otimes\tilde{\mathbf{b}}_{\mathcal{Y}})\penalty\ |\penalty\ \boldsymbol{\theta}_{j}\in CR_{\boldsymbol{\vartheta}_{j}}\}
is an
\alpha\cdot 100\%
confidence region for
(\mathbf{b}_{\mathcal{X},\,j}\otimes\tilde{\mathbf{b}}_{\mathcal{Y}})^{\top}\boldsymbol{\vartheta}_{j}=\clr[h_{j}]
.
Using that the inverse clr transformation is isomorphic, as well, we obtain that

\{(\mathbf{b}_{\mathcal{X},\,j}\begin{picture}\put(1.5,0.6){\circle{2.0}}\put(1.5,0.6){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){$\otimes$}}\end{picture}\mathbf{b}_{\mathcal{Y}})^{\top}\boldsymbol{\theta}_{j}\in\spano((\mathbf{b}_{\mathcal{X},\,j}\begin{picture}\put(1.5,0.6){\circle{2.0}}\put(1.5,0.6){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){$\otimes$}}\end{picture}\mathbf{b}_{\mathcal{Y}})_{[1]},\ldots,(\mathbf{b}_{\mathcal{X},\,j}\begin{picture}\put(1.5,0.6){\circle{2.0}}\put(1.5,0.6){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){$\otimes$}}\end{picture}\mathbf{b}_{\mathcal{Y}})_{[K_{j}]})\penalty\ |\penalty\ \boldsymbol{\theta}_{j}\in CR_{\boldsymbol{\vartheta}_{j}}\}
is an
\alpha\cdot 100\%
confidence region for
(\mathbf{b}_{\mathcal{X},\,j}\begin{picture}\put(1.5,0.6){\circle{2.0}}\put(1.5,0.6){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){$\otimes$}}\end{picture}\mathbf{b}_{\mathcal{Y}})^{\top}\boldsymbol{\vartheta}_{j}=h_{j}
.

Denoting the probability measure corresponding to the
\chi^{2}(K_{j})
distribution with
\mathbb{P}_{\chi^{2}(K_{j})}
and considering a random variable
T\sim\chi^{2}(K_{j})
, we obtain p-values for
\boldsymbol{\vartheta}_{j}=\mathbf{0}
and equivalently for
h_{j}(\mathbf{x})=0\in B^{2}(\mu)
for all
\mathbf{x}\in\mathcal{X}
and for
\clr[h_{j}(\mathbf{x})]=0\in L^{2}_{0}(\mu)
for all
\mathbf{x}\in\mathcal{X}
via

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{\chi^{2}(K_{j})}\left(T\geq(\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{j}-\boldsymbol{\vartheta}_{j})^{\top}\hat{\mathbf{V}}_{j}^{-1}(\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{j}-\boldsymbol{\vartheta}_{j})\penalty\ |\penalty\ \boldsymbol{\vartheta}_{j}=\mathbf{0}\right)

\displaystyle=1-F_{\chi^{2}(K_{j})}(\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{j}^{\top}\hat{\mathbf{V}}_{j}^{-1}\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{j}).\qed

Proof of Theorem
2.4
.

Per construction, we have
\tilde{f}_{\mathbf{x}^{(l)},\boldsymbol{\theta}}=\tilde{f}_{\mathbf{x}_{i},\boldsymbol{\theta}}
for all
i\in\mathcal{I}^{(l)}
and

\boldsymbol{\theta}\in\mathbb{R}^{K}
.
The (shifted) log-likelihoods of interest are

\displaystyle\ell^{\mathrm{mn}}_{\mathcal{Z}}(\boldsymbol{\theta})

\displaystyle=\sum_{l=1}^{L}\sum_{g=1}^{\Gamma^{(l)}}n_{g}^{(l)}\Bigl(\tilde{f}_{\mathbf{x}^{(l)},\boldsymbol{\theta}}(u_{g}^{(l)})-\log\underbrace{\sum_{g^{\prime}=1}^{\Gamma^{(l)}}\Delta_{g^{\prime}}^{(l)}\exp[\tilde{f}_{\mathbf{x}^{(l)},\boldsymbol{\theta}}(u_{g^{\prime}}^{(l)})]}_{=:\Sigma(\mathcal{Z}^{(l)})}\Bigr)

\displaystyle\ell(\boldsymbol{\theta})

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{N}\bigl(\tilde{f}_{\mathbf{x}_{i},\boldsymbol{\theta}}(y_{i})-\log\int_{\mathcal{Y}}\exp(\tilde{f}_{\mathbf{x}_{i},\boldsymbol{\theta}})\,\mathrm{d}\mu\bigr).

Using
\Delta_{g^{\prime}}^{(l)}=w_{g^{\prime}-G^{(l)}}
,
u_{g^{\prime}}^{(l)}=t_{g^{\prime}-G^{(l)}}
for
g^{\prime}=G^{(l)}+1,\ldots,G^{(l)}+D=\Gamma^{(l)}
, we get

\displaystyle\Sigma(\mathcal{Z}^{(l)})

\displaystyle=\sum_{g^{\prime}=1}^{\Gamma^{(l)}}\Delta_{g^{\prime}}^{(l)}\exp[\tilde{f}_{\mathbf{x}^{(l)},\boldsymbol{\theta}}(u_{g^{\prime}}^{(l)})]=\underbrace{\sum_{g^{\prime}=1}^{G^{(l)}}\Delta_{g^{\prime}}^{(l)}\exp[\tilde{f}_{\mathbf{x}^{(l)},\boldsymbol{\theta}}(u_{g^{\prime}}^{(l)})]}_{=:\sigma(\mathcal{Z}^{(l)})}+\sum_{g^{\prime}=1}^{D}w_{g^{\prime}}\exp[\tilde{f}_{\mathbf{x}^{(l)},\boldsymbol{\theta}}(t_{g^{\prime}})]

\displaystyle=\sigma(\mathcal{Z}^{(l)})+\int_{\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{d}}}\exp(\tilde{f}_{\mathbf{x}^{(l)},\boldsymbol{\theta}})\,\mathrm{d}\delta.

(6.28)

Let
\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c}}=\emptyset
, i.e., we consider the discrete special case with
\mathcal{Y}=\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{d}}=\{t_{1},\ldots,t_{D}\}
and
\mu=\delta
.
Then,
G^{(l)}=0
and
\Gamma^{(l)}=D

for all
l=1,\ldots,L
.
Thus,

\displaystyle\ell^{\mathrm{mn}}_{\mathcal{Z}}(\boldsymbol{\theta})

\displaystyle=\sum_{l=1}^{L}\sum_{g=1}^{D}n_{g}^{(l)}\Bigl(\tilde{f}_{\mathbf{x}^{(l)},\boldsymbol{\theta}}(u_{g}^{(l)})-\log\int_{\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{d}}}\exp(\tilde{f}_{\mathbf{x}^{(l)},\boldsymbol{\theta}})\,\mathrm{d}\delta\Bigr)

\displaystyle=\sum_{l=1}^{L}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{d}}^{(l)}}\sum_{g=1}^{D}\mathbbm{1}_{\{t_{g}\}}(y_{i})\Bigl(\tilde{f}_{\mathbf{x}^{(l)},\boldsymbol{\theta}}(t_{g})-\log\int_{\mathcal{Y}}\exp(\tilde{f}_{\mathbf{x}^{(l)},\boldsymbol{\theta}})\,\mathrm{d}\mu\Bigr)

\displaystyle\overset{(\ast_{1})}{=}\sum_{l=1}^{L}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{d}}^{(l)}}\Bigl(\tilde{f}_{\mathbf{x}^{(l)},\boldsymbol{\theta}}(y_{i})-\log\int_{\mathcal{Y}}\exp(\tilde{f}_{\mathbf{x}_{i},\boldsymbol{\theta}})\,\mathrm{d}\mu\Bigr)

\displaystyle\overset{(\ast_{2})}{=}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\bigl(\tilde{f}_{\mathbf{x}_{i},\boldsymbol{\theta}}(y_{i})-\log\int_{\mathcal{Y}}\exp(\tilde{f}_{\mathbf{x}_{i},\boldsymbol{\theta}})\,\mathrm{d}\mu\bigr)=\ell(\boldsymbol{\theta}),

(6.29)

where we used in
(\ast_{1})
that for every
l\in\{1,\ldots,L\}
and every
i\in\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{d}}^{(l)}
, there exists a unique
g_{i}\in\{1,\ldots,D\}
with
y_{i}=t_{g_{i}}
, since the
y_{i}
are observations of the (distinct) values in
\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{d}}
.
In
(\ast_{2})
, we used that in the discrete case
\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{d}}^{(l)}=\mathcal{I}^{(l)}
for all
l=1\ldots,L
, i.e., the sets
\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{d}}^{(l)}
form a partition of
\{1,\ldots,N\}
for
l=1,\ldots,L
.
Adding the penalty term to
6.29
yields

\ell^{\mathrm{mn}}_{\mathcal{Z},\mathrm{pen}}(\boldsymbol{\theta})=\ell_{\mathrm{pen}}(\boldsymbol{\theta})
, which
directly implies equality of the penalized Fisher informations, i.e.,

\mathbf{F}^{\mathrm{mn}}_{\mathcal{Z}}(\boldsymbol{\theta})=\mathbf{F}(\boldsymbol{\theta})
.

Now, let
\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c}}\neq\emptyset
.
We show the convergence for the unpenalized log-likelihoods, i.e.,

\lim_{\Delta\rightarrow 0}\ell^{\mathrm{mn}}_{\mathcal{Z}}(\boldsymbol{\theta})=\ell(\boldsymbol{\theta})
,
which immediately implies convergence for the penalized log-likelihoods, since the penalty term is constant regarding
\Delta
.

For this purpose, we need to show that for all
\varepsilon>0
there exists a
\delta>0
, such that for all partitions
\mathcal{Z}^{(1)},\ldots,\mathcal{Z}^{(L)}
with overall maximal bin width
\Delta<\delta
, we have
\left|\ell^{\mathrm{mn}}_{\mathcal{Z}}(\boldsymbol{\theta})-\ell(\boldsymbol{\theta})\right|<\varepsilon
.
For this purpose, let
\varepsilon>0
and thus also
\frac{\varepsilon}{N}>0
.
Let
l\in\{1,\ldots,L\}
.
Define
\zeta^{(l)}:=\int_{\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{d}}}\exp(\tilde{f}_{\mathbf{x}^{(l)},\boldsymbol{\theta}})\,\mathrm{d}\delta\geq 0
.
Since the function
\xi\mapsto\log(\xi+\zeta^{(l)})
is continuous for all
\xi>0
, there exists an
\tilde{\varepsilon}^{(l)}>0
, such that for all
\xi>0
with
|\xi-\int_{\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c}}}\exp(\tilde{f}_{\mathbf{x}^{(l)},\boldsymbol{\theta}})\,\mathrm{d}\lambda|<\tilde{\varepsilon}^{(l)}
, we have
|\log(\xi+\zeta^{(l)})-\log(\int_{\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c}}}\exp(\tilde{f}_{\mathbf{x}^{(l)},\boldsymbol{\theta}})\,\mathrm{d}\lambda+\zeta^{(l)})|<\frac{\varepsilon}{N}
.
Set
\tilde{\varepsilon}:=\min\{\tilde{\varepsilon}^{(1)},\ldots,\tilde{\varepsilon}^{(L)}\}>0
.
Since
\sigma(\mathcal{Z}^{(l)})>0
is a Riemann sum of the integrable function
\exp(\tilde{f}_{\mathbf{x}^{(l)},\boldsymbol{\theta}})
, for
\tilde{\varepsilon}>0
there exists a
\delta^{(l)}_{\tilde{\varepsilon}}>0
such that every partition
\mathcal{Z}^{(l)}
with maximal bin width

\Delta^{(l)}:=\max\{\Delta_{g}^{(l)}\penalty\ |\penalty\ g=1,\ldots,G^{(l)}\}<\delta^{(l)}_{\tilde{\varepsilon}}
fulfills
|\sigma(\mathcal{Z}^{(l)})-\int_{\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c}}}\exp(\tilde{f}_{\mathbf{x}^{(l)},\boldsymbol{\theta}})\,\mathrm{d}\lambda|<\tilde{\varepsilon}

(
Laczkovich & Sós 2015a
, Theorem 14.23 (iii))
.
Set
\delta_{\tilde{\varepsilon}}:=\min\{\delta^{(1)}_{\tilde{\varepsilon}},\ldots,\delta^{(L)}_{\tilde{\varepsilon}}\}
.
If there exist
y_{i}\neq y_{j}
with
i,j\in\bigcup_{l=1}^{L}\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{c}}^{(l)}
, set
\delta_{y}:=\min\{|y_{i}-y_{j}|\penalty\ |\penalty\ y_{i}\neq y_{j},\penalty\ i,j\in\bigcup_{l=1}^{L}\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{c}}^{(l)}\}>0
, and
\delta:=\min\{\delta_{y},\delta_{\tilde{\varepsilon}}\}>0
.
Otherwise, set
\delta:=\delta_{\tilde{\varepsilon}}>0
.
13
13

13

Note that the latter case corresponds to only having observed one distinct continuous value overall.
This is included for completeness of the theory, but in practice is highly unlikely unless the number
N
of observations is very small (in which case regression results and results of statistical analyses.

Now, let
\mathcal{Z}^{(1)},\ldots,\mathcal{Z}^{(L)}
be partitions with
\Delta<\delta
and let
l\in\{1,\ldots,L\}
.
We have
\Delta^{(l)}\leq\Delta<\delta\leq\delta_{\tilde{\varepsilon}}\leq\delta^{(l)}_{\tilde{\varepsilon}}
, which yields
|\sigma(\mathcal{Z}^{(l)})-\int_{\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c}}}\exp(\tilde{f}_{\mathbf{x}^{(l)},\boldsymbol{\theta}})\,\mathrm{d}\lambda|<\tilde{\varepsilon}\leq\tilde{\varepsilon}^{(l)}
, implying
|\log(\sigma(\mathcal{Z}^{(l)})+\zeta^{(l)})-\log(\int_{\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c}}}\exp(\tilde{f}_{\mathbf{x}^{(l)},\boldsymbol{\theta}})\,\mathrm{d}\lambda+\zeta^{(l)})|<\frac{\varepsilon}{N}\penalty\ (\ast_{3})
.
Assume there exist
g\in\{1,\ldots,G^{(l)}\}
and
i,j\in\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{c}}^{(l)}
, such that

y_{i}\neq y_{j}
and

y_{i},y_{j}\in U_{g}^{(l)}
. Then, we have
|y_{i}-y_{j}|\leq a_{g}^{(l)}-a_{g-1}^{(l)}\leq\Delta<\delta\leq\delta_{y}
, which is a contradiction.
Thus, for each
g\in\{1,\ldots,G^{(l)}\}
, there exists at most one
y_{g}^{(l)}\in\{y_{i}\penalty\ |\penalty\ i\in\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{c}}^{(l)}\}
such that
y_{g}^{(l)}\in U_{g}^{(l)}
.
Then, for
g\in\{1,\ldots,G^{(l)}\}
,

n_{g}^{(l)}=\begin{cases}|\{i\in\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{c}}^{(l)}\penalty\ |\penalty\ y_{i}=y_{g}^{(l)}\}|&,\exists!\,y_{g}^{(l)}\in\{y_{i}\penalty\ |\penalty\ i\in\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{c}}^{(l)}\}:y_{g}^{(l)}\in U_{g}^{(l)}\\
0&,\text{otherwise}\end{cases}\,.

Recall that if such a unique
y_{g}^{(l)}
exists, we choose
u_{g}^{(l)}=y_{g}^{(l)}
.
Thus,
for each
i\in\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{c}}^{(l)}
, there exists a unique
g_{i}\in\{1,\ldots,G^{(l)}\}
such that
y_{i}=u_{g_{i}}^{(l)}
(and
n_{g_{i}}^{(l)}>0
), since the intervals
U_{g}^{(l)}
are disjoint for
g=1,\ldots,G^{(l)}
, and
as in argument
(\ast_{1})
in (
6.29
), for each
i\in\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{d}}^{(l)}
, there exists a unique
g_{i}\in\{G^{(l)}+1,\ldots,G^{(l)}+D\}
such that
y_{i}=t_{g_{i}-G^{(l)}}=u_{g_{i}}^{(l)}
.
Then, we get for the shifted multinomial log-likelihood,

\displaystyle\ell^{\mathrm{mn}}_{\mathcal{Z}}(\boldsymbol{\theta})

\displaystyle=\sum_{l=1}^{L}\sum_{g=1}^{G^{(l)}}n_{g}^{(l)}\Bigl(\tilde{f}_{\mathbf{x}^{(l)},\boldsymbol{\theta}}(u_{g}^{(l)})-\log\Sigma(\mathcal{Z}^{(l)})\Bigr)+

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt\sum_{l=1}^{L}\sum_{g=G^{(l)}+1}^{\Gamma^{(l)}}n_{g}^{(l)}\Bigl(\tilde{f}_{\mathbf{x}^{(l)},\boldsymbol{\theta}}(u_{g}^{(l)})-\log\Sigma(\mathcal{Z}^{(l)})\Bigr)

\displaystyle=\sum_{l=1}^{L}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{c}}^{(l)}}\Bigl(\tilde{f}_{\mathbf{x}_{i},\boldsymbol{\theta}}(y_{i})-\log\Sigma(\mathcal{Z}^{(l)})\Bigr)+\sum_{l=1}^{L}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{d}}^{(l)}}\Bigl(\tilde{f}_{\mathbf{x}_{i},\boldsymbol{\theta}}(y_{i})-\log\Sigma(\mathcal{Z}^{(l)})\Bigr)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{N}\tilde{f}_{\mathbf{x}_{i},\boldsymbol{\theta}}(y_{i})-\sum_{l=1}^{L}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}^{(l)}}\log\Sigma(\mathcal{Z}^{(l)}),

where we used in the last equation that
\mathcal{I}^{(l)}
form a partition of
\{1,\ldots,N\}
for
l=1,\ldots,L
, and
\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{c}}^{(l)}
and
\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{d}}^{(l)}
form a partition of
\mathcal{I}^{(l)}
for each
l=1,\ldots,L
.
Finally, we then have

\displaystyle\left|\ell^{\mathrm{mn}}_{\mathcal{Z}}(\boldsymbol{\theta})-\ell(\boldsymbol{\theta})\right|

\displaystyle=\Big|\sum_{i=1}^{N}\tilde{f}_{\mathbf{x}_{i},\boldsymbol{\theta}}(y_{i})-\sum_{l=1}^{L}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}^{(l)}}\log\Sigma(\mathcal{Z}^{(l)})\,-

\displaystyle\hskip 19.91684pt\Bigl(\sum_{i=1}^{N}\tilde{f}_{\mathbf{x}_{i},\boldsymbol{\theta}}(y_{i})-\sum_{i=1}^{N}\log\int_{\mathcal{Y}}\exp(\tilde{f}_{\mathbf{x}_{i},\boldsymbol{\theta}})\,\mathrm{d}\mu\Bigr)\Big|

\displaystyle=\Big|\sum_{l=1}^{L}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}^{(l)}}\Bigl(\log\bigl(\sigma(\mathcal{Z}^{(l)})+\int_{\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{d}}}\exp(\tilde{f}_{\mathbf{x}^{(l)},\boldsymbol{\theta}})\,\mathrm{d}\delta\bigr)-

\displaystyle\hskip 73.97733pt\log\bigl(\int_{\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c}}}\exp(\tilde{f}_{\mathbf{x}^{(l)},\boldsymbol{\theta}})\,\mathrm{d}\lambda+\int_{\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{d}}}\exp(\tilde{f}_{\mathbf{x}^{(l)},\boldsymbol{\theta}})\,\mathrm{d}\delta\Bigr)\Big|

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{l=1}^{L}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}^{(l)}}\Big|\Bigl(\log\bigl(\sigma(\mathcal{Z}^{(l)})+\zeta^{(l)}\bigr)-

\displaystyle\hskip 73.97733pt\log\bigl(\int_{\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c}}}\exp(\tilde{f}_{\mathbf{x}^{(l)},\boldsymbol{\theta}})\,\mathrm{d}\lambda+\zeta^{(l)}\bigr)\Bigr)\Big|

\displaystyle\overset{(\ast_{3})}{<}\sum_{l=1}^{L}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}^{(l)}}\frac{\varepsilon}{N}=N\frac{\varepsilon}{N}=\varepsilon,

which proves
\lim_{\Delta\rightarrow 0}\ell^{\mathrm{mn}}_{\mathcal{Z}}(\boldsymbol{\theta})=\ell(\boldsymbol{\theta})
and thus

\lim_{\Delta\rightarrow 0}\ell^{\mathrm{mn}}_{\mathcal{Z},\mathrm{pen}}(\boldsymbol{\theta})=\ell_{\mathrm{pen}}(\boldsymbol{\theta})
.

Now, we show the convergence of the Hessian matrices
\mathbf{H}^{\text{mn}}_{\mathcal{Z}}(\boldsymbol{\theta})
of
\ell^{\text{mn}}_{\mathcal{Z}}(\boldsymbol{\theta})
to the Hessian matrix
\mathbf{H}(\boldsymbol{\theta})
of
\ell(\boldsymbol{\theta})
for decreasing bin widths, which is equivalent to convergence of the (unpenalized) Fisher informations.
For this purpose, we use the notation
\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{[k]}
to denote the
k
-th entry of a vector
\boldsymbol{\gamma}
.
As in the proof of Proposition
6.6
, we have

\ell(\boldsymbol{\theta})=\sum_{i=1}^{N}[\tilde{\mathbf{b}}(\mathbf{x}_{i})(y_{i})^{\top}\boldsymbol{\theta}-C_{i}(\boldsymbol{\theta})]

with Hessian matrix
\mathbf{H}(\boldsymbol{\theta})=\sum_{i=1}^{N}\mathbf{H}_{i}(\boldsymbol{\theta})
,
where
\mathbf{H}_{i}(\boldsymbol{\theta})\in\mathbb{R}^{K\times K}
denotes the Hessian matrix of

C_{i}(\boldsymbol{\theta})=-\log\int_{\mathcal{Y}}\exp[\tilde{\mathbf{b}}(\mathbf{x}_{i})^{\top}\boldsymbol{\theta}]\,\mathrm{d}\mu
for
i=1,\ldots,N
.
By (
6.7
), the
(k,k^{\prime})
-th element of
\mathbf{H}_{i}(\boldsymbol{\theta}),\penalty\ i=1,\ldots,N
, is

\displaystyle\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial\theta_{k}\partial\theta_{k^{\prime}}}C_{i}(\boldsymbol{\theta})

\displaystyle=-\frac{\int_{\mathcal{Y}}\exp[\tilde{\mathbf{b}}(\mathbf{x}_{i})^{\top}\boldsymbol{\theta}]\,(\tilde{\mathbf{b}}(\mathbf{x}_{i}))_{[k]}\,(\tilde{\mathbf{b}}(\mathbf{x}_{i}))_{[k^{\prime}]}\,\mathrm{d}\mu}{\int_{\mathcal{Y}}\exp[\tilde{\mathbf{b}}(\mathbf{x}_{i})^{\top}\boldsymbol{\theta}]\,\mathrm{d}\mu}

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt+\frac{\int_{\mathcal{Y}}\exp[\tilde{\mathbf{b}}(\mathbf{x}_{i})^{\top}\boldsymbol{\theta}]\cdot(\tilde{\mathbf{b}}(\mathbf{x}_{i}))_{[k]}\,\mathrm{d}\mu}{\int_{\mathcal{Y}}\exp[\tilde{\mathbf{b}}(\mathbf{x}_{i})^{\top}\boldsymbol{\theta}]\,\mathrm{d}\mu}\,\frac{\int_{\mathcal{Y}}\exp[\tilde{\mathbf{b}}(\mathbf{x}_{i})^{\top}\boldsymbol{\theta}]\cdot(\tilde{\mathbf{b}}(\mathbf{x}_{i}))_{[k^{\prime}]}\,\mathrm{d}\mu}{\int_{\mathcal{Y}}\exp[\tilde{\mathbf{b}}(\mathbf{x}_{i})^{\top}\boldsymbol{\theta}]\,\mathrm{d}\mu}\,.

Similarly, consider

\ell^{\text{mn}}_{\mathcal{Z}}(\boldsymbol{\theta})=\sum_{l=1}^{L}\sum_{g=1}^{\Gamma^{(l)}}n_{g}^{(l)}[\tilde{\mathbf{b}}(\mathbf{x}^{(l)})(u_{g}^{(l)})^{\top}\boldsymbol{\theta}-C_{\mathcal{Z}}^{\text{mn}\,(s)}(\boldsymbol{\theta})]

with Hessian matrix

\mathbf{H}^{\text{mn}}_{\mathcal{Z}}(\boldsymbol{\theta})=\sum_{l=1}^{L}\sum_{g=1}^{\Gamma^{(l)}}n_{g}^{(l)}\mathbf{H}_{\mathcal{Z}}^{\text{mn}\,(s)}(\boldsymbol{\theta})

where
\mathbf{H}_{\mathcal{Z}}^{\text{mn}\,(s)}(\boldsymbol{\theta})

denotes the Hessian matrix of

C_{\mathcal{Z}}^{\text{mn}\,(s)}(\boldsymbol{\theta})=-\log\sum_{g^{\prime}=1}^{\Gamma^{(l)}}\Delta_{g^{\prime}}^{(l)}\exp[\tilde{\mathbf{b}}(\mathbf{x}^{(l)})(u_{g^{\prime}}^{(l)})^{\top}\boldsymbol{\theta}]

for
l=1,\ldots,L
.
By (
6.10
), the
(k,k^{\prime})
-th element of
\mathbf{H}_{\mathcal{Z}}^{\text{mn}\,(s)}(\boldsymbol{\theta})
is

\displaystyle\penalty\ \frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial\theta_{k}\partial\theta_{k^{\prime}}}C_{\mathcal{Z}}^{\text{mn}\,(s)}(\boldsymbol{\theta})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\penalty\ -\frac{\sum_{g^{\prime}=1}^{\Gamma^{(l)}}\bigl[\Delta_{g^{\prime}}^{(l)}\exp[\tilde{\mathbf{b}}(\mathbf{x}^{(l)})(u_{g^{\prime}}^{(l)})^{\top}\boldsymbol{\theta}]\,(\tilde{\mathbf{b}}(\mathbf{x}^{(l)})(u_{g^{\prime}}^{(l)}))_{[k]}\,(\tilde{\mathbf{b}}(\mathbf{x}^{(l)})(u_{g^{\prime}}^{(l)}))_{[k^{\prime}]}\bigr]}{\sum_{g^{\prime}=1}^{\Gamma^{(l)}}\Delta_{g^{\prime}}^{(l)}\exp[\tilde{\mathbf{b}}(\mathbf{x}^{(l)})(u_{g^{\prime}}^{(l)})^{\top}\boldsymbol{\theta}]}

\displaystyle\penalty\ +\frac{\sum_{g^{\prime}=1}^{\Gamma^{(l)}}\bigl[\Delta_{g^{\prime}}^{(l)}\exp[\tilde{\mathbf{b}}(\mathbf{x}^{(l)})(u_{g^{\prime}}^{(l)})^{\top}\boldsymbol{\theta}]\,(\tilde{\mathbf{b}}(\mathbf{x}^{(l)})(u_{g^{\prime}}^{(l)}))_{[k]}\bigr]}{\sum_{g^{\prime}=1}^{\Gamma^{(l)}}\Delta_{g^{\prime}}^{(l)}\exp[\tilde{\mathbf{b}}(\mathbf{x}^{(l)})(u_{g^{\prime}}^{(l)})^{\top}\boldsymbol{\theta}]}

\displaystyle\penalty\ \cdot\frac{\sum_{g^{\prime}=1}^{\Gamma^{(l)}}\bigl[\Delta_{g^{\prime}}^{(l)}\exp[\tilde{\mathbf{b}}(\mathbf{x}^{(l)})(u_{g^{\prime}}^{(l)})^{\top}\boldsymbol{\theta}]\,(\tilde{\mathbf{b}}(\mathbf{x}^{(l)})(u_{g^{\prime}}^{(l)}))_{[k^{\prime}]}\bigr]\,}{\sum_{g^{\prime}=1}^{\Gamma^{(l)}}\Delta_{g^{\prime}}^{(l)}\exp[\tilde{\mathbf{b}}(\mathbf{x}^{(l)})(u_{g^{\prime}}^{(l)})^{\top}\boldsymbol{\theta}]}.

As in (
6.28
), we split each of the appearing sums over
g^{\prime}=1,\ldots,\Gamma^{(l)}
into the respecting sums over
g^{\prime}=1,\ldots,G^{(l)}
and over
g^{\prime}=G^{(l)}+1,\ldots,\Gamma^{(l)}
.
In each case, the former sum is a Riemann sum of the corresponding integrable function and thus converges against the function’s integral over
\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c}}
with respect to
\lambda
for
\Delta\rightarrow 0
, while the latter sum equals the integral over
\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{d}}
with respect to
\delta
.
The sum of both integrals corresponds to the integral over
\mathcal{Y}
with respect to
\mu
.
Note that the limiting integral in the denominators,
\int_{\mathcal{Y}}\exp[\tilde{\mathbf{b}}(\mathbf{x}^{(l)})^{\top}\boldsymbol{\theta}]\,\mathrm{d}\mu
, is positive and finite, while the integrals in the numerators are also finite, since all integrands are finite on
\mathcal{Y}
and
\mu(\mathcal{Y})<\infty
.
Thus, sums, products, and quotients are preserved under the limit
(
Laczkovich & Sós 2015a
, Theorems 5.12, 5.14 and 5.16)
and the limit of (
6.10
) for
\Delta\rightarrow 0
is

\displaystyle-\frac{\int_{\mathcal{Y}}\exp[\tilde{\mathbf{b}}(\mathbf{x}^{(l)})^{\top}\boldsymbol{\theta}]\,(\tilde{\mathbf{b}}(\mathbf{x}^{(l)}))_{[k]}\,(\tilde{\mathbf{b}}(\mathbf{x}^{(l)}))_{[k^{\prime}]}\,\mathrm{d}\mu}{\int_{\mathcal{Y}}\exp[\tilde{\mathbf{b}}(\mathbf{x}^{(l)})^{\top}\boldsymbol{\theta}]\,\mathrm{d}\mu}

\displaystyle+\frac{\int_{\mathcal{Y}}\exp[\tilde{\mathbf{b}}(\mathbf{x}^{(l)})^{\top}\boldsymbol{\theta}]\,(\tilde{\mathbf{b}}(\mathbf{x}^{(l)}))_{[k]}\,\mathrm{d}\mu}{\int_{\mathcal{Y}}\exp[\tilde{\mathbf{b}}(\mathbf{x}^{(l)})^{\top}\boldsymbol{\theta}]\,\mathrm{d}\mu}\,\frac{\int_{\mathcal{Y}}\exp[\tilde{\mathbf{b}}(\mathbf{x}^{(l)})^{\top}\boldsymbol{\theta}]\,(\tilde{\mathbf{b}}(\mathbf{x}^{(l)}))_{[k^{\prime}]}\,\mathrm{d}\mu}{\int_{\mathcal{Y}}\exp[\tilde{\mathbf{b}}(\mathbf{x}^{(l)})^{\top}\boldsymbol{\theta}]\,\mathrm{d}\mu}.

Finally, we then have

\displaystyle\lim_{\Delta\rightarrow 0}\mathbf{H}^{\text{mn}}_{\mathcal{Z}}(\boldsymbol{\theta})=\sum_{l=1}^{L}|\mathcal{I}^{(l)}|\,\lim_{\Delta\rightarrow 0}\mathbf{H}_{\mathcal{Z}}^{\text{mn}\,(s)}(\boldsymbol{\theta})=\sum_{i=1}^{N}\mathbf{H}_{i}(\boldsymbol{\theta})=\mathbf{H}(\boldsymbol{\theta}),

where we used
\mathbf{x}_{i}=\mathbf{x}^{(l)}
for all
i\in\mathcal{I}^{(l)}
.
Since the Hessian matrices of the penalized log-likelihoods
\ell^{\mathrm{mn}}_{\mathcal{Z},\mathrm{pen}}(\boldsymbol{\theta})
and
\ell_{\mathrm{pen}}(\boldsymbol{\theta})
are given by
\mathbf{H}^{\text{mn}}_{\mathcal{Z}}(\boldsymbol{\theta})+D^{2}\pen(\boldsymbol{\theta})
and
\mathbf{H}(\boldsymbol{\theta})+D^{2}\pen(\boldsymbol{\theta})
, where
D^{2}\pen(\boldsymbol{\theta})
is constant in
\Delta
, we obtain
\lim_{\Delta\rightarrow 0}\mathbf{F}^{\text{mn}}_{\mathcal{Z},\mathrm{pen}}(\boldsymbol{\theta})=\mathbf{F}_{\mathrm{pen}}(\boldsymbol{\theta})
.

Now assume, all involved penalized Fisher informations are invertible.
Since the matrix inversion function
\inv:\GL(K,\mathbb{R})\rightarrow\GL(K,\mathbb{R}),\,\mathbf{F}\mapsto\mathbf{F}^{-1}
is continuous (compare Lemma
6.7
), we obtain
\lim_{\Delta\rightarrow 0}(\mathbf{F}^{\text{mn}}_{\mathcal{Z},\mathrm{pen}}(\boldsymbol{\theta}))^{-1}=(\mathbf{F}_{\mathrm{pen}}(\boldsymbol{\theta}))^{-1}
. ∎

Proof of Theorem
2.5
.

1)

The uniqueness of
\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{\mathcal{Z}}^{\mathrm{mn}}
follows completely analogously to the proof of the uniqueness of
\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}
in Theorem
2.1
, using Proposition
6.6

2)
to obtain strict concavity of
\ell_{\mathcal{Z},\pen}^{\mathrm{mn}}(\boldsymbol{\theta})
on
\mathbb{R}^{K}
.

2)

For
m\in\mathbb{N}
, consider
\mathcal{Z}_{m}\in\Xi^{L}
with
\lim_{m\rightarrow\infty}\Delta(\mathcal{Z}_{m})=0
.
We use
Rockafellar & Wets 2009a
to obtain asymptotic existence and convergence of the minimizers of
-\ell_{\mathcal{Z}_{m},\mathrm{pen}}^{\mathrm{mn}}
, which correspond to the PMLEs of
\ell_{\mathcal{Z}_{m},\mathrm{pen}}^{\mathrm{mn}}
.
The theorem requires that
-\ell_{\mathcal{Z}_{m},\mathrm{pen}}^{\mathrm{mn}}
and
-\ell_{\mathrm{pen}}
are lower semicontinuous and proper and that the sequence of functions
(-\ell_{\mathcal{Z}_{m},\mathrm{pen}}^{\mathrm{mn}})_{m\in\mathbb{N}}
is eventually level-bounded and epi-converges to
\ell_{\mathrm{pen}}
.
We already showed in the proof of Theorem
2.1
that
-\ell_{\mathrm{pen}}
is lower semicontinuous and proper.
For the functions
-\ell_{\mathcal{Z}_{m},\mathrm{pen}}^{\mathrm{mn}}
this follows analogously.
Furthermore, by Proposition
6.6
,
-\ell_{\mathcal{Z}_{m},\mathrm{pen}}^{\mathrm{mn}}
and
-\ell_{\mathrm{pen}}
are convex on
\mathbb{R}^{K}
(using assumptions
(BX)
and
(BU)
).
Together with
\dom(-\ell_{\mathrm{pen}})=\mathbb{R}^{K}
having nonempty interior
and
\lim_{m\rightarrow\infty}(-\ell_{\mathcal{Z}_{m},\mathrm{pen}}^{\mathrm{mn}}(\boldsymbol{\theta}))=-\ell_{\mathrm{pen}}(\boldsymbol{\theta})
for all
\boldsymbol{\theta}\in\mathbb{R}^{K}
(Theorem
2.4
),
this implies that
-\ell_{\mathcal{Z}_{m},\mathrm{pen}}^{\mathrm{mn}}
epi-converges to
-\ell_{\mathrm{pen}}

(
Rockafellar & Wets 2009a
, Theorem 7.17)
.
Moreover, by Theorem
6.10
,
-\ell_{\mathrm{pen}}
is level-bounded (using assumption
(S

<

)
).
Then, the sequence
(-\ell_{\mathcal{Z}_{m},\mathrm{pen}}^{\mathrm{mn}})_{m\in\mathbb{N}}
is eventually level-bounded
(
Rockafellar & Wets 2009a
, Exercise 7.32 (c))
.
As
\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}
exists and is unique by Theorem
2.1
, we obtain from
Rockafellar & Wets 2009a
, that there exists an
M\in\mathbb{N}
such that the minimizer
\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{\mathrm{mn}}_{\mathcal{Z}_{m}}
of
-\ell_{\mathcal{Z}_{m},\mathrm{pen}}^{\mathrm{mn}}(\boldsymbol{\theta})
exists (and is unique by
1)
, using assumption
(BU)
) for all
m\geq M
and
\lim_{m\rightarrow\infty}\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{\mathcal{Z}_{m}}^{\mathrm{mn}}=\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}
.
∎

Proof of Theorem
2.6
.

Note that
Baker 1994c
provide a proof of this Theorem for unpenalized estimation, which might be extended to our penalized setting.
When we became aware of their work, we already derived the following proof, which pursues a different strategy.
While
Baker 1994c
follow a two-step/substitution approach regarding maximization of
\boldsymbol{\tau}
and
\boldsymbol{\theta}
and use results of
Richards 1961a
, our proof uses a parameter transformation as proposed by
Palmgren 1981a
.

We first show that the PMLEs are identical, extending the work of
Palmgren 1981a
for a linear predictor
\eta_{g}^{(l)}(\boldsymbol{\theta})
without penalization to an arbitrary predictor with penalized estimation.
Setting
\Pen(\boldsymbol{\theta}):=\exp(\pen(\boldsymbol{\theta}))
, the penalized likelihood function corresponding to the Poisson distribution assumption
1)
is

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{pen}}^{\mathrm{po}}(\boldsymbol{\theta},\boldsymbol{\tau})

\displaystyle=\prod_{l=1}^{L}\prod_{g=1}^{\Gamma^{(l)}}\biggl(\frac{\lambda_{g}^{(l)}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta},\boldsymbol{\tau}\right)^{n_{g}^{(l)}}}{n_{g}^{(l)}!}\exp\left(-\lambda_{g}^{(l)}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta},\boldsymbol{\tau}\right)\right)\biggr)\,\Pen(\boldsymbol{\theta}).

(6.30)

Consider the transformation

\psi:\mathbb{R}^{K}\times\mathbb{R}^{L}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{K}\times(\mathbb{R}^{+})^{L},(\boldsymbol{\theta},\boldsymbol{\tau})\mapsto(\boldsymbol{\theta},\boldsymbol{\lambda}),

with
\boldsymbol{\lambda}=(\lambda^{(1)},\ldots,\lambda^{(L)})
and
\lambda^{(l)}:=\sum_{g=1}^{\Gamma^{(l)}}\lambda_{g}^{(l)}(\boldsymbol{\theta},\boldsymbol{\tau})=\sum_{g=1}^{\Gamma^{(l)}}\exp(\eta_{g}^{(l)}(\boldsymbol{\theta})+\tau^{(l)})
for
l=1,\ldots,L
.
The transformation is bijective with inverse transformation

\psi^{-1}:\mathbb{R}^{K}\times(\mathbb{R}^{+})^{L}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{K}\times\mathbb{R}^{L},(\boldsymbol{\theta},\boldsymbol{\lambda})\mapsto(\boldsymbol{\theta},\boldsymbol{\tau}),

with
\tau^{(l)}:=\log\lambda^{(l)}-\log(\sum_{g=1}^{\Gamma^{(l)}}\exp(\eta_{g}^{(l)}(\boldsymbol{\theta})))
for
l=1,\ldots,L
.
Thus,
\psi
and
\psi^{-1}
preserve maximizers of the penalized likelihood (
6.30
).
Reparameterizing
(\boldsymbol{\theta},\boldsymbol{\tau})=\psi^{-1}(\boldsymbol{\theta},\boldsymbol{\lambda})
yields

\displaystyle\lambda_{g}^{(l)}(\boldsymbol{\theta},\boldsymbol{\tau})=\lambda_{g}^{(l)}\bigl(\psi^{-1}(\boldsymbol{\theta},\boldsymbol{\lambda})\bigr)

\displaystyle=\frac{\exp\bigl(\eta_{g}^{(l)}(\boldsymbol{\theta})\bigr)\,\lambda^{(l)}}{\sum_{g^{\prime}=1}^{\Gamma^{(l)}}\exp\bigl(\eta_{g^{\prime}}^{(l)}(\boldsymbol{\theta})\bigr)}

(6.31)

and
\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{pen}}^{\mathrm{po}}\circ\psi^{-1}
gives us the penalized likelihood

\displaystyle\check{\mathcal{L}}_{\mathrm{pen}}^{\mathrm{po}}(\boldsymbol{\theta},\boldsymbol{\lambda})

\displaystyle=\prod_{l=1}^{L}\prod_{g=1}^{\Gamma^{(l)}}\Biggl(\frac{\left(\frac{\exp(\eta_{g}^{(l)}(\boldsymbol{\theta}))\,\lambda^{(l)}}{\sum_{g^{\prime}=1}^{\Gamma^{(l)}}\exp(\eta_{g^{\prime}}^{(l)}(\boldsymbol{\theta}))}\right)^{n_{g}^{(l)}}}{n_{g}^{(l)}!}\,\exp\Biggl(\frac{-\exp\left(\eta_{g}^{(l)}(\boldsymbol{\theta})\right)\,\lambda^{(l)}}{\sum_{g^{\prime}=1}^{\Gamma^{(l)}}\exp\left(\eta_{g^{\prime}}^{(l)}(\boldsymbol{\theta})\right)}\Biggr)\Biggr)\,\Pen(\boldsymbol{\theta})

\displaystyle=\prod_{l=1}^{L}\Biggl(\frac{n^{(l)}!}{\prod_{g=1}^{\Gamma^{(l)}}n_{g}^{(l)}!}\prod_{g=1}^{\Gamma^{(l)}}\Biggl(\frac{\exp\bigl(\eta_{g}^{(l)}(\boldsymbol{\theta})\bigr)}{\sum_{g^{\prime}=1}^{\Gamma^{(l)}}\exp\bigl(\eta_{g^{\prime}}^{(l)}(\boldsymbol{\theta})\bigr)}\Biggr)^{n_{g}^{(l)}}\Biggr)\,\Pen(\boldsymbol{\theta})

\displaystyle\hskip 19.91684pt\cdot\prod_{l=1}^{L}\Bigl(\frac{(\lambda^{(l)})^{n^{(l)}}}{n^{(l)}!}\exp(-\lambda^{(l)})\Bigr),

(6.32)

where we used
\sum_{g=1}^{\Gamma^{(l)}}n_{g}^{(l)}=n^{(l)}
in the last equation.
Thus, the penalized likelihood
\check{\mathcal{L}}_{\mathrm{pen}}^{\mathrm{po}}(\boldsymbol{\theta},\boldsymbol{\lambda})
factorizes into two functions, one modeling
\boldsymbol{\theta}
and the other modeling
\boldsymbol{\lambda}
, which we denote with
\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{pen}}^{\mathrm{mn}}(\boldsymbol{\theta})
and
\mathcal{L}^{\mathrm{po}}(\boldsymbol{\lambda})
. To maximize the overall likelihood, we can maximize both parts separately.
The first part
\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{pen}}^{\mathrm{mn}}(\boldsymbol{\theta})
is the penalized likelihood of a multinomial distribution with probabilities

p_{g}^{(l)}(\boldsymbol{\theta})=\frac{\exp(\eta_{g}^{(l)}(\boldsymbol{\theta}))}{\sum_{g^{\prime}=1}^{\Gamma^{(l)}}\exp(\eta_{g^{\prime}}^{(l)}(\boldsymbol{\theta}))}
and observations
\mathbf{n}^{(l)}
, i.e., maximization yields the multinomial PMLE
\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{\mathrm{mn}}
for
\boldsymbol{\theta}
.
The second part
\mathcal{L}^{\mathrm{po}}(\boldsymbol{\lambda})
yields the Poisson MLEs
\hat{\lambda}^{(l)}
for
\lambda^{(l)}
based on one observation
n^{(l)}
, i.e.,
\hat{\lambda}^{(l)}=n^{(l)}
for
l=1,\ldots,L
. Then, due to the invariance of the estimators under
\psi
and
\psi^{-1}
, the PMLEs for
\boldsymbol{\theta}
are identical,
\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{\mathrm{po}}=\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{\mathrm{mn}}
.

We now show the statements on the inverse Fisher informations.
Here, our contribution goes well beyond the work of
Palmgren 1981a
, who, for a linear predictor without penalization, compares the Fisher informations corresponding to
\check{\mathcal{L}}^{\mathrm{po}}
and
\mathcal{L}^{\mathrm{mn}}
(denoting the likelihoods
\check{\mathcal{L}}_{\mathrm{pen}}^{\mathrm{po}}
and
\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{pen}}^{\mathrm{mn}}
without penalization).
We take the additional step of considering the Fisher informations corresponding to
\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{pen}}^{\mathrm{po}}=\check{\mathcal{L}}_{\mathrm{pen}}^{\mathrm{po}}\circ\psi
, which is of great importance for our work, since in practice, our models are estimated on this level.
Denote the (penalized) log-likelihoods corresponding to the (penalized) likelihoods considered so far with
\ell
and the respective indices etc. and set
\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}:=\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{\mathrm{po}}=\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{\mathrm{mn}}
.
Furthermore, denote the differential operators for first and second order derivatives with respect to the full argument vector of a function with
D
and
D^{2}
and with respect to some subvector
\boldsymbol{\gamma}
with
D_{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}
and
D^{2}_{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}
.
Transforming (
6.32
) to the level of log-likelihoods yields

\displaystyle\check{\ell}_{\mathrm{pen}}^{\mathrm{po}}(\boldsymbol{\theta},\boldsymbol{\lambda})=\ell_{\mathrm{pen}}^{\mathrm{mn}}(\boldsymbol{\theta})+\ell^{\mathrm{po}}(\boldsymbol{\lambda}).

(6.33)

Since
\ell_{\mathrm{pen}}^{\mathrm{mn}}(\boldsymbol{\theta})
is independent of
\boldsymbol{\lambda}
, while
\ell^{\mathrm{po}}(\boldsymbol{\lambda})
is independent of
\boldsymbol{\theta}
,
we obtain a block diagonal form for the Hessian matrix of the penalized log-likelihood
\check{\ell}_{\mathrm{pen}}^{\mathrm{po}}(\boldsymbol{\theta},\boldsymbol{\lambda})
,

\displaystyle D^{2}\check{\ell}_{\mathrm{pen}}^{\mathrm{po}}(\boldsymbol{\theta},\boldsymbol{\lambda})=\begin{pmatrix}D^{2}\ell_{\mathrm{pen}}^{\mathrm{mn}}(\boldsymbol{\theta})&\mathbf{0}\\
\mathbf{0}&D^{2}\ell^{\mathrm{po}}(\boldsymbol{\lambda})\end{pmatrix}.

(6.34)

In particular,
D^{2}_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}\check{\ell}_{\mathrm{pen}}^{\mathrm{po}}(\boldsymbol{\theta},\boldsymbol{\lambda})=D^{2}\ell_{\mathrm{pen}}^{\mathrm{mn}}(\boldsymbol{\theta})
, i.e., the upper left
K\times K
submatrix of the Fisher information corresponding to
\check{\ell}_{\mathrm{pen}}^{\mathrm{po}}(\boldsymbol{\theta},\boldsymbol{\lambda})
is identical to the Fisher information obtained from the multinomial distributional assumption
2)
for all

\boldsymbol{\theta}\in\mathbb{R}^{K}
. To obtain the Hessian matrix of the penalized log-likelihood
\ell_{\mathrm{pen}}^{\mathrm{po}}=\check{\ell}_{\mathrm{pen}}^{\mathrm{po}}\circ\psi
, we apply the chain rule, which gives

\displaystyle D\ell_{\mathrm{pen}}^{\mathrm{po}}(\boldsymbol{\theta},\boldsymbol{\tau})

\displaystyle=[D\check{\ell}_{\mathrm{pen}}^{\mathrm{po}}\circ\psi](\boldsymbol{\theta},\boldsymbol{\tau})\,D\psi(\boldsymbol{\theta},\boldsymbol{\tau}),

\displaystyle D^{2}\ell_{\mathrm{pen}}^{\mathrm{po}}(\boldsymbol{\theta},\boldsymbol{\tau})

\displaystyle=(D\psi(\boldsymbol{\theta},\boldsymbol{\tau}))^{\top}\,[D^{2}\check{\ell}_{\mathrm{pen}}^{\mathrm{po}}\circ\psi](\boldsymbol{\theta},\boldsymbol{\tau})\,D\psi(\boldsymbol{\theta},\boldsymbol{\tau})

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt+[D\check{\ell}_{\mathrm{pen}}^{\mathrm{po}}\circ\psi](\boldsymbol{\theta},\boldsymbol{\tau})\,D^{2}\psi(\boldsymbol{\theta},\boldsymbol{\tau}),

(6.35)

where
D^{2}\psi(\boldsymbol{\theta},\boldsymbol{\tau})=(D^{2}\psi_{1}(\boldsymbol{\theta},\boldsymbol{\tau}),\ldots,D^{2}\psi_{K+L}(\boldsymbol{\theta},\boldsymbol{\tau}))^{\top}
.
Inserting
(\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}},\hat{\boldsymbol{\tau}})
, the last summand is zero since
\psi(\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}},\hat{\boldsymbol{\tau}})
maximizes
\check{\ell}_{\mathrm{pen}}^{\mathrm{po}}
and thus

\displaystyle D^{2}\ell_{\mathrm{pen}}^{\mathrm{po}}(\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}},\hat{\boldsymbol{\tau}})

\displaystyle=(D\psi(\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}},\hat{\boldsymbol{\tau}})^{\top}\,[D^{2}\check{\ell}_{\mathrm{pen}}^{\mathrm{po}}\circ\psi](\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}},\hat{\boldsymbol{\tau}})\,D\psi(\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}},\hat{\boldsymbol{\tau}}).

(6.36)

Setting

\psi_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}:\mathbb{R}^{K}\times\mathbb{R}^{L}\rightarrow(\mathbb{R}^{+})^{L}
,
(\boldsymbol{\theta},\boldsymbol{\tau})\mapsto\boldsymbol{\lambda}=(\psi_{K+1}(\boldsymbol{\theta},\boldsymbol{\tau}),\ldots,\psi_{K+L}(\boldsymbol{\theta},\boldsymbol{\tau}))
,
we have

\displaystyle D\psi(\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}},\hat{\boldsymbol{\tau}})=\begin{pmatrix}\mathbf{I}_{K}&\mathbf{0}\\
D_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}\psi_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}(\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}},\hat{\boldsymbol{\tau}})&D_{\boldsymbol{\tau}}\psi_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}(\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}},\hat{\boldsymbol{\tau}})\end{pmatrix},

(6.37)

which at
(\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}},\hat{\boldsymbol{\tau}})
(omitting the argument in all functions for the sake of readability) yields

\displaystyle D^{2}\ell_{\mathrm{pen}}^{\mathrm{po}}

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}D^{2}\ell^{\mathrm{mn}}_{\mathrm{pen}}+(D_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}\psi_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}})^{\top}\,[D^{2}\ell^{\mathrm{po}}\circ\psi_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}]\,D_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}\psi_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}&(D_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}\psi_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}})^{\top}\,[D^{2}\ell^{\mathrm{po}}\circ\psi_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}]\,D_{\boldsymbol{\tau}}\psi_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}\\
(D_{\boldsymbol{\tau}}\psi_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}})^{\top}\,[D^{2}\ell^{\mathrm{po}}\circ\psi_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}]\,D_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}\psi_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}&(D_{\boldsymbol{\tau}}\psi_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}})^{\top}\,[D^{2}\ell^{\mathrm{po}}\circ\psi_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}]\,D_{\boldsymbol{\tau}}\psi_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}\end{pmatrix}.

(6.38)

In particular,
(\hat{\mathbf{F}}^{\mathrm{po}})_{[K]}\neq\hat{\mathbf{F}}^{\mathrm{mn}}
.
To show the equality for the inverted matrices, note that (
6.36
) yields

\displaystyle(D^{2}\ell_{\mathrm{pen}}^{\mathrm{po}}(\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}},\hat{\boldsymbol{\tau}}))^{-1}=(D\psi(\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}},\hat{\boldsymbol{\tau}}))^{-1}\,([D^{2}\check{\ell}_{\mathrm{pen}}^{\mathrm{po}}\circ\psi](\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}},\hat{\boldsymbol{\tau}}))^{-1}\,((D\psi(\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}},\hat{\boldsymbol{\tau}}))^{\top})^{-1}.

Since the block matrix structure of (
6.34
) is preserved under invertation, we have

\displaystyle\left([D^{2}\check{\ell}_{\mathrm{pen}}^{\mathrm{po}}\circ\psi](\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}},\hat{\boldsymbol{\tau}})\right)^{-1}

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}(D^{2}\ell_{\mathrm{pen}}^{\mathrm{mn}}(\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}))^{-1}&\mathbf{0}\\
\mathbf{0}&([D^{2}\ell^{\mathrm{po}}\circ\psi_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}](\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}},\hat{\boldsymbol{\tau}}))^{-1}\end{pmatrix}.

Furthermore,

\displaystyle\left(D\psi(\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}},\hat{\boldsymbol{\tau}})\right)^{-1}

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}\mathbf{I}_{K}&\mathbf{0}\\
-(D_{\boldsymbol{\tau}}\psi_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}(\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}},\hat{\boldsymbol{\tau}}))^{-1}\,D_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}\psi_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}(\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}},\hat{\boldsymbol{\tau}})&(D_{\boldsymbol{\tau}}\psi_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}(\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}},\hat{\boldsymbol{\tau}}))^{-1}\end{pmatrix}.

(6.39)

Setting
\hat{\mathbf{A}}=(D_{\boldsymbol{\tau}}\psi_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}(\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}},\hat{\boldsymbol{\tau}}))^{-1}\,D_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}\psi_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}(\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}},\hat{\boldsymbol{\tau}})
and
\hat{\mathbf{B}}:=([D^{2}\ell^{\mathrm{po}}\circ\psi_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}](\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}},\hat{\boldsymbol{\tau}}))^{-1}
, we obtain for the inverse of
D^{2}\ell^{\mathrm{po}}_{\mathrm{pen}}(\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}},\hat{\boldsymbol{\tau}})
(again omitting the argument
(\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}},\hat{\boldsymbol{\tau}})
in all functions)

\displaystyle(D^{2}\ell_{\mathrm{pen}}^{\mathrm{po}})^{-1}

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}(D^{2}\ell_{\mathrm{pen}}^{\mathrm{mn}})^{-1}&-(D^{2}\ell_{\mathrm{pen}}^{\mathrm{mn}})^{-1}\,\hat{\mathbf{A}}^{\top}\\
-\hat{\mathbf{A}}\,(D^{2}\ell^{\mathrm{mn}}_{\mathrm{pen}})^{-1}&\hat{\mathbf{A}}\,(D^{2}\ell^{\mathrm{mn}}_{\mathrm{pen}})^{-1}\,\hat{\mathbf{A}}^{\top}+(D_{\boldsymbol{\tau}}\psi_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}})^{-1}\,\hat{\mathbf{B}}\,((D_{\boldsymbol{\tau}}\psi_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}})^{-1})^{\top}\end{pmatrix},

which shows
((\hat{\mathbf{F}}^{\mathrm{po}})^{-1})_{[K]}=(\hat{\mathbf{F}}^{\mathrm{mn}})^{-1}
.
Taking the expectation
\mathbb{E}^{\mathrm{po}}_{\boldsymbol{\theta},\boldsymbol{\tau}}
of (
6.35
) yields

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}^{\mathrm{po}}_{\boldsymbol{\theta},\boldsymbol{\tau}}(D^{2}\ell_{\mathrm{pen}}^{\mathrm{po}}(\boldsymbol{\theta},\boldsymbol{\tau}))

\displaystyle=(D\psi(\boldsymbol{\theta},\boldsymbol{\tau})^{\top}\,\mathbb{E}^{\mathrm{po}}_{\boldsymbol{\theta},\boldsymbol{\tau}}\left([D\check{\ell}_{\mathrm{pen}}^{\mathrm{po}}\circ\psi](\boldsymbol{\theta},\boldsymbol{\tau})\right)\,D^{2}\psi(\boldsymbol{\theta},\boldsymbol{\tau})\,D\psi(\boldsymbol{\theta},\boldsymbol{\tau})

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt+\mathbb{E}^{\mathrm{po}}_{\boldsymbol{\theta},\boldsymbol{\tau}}\left([D\check{\ell}_{\mathrm{pen}}^{\mathrm{po}}\circ\psi](\boldsymbol{\theta},\boldsymbol{\tau})\right)\,D^{2}\psi(\boldsymbol{\theta},\boldsymbol{\tau}),

where the last summand is zero.
To see this, first note that the
(K+1)
-th to
(K+L)
-th elements in the row vector

\mathbb{E}^{\mathrm{po}}_{\boldsymbol{\theta},\boldsymbol{\tau}}([D\check{\ell}_{\mathrm{pen}}^{\mathrm{po}}\circ\psi](\boldsymbol{\theta},\boldsymbol{\tau}))
, are

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}^{\mathrm{po}}_{\boldsymbol{\theta},\boldsymbol{\tau}}\left(\frac{\partial}{\partial\lambda^{(l)}}\ell^{\mathrm{po}}(\psi_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}(\boldsymbol{\theta},\boldsymbol{\tau}))\right)=\frac{\sum_{g=1}^{\Gamma^{(l)}}\mathbb{E}^{\mathrm{po}}_{\boldsymbol{\theta},\boldsymbol{\tau}}(N_{g}^{(l)})}{\psi_{K+s}(\boldsymbol{\theta},\boldsymbol{\tau})}-1=\frac{\sum_{g=1}^{\Gamma^{(l)}}\lambda_{g}^{(l)}(\boldsymbol{\theta},\boldsymbol{\tau})}{\sum_{g=1}^{\Gamma^{(l)}}\lambda_{g}^{(l)}(\boldsymbol{\theta},\boldsymbol{\tau})}-1=0,

where
l=1,\ldots,L
.
Furthermore,
D^{2}\psi_{k}(\boldsymbol{\theta},\boldsymbol{\tau})=\mathbf{0}
for
k=1,\ldots,K
, i.e., the first
K
elements of
D^{2}\psi(\boldsymbol{\theta},\boldsymbol{\tau})
are
\mathbf{0}
.
Together, this yields
\mathbb{E}^{\mathrm{po}}_{\boldsymbol{\theta},\boldsymbol{\tau}}([D\check{\ell}_{\mathrm{pen}}^{\mathrm{po}}\circ\psi](\boldsymbol{\theta},\boldsymbol{\tau}))\,D^{2}\psi(\boldsymbol{\theta},\boldsymbol{\tau})=\mathbf{0}
.
Since (
6.37
) holds for arbitrary
(\boldsymbol{\theta},\boldsymbol{\tau})
(not only for
(\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}},\hat{\boldsymbol{\tau}})
) an equivalent computation as the one leading to (
6.38
) yields
(ommitting the argument
(\boldsymbol{\theta},\boldsymbol{\tau})
in all functions)

\displaystyle(\mathbb{E}^{\mathrm{po}}_{\boldsymbol{\theta},\boldsymbol{\tau}}(D^{2}\ell_{\mathrm{pen}}^{\mathrm{po}}))_{[K]}=\mathbb{E}^{\mathrm{po}}_{\boldsymbol{\theta},\boldsymbol{\tau}}(D^{2}\ell^{\mathrm{mn}}_{\mathrm{pen}})+(D_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}\psi_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}})^{\top}\,\mathbb{E}^{\mathrm{po}}_{\boldsymbol{\theta},\boldsymbol{\tau}}([D^{2}\ell^{\mathrm{po}}\circ\psi_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}])\,D_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}\psi_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}.

(6.40)

Furthermore,

\displaystyle\bigl(\mathbb{E}^{\mathrm{po}}_{\boldsymbol{\theta},\boldsymbol{\tau}}(D^{2}\ell_{\mathrm{pen}}^{\mathrm{po}}(\boldsymbol{\theta},\boldsymbol{\tau}))\bigr)^{-1}

\displaystyle=\penalty\

\displaystyle(D\psi(\boldsymbol{\theta},\boldsymbol{\tau}))^{-1}\,\bigl(\mathbb{E}^{\mathrm{po}}_{\boldsymbol{\theta},\boldsymbol{\tau}}([D^{2}\check{\ell}_{\mathrm{pen}}^{\mathrm{po}}\circ\psi](\boldsymbol{\theta},\boldsymbol{\tau}))\bigr)^{-1}\,((D\psi(\boldsymbol{\theta},\boldsymbol{\tau}))^{\top})^{-1}.

Since (
6.39
) holds for arbitrary
(\boldsymbol{\theta},\boldsymbol{\tau})
(not only for
(\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}},\hat{\boldsymbol{\tau}})
) and since

\displaystyle\bigl(\mathbb{E}^{\mathrm{po}}_{\boldsymbol{\theta},\boldsymbol{\tau}}([D^{2}\check{\ell}_{\mathrm{pen}}^{\mathrm{po}}\circ\psi](\boldsymbol{\theta},\boldsymbol{\tau}))\bigr)^{-1}

\displaystyle=\penalty\

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}(\mathbb{E}^{\mathrm{po}}_{\boldsymbol{\theta},\boldsymbol{\tau}}(D^{2}\ell_{\mathrm{pen}}^{\mathrm{mn}}(\boldsymbol{\theta})))^{-1}&\mathbf{0}\\
\mathbf{0}&(\mathbb{E}^{\mathrm{po}}_{\boldsymbol{\theta},\boldsymbol{\tau}}([D^{2}\ell^{\mathrm{po}}\circ\psi_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}](\boldsymbol{\theta},\boldsymbol{\tau})))^{-1}\end{pmatrix}

has again block matrix structure, setting
\mathbf{A}=(D_{\boldsymbol{\tau}}\psi_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}(\boldsymbol{\theta},\boldsymbol{\tau}))^{-1}\,D_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}\psi_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}(\boldsymbol{\theta},\boldsymbol{\tau})
and
\mathbf{B}:=([D^{2}\ell^{\mathrm{po}}\circ\psi_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}](\boldsymbol{\theta},\boldsymbol{\tau}))^{-1}
, we obtain

\displaystyle\bigl(\mathbb{E}^{\mathrm{po}}_{\boldsymbol{\theta},\boldsymbol{\tau}}(D^{2}\ell_{\mathrm{pen}}^{\mathrm{po}}(\boldsymbol{\theta},\boldsymbol{\tau}))\bigr)^{-1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\hskip-2.84544pt\begin{pmatrix}(\mathbb{E}^{\mathrm{po}}_{\boldsymbol{\theta},\boldsymbol{\tau}}(D^{2}\ell_{\mathrm{pen}}^{\mathrm{mn}}(\boldsymbol{\theta})))^{-1}&-(\mathbb{E}^{\mathrm{po}}_{\boldsymbol{\theta},\boldsymbol{\tau}}(D^{2}\ell_{\mathrm{pen}}^{\mathrm{mn}}(\boldsymbol{\theta})))^{-1}\,\mathbf{A}^{\top}\\
-\mathbf{A}\,(\mathbb{E}^{\mathrm{po}}_{\boldsymbol{\theta},\boldsymbol{\tau}}(D^{2}\ell_{\mathrm{pen}}^{\mathrm{mn}}(\boldsymbol{\theta})))^{-1}&\mathbf{A}\,(\mathbb{E}^{\mathrm{po}}_{\boldsymbol{\theta},\boldsymbol{\tau}}(D^{2}\ell_{\mathrm{pen}}^{\mathrm{mn}}(\boldsymbol{\theta})))^{-1}\,\mathbf{A}^{\top}+(D_{\boldsymbol{\tau}}\psi_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}})^{-1}\,\mathbf{B}\,((D_{\boldsymbol{\tau}}\psi_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}})^{-1})^{\top}\end{pmatrix}\hskip-2.84544pt.

Thus,
((\mathbb{E}^{\mathrm{po}}_{\boldsymbol{\theta},\boldsymbol{\tau}}(\mathbf{F}^{\mathrm{po}}))^{-1})_{[K]}=(\mathbb{E}^{\mathrm{po}}_{\boldsymbol{\theta},\boldsymbol{\tau}}(\mathbf{F}^{\mathrm{mn}}))^{-1}
.
We now show that on the parameter space restricted by
\boldsymbol{\lambda}=\mathbf{n}
, this is identical to

(\mathbb{E}^{\mathrm{mn}}_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\mathbf{F}^{\mathrm{mn}}))^{-1}
.
We have

\displaystyle D^{2}_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}\ell_{\mathrm{pen}}^{\mathrm{mn}}(\boldsymbol{\theta})=\sum_{l=1}^{L}\sum_{g=1}^{\Gamma^{(l)}}n_{g}^{(l)}D^{2}_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}\log(p_{g}^{(l)}(\boldsymbol{\theta}))+D^{2}_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}\pen(\boldsymbol{\theta}).

Taking the expectations
\mathbb{E}^{\mathrm{po}}_{\boldsymbol{\theta},\boldsymbol{\tau}}
and
\mathbb{E}^{\mathrm{mn}}_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}
(replacing the observations
n_{g}^{(l)}
with the random variables
N_{g}^{(l)}
),

D^{2}_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}\log(p_{g}^{(l)}(\boldsymbol{\theta}))
and
D^{2}_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}\pen(\boldsymbol{\theta})
are constants and thus the expectations
\mathbb{E}^{\mathrm{po}}_{\boldsymbol{\theta},\boldsymbol{\tau}}(D^{2}_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}\ell_{\mathrm{pen}}^{\mathrm{mn}}(\boldsymbol{\theta}))
and
\mathbb{E}^{\mathrm{mn}}_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(D^{2}_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}\ell_{\mathrm{pen}}^{\mathrm{mn}}(\boldsymbol{\theta}))
only differ in
\mathbb{E}^{\mathrm{po}}_{\boldsymbol{\theta},\boldsymbol{\tau}}(N_{g}^{(l)})=\lambda_{g}^{(l)}(\boldsymbol{\theta},\boldsymbol{\tau})
and
\mathbb{E}^{\mathrm{mn}}_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(N_{g}^{(l)})=n^{(l)}\,p_{g}^{(l)}(\boldsymbol{\theta})
for
l=1,\ldots,L,\,g=1,\ldots,\Gamma^{(l)}
.
Under the restriction
\lambda^{(l)}=n^{(l)}
for all
l=1,\ldots,L
(corresponding to the estimator
\hat{\lambda}^{(l)}
of (
6.32
)), (
6.31
) gives us
\lambda_{g}^{(l)}(\boldsymbol{\theta},\boldsymbol{\tau})=n^{(l)}\,p_{g}^{(l)}(\boldsymbol{\theta})
.
Thus,
\mathbb{E}^{\mathrm{po}}_{\boldsymbol{\theta},\boldsymbol{\tau}}(D^{2}_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}\ell_{\mathrm{pen}}^{\mathrm{mn}}(\boldsymbol{\theta})\,|\,\boldsymbol{\lambda}=\mathbf{n})=\mathbb{E}^{\mathrm{mn}}_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(D^{2}_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}\ell_{\mathrm{pen}}^{\mathrm{mn}}(\boldsymbol{\theta}))
, which yields
((\mathbb{E}^{\mathrm{po}}_{\boldsymbol{\theta},\boldsymbol{\tau}}(\mathbf{F}^{\mathrm{po}}\,|\,\boldsymbol{\lambda}=\mathbf{n}))^{-1})_{[K]}=(\mathbb{E}^{\mathrm{mn}}_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\mathbf{F}^{\mathrm{mn}}))^{-1}
.
Furthermore, together with (
6.40
) we obtain
(\mathbb{E}^{\mathrm{po}}_{\boldsymbol{\theta},\boldsymbol{\tau}}(\mathbf{F}^{\mathrm{po}}\,|\,\boldsymbol{\lambda}=\mathbf{n}))_{[K]}\neq\mathbb{E}^{\mathrm{mn}}_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\mathbf{F}^{\mathrm{mn}})
.
∎

Formulation and Proof of Lemma
6.16

Recall the notation introduced to construct multinomial data in Section
2.3
,
in particular the sets of indices

\mathcal{I}^{(l)}:=\{i\in\{1,\ldots,N\}\penalty\ |\penalty\ \mathbf{x}_{i}=\mathbf{x}^{(l)}\}
,

\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{d}}^{(l)}:=\{i\in\mathcal{I}^{(l)}\penalty\ |\penalty\ y_{i}\in\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{d}}\}

and
\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{c}}^{(l)}:=\{i\in\mathcal{I}^{(l)}\penalty\ |\penalty\ y_{i}\in\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{c}}\setminus\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{d}}\}

for
l=1,\ldots,L
.
To formalize the statement that finer partitions of
\mathcal{I}^{(l)}
,
l\in\{1,\ldots,L\}
can be used for count data construction, without changing estimation results in Lemma
6.16
below, we extend this notation:
For
l\in\{1,\ldots,L\}
let
\mathcal{I}_{1}^{(l)},\ldots,\mathcal{I}_{\rho^{(l)}}^{(l)}
be a partition of
\mathcal{I}^{(l)}
, where
\rho^{(l)}\in\mathbb{N}
and
\rho^{(l)}\leq n^{(l)}
.
For
r\in\{1,\ldots,\rho^{(l)}\}
, let
\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{d}\,r}^{(l)}:=\{i\in\mathcal{I}_{r}^{(l)}\penalty\ |\penalty\ y_{i}\in\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{d}}\}
and
\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{c}\,r}^{(l)}:=\mathcal{I}_{r}^{(l)}\setminus\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{d}\,r}^{(l)}
.
Furthermore,
for
l\in\{1,\ldots,L\}
and
r\in\{1,\ldots,\rho^{(l)}\}

set

\mathcal{I}_{g}^{(l)}:=\begin{cases}\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{c}}^{(l)}&\text{for}\penalty\ g=1,\ldots,G^{(l)}\\
\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{d}}^{(l)}&\text{for}\penalty\ g=G^{(l)}+1,\ldots,\Gamma^{(l)}\end{cases}\penalty\ \text{and}\penalty\ \mathcal{I}_{g\,r}^{(l)}:=\begin{cases}\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{c}\,r}^{(l)}&\text{for}\penalty\ g=1,\ldots,G^{(l)}\\
\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{d}\,r}^{(l)}&\text{for}\penalty\ g=G^{(l)}+1,\ldots,\Gamma^{(l)}\end{cases}\,.

Recall that
\mathbf{A}_{[K]}
denotes the upper left
K\times K
submatrix of a matrix
\mathbf{A}
.

Lemma 6.16
.

For
g=1,\ldots,\Gamma^{(l)}
,
l=1,\ldots,L
, and
r=1,\ldots,\rho^{(l)}
, let
n_{g}^{(l)}=\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}_{g}^{(l)}}\mathbbm{1}_{U_{g}^{(l)}}(y_{i})

and

n_{g\,r}^{(l)}=\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}_{g\,r}^{(l)}}\mathbbm{1}_{U_{g}^{(l)}}(y_{i})
.
For
n_{g}^{(l)}
, consider the distributional assumption as in Theorem
2.6

1)
, i.e.,
view
n_{g}^{(l)}
as realizations of Poisson variables
N_{g}^{(l)}\sim Po(\lambda_{g}^{(l)}(\boldsymbol{\theta},\boldsymbol{\tau}))
, independent for
g=1,\ldots,\Gamma^{(l)},l=1,\ldots,L
, where the Poisson rates are modeled via

\lambda_{g}^{(l)}(\boldsymbol{\theta},\boldsymbol{\tau})=\exp(\eta_{g}^{(l)}(\boldsymbol{\theta})+\tau^{(l)})

with a predictor
\eta_{g}^{(l)}(\boldsymbol{\theta})\in\mathbb{R}
for an unknown coefficient vector

\boldsymbol{\theta}\in\mathbb{R}^{K}
and

\boldsymbol{\tau}=(\tau^{(1)},\ldots,\tau^{(L)})^{\top}\in\mathbb{R}^{L}
.
Set
\lambda^{(l)}:=\sum_{g=1}^{\Gamma^{(l)}}\lambda_{g}^{(l)}(\boldsymbol{\theta},\boldsymbol{\tau})
,
n^{(l)}:=\sum_{g=1}^{\Gamma^{(l)}}n_{g}^{(l)}
,
\boldsymbol{\lambda}=(\lambda^{(1)},\ldots,\lambda^{(L)})
, and
\mathbf{n}=(n^{(1)},\ldots,n^{(L)})
for
l=1,\ldots,L
.
Given an additive penalty
\pen(\boldsymbol{\theta})
on the log-likelihood, denote the PMLE for
(\boldsymbol{\theta},\boldsymbol{\tau})
with
(\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}},\hat{\boldsymbol{\tau}})
,
the penalized Fisher information at
(\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}},\hat{\boldsymbol{\tau}})

with
\hat{\mathbf{F}}_{\mathrm{pen}}

and the expected penalized Fisher information (with respect to the Poisson distributional assumption given
(\boldsymbol{\theta},\boldsymbol{\tau})
) on the restricted parameter space

\{(\boldsymbol{\theta},\boldsymbol{\tau})\in\mathbb{R}^{K}\times\mathbb{R}^{L}\penalty\ |\penalty\ \boldsymbol{\lambda}=\mathbf{n}\}
with
\mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol{\theta},\boldsymbol{\tau}}(\mathbf{F}_{\mathrm{pen}}\penalty\ |\penalty\ \boldsymbol{\lambda}=\mathbf{n})
.

1)

Viewing
n_{g\,r}^{(l)}
as realizations of Poisson variables
N_{g\,r}^{(l)}\sim Po(\lambda_{g}^{(l)}(\boldsymbol{\theta},\boldsymbol{\tau}))
, independent for
g=1,\ldots,\Gamma^{l)},r=1,\ldots,\rho^{(l)},l=1,\ldots,L
,
given the same penalty term
\pen(\boldsymbol{\theta})
as above,
the PMLE for
\boldsymbol{\theta}
equals
\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}
, the upper left
K\times K
submatrix of the inverse penalized Fisher information at the PMLEs for
(\boldsymbol{\theta},\boldsymbol{\tau})
equals
((\hat{\mathbf{F}}_{\mathrm{pen}})^{-1})_{[K]}

and the upper left
K\times K
submatrix of the inverse expected penalized Fisher information equals
((\mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol{\theta},\boldsymbol{\tau}}(\mathbf{F}_{\mathrm{pen}}\,|\,\boldsymbol{\lambda}=\mathbf{n}))^{-1})_{[K]}
.

2)

Viewing
n_{g\,r}^{(l)}
as realizations of Poisson variables
N_{g\,r}^{(l)}\sim Po(\omega_{g\,r}^{(l)}\,\lambda_{g}^{(l)}(\boldsymbol{\theta},\boldsymbol{\tau})
, independent for
g=1,\ldots,\Gamma^{l)},r=1,\ldots,\rho^{(l)},l=1,\ldots,L
, where
\omega_{g\,r}^{(l)}>0
are arbitrary weights with
\sum_{r=1}^{\rho^{(l)}}\omega_{g\,r}^{(l)}=1
, yields a penalized likelihood function which is proportional to the one of distributional assumption
on
n_{g}^{(l)}
,
i.e., in particular the same PMLE
(\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}},\hat{\boldsymbol{\tau}})
for
(\boldsymbol{\theta},\boldsymbol{\tau})
and identical penalized Fisher informations
\hat{\mathbf{F}}_{\mathrm{pen}}
and
\mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol{\theta},\boldsymbol{\tau}}(\mathbf{F}_{\mathrm{pen}}\penalty\ |\penalty\ \boldsymbol{\lambda}=\mathbf{n})
.

Remark 6.17
.

1)

Distributional assumption
1)
“only” yields equal results with respect to
\boldsymbol{\theta}
.
By adjusting the Poisson rates via weights in
2)
, we obtain the same estimation results with respect to the whole vector of unknown parameters
(\boldsymbol{\theta},\boldsymbol{\tau})
.
As we are only interested in estimating
\boldsymbol{\theta}
, it is sufficient to use distributional assumption
1)
.

2)

By Theorem
2.6
, the estimation results with respect to
\boldsymbol{\theta}
obtained by both distributional assumptions
1)
and
2)
on
n_{g\,r}^{(l)}
are also identical to the ones obtained by the multinomial distributional assumption on
\mathbf{n}^{(l)}=(n_{1}^{(l)},\ldots,n_{\Gamma^{(l)}}^{(l)})\in\mathbb{N}^{\Gamma^{(l)}}
as in Theorem
2.6

2)
.

3)

In the most extreme case,
\rho^{(l)}=n^{(l)}
for all
l=1,\ldots,L
, the construction in the lemma yields one vector of counts per observation (with one count of
1
and all others
0
).
While this is usually not desirable with respect to computation time in practice, since it inflates the size of the data set artificially, it shows the estimation does not depend on aggregation of observations and the presented approach is thus overall coherent.

Proof of Lemma
6.16
.

1)

First, note that per construction, the sets
\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{c}\,1}^{(l)},\ldots,\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{c}\,\rho^{(l)}}^{(l)}
form a partition of
\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{c}}^{(l)}
for
l=1,\ldots,L
(analogously for
\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{d}}^{(l)}
).
This yields

\displaystyle\sum_{r=1}^{\rho^{(l)}}\left(n_{g\,r}^{(l)}\right)=\sum_{r=1}^{\rho^{(l)}}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}_{g\,r}^{(l)}}\mathbbm{1}_{U_{g}^{(l)}}(y_{i})=\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}_{g}^{(l)}}\mathbbm{1}_{U_{g}^{(l)}}(y_{i})=n_{g}^{(l)},

for
g=1,\ldots,\Gamma^{(l)}
.
With
\Pen(\boldsymbol{\theta}):=\exp(\pen(\boldsymbol{\theta}))
, the penalized likelihood function given independent observations
n_{g\,r}^{(l)}
of
N_{g\,r}^{(l)}\sim Po(\lambda_{g}^{(l)}(\boldsymbol{\theta},\boldsymbol{\tau}))
is

\displaystyle\prod_{l=1}^{L}\prod_{g=1}^{\Gamma^{(l)}}\prod_{r=1}^{\rho^{(l)}}\Biggl(\frac{\lambda_{g}^{(l)}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta},\boldsymbol{\tau}\right)^{n_{g\,r}^{(l)}}}{n_{g\,r}^{(l)}!}\exp\left(-\lambda_{g}^{(l)}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta},\boldsymbol{\tau}\right)\right)\Biggr)\,\Pen(\boldsymbol{\theta}).

As in the Proof of Theorem
2.6
, we reparameterize
(\boldsymbol{\theta},\boldsymbol{\tau})=\psi^{-1}(\boldsymbol{\theta},\boldsymbol{\lambda})
to obtain
\lambda_{g}^{(l)}(\boldsymbol{\theta},\boldsymbol{\tau})=\lambda_{g}^{(l)}\left(\psi^{-1}(\boldsymbol{\theta},\boldsymbol{\lambda})\right)=[\exp(\eta_{g}^{(l)}(\boldsymbol{\theta}))\,\lambda^{(l)}]\,/\,[\sum_{g^{\prime}=1}^{\Gamma^{(l)}}\exp(\eta_{g^{\prime}}^{(l)}(\boldsymbol{\theta}))]
.
Then, analogously to (
6.32
), the penalized likelihood is proportional to

\displaystyle\prod_{l=1}^{L}\Biggl(\frac{n^{(l)}!}{\prod_{g=1}^{\Gamma^{(l)}}n_{g}^{(l)}!}\prod_{g=1}^{\Gamma^{(l)}}\Biggl(\frac{\exp\bigl(\eta_{g}^{(l)}(\boldsymbol{\theta})\bigr)}{\sum_{g^{\prime}=1}^{\Gamma^{(l)}}\exp\bigl(\eta_{g^{\prime}}^{(l)}(\boldsymbol{\theta})\bigr)}\Biggr)^{n_{g}^{(l)}}\Biggr)\,\Pen(\boldsymbol{\theta})

\displaystyle\cdot\prod_{l=1}^{L}\Bigl(\frac{(\rho^{(l)}\,\lambda^{(l)})^{n^{(l)}}}{n^{(l)}!}\exp(-\rho^{(l)}\,\lambda^{(l)})\Bigr).

The first part, modeling
\boldsymbol{\theta}
, is identically to the first part in the factorized penalized likelihood (
6.32
) and thus yields
\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}
as PMLE for
\boldsymbol{\theta}
. The second part, which models
\lambda^{(l)}
(and indirectly
\tau^{(l)}
) for
l=1,\ldots,L
, differs by the additional factor
\rho^{(l)}
.
Since the structure of the factorization is equal to the one in (
6.32
), the proof that the upper
K\times K
submatrices of the inverse penalized Fisher information at the PMLE for
(\boldsymbol{\theta},\boldsymbol{\tau})
and of the inverse of the expected version, i.e., the submatrices corresponding to
\boldsymbol{\theta}
, are identical to
((\hat{\mathbf{F}}_{\mathrm{pen}})^{-1})_{[K]}
and
((\mathbb{E}^{\mathrm{po}}_{\boldsymbol{\theta},\boldsymbol{\tau}}(\mathbf{F}_{\mathrm{pen}}\,|\,\boldsymbol{\lambda}=\mathbf{n}))^{-1})_{[K]}
, can be carried out analogously to the respective part of the Proof of Theorem
2.6
.

2)

Using the adjusted Poisson rates, i.e., viewing
n_{g\,r}^{(l)}
as independent observations of
N_{g\,r}^{(l)}\sim Po(\omega_{g\,r}^{(l)}\,\lambda_{g}^{(l)}(\boldsymbol{\theta},\boldsymbol{\tau}))
, where
r=1,\ldots,\rho^{(l)}
,
g=1,\ldots,\Gamma^{(l)}
,
l=1,\ldots,L
, the penalized likelihood function is

\displaystyle\prod_{l=1}^{L}\prod_{g=1}^{\Gamma^{(l)}}\prod_{r=1}^{\rho^{(l)}}\Biggl(\frac{\omega_{g\,r}^{(l)}\,\lambda_{g}^{(l)}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta},\tau^{(l)}\right)^{n_{g\,r}^{(l)}}}{n_{g\,r}^{(l)}!}\exp\left(-\omega_{g\,r}^{(l)}\,\lambda_{g}^{(l)}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta},\tau^{(l)}\right)\right)\Biggr)\,\Pen(\boldsymbol{\theta})

\displaystyle\propto\penalty\

\displaystyle\prod_{l=1}^{L}\prod_{g=1}^{\Gamma^{(l)}}\Bigl(\lambda_{g}^{(l)}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta},\tau^{(l)}\right)^{n_{g}^{(l)}}\exp\left(-\lambda_{g}^{(l)}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta},\tau^{(l)}\right)\right)\Bigr)\,\Pen(\boldsymbol{\theta})=\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{pen}}^{\mathrm{po}}(\boldsymbol{\theta},\boldsymbol{\tau}),

where
\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{pen}}^{\mathrm{po}}(\boldsymbol{\theta},\boldsymbol{\tau})
is the penalized likelihood (
6.30
) given independent observations
n_{g}^{(l)}
of
N_{g}^{(l)}\sim Po(\lambda_{g}^{(l)}(\boldsymbol{\theta},\boldsymbol{\tau}))
. Hence, the penalized likelihoods are proportional and thus yield the same estimation results.
∎

7
Transforming effects to desired reference coding

The function
densreg()
used for fitting density regression models contained in our
R
package
DensityRegression
(developer version on
https://github.com/Eva2703/DensityRegression
)
uses the function
gam()
of the well-known package
mgcv

(
Wood 2017c
)
for Poisson regression. To the best of the author’s knowledge, the reference coding used in
Maier et al. 2025c
cannot be specified directly using
mgcv::gam()
, since it does not seem intended to specify a reference value for a continuous covariate like
year
.
Thus, also the reference
year
cannot be specified directly in
densreg()
.
Instead, we transform the received estimated effects, appropriately.
The model actually estimated via
densreg()
is

\displaystyle f_{\text{\emph{West\_East, c\_age, year}}}

\displaystyle=\tilde{\beta}_{0}\oplus\tilde{\beta}_{\text{\emph{West\_East}}}\oplus\tilde{\beta}_{c\_age}\oplus\tilde{\beta}_{\text{\emph{c\_age, West\_East}}}

\displaystyle\hskip 12.80365pt\oplus\tilde{g}_{0}(year)\oplus\tilde{g}_{\text{\emph{West\_East}}}(year)\oplus\tilde{g}_{c\_age}(year)

\displaystyle\hskip 12.80365pt\oplus\tilde{g}_{\text{\emph{c\_age, West\_East}}}(year),

(7.1)

with reference categories
West
for
West_East
and
other
for
c_age
.
14
14

14

Regarding the interaction effects for
West_East
and
c_age
, note that it is not possible to use the argument
by
of the smoother
mgcv::ti()
with more than one variable.
Thus, we combine
c_age
and
West_East
in a new covariate for the corresponding interaction terms, where all combinations containing either
\text{\emph{West\_East}}=\text{\emph{West}}
or
\text{\emph{c\_age}}=\text{\emph{other}}
are set to the same (reference) category, such that they all have an effect of zero, in accordance with the reference coding.

The effects as discussed in Section
9
, i.e., with reference category
1991
for
year
, are obtained by adding the respective smooth effect for
1991
for (group specific) intercepts and subtracting it for (group specific) smooth effects:

\displaystyle\beta_{0}

\displaystyle=\tilde{\beta}_{0}\oplus\tilde{g}_{0}(1991)

\displaystyle\beta_{\text{\emph{West\_East}}}

\displaystyle=\tilde{\beta}_{\text{\emph{West\_East}}}\oplus\tilde{g}_{\text{\emph{West\_East}}}(1991)

\displaystyle\beta_{\text{\emph{c\_age}}}

\displaystyle=\tilde{\beta}_{\text{\emph{c\_age}}}\oplus\tilde{g}_{\text{\emph{c\_age}}}(1991)

\displaystyle\beta_{\text{\emph{c\_age, West\_East}}}

\displaystyle=\tilde{\beta}_{\text{\emph{c\_age, West\_East}}}\oplus\tilde{g}_{\text{\emph{c\_age, West\_East}}}(1991)

\displaystyle g_{0}(\text{\emph{year}})

\displaystyle=\tilde{g}_{0}(\text{\emph{year}})\ominus\tilde{g}_{0}(1991)

\displaystyle g_{\text{\emph{West\_East}}}(\text{\emph{year}})

\displaystyle=\tilde{g}_{\text{\emph{West\_East}}}(year)\ominus\tilde{g}_{West\_East}(1991)

\displaystyle g_{\text{\emph{c\_age}}}(\text{\emph{year}})

\displaystyle=\tilde{g}_{\text{\emph{c\_age}}}(\text{\emph{year}})\ominus\tilde{g}_{\text{\emph{c\_age}}}(1991)

\displaystyle g_{\text{\emph{c\_age, West\_East}}}(\text{\emph{year}})

\displaystyle=\tilde{g}_{\text{\emph{c\_age, West\_East}}}(\text{\emph{year}})\ominus\tilde{g}_{\text{\emph{c\_age, West\_East}}}(1991)

and the resulting model is equivalent to model (
7.1
), i.e.,

\displaystyle f_{\text{\emph{West\_East, c\_age, year}}}

\displaystyle=\beta_{0}\oplus\beta_{\text{\emph{West\_East}}}\oplus\beta_{c\_age}\oplus\beta_{\text{\emph{c\_age, West\_East}}}

\displaystyle\hskip 12.80365pt\oplus g_{0}(year)\oplus g_{\text{\emph{West\_East}}}(year)\oplus g_{c\_age}(year)

\displaystyle\hskip 12.80365pt\oplus g_{\text{\emph{c\_age, West\_East}}}(year)

\displaystyle=\tilde{\beta}_{0}\oplus\tilde{\beta}_{West\_East}\oplus\tilde{\beta}_{c\_age}\oplus\tilde{\beta}_{c\_age,\,West\_East}

\displaystyle\hskip 12.80365pt\oplus\tilde{g}_{0}(year)\oplus\tilde{g}_{\text{\emph{West\_East}}}(year)\oplus\tilde{g}_{c\_age}(year)

\displaystyle\hskip 12.80365pt\oplus\tilde{g}_{c\_age,\,West\_East}(year).

To obtain confidence regions for the transformed effects, note that all transformations are of the form

\hat{h}=\bigoplus_{j\in\mathcal{J}}\varsigma_{j}\odot\hat{h}_{j}(\mathbf{x}_{i_{j}})=\bigoplus_{j\in\mathcal{J}}\varsigma_{j}\odot(\mathbf{b}_{\mathcal{X},\,j}(\mathbf{x}_{i_{j}})\begin{picture}\put(1.5,0.6){\circle{2.0}}\put(1.5,0.6){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){$\otimes$}}\end{picture}\mathbf{b}_{\mathcal{Y}})^{\top}\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{\mathrm{po},\,j}
,
where
\varsigma_{j}\in\{-1,1\},\penalty\ \mathcal{J}\subseteq\{1,\ldots,J\}
, and
i_{j}\in\{1,\ldots,N\}
for
j\in\mathcal{J}
, where
\hat{h}_{j}(\mathbf{x}_{i_{j}})
denote estimated partial effects, compare Section
2.2
.
Confidence regions can be constructed as in Lemma
2.3
, but letting the covariate
\mathbf{x}
vary with
j\in\mathcal{J}
and adding the signs
\varsigma_{j}
to the definition of the transformation matrix, i.e., setting

\mathbf{A}=\sum_{j\in\mathcal{J}}\varsigma_{j}\cdot(\mathbf{b}_{\mathcal{X}}(\mathbf{x}_{i_{j}})^{\top}\otimes\mathbf{I}_{K_{\mathcal{Y}}})\mathbf{S}_{j}
.

8
Including observation weights

The SOEP data analyzed in Section
9
contains sampling weights for all individual observations.
Denote the sampling weight of the
i
-th observation with
\gamma_{i}
,
i=1,\ldots,N
.
They would reasonably be included by replacing the counts
n_{g}^{(l)}
in Section
2.3
with weighted counts, i.e., setting

n_{g}^{(l)}:=\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}_{g}^{(l)}}\gamma_{i}\mathbbm{1}_{U_{g}^{(l)}}(y_{i})
for
g=1,\ldots,\Gamma^{(l)},l=1,\ldots,L
,
where
\mathcal{I}_{g}^{(l)}:=\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{c}}^{(l)}
, if
g=1,\ldots,G^{(l)}
, and
\mathcal{I}_{g}^{(l)}:=\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{d}}^{(l)}
, if
g=G^{(l)}+1,\ldots,\Gamma^{(l)}
.
However, this is not feasible, since the weights
\gamma_{i}
included in the SOEP data are not natural numbers, but positive real numbers.
15
15

15

Actually, there are even weights of exactly zero. The corresponding observations were removed from the data set, with
151,838
observations having positive real weights remaining.

Thus, also the weighted counts
n_{g}^{(l)}
are not natural numbers and theoretically cannot serve as response observations for a Poisson model.
Consequently, trying to estimate such a model via the function
gam()
of the
R
package
mgcv

(
Wood 2017c
)
, which our newly developed
R
package
DensityRegression
uses to fit Poisson models, produces an error.
In the following, we thus present a work-around, which yields the desired likelihood of a weighted counts Poisson model, but is specifiable in
gam()
.
For this purpose, we use the (unweighted) counts
n_{g}^{(l)}
as defined in Section
2.3
as observed response values, but appropriately weight the log-likelihood contributions (specifiable via the
gam()
-argument
weights
) and include offsets (specifiable in
gam()
via an
offset
in the
formula
of the model) that cancel the undesired terms. In the function
densreg()
, which is used to fit regression models with our approach in our
R
package
DensityRegression
, the sample weights
\gamma_{1},\ldots,\gamma_{N}
can be specified via the argument
sample_weights
.
The weights for the log-likelihood contributions and the offset are then constructed appropriately by
densreg()
.

The weights for the log-likelihood contributions are

\displaystyle v_{g}^{(l)}

\displaystyle:=\begin{cases}\frac{\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}_{g}^{(l)}}\gamma_{i}\mathbbm{1}_{U_{g}^{(l)}}(y_{i})}{\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}_{g}^{(l)}}\mathbbm{1}_{U_{g}^{(l)}}(y_{i})}=\frac{\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}_{g}^{(l)}}\gamma_{i}\mathbbm{1}_{U_{g}^{(l)}}(y_{i})}{n_{g}^{(l)}}&,n_{g}^{(l)}>0\\
1&,n_{g}^{(l)}=0\end{cases},\quad l=1,\ldots,L,\penalty\ g=1,\ldots,\Gamma^{(l)}.

The Poisson rates then are modeled with offsets
-\log v_{g}^{(l)}
, i.e.,
\tilde{\lambda}_{g}^{(l)}:=\exp(\eta_{g}^{(l)}-\log v_{g}^{(l)})=\frac{\lambda_{g}^{(l)}}{v_{g}^{(l)}}
, where
\lambda_{g}^{(l)}:=\exp(\eta_{g}^{(l)})
is the Poisson rate of the corresponding model without offset,
l=1,\ldots,L,\penalty\ g=1,\ldots,\Gamma^{(l)}
.
Then, the
l
-th log-likelihood contribution in the Poisson model with weighted log-likelihood contributions and offsets is (omitting additive constants via
\propto
)

\displaystyle\sum_{g=1}^{\Gamma^{(l)}}v_{g}^{(l)}\cdot\left(n_{g}^{(l)}\log\tilde{\lambda}_{g}^{(l)}-\tilde{\lambda}_{g}^{(l)}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{g=1}^{\Gamma^{(l)}}\left(v_{g}^{(l)}n_{g}^{(l)}\log\lambda_{g}^{(l)}-v_{g}^{(l)}n_{g}^{(l)}\log v_{g}^{(l)}-v_{g}^{(l)}\frac{\lambda_{g}^{(l)}}{v_{g}^{(l)}}\right)

\displaystyle\propto\sum_{g=1}^{\Gamma^{(l)}}\left(v_{g}^{(l)}n_{g}^{(l)}\log\lambda_{g}^{(l)}-\lambda_{g}^{(l)}\right),

which corresponds to the
l
-th log-likelihood contribution in a Poisson model with rate
\lambda_{g}^{(l)}
and observations

\displaystyle v_{g}^{(l)}n_{g}^{(l)}

\displaystyle=\frac{\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}_{g}^{(l)}}\gamma_{i}\mathbbm{1}_{U_{g}^{(l)}}(y_{i})}{\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}_{g}^{(l)}}\mathbbm{1}_{U_{g}^{(l)}}(y_{i})}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}_{g}^{(l)}}\mathbbm{1}_{U_{g}^{(l)}}(y_{i})=\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}_{g}^{(l)}}\gamma_{i}\mathbbm{1}_{U_{g}^{(l)}}(y_{i}),\penalty\ g=1,\ldots,\Gamma^{(l)},

i.e., to the
l
-th log-likelihood contribution of a Poisson model using the weighted counts as observations as desired.

9.3
Estimated effects

This section contains all estimated effects of model (
3.1
) with a sample of their
95\%
confidence region (sample size:
100
). They are structured similar to Figures
3.1
to
3.3
, however with one panel per covariate value to avoid overplotting.
For interaction effects, we display the sum of the intercept, the respective effect, and the corresponding main effects for the estimated conditional densities (left side of the figures).
Note that for some effects, (some) functions sampled from the confidence regions spread out for
s\rightarrow 0
and
s\rightarrow 1
with
s\in(0,1)
, inflating the range of function values.
In these cases we restrict the limits of the y-axis to an interval, which allows to reasonably view the estimated functions, cutting off some of the functions sampled from the confidence regions.

9.4
Estimated conditional densities (predictions)

Figure 9.16
:
Estimated conditional densities [upper
2\times 3
panels] and corresponding relative frequencies [lower
2\times 3
panels] for
West
vs.
East
Germany [rows] and all three values of
c_age
[columns].

Figure
9.16
shows the estimated conditional densities obtained from model (
3.1
) for the different combinations of covariate values in the upper part. The different
years
are illustrated in one panel, respectively, distinguishable from each other via a color gradient and different line types.
For the discrete values with positive probability mass,
0
and
1
, the density values are visualized as dashes and shifted slightly outwards for better distinction.
Their smoothly estimated development over time, as well as the one of dual-earner households (corresponding to the Lebesgue integrals of the densities), is shown explicitly in the lower part of the figure.
We briefly point out some interesting results visible in this figure.
In
West
Germany in all three categories of
c_age
, the share of nonworking women (
s=0
) is decreasing over time while the share of dual-earner households (
s\in(0,1)
) is increasing.
Simultaneously, the probability mass for lower positive income shares increases, indicating an increase of previously nonworking women in part-time employment.
Without considering mixed densities for single- and double-earner couples, but only continuous densities for double-earner couples on
(0,1)
, this might be misinterpreted as a shift from larger to smaller income shares, nicely showing the necessity of considering mixed densities.
Furthermore, there is more probability mass at lower positive income shares for couples with minor children (
0-6
and
7-18
) compared to those without minor children (
other
) in
West
Germany, reflecting that women are rather working part-time in the presence of children.
In
East
Germany the shapes of the densities are less dependent on the age of the youngest child.
Especially for couples without minor children (
other
), the densities are close to symmetric with a mode around
s=0.45
.
For couples with minor children (
0-6
and
7-18
), this is only the case for the earlier
years
, while over time, probability mass tends to shift towards smaller income shares.
Again, this might include previously nonworking women switching to part-time employment even though there is not such a clear decline of nonworking women as in
West
Germany.
Actually, for couples with small children (
0-6
), the frequency of nonworking women even increases from 1991 to 1998 in
East
Germany, reaching a similar level as in
West
Germany, before decreasing again.
All in all, accompanying the findings of
Maier et al. 2025c
, our observations reflect the different social norms established in the two parts of Germany before reunification in 1990: In
East
Germany, comprehensive child care was available and women were expected to work, while in
West
Germany, it was more common for mothers to be unemployed and instead take care of the children themselves.

10
Simulation study

This section contains further figures related to the simulation study presented in Section
4
.
Figure
10.1
is the extended version of Figure
4.1
, including interaction effects.
Figure
10.2
shows the empirical coverage rates for all partial effects based on Lemma
6.15
, i.e., simultaneous in covariates.
Compared to Figure
4.2
, interaction effects are added, while excluding prediction.
Instead, in this section,
\mathrm{relMSE}(\hat{f})
, which is computed based on Lemma
2.3
, is shown in Figure
10.3
together with the empirical coverage rates for all partial effects, also based on Lemma
2.3
, i.e., point-wise in the covariates, since conceptually they belong together.

:
RelMSE for prediction and main effects for different values of
G
[columns] and
N
[rows]. Numbers following a plus-sign at the right of a panel give the number of values larger than
13
(vertical line).
Empty space means all values are visible.

:
Empirical coverage rates (empCR) for all partial effects (simultaneous in covariates) for different values of
G
[columns] and
N
[rows].

:
Empirical coverage rates (empCR) for prediction and all partial effects (point-wise regarding covariates) for different values of

[columns] and
N
[rows].
The boxplots for predictions and smooth effects summarize the coverage rates for the unique covariate values (
177
for prediction,

for effects involving
year
, but not

, and

for interaction effects containing
year
and
East
).
